For the secure file-intake platform, organisation onboarding should be deployment-configurable, supporting manual, privileged API and self-service onboarding with optional approval. Continue planning registration, organisation activation, invitations, verification, anti-abuse controls and tenant lifecycle.
Secure File Intake — Architecture v1.8
Organisation registration · Activation · Invitations · Verification · Anti-abuse · Tenant lifecycle

Decision confirmed: Organisation onboarding is deployment-configurable, supporting manual provisioning, privileged API provisioning and public self-service registration, with optional approval.

I'd implement a single organisation provisioning workflow regardless of how the registration originates. The entry point determines the initial trust level and approval requirements, but not how the organisation is stored or administered.

The key distinction is that creating an organisation must not automatically grant it permission to accept public uploads.

1. Onboarding configuration
Three entry points, controlled by deployment policy:

Method

Initiated by

Default

Manual

Platform administrator

Enabled

Privileged API

Authorised platform service client

Enabled

Self-service

Public applicant

Disabled

Self-service registration should be opt-in for self-hosted installations.

An installation used exclusively by Swoovr, for example, may never need public organisation registration.

Example configuration
{
  "Onboarding": {
    "ManualProvisioning": true,
    "ApiProvisioning": true,
    "SelfService": {
      "Enabled": false,
      "RequireEmailVerification": true,
      "RequirePlatformApproval": true,
      "RequireMfaBeforeActivation": true,
      "AllowedEmailDomains": [],
      "BlockedEmailDomains": [],
      "MaximumPendingRegistrations": 100
    }
  }
}

These settings are deployment-level policy. Individual organisations cannot enable self-service registration for the platform.

An organisation may separately configure whether its own administrators can invite additional members.

2. Organisation lifecycle
I'd separate organisation status from onboarding progress.

An organisation might be active but still have optional onboarding tasks outstanding.

Pending registration

Application created · Email verification required

Pending approval

Platform review, if required

Provisioning

Owner enrolment · Security policy · Key setup

Active

Public intake enabled when readiness checks pass

Operational states should include:

Status

Behaviour

pending

Registration exists; no public uploads

active

Normal operation, subject to resource readiness

suspended

New uploads and privileged mutations blocked

deletion_pending

Controlled offboarding and retention review

deleted

Logical tenant removal completed

Rejected applications should have a separate terminal registration state, without needing to become active organisations.

Suspension should not automatically delete files, disable audit recording or stop legally required retention operations.

3. Registration workflow
For self-service registration, I'd use the following process.

Applicant supplies organisation name and administrator email.

Platform applies registration rate limits and anti-abuse checks.

Applicant verifies control of the email address.

Platform creates or updates a pending registration.

If approval is required, the application enters a review queue.

On approval, the owner receives a short-lived enrolment invitation.

Owner establishes authentication and MFA.

Organisation provisioning and security setup begin.

Readiness checks determine whether public intake can be enabled.

The registration interface should not require encryption keys or storage configuration before the applicant has an authenticated organisation administration session.

Registration UX
Register an organisation
Create an account to manage secure document submissions.

Organisation name
Example Organisation
Administrator email
admin@example.com
Email verification is required before activation.

Continue

Illustrative registration screen; controls are not connected to a live service.
For anti-enumeration, registration responses should not disclose whether an email already belongs to an existing account.

4. Manual and API provisioning
Manual and API provisioning should call the same application service.

POST /api/platform/organisations
Authorization: Bearer <platform-token>
Idempotency-Key: <unique-request-id>
{
  "name": "Example Organisation",
  "owner": {
    "email": "admin@example.com"
  },
  "provisioning": {
    "activation": "after_readiness",
    "requireApproval": false
  }
}

The server decides whether the requesting principal is permitted to bypass approval. A client-supplied requireApproval: false must not override deployment policy.

For service integrations, I'd support external references:

{
  "externalReference": {
    "system": "swoovr",
    "value": "tenant-123"
  }
}

This permits an integrating application to correlate its tenant with the intake organisation without exposing internal database identifiers as the primary integration contract.

External references must be unique within their defined namespace.

5. Organisation owner invitations
The initial owner should be invited rather than created with a default password.

An invitation should contain:

Organisation identifier.

Intended email address.

Intended role.

Expiration timestamp.

Single-use random token.

Invitation status and audit reference.

The raw token should be shown or delivered only once and stored as a keyed hash.

Invitation states
pending
  ├── accepted
  ├── expired
  ├── revoked
  └── superseded
Accepting an invitation must not silently link an account solely because its email matches.

For built-in authentication, require email verification and normal account enrolment.

For external OIDC, bind the membership to the verified issuer and subject after the required invitation checks.

A person who already belongs to another organisation should be able to accept an invitation using the same internal user identity, without merging organisation permissions.

6. Verification levels
I'd avoid treating all organisations as either "verified" or "unverified".

Instead, store independently verified facts.

Verification

Meaning

Email verified

Applicant controls the stated mailbox

Domain verified

Organisation demonstrated control of a DNS domain

Administrator authenticated

Owner completed required authentication

Platform approved

Authorised platform reviewer approved onboarding

Billing verified

Payment arrangement confirmed, if applicable

Legal identity verified

Separately performed business verification, if supported

Domain verification does not establish that a company is legally registered.

Similarly, platform approval is an operational decision, not proof of legal identity.

For the initial release, I'd require only email verification, owner authentication and any deployment-required platform approval.

Domain and business verification can be optional capabilities.

7. Activation readiness
An organisation should become capable of accepting uploads only after passing a readiness check.

Organisation activation requirements

Illustrative progress

3 of 8


Owner enrolled and authenticated


Required MFA completed


Encryption recipient registered and active


Recovery policy explicitly selected


Storage profile configured and tested


Retention policy configured


Audit persistence available


Public upload security policy valid

Interactive planning example, not an actual deployment readiness assessment.
I'd distinguish organisation activation from dropbox activation.

An organisation may be active and able to configure resources even when a particular dropbox cannot accept uploads.

Each dropbox must pass its own encryption, storage, retention and access-policy validation before activation.

8. Anti-abuse controls
Public organisation registration creates a different attack surface from public file uploads.

I'd include:

Rate limits by IP, email and registration fingerprint.

Disposable-email controls as a configurable signal, not an absolute proof of abuse.

Registration quotas and pending-application expiry.

Email verification before allocating expensive resources.

Optional CAPTCHA or equivalent challenge after suspicious activity.

Domain ownership checks where configured.

Duplicate registration detection with manual review.

Delayed resource provisioning until approval.

Restricted capabilities for newly activated self-service organisations.

Avoid collecting unnecessary personal information or permanently storing device fingerprints merely for anti-abuse purposes.

For privacy, retain registration and abuse-prevention data only as long as justified by the applicable policy.

9. Suspension and reactivation
Suspension should support a reason and an enforcement scope.

I'd distinguish:

Administrative suspension: The organisation cannot create or modify resources, but authorised retention and audit operations continue.

Intake suspension: Public upload sessions cannot be created or finalised.

Security lockdown: New sessions, API credentials and sensitive operations are restricted while incident response proceeds.

A full suspension may combine these restrictions.

The platform must define whether existing upload sessions are cancelled immediately. For security lockdown, I would default to invalidating them.

Reactivation requires the relevant readiness and security checks to pass again.

10. Organisation deletion and offboarding
Deleting an organisation must not be equivalent to deleting a database row.

I'd use a controlled workflow:

Authorised actor requests offboarding.

Platform identifies active dropboxes, submissions, holds and retention obligations.

New public intake is disabled.

Organisation receives an opportunity to export permitted metadata and ciphertext.

Retention and legal-hold restrictions are evaluated.

Eligible data is deleted through durable deletion jobs.

External storage deletion is reconciled.

Audit tombstones and required governance records are retained according to policy.

Organisation reaches the terminal deleted state when defined completion criteria are satisfied.

For BYO storage, the platform can revoke its own access and delete objects where permitted, but cannot guarantee deletion of copies retained independently by the storage owner.

I'd make organisation offboarding a high-risk operation requiring independent approval.

11. Database additions
organisation_registrations
organisation_registration_verifications
organisation_approval_decisions
organisation_invitations
organisation_onboarding_progress
organisation_lifecycle_events
organisation_suspensions
organisation_offboarding_requests
organisation_external_references
All lifecycle transitions should be transactional and auditable.

Invitation redemption and activation must use concurrency-safe state transitions.

12. API endpoints
POST /public/organisation-registrations
POST /public/organisation-registrations/verify-email

GET  /api/platform/organisation-registrations
POST /api/platform/organisation-registrations/{id}/approve
POST /api/platform/organisation-registrations/{id}/reject

POST /api/platform/organisations
POST /api/platform/organisations/{id}/suspend
POST /api/platform/organisations/{id}/reactivate

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/readiness
POST /api/organisations/{id}/invitations
POST /public/invitations/{token}/accept

POST /api/organisations/{id}/offboarding-requests
For public invitation acceptance, I'd avoid putting the raw token in a URL path where it may be captured by logs. A safer approach is a landing page that redeems the token through a protected POST request.

13. Implementation priorities
I'd implement onboarding in this order:

Organisation provisioning service and lifecycle state machine.

Manual and privileged API creation.

Initial owner invitations and enrolment.

Activation readiness checks.

Suspension and reactivation.

Self-service registration and email verification.

Optional platform approval workflow.

Anti-abuse and registration quotas.

Controlled offboarding.

Domain and advanced business verification.

This gives standalone and enterprise deployments a working provisioning process before introducing public registration.

14. Next decision: organisation limits and quotas
We now need to decide how organisations receive resource limits.

This affects storage consumption, upload concurrency, dropbox creation, API usage and whether the platform can eventually support managed hosting or commercial plans.


No built-in organisation quotas

Operators manage capacity through infrastructure and external controls.


Fixed deployment-wide quotas

Every organisation receives the same resource limits, configured by the deployment operator.


Policy-based quotas per organisation

Deployment defaults with organisation-specific limits, API/UI management, usage monitoring and enforcement.

For the secure file-intake platform, resource limits should use policy-based quotas per organisation with deployment defaults and organisation-specific limits. Continue planning storage quotas, bandwidth, upload concurrency, API limits, usage accounting, enforcement and operational alerts.
Secure File Intake — Architecture v1.9
Resource quotas · Bandwidth · Concurrency · API limits · Usage accounting · Enforcement · Alerts

Decision confirmed: Resource limits will be policy-based, with deployment defaults and organisation-specific limits.

I'd build quotas as a first-class platform service rather than embedding limits separately in the upload API, storage providers and background workers.

The main requirements are:

Predictable limits for standalone and multi-tenant deployments.

No cross-tenant resource exhaustion.

Accurate accounting across multiple API instances.

Configurable limits without introducing billing dependencies.

Clear reporting through API, CLI and optional web administration.

Safe behaviour when limits are reached.

Importantly, quotas are not billing. The platform can measure usage without implementing subscriptions, invoices or payment processing.

1. Quota hierarchy
I'd establish four policy levels.

Deployment limits

Hard ceilings protecting the entire installation

Organisation policy

Default limits or approved organisation-specific overrides

Dropbox limits

Optional restrictions for an individual dropbox

Submission limits

File count, file size, total bytes and upload-session constraints

The effective limit is the most restrictive applicable limit, unless a deployment-authorised policy explicitly permits an exception.

An organisation administrator can lower its own limits but cannot exceed deployment-enforced ceilings.

For example, an organisation might have 500 GiB of storage capacity while a particular dropbox allows only 2 GiB of total uploaded data.

2. Quota categories
I'd define quotas in a versioned registry.

Category

Example metric

Enforcement

Storage

Retained ciphertext bytes

Hard

Storage objects

Number of retained encrypted files

Hard

Dropboxes

Active dropbox count

Hard

Submissions

Completed submissions per day

Configurable

Upload size

Maximum file and submission bytes

Hard

Upload concurrency

Active sessions and transfers

Hard

Bandwidth

Ingress/egress bytes per period

Hard or soft

API requests

Requests per minute

Rate limit

Webhooks

Events delivered per period

Soft/backpressure

Background jobs

Concurrent organisation jobs

Fair scheduling

Audit exports

Concurrent and periodic exports

Hard

I'd distinguish hard quotas from soft thresholds.

A hard quota blocks new operations that would exceed the limit.

A soft threshold produces alerts without automatically preventing an operation.

3. Storage accounting
Storage accounting is more complicated than simply summing file sizes.

A submitted file includes encrypted chunks, manifests, recipient envelopes and potentially provider-side versions.

I'd track:

logical_plaintext_bytes
ciphertext_payload_bytes
metadata_bytes
retained_object_bytes
pending_upload_bytes
superseded_file_bytes
deletion_pending_bytes
The server cannot independently verify plaintext size under end-to-end encryption. A client-declared plaintext length should therefore be treated as untrusted metadata.

Quota enforcement must use independently verifiable ciphertext lengths, not client-reported plaintext sizes.

For operational reporting, I'd distinguish:

Committed storage: Successfully finalised ciphertext still retained.

Reserved storage: Capacity allocated to active uploads.

Pending deletion: Objects awaiting confirmed deletion.

Physical provider usage: Actual provider-reported storage where available.

These values will not always be identical.

Provider versioning, multipart uploads, backups and delayed deletion can produce additional storage consumption.

4. Storage reservations
I'd use reservations to prevent concurrent uploads from exceeding quotas.

Suppose an organisation has 10 GiB remaining and two uploaders each attempt to upload 8 GiB.

Without reservations, both could begin uploading and exceed the limit.

With reservations, the first upload reserves 8 GiB, leaving 2 GiB available.

The second upload is rejected or offered a smaller permitted allocation.

Reservation lifecycle
1

Reserve capacity

Atomically check available quota and create a reservation

2

Upload ciphertext

Track transferred bytes and enforce reserved maximum

3

Finalise

Verify stored ciphertext and convert reservation into committed usage

4

Release unused capacity

Return unused reservation after successful completion

5

Expire abandoned uploads

Reconcile multipart objects before releasing reservations

Reservations must be transactional and safe across multiple API replicas.

For PostgreSQL, I'd use atomic conditional updates or row locking.

For SQLite, use short write transactions.

A reservation must not be released merely because an upload session expired; storage cleanup must first confirm whether billable objects remain.

5. Example quota policy
{
  "organisationId": "org_123",
  "policyVersion": 4,
  "limits": {
    "storageBytes": 536870912000,
    "maxActiveDropboxes": 100,
    "maxFileBytes": 1073741824,
    "maxSubmissionBytes": 5368709120,
    "maxFilesPerSubmission": 20,
    "maxConcurrentUploads": 25,
    "apiRequestsPerMinute": 600,
    "monthlyIngressBytes": 1099511627776,
    "monthlyEgressBytes": 549755813888
  },
  "alerts": {
    "warningPercent": 80,
    "criticalPercent": 95
  }
}

The values are illustrative, not proposed universal defaults.

I'd store all byte limits as 64-bit integers, use UTC for accounting periods and require explicit units in the API.

For time-window quotas, the policy must also specify the period boundary and whether usage resets on a calendar schedule or rolling window.

6. Bandwidth quotas
Bandwidth needs separate accounting for inbound and outbound traffic.

Even though the platform is inbound-only, authorised reviewers and automation workers still download ciphertext.

Therefore:

Ingress: Encrypted uploads entering platform-managed or organisation-managed storage.

Egress: Ciphertext retrieved by authorised organisation clients.

Internal transfer: Storage migrations, replication and operational transfers.

I'd keep internal transfer accounting separate from user-facing bandwidth quotas.

For direct-to-S3/R2 uploads, the API cannot rely on HTTP request sizes because the data bypasses it.

The storage adapter must reconcile transfer receipts, object sizes and provider metrics where supported.

Provider metrics may be delayed or incomplete, so bandwidth usage should have an explicit accuracy classification.

For BYO storage, the organisation's provider remains the authority for actual storage and network charges.

7. Upload concurrency
I'd distinguish three limits:

Limit

Purpose

Active upload sessions

Prevent excessive reserved capacity

Active file transfers

Control simultaneous storage operations

Active uploads per dropbox

Prevent one public dropbox monopolising capacity

The limits should be enforced through short-lived leases.

A crashed API instance must not permanently consume a concurrency slot.

For distributed deployments, concurrency enforcement needs shared state. Local in-memory counters are insufficient.

I'd avoid using Redis as a mandatory dependency. The existing database-backed coordination layer is adequate initially.

Lease model
upload_concurrency_leases
  lease_id
  organisation_id
  dropbox_id
  upload_session_id
  expires_at
  heartbeat_at
  state
Lease expiry should be reconciled with upload-session and storage state before capacity is reused.

8. API rate limits
Rate limits are distinct from quotas.

A quota might allow 100,000 API requests per day.

A rate limit might allow only 100 requests per minute to prevent bursts.

I'd apply limits at several dimensions:

Deployment-wide.

Organisation.

API client.

Authenticated human.

Public dropbox.

Source IP or network, where appropriate.

Public endpoints need stricter controls for email verification, PIN attempts, one-time code redemption and upload-session creation.

An API client should receive standard rate-limit response headers where appropriate, plus a Retry-After value for temporary throttling.

Example response
HTTP/1.1 429 Too Many Requests
Retry-After: 30
Content-Type: application/problem+json
{
  "type": "https://docs.example.com/problems/rate-limited",
  "title": "Rate limit exceeded",
  "status": 429,
  "detail": "Retry after the indicated interval."
}

For resource quota exhaustion, I'd use a distinct application problem type so clients can differentiate it from temporary throttling.

9. Usage accounting architecture
I'd use a durable usage ledger with materialised counters.

Uploads

Downloads

API requests

Usage accounting service

Idempotent events · Reservations · Reconciliation

Usage ledger

Durable accounting records

Usage counters

Fast quota checks

I would not record every transferred chunk as a permanent accounting event unless required.

Instead, record meaningful events such as reservation created, reservation adjusted, file committed, file deleted and transfer reconciled.

API request rates can use bounded aggregation windows rather than an unbounded per-request ledger.

Suggested tables
quota_policies
quota_policy_assignments
quota_reservations
quota_usage_counters
quota_usage_events
quota_accounting_periods
quota_reconciliation_runs
quota_alert_rules
quota_alert_events
upload_concurrency_leases
Each usage event needs a stable idempotency key.

A retry must not double-count a committed file.

10. Enforcement behaviour
I'd define consistent outcomes.

Condition

Behaviour

File exceeds size limit

Reject before upload

Organisation storage exhausted

Reject new reservations

Concurrent upload limit reached

Temporary throttling

API rate limit exceeded

HTTP 429

Monthly bandwidth exhausted

Reject or throttle new transfers according to policy

Active dropbox limit reached

Reject activation/creation as defined by policy

Storage usage crosses warning threshold

Alert only

Storage usage crosses critical threshold

Alert and optionally restrict new intake

Existing files exceed reduced quota

Preserve files; restrict new consumption

Reducing a quota must never trigger automatic deletion.

Likewise, exceeding a quota must not bypass retention, legal holds or deletion approvals.

Important availability rule
Quota exhaustion should not prevent authorised administrators from accessing security controls, requesting deletion, exporting audit records or resolving incidents.

I'd reserve a separate operational capacity budget for essential administration.

11. Operational alerts
I'd support configurable thresholds and event-driven alerts.

Examples:

quota.storage.warning
quota.storage.critical
quota.storage.exceeded
quota.bandwidth.warning
quota.bandwidth.exceeded
quota.upload_concurrency.saturated
quota.api_rate.sustained_throttling
quota.reservation.leaked
quota.accounting.drift_detected
quota.storage_provider.capacity_warning
Alerts should be deduplicated and have cooldowns.

A quota remaining above 95% for several hours should not generate thousands of repeated notifications.

The platform should expose alerts through the admin UI, API, CLI, metrics and optional webhook forwarding.

12. Organisation usage dashboard
Resource Usage
Example
Storage

382 GiB / 500 GiB

740 GiB / 1 TiB

Monthly egress

210 GiB / 500 GiB

Active dropboxes

68 / 100

Illustrative values. Real usage may lag behind storage-provider reconciliation.

The dashboard should distinguish current usage, reserved capacity and pending deletion where relevant.

For platform administrators, the same view can aggregate organisations and identify unusually high consumption.

13. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/quotas
PUT  /api/organisations/{id}/quotas

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/usage

Organisation administrators may view usage and request lower limits.

Increasing an organisation's limits beyond its assigned policy requires platform-level authorisation.

Quota changes should be audited and versioned.

14. Implementation priorities
I'd build this in the following order:

Quota policy registry and effective-policy resolution.

Storage reservations and committed usage counters.

Upload concurrency leases.

API rate limiting.

Usage events and idempotent accounting.

Storage-provider reconciliation.

Bandwidth accounting.

Alerts and dashboards.

Advanced per-organisation overrides.

Usage exports and historical reporting.

For the first end-to-end release, storage reservations, file-size limits and concurrency controls are the essential components.

15. Next decision: deployment topology and tenant isolation
We've already selected standalone and distributed deployment support. We now need to decide whether a distributed deployment should support multiple organisations in one shared database and storage environment, or provide stronger per-organisation isolation options.


Shared multi-tenant infrastructure

Organisations share the database and infrastructure, with strict logical isolation enforced by the application and database policies.


Dedicated infrastructure per organisation

Each organisation uses its own database and storage resources, prioritising isolation over operational simplicity.


Hybrid isolation model

Shared infrastructure by default, with optional dedicated database and storage resources for selected organisations.

For the secure file-intake platform, tenant isolation should use a hybrid model with shared infrastructure by default and optional dedicated database and storage resources per organisation. Continue planning database isolation, storage isolation, tenant routing, migration, operational boundaries and deployment complexity.
Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.0
Hybrid tenant isolation · Database routing · Dedicated storage · Tenant migration · Operations · Deployment complexity

Decision confirmed: The platform supports shared multi-tenant infrastructure by default, with optional dedicated database and storage resources for individual organisations.

I would implement this as a shared control plane with independently assignable tenant data planes.

This is an important architectural decision because it affects database design, transactions, background workers, audit, backup and migration. It is worth establishing the boundaries before implementation.

1. Isolation tiers
I'd support four configurations, with database and storage isolation independently selectable.

Tier

Database

Storage

Typical use

Shared

Shared PostgreSQL

Shared bucket

Standard organisations

Dedicated storage

Shared PostgreSQL

Dedicated bucket/account

Organisations requiring storage separation

Dedicated database

Dedicated PostgreSQL database

Shared bucket

Organisations requiring database isolation

Fully dedicated

Dedicated PostgreSQL database

Dedicated bucket/account

Higher-isolation organisations

These are deployment configurations, not commercial subscription tiers.

For standalone installations, SQLite and local filesystem storage remain supported. The hybrid model applies primarily to distributed PostgreSQL deployments.

Dedicated resources provide stronger infrastructure separation, but do not automatically provide complete security isolation if the same application processes and administrative credentials can access every tenant.

2. Control plane versus data plane
Shared control plane

Organisation registry · Identity · Routing · Policy · Operations

Tenant routing service

Resolves authoritative database and storage assignments

Shared data plane

Shared PostgreSQL · Shared storage

Default
Dedicated data plane

Tenant PostgreSQL · Dedicated storage

Optional
The control plane must know where each organisation's data resides, but should not store its encrypted file contents.

Control-plane data
Organisation identity and lifecycle.

Tenant routing assignments.

Deployment-level security policy.

Platform administrators and governance.

Infrastructure health and migration orchestration.

References to database and storage credentials.

Platform-level audit and operations.

Tenant data-plane data
Dropboxes and access policies.

Submissions and revisions.

Encrypted file metadata and envelopes.

Organisation-specific retention and legal holds.

Organisation members' resource permissions.

Organisation audit events.

Usage counters and quota reservations.

There will be some shared identity references across the boundary. Those should use stable IDs rather than database foreign keys spanning separate databases.

3. Database isolation
Shared PostgreSQL
For standard organisations, I'd use shared tables with an explicit organisation_id.

CREATE TABLE submissions (
    id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
    organisation_id UUID NOT NULL,
    dropbox_id UUID NOT NULL,
    status TEXT NOT NULL,
    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

CREATE INDEX ix_submissions_organisation
    ON submissions (organisation_id, created_at DESC);

Every tenant-owned table must include the organisation identifier unless its ownership is unambiguously enforced through another mechanism.

I'd favour explicit organisation identifiers because they simplify auditing, partitioning, migrations and isolation testing.

PostgreSQL row-level security can provide defence in depth.

For example:

ALTER TABLE submissions ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE submissions FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;

CREATE POLICY submissions_tenant_isolation
ON submissions
USING (
    organisation_id =
        NULLIF(current_setting('app.organisation_id', true), '')::uuid
)
WITH CHECK (
    organisation_id =
        NULLIF(current_setting('app.organisation_id', true), '')::uuid
);

The application must set the tenant context transaction-locally using a trusted, validated organisation identifier.

The runtime database role must not own the tables or have BYPASSRLS, and connection pooling must not leak tenant context between requests.

RLS supplements application authorisation; it does not replace it.

Dedicated PostgreSQL
A dedicated tenant database should use the same logical tenant schema as the shared database.

This is critical.

I would not maintain separate entity models or migrations for shared and dedicated tenants.

A dedicated database can retain organisation_id columns for schema compatibility and additional validation.

The benefit is that moving a tenant between database tiers does not require rewriting every query.

4. Database routing
Tenant routing must be resolved server-side.

A client should never be able to select a database by supplying a connection string, storage profile or arbitrary routing header.

I'd use a registry similar to:

tenant_database_assignments
  organisation_id
  database_cluster_id
  database_name
  assignment_version
  state
  assigned_at
  updated_at
A separate registry holds database cluster configuration and references to secrets.

Routing interface
public interface ITenantDatabaseResolver
{
    ValueTask<TenantDatabaseAssignment> ResolveAsync(
        Guid organisationId,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record TenantDatabaseAssignment(
    Guid OrganisationId,
    string DatabaseClusterId,
    string DatabaseName,
    long AssignmentVersion);

The resolver returns an authorised, server-controlled assignment.

The application then obtains a connection through a bounded connection-factory pool.

Do not create an unbounded number of connection pools when hundreds or thousands of dedicated databases exist.

Routing cache
A short-lived cache is acceptable, but migrations require an explicit invalidation mechanism.

Every routing assignment should carry a version.

Workers and API instances must reject stale assignments during tenant cutover rather than continuing to write to the old database.

5. Storage isolation
We already agreed that storage may be deployment-managed or organisation-managed.

Hybrid tenant isolation extends that model.

I'd support:

Storage mode

Isolation

Shared bucket

Tenant-specific object namespace with scoped application access

Dedicated bucket

Separate bucket with tenant-specific credentials

Dedicated account

Separate cloud account/project and credentials

BYO storage

Organisation-owned provider credentials and policies

Local filesystem

Dedicated directory or volume with filesystem isolation

For a shared bucket, a prefix is not itself a security boundary.

Storage credentials and object-access policies must restrict what the application or tenant can access.

For direct uploads, issue credentials or presigned operations scoped to a specific upload session and object key.

Object identifiers
I'd use opaque object keys:

tenants/{organisation-id}/objects/{random-object-id}
The path is an organisational convention, not proof of authorisation.

The API must resolve the authoritative object key from its own database.

Never accept an arbitrary object key from a public upload or download request.

6. Tenant migration
Migration between shared and dedicated infrastructure must be an explicit, durable operation.

I'd support these transitions:

Shared database → dedicated database.

Dedicated database → shared database.

Shared storage → dedicated storage.

Dedicated storage → another provider.

Fully dedicated → another dedicated environment.

The migration engine should handle database and storage separately, but coordinate them when both change.

Migration workflow
1

Preflight

Validate destination schema, capacity, credentials and tenant policy

2

Prepare destination

Create tenant schema and storage resources

3

Copy and verify

Transfer tenant records and ciphertext; verify counts, hashes and references

4

Quiesce writes

Pause new writes and drain or cancel active sessions and jobs

5

Final synchronisation

Copy remaining changes and reconcile source/destination state

6

Cut over routing

Atomically publish a new authoritative tenant assignment

7

Verify and retire source

Check health, retain rollback evidence and clean up under policy

I'd choose a write-freeze cutover for the first implementation, rather than attempting live dual-write migration.

Dual writes across independent databases create substantial consistency and rollback problems.

A controlled maintenance window is more predictable and easier to verify.

Critical migration safeguards
Capture a consistent source snapshot.

Include all tenant records, jobs, audit history and encryption envelopes.

Preserve stable IDs and audit sequence numbers.

Do not decrypt files during migration.

Verify ciphertext integrity.

Block new writes during final cutover.

Use fencing to prevent stale workers from writing to the old assignment.

Do not delete the source until the destination has passed validation.

Rollback must be defined separately for failures before and after destination writes resume.

Once new writes occur on the destination, switching back is not simply a routing change.

7. Background worker isolation
The existing durable job system needs to become tenant-routing aware.

Every tenant job should carry:

{
  "jobId": "job_123",
  "organisationId": "org_456",
  "type": "retention.evaluate",
  "routingVersion": 12,
  "payloadVersion": 1
}

Workers must resolve the current tenant assignment before executing.

If the job's routing version is stale, the worker should refresh or safely reschedule it.

A tenant migration must account for leased jobs, retries and outbox events.

I'd use separate logical queues or scheduling partitions for tenants where required, without mandating separate message brokers.

For high-isolation tenants, dedicated worker processes can be an optional deployment feature.

8. Cross-database transactions
A shared control plane and dedicated tenant databases introduce distributed consistency problems.

I would avoid distributed database transactions.

Instead:

Use local transactions within each database.

Use durable outbox events.

Make operations idempotent.

Use explicit workflow state machines for cross-boundary operations.

Reconcile partial failures.

For example, organisation activation may require updating the control-plane lifecycle state and preparing a tenant database.

Those actions cannot be assumed atomic.

The organisation should remain in a provisioning state until both operations are confirmed.

9. Audit isolation
Tenant audit events should remain in the tenant data plane.

Platform-level administrative and routing events belong to the control plane.

For dedicated tenants, audit records may be stored in the dedicated database, with signed checkpoints exported to an independently controlled destination.

Migration must preserve audit-chain continuity.

I'd record a signed migration checkpoint containing the source assignment, destination assignment, tenant sequence and verified snapshot digest.

This provides evidence of continuity without claiming that a database administrator cannot tamper with all local records.

10. Backup and disaster recovery
Hybrid isolation requires different backup scopes.

Resource

Backup responsibility

Control-plane database

Platform operator

Shared tenant database

Platform operator

Dedicated platform-managed database

Platform operator

BYO tenant database, if supported

Explicitly assigned by contract/configuration

Shared storage

Platform operator

Dedicated platform-managed storage

Platform operator

BYO storage

Organisation, unless separately arranged

Organisation private keys

Organisation

For the first release, I'd support platform-managed dedicated databases.

Organisation-managed database credentials introduce additional security and compatibility concerns and can be considered separately.

Backups must be tested through actual restoration.

A dedicated database backup should permit restoring that tenant without requiring restoration of unrelated organisations.

For shared databases, tenant-level restoration requires a controlled extraction and reconciliation process rather than simply restoring the entire database over production.

11. Operational boundaries
Platform operators should be able to see infrastructure health across all tenants without automatically seeing tenant-sensitive content.

I'd separate:

Platform operational visibility: database availability, storage health, job failures, quotas, routing status and audit integrity.

Tenant administration: dropboxes, submission metadata, members, encryption keys and retention policies.

Cryptographic access: possession of an authorised private key, independently of administrative access.

A dedicated tenant database does not remove the need for RBAC or cryptographic separation.

12. Deployment complexity
The hybrid model increases complexity in several areas.

Area

Shared only

Hybrid

Database connections

One primary pool

Multiple bounded pools

Schema migrations

One tenant schema deployment

Schema version tracking per database

Backups

Shared backup process

Shared and tenant-specific backups

Tenant routing

Simple

Authoritative routing registry

Worker scheduling

Shared

Routing-aware and optionally isolated

Tenant migration

Not required

Dedicated migration engine

Monitoring

Aggregate

Aggregate plus per-database health

Incident response

Shared blast radius

More granular isolation options

I would implement shared tenancy first, but design the repository and data-access abstractions for hybrid routing from the beginning.

That avoids introducing dedicated databases through a later architectural rewrite.

13. Suggested database layout
Control Plane PostgreSQL
├── platform_organisations
├── platform_identities
├── platform_roles
├── tenant_database_assignments
├── database_clusters
├── storage_resource_registry
├── platform_operations
├── platform_audit_events
└── tenant_migration_operations

Shared Tenant PostgreSQL
├── dropboxes
├── submissions
├── submission_revisions
├── encrypted_files
├── file_key_envelopes
├── retention_policies
├── legal_holds
├── tenant_audit_events
├── usage_counters
└── durable_jobs

Dedicated Tenant PostgreSQL
└── Same tenant schema as shared database
For standalone SQLite deployments, the control-plane and tenant schemas can reside in the same database while remaining logically separated in the application.

14. Implementation priorities
I'd implement hybrid isolation in this order:

Shared control-plane and tenant-data-plane boundaries.

Tenant-scoped repository interfaces.

Shared PostgreSQL with RLS and isolation tests.

Authoritative tenant routing registry.

Dedicated storage profiles and scoped credentials.

Dedicated PostgreSQL database provisioning.

Routing-aware workers and connection pooling.

Tenant migration engine with write-freeze cutover.

Dedicated backup and restore workflows.

Optional dedicated worker deployments.

The main architectural constraint is that no application service should assume all organisations share one database connection. That assumption is difficult to remove later.

15. Next decision: hosting model
We now need to decide how much deployment infrastructure the product should manage itself.

The application already supports standalone and distributed operation. The remaining question is whether it should provision cloud infrastructure or simply run on infrastructure supplied by the operator.


Operator-managed infrastructure

The platform connects to databases, storage and infrastructure provisioned by the deployment operator. Includes deployment examples and validation tools.


Built-in infrastructure provisioning

The platform can create and manage supported cloud databases, buckets and other infrastructure resources.


Optional provisioning integrations

Operator-managed by default, with optional Terraform/OpenTofu or provider-specific provisioning integrations.

For the secure file-intake platform, hosting should use operator-managed infrastructure only. Continue planning infrastructure provisioning, deployment automation, secrets, upgrades, environment setup and operations.
Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.1
Operator-managed infrastructure · Deployment automation · Secrets · Upgrades · Environment configuration · Operations

Decision confirmed: Secure File Intake will not create, purchase, resize or manage cloud infrastructure.

The deployment operator supplies the database, storage, networking, certificates and any supporting infrastructure. The application validates its configuration, connects to those resources and manages its own application lifecycle.

This applies equally to standalone, containerised and distributed deployments.

1. Infrastructure responsibility boundaries
I'd establish a strict separation between infrastructure ownership and application management.

Resource

Operator responsibility

Application responsibility

Compute

Servers, containers, orchestration

Application processes and health

Database

Provisioning, backups, credentials

Schema, queries, migrations

Object storage

Buckets, accounts, access policies

Encrypted objects and lifecycle

Networking

DNS, firewall, ingress, TLS

Routes, headers, security validation

Secrets

Secret stores and delivery

Loading, validation, redaction

Monitoring

Monitoring infrastructure

Metrics, traces, health endpoints

Deployment

Rollout and rollback execution

Version checks and readiness

Tenant isolation

Dedicated resources where required

Tenant routing and access enforcement

The application may create or delete objects inside an authorised storage profile, but it must not provision the underlying bucket or cloud account.

Likewise, it can migrate its database schema but must not create a managed PostgreSQL instance.

2. Supported deployment modes
I'd retain three deployment configurations.

Standalone

Single Native AOT executable with SQLite, local filesystem or configured object storage, and in-process background workers.

Small installations
Container deployment

OCI image with configurable API and worker roles, connected to operator-provided database and storage.

Standard hosting
Distributed deployment

Multiple API replicas and worker instances using PostgreSQL, shared storage and authoritative tenant routing.

Higher scale
All three modes should run the same domain and application services.

Standalone deployments should not require Redis, RabbitMQ, Kubernetes or an external identity provider.

3. Deployment artifacts
I'd publish:

releases/
├── linux-x64/
├── linux-arm64/
├── win-x64/
├── osx-arm64/
├── containers/
├── compose/
├── kubernetes/
├── configuration/
├── migrations/
├── checksums/
├── sbom/
└── signatures/
The Linux, Windows and macOS releases would contain platform-specific Native AOT executables.

Container images should be multi-architecture where practical.

Each release must identify its application version, supported database schema range, configuration schema version, supported encryption envelope versions and API compatibility.

I would use immutable release artifacts rather than building application code directly on production servers.

4. Deployment configuration
Configuration should follow standard .NET conventions, with strict startup validation.

Suggested precedence:

Packaged defaults.

appsettings.json.

Environment-specific configuration.

Environment variables.

Explicit command-line arguments.

Sensitive configuration should come from environment variables, mounted secret files or an operator-managed secret provider.

Example production configuration
{
  "Deployment": {
    "Mode": "distributed",
    "Role": "api",
    "PublicBaseUrl": "https://intake.example.com"
  },
  "Database": {
    "Provider": "postgresql",
    "ConnectionStringSecret": "database/main"
  },
  "Storage": {
    "Provider": "s3",
    "Bucket": "secure-intake-data",
    "Endpoint": "https://storage.example.com",
    "CredentialsSecret": "storage/primary"
  },
  "Security": {
    "Profile": "production",
    "RequireHttps": true
  },
  "Workers": {
    "Enabled": false
  }
}

Secret references are logical identifiers. The operator configures how they resolve.

I'd prohibit credentials directly in normal configuration files for production unless explicitly delivered through a protected secret-file mechanism.

5. Secrets management
Secrets fall into several categories.

Secret

Used for

Database credentials

Control-plane and tenant database access

Storage credentials

Ciphertext object operations

Authentication signing keys

Tokens and session infrastructure

Session encryption keys

Protecting application session state

Webhook signing secrets

Authenticating outgoing events

Audit signing keys

Audit checkpoint signatures

Email provider credentials

Verification and invitation delivery

External OIDC credentials

Identity-provider integrations

Organisation private decryption keys are deliberately excluded.

The intake server should not possess them.

Secret provider abstraction
public interface ISecretResolver
{
    ValueTask<SecretValue> ResolveAsync(
        string reference,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record SecretValue(
    ReadOnlyMemory<byte> Value,
    string? Version);

Implementations could include environment variables, mounted files and supported external secret stores.

I would start with environment variables and mounted files, then add provider-specific integrations only where justified.

Secrets should be redacted from logs, diagnostics, exception messages and support bundles.

6. Secret rotation
Rotation must not require rebuilding the application.

For secrets that support overlapping validity, I'd allow a controlled transition:

1

Install new secret

Operator updates the secret source

2

Validate access

Application confirms the new credential works

3

Activate new version

New operations use the replacement credential

4

Drain old usage

Existing connections and sessions finish or are revoked

5

Revoke old secret

Operator removes the previous credential

Not every secret supports hot reload.

Database credentials, signing keys and storage credentials may require different rotation mechanisms.

I'd document each supported rotation procedure explicitly.

7. Deployment automation
The application should ship with deployment examples, but not an infrastructure provisioning engine.

For example:

secure-intake config validate

secure-intake database status

secure-intake database migrate

secure-intake storage test

secure-intake deployment preflight

secure-intake deployment readiness

These commands inspect or manage application state inside already-provisioned resources.

They must not create cloud databases, buckets, networks or virtual machines.

For automation, all commands should support machine-readable JSON output and meaningful exit codes.

Exit codes
Code

Meaning

0

Success

2

Invalid configuration

3

Authentication or authorisation failure

4

Dependency unavailable

5

Migration or schema incompatibility

6

Security policy violation

7

Operation failed

8

Partial completion requiring reconciliation

These codes are proposed CLI conventions, not HTTP status codes.

8. Database migrations
I'd ship versioned, checksum-verified migrations for SQLite and PostgreSQL.

For distributed deployments, migrations should run as a separate deployment step.

The API should not automatically apply schema changes during startup when multiple replicas are running.

Upgrade workflow
1

Preflight

Check configuration, schema compatibility and dependencies

2

Backup

Operator verifies recoverable database and storage backups

3

Expand schema

Apply backward-compatible migrations

4

Deploy application

Roll out API and worker instances

5

Verify

Check health, job processing, audit and storage

6

Contract schema

Remove obsolete structures only after compatibility window

For hybrid tenancy, migration status must be tracked per database assignment.

A release should declare which schema versions it supports.

An API instance encountering an incompatible tenant schema should fail that tenant's requests safely rather than attempting uncoordinated migration.

9. Rollback strategy
Application rollback and database rollback are not the same operation.

I'd support rolling back the application to a previously compatible version.

However, destructive database migrations may not be reversible.

Therefore:

Prefer expand-and-contract migrations.

Keep previous application versions compatible during rollout.

Delay destructive schema changes.

Test rollback before production deployment.

Use backup restoration only as a controlled disaster-recovery operation.

Encryption envelope versions must remain readable by supported clients throughout the compatibility window.

A deployment must not remove a decryption suite merely because it is no longer permitted for new uploads.

10. Environment management
I'd support explicit environments such as:

development
test
staging
production
These are deployment labels, not substitutes for security profiles.

A staging deployment can run the production security profile.

I'd recommend that staging mirrors production behaviour as closely as practical, including authentication, storage integration, migrations and background workers.

Development conveniences should require explicit opt-in and must not be enabled merely because the environment name is development.

11. Operational health
The application should expose:

GET /health/live
GET /health/ready
GET /health/dependencies
/health/live should answer whether the process is alive without depending on external services.

/health/ready should indicate whether the instance can safely accept its assigned workload.

Detailed dependency health must require administrative authentication.

Example operational status
Deployment Health
Example
API

Operational

Control-plane database

Operational

Object storage

Operational

Background workers

Degraded

Audit persistence

Operational

Illustrative statuses only.
Metrics should cover request latency, upload throughput, storage failures, database connection pools, job lag, quota saturation and audit persistence.

For distributed deployments, OpenTelemetry should be the primary observability integration.

12. Operational runbooks
I'd ship documented procedures for:

Initial installation and bootstrap.

Database and storage connection failures.

Secret rotation.

Application upgrades.

Failed migrations.

Backup restoration.

Tenant migration.

Audit integrity failure.

Compromised API credentials.

Compromised encryption keys.

Storage-provider outages.

Worker queue backlogs.

Organisation suspension and offboarding.

These runbooks should be versioned alongside the application.

13. Deployment verification
A production preflight command should produce a structured report.

{
  "applicationVersion": "2.1.0",
  "securityProfile": "production",
  "checks": {
    "configuration": "passed",
    "database": "passed",
    "storage": "passed",
    "audit": "passed",
    "secrets": "passed",
    "schemaCompatibility": "passed"
  },
  "ready": true
}

The version is illustrative.

Preflight should avoid printing secrets or sensitive connection details.

It should also distinguish fatal failures from warnings and temporary dependency outages.

14. Implementation priorities
I'd build deployment support in this order:

Configuration schema and validation.

Standalone Native AOT packaging.

Container packaging.

Secret resolver abstraction.

Database migration CLI.

Health and readiness endpoints.

Distributed API and worker roles.

Rolling upgrade compatibility checks.

Hybrid-tenant migration support.

Operational runbooks and release verification.

The application should remain usable without Kubernetes or cloud-specific tooling.

15. Next decision: email and notifications
The platform already requires email for verification, invitations and some security notifications.

We should decide whether outbound notifications are limited to email or whether the platform should provide a general notification framework.

This does not affect the inbound-only file policy: notifications are operational messages, not file delivery.


Email only

SMTP or supported email providers for verification, invitations, receipts and operational alerts.


Email plus configurable notification channels

Email as the default, with optional webhooks and other operator-configured notification destinations.


External notification service only

The platform emits events and relies on an external service for delivery, including email.

For the secure file-intake platform, notifications should use email plus configurable notification channels, including webhooks. Continue planning notification providers, templates, event routing, delivery reliability, privacy and operational alerts.
Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.2
Notification providers · Templates · Event routing · Reliable delivery · Privacy · Operational alerts

Decision confirmed: The platform will include a configurable notification framework, with email as the primary human notification channel and webhooks for integrations and automation.

The notification system must remain separate from file delivery. It may notify someone that a submission exists, but it must never attach submitted files, include decryption keys or generate public download links.

I'd implement notifications as an event-driven module backed by the existing durable outbox and worker infrastructure.

1. Notification architecture
Submissions

Security

Operations

Durable event outbox

Committed with the business transaction

Notification router

Policy · Recipients · Preferences · Templates

Email

Webhooks

Internal alerts

Business services should emit typed events rather than directly calling an SMTP server or webhook endpoint.

That keeps notification failures from breaking unrelated business operations.

For security-critical events, the application must still ensure that the underlying action and its audit record are committed correctly before notifying anyone.

2. Notification categories
I'd define four categories.

Category

Examples

Preferences

Transactional

Email verification, invitation, recovery enrolment

Mandatory

Submission

Submission received, status changed, revision added

Configurable

Security

New admin, key revoked, recovery approved, suspicious access

Mandatory for designated recipients

Operational

Storage failures, quota warnings, webhook failures

Configurable with enforced minimums

Mandatory notifications should not be suppressible by ordinary organisation users.

However, an organisation may choose the designated security contacts and supported delivery channels.

3. Notification event registry
Every event should have a stable name, version and documented payload schema.

Examples:

organisation.invitation.created
organisation.activated
organisation.suspended

submission.received
submission.revision.added
submission.status.changed
submission.withdrawal.requested

dropbox.expiring
dropbox.expired

security.admin.added
security.api_credential.revoked
security.encryption_key.revoked
security.recovery.requested
security.recovery.approved

quota.storage.warning
quota.storage.exceeded

storage.connection.failed
webhook.delivery.failed
audit.integrity.failed
I'd separate internal domain events from externally published webhook events.

Not every internal event should automatically become available to third-party subscribers.

4. Email providers
I'd support a provider abstraction.

Initial implementations:

SMTP with TLS.

A generic HTTPS email-provider adapter, with specific integrations added where needed.

SMTP should be available in the first release because it works well for self-hosted installations.

Provider configuration
{
  "Notifications": {
    "Email": {
      "Enabled": true,
      "Provider": "smtp",
      "FromAddress": "notifications@example.com",
      "FromName": "Secure Intake",
      "Smtp": {
        "Host": "smtp.example.com",
        "Port": 587,
        "Security": "starttls",
        "UsernameSecret": "email/username",
        "PasswordSecret": "email/password"
      }
    }
  }
}

Production SMTP configuration should validate TLS behaviour and certificate trust.

I'd prohibit silently falling back to unencrypted SMTP when TLS negotiation fails.

5. Organisation-specific email identity
Some organisations will want branded emails.

I'd support optional organisation-level sender configuration, subject to deployment policy.

For example:

From: Secure Documents <documents@example.org>
Reply-To: support@example.org
Custom sender domains should require verification and suitable SPF, DKIM and DMARC configuration.

The platform should not allow an organisation to impersonate arbitrary email domains.

If custom sending is not configured, use the platform's verified sender identity.

6. Templates
I'd provide built-in templates with controlled customisation.

Templates should be versioned and associated with a specific event.

For example:

submission.received
  email.subject
  email.html
  email.text

security.recovery.requested
  email.subject
  email.html
  email.text
Templates may include approved variables:

{{ organisation.name }}
{{ dropbox.name }}
{{ submission.reference }}
{{ event.timestamp }}
{{ action.url }}
Do not expose arbitrary object properties to templates.

Template variables must be explicitly allowlisted by event type.

Example email
Subject

Document submission received

Submission received
Your documents have been submitted successfully.

Reference

SI-8K4P2M

Keep this reference if you need to contact the organisation about your submission.

No documents are attached to this email.

Illustrative template, not a sent email.
Email templates should include both HTML and plain-text versions.

Organisation branding should be limited to safe fields such as display name, logo and approved colours.

No arbitrary JavaScript, remote CSS or untrusted HTML should be permitted.

7. Template localisation
I'd support locale-specific templates from the beginning.

For example:

templates/
  en/
    submission.received.html
    submission.received.txt
  en-AU/
    submission.received.html
    submission.received.txt
  ja/
    submission.received.html
    submission.received.txt
Fallback should be deterministic:

Recipient's supported locale.

Organisation's default locale.

Deployment default locale.

User-generated submission content should not be automatically translated.

Only application-owned notification text is localised.

8. Notification routing
Routing should be policy-based.

For example:

{
  "eventType": "submission.received",
  "channels": [
    {
      "type": "email",
      "recipient": "dropbox_managers"
    },
    {
      "type": "webhook",
      "subscription": "integration_primary"
    }
  ]
}

Recipients should be resolved at dispatch time or captured as a versioned delivery snapshot, depending on the event.

Security-sensitive notifications should capture the intended recipients at event creation to prevent later policy changes from silently suppressing an alert.

The routing engine should support deduplication, suppression windows and escalation policies.

9. Webhook delivery
Webhooks should use HTTPS only in production.

Each subscription should have:

Organisation ownership.

Endpoint URL.

Subscribed event types.

Signing secret or asymmetric verification configuration.

Enabled/disabled state.

Delivery timeout.

Retry policy.

Last successful delivery.

Failure status.

Example webhook payload
{
  "id": "evt_01JEXAMPLE",
  "type": "submission.received",
  "version": 1,
  "occurredAt": "2026-11-03T04:00:00Z",
  "organisationId": "org_123",
  "data": {
    "submissionId": "sub_456",
    "dropboxId": "dbx_789",
    "reference": "SI-8K4P2M"
  }
}

The payload should contain identifiers and minimal metadata, not submitted file contents.

An integration retrieves authorised ciphertext separately through the authenticated API.

Signing
I'd use HMAC-SHA256 initially, signing the timestamp and exact transmitted payload bytes with an unambiguous framing format.

The signature header should include a key identifier to support rotation.

Receivers must verify signatures using constant-time comparison, enforce a timestamp tolerance and deduplicate event IDs.

10. Webhook SSRF protection
Because organisations can configure webhook destinations, webhook delivery creates a server-side request forgery risk.

I'd enforce:

HTTPS only.

DNS resolution and IP validation.

Blocking loopback, link-local, private and metadata-service addresses unless explicitly allowed by a trusted deployment-level policy.

Redirects disabled by default.

Revalidation on every delivery connection.

Connection, response-size and timeout limits.

No forwarding of internal credentials.

Network-level egress restrictions where possible.

URL validation alone is insufficient. DNS rebinding and proxy configuration can undermine naïve checks.

For high-isolation deployments, webhook delivery can run in a separately restricted worker process.

11. Delivery reliability
I'd use the existing durable jobs and outbox infrastructure.

1

Event committed

Domain event saved in the same transaction as the business change

2

Routing evaluated

Eligible recipients and channels resolved

3

Delivery queued

One durable delivery record per destination

4

Attempt delivery

Provider adapter sends the message

5

Confirm or retry

Success recorded or failure scheduled with backoff

6

Escalate failure

Permanent failures enter a dead-letter state

Delivery semantics should be at least once, not exactly once.

If a worker crashes after a provider accepts a message but before recording success, the message may be delivered again.

Webhook consumers should deduplicate by event ID.

For email, use provider idempotency features where available and otherwise minimise duplicate delivery through stable message identifiers and careful retry handling.

12. Retry policy
I'd distinguish transient and permanent failures.

Failure

Behaviour

HTTP 429

Retry using bounded Retry-After

HTTP 5xx

Exponential backoff with jitter

Timeout

Retry

DNS failure

Retry, then escalate

HTTP 401/403

Pause or fail according to policy

HTTP 404/410

Treat as permanent after configured checks

Invalid webhook configuration

Disable delivery and alert

SMTP temporary failure

Retry

SMTP permanent rejection

Stop retrying and record failure

Retry budgets should be configurable within deployment limits.

A failing webhook must not indefinitely block unrelated events or organisations.

13. Notification privacy
Notifications should be classified by sensitivity.

I'd use three payload levels:

Classification

Allowed content

Public-safe

Generic confirmation and reference

Organisation-internal

Submission identifiers and operational metadata

Security-sensitive

Restricted security event details

Email is not an end-to-end encrypted transport.

Therefore, sensitive file metadata should be excluded unless explicitly justified by policy.

For example, a notification should say that a submission was received rather than exposing filenames, personal details or document contents.

Notification logs should record delivery metadata without retaining complete email bodies indefinitely.

14. Operational alerting
Operational alerts should use the same framework, but with separate routing policies.

Examples:

storage.provider.unavailable
storage.integrity.failure
database.connection.exhausted
worker.queue.backlog
audit.persistence.failure
audit.integrity.failure
quota.storage.critical
security.recovery.approved
security.key.compromised
webhook.delivery.dead_lettered
Critical alerts must not depend solely on the failing subsystem.

For example, if database audit persistence is unavailable, an external monitoring system should still be capable of detecting and reporting the outage.

I'd expose health metrics and alert conditions through OpenTelemetry-compatible instrumentation.

15. Notification administration
The organisation admin UI should include:

Page

Functions

Email settings

Sender identity, provider status, test delivery

Templates

Preview, localisation and controlled customisation

Event subscriptions

Select events and recipients

Webhooks

Endpoints, signing secrets, test delivery

Delivery history

Attempts, status and failure details

Alerts

Thresholds, routing and escalation

Platform administrators should be able to enforce mandatory security notifications and deployment-wide delivery restrictions.

Organisation administrators should not be able to suppress platform security alerts.

Delivery dashboard concept

Notification delivery records should reference the originating event and tenant.

For hybrid database deployments, tenant events may originate in dedicated databases while delivery workers operate centrally.

17. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/notifications/settings

Test webhook deliveries must be explicitly marked as tests and use synthetic data.

They should never expose real submission metadata merely to validate connectivity.

18. Implementation priorities
I'd implement notifications in this order:

Typed event registry and durable outbox integration.

SMTP provider.

Built-in transactional templates.

Invitation and verification emails.

Submission notifications.

Webhook subscriptions and HMAC signing.

Retry, dead-letter and delivery history.

Operational alerts.

Organisation template customisation.

Additional providers and escalation routing.

This provides a useful notification system without making external services mandatory.

19. Next decision: branding and custom domains
We should now decide how much organisation-specific branding the platform supports.

This affects the public upload portal, email templates, organisation administration and DNS/TLS configuration.


Basic organisation branding

Organisation name, logo, colours and introductory text. All upload portals use the platform domain.


Branding plus optional custom domains

Organisation branding with verified custom domains. DNS and TLS remain operator-managed.


Minimal branding

Consistent platform identity with only organisation names and dropbox descriptions.

For the secure file-intake platform, branding should use organisation branding plus optional verified custom domains, with operator-managed DNS and TLS. Continue planning public portal branding, custom domains, domain verification, TLS, URL routing, email identity and security.
Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.3
Organisation branding · Custom domains · DNS verification · TLS · URL routing · Email identity · Security

Decision confirmed: Organisations can customise their public upload experience and optionally use verified custom domains. DNS configuration, certificate provisioning and TLS termination remain the responsibility of the deployment operator.

I'd implement branding as a tenant-scoped presentation layer, separate from the encryption protocol, upload workflow and security controls.

A branded portal should look like the organisation's service without allowing that organisation to replace or weaken security-critical application behaviour.

1. Branding capabilities
I'd support the following customisation:

Setting

Organisation configurable

Constraints

Display name

Yes

Length and content validation

Logo

Yes

Validated image formats

Primary colour

Yes

Contrast requirements

Secondary colour

Yes

Contrast requirements

Light/dark appearance

Yes

Approved theme tokens

Welcome heading

Yes

Plain text

Introductory text

Yes

Sanitised, limited formatting

Footer text

Yes

Sanitised, limited formatting

Contact information

Yes

Validated

Language

Yes

Supported application locales

Custom domain

Optional

Verification and operator activation

Custom JavaScript

No

Prohibited

Arbitrary HTML/CSS

No

Prohibited

The organisation can control presentation, not the underlying security model.

In particular, branding must not hide required privacy notices, security warnings, upload restrictions or submission confirmations.

Branding inheritance
I'd use three levels:

Deployment branding
    ↓
Organisation branding
    ↓
Dropbox-specific branding overrides
Dropbox-specific overrides should be limited to the title, description, optional logo and approved theme settings.

A dropbox should not have its own unrestricted CSS or JavaScript.

2. Public upload portal
The upload portal should be a small, purpose-built application.

It does not need the full administrative frontend.

Example Organisation
Secure document intake

Submit your documents
Upload the requested files securely. Your documents are encrypted before they leave your device.

Select files to upload

Maximum 1 GiB per file · 5 GiB per submission

Illustrative interface
Files are encrypted in your browser for authorised recipients.

Continue

Static design concept. No upload or encryption occurs here.
The real portal would have explicit stages for any required access challenge, uploader identity verification, file selection, encryption, upload progress and receipt.

Security-critical wording should be supplied by the application, not arbitrary organisation-authored content.

3. Custom domain model
I'd support both platform-managed URLs and organisation custom domains.

Examples:

https://intake.example.com/u/example-org
https://intake.example.com/d/abc123

https://documents.example.org/
https://documents.example.org/d/abc123
The platform domain must always be available as an administrative fallback unless the deployment operator explicitly disables it.

A custom domain should be associated with exactly one organisation within the deployment.

Multiple domains per organisation can be supported, but only one should be the canonical public domain at a time.

Domain registry
organisation_domains
  id
  organisation_id
  hostname
  verification_status
  activation_status
  verification_token_hash
  verified_at
  verification_expires_at
  tls_status
  canonical
  created_at
  updated_at
Hostnames must be normalised using a consistent IDNA implementation and stored in canonical form.

Uniqueness must be enforced on the normalised hostname.

I'd reject wildcard hostnames in the initial release.

4. Domain verification
Domain verification should prove that the applicant controls the relevant DNS configuration.

I'd use a DNS TXT challenge.

For example, the organisation wants:

documents.example.org
The platform generates a random challenge and instructs the operator or domain owner to publish:

_secure-intake.documents.example.org TXT "secure-intake-verification=<random-token>"
The application verifies the DNS record before marking the domain as verified.

A TXT challenge is preferable to treating an existing CNAME as sufficient proof of ownership.

Verification workflow
1

Request domain

Organisation administrator enters the desired hostname

2

Generate challenge

Platform creates a time-limited random verification token

3

Configure DNS

Domain owner publishes the required TXT record

4

Verify ownership

Platform resolves and validates the challenge

5

Operator configures routing

DNS, reverse proxy and TLS are prepared externally

6

Activate domain

Platform confirms routing and TLS readiness before enabling

Verification should expire and require renewal after material ownership changes or a configured interval.

Deleting and re-adding a domain must not allow reuse of an old verification token.

5. DNS and TLS responsibilities
The application should provide configuration instructions but not manage DNS providers or certificate authorities.

For example, the operator may configure:

documents.example.org CNAME intake.example.com
Or use another supported routing arrangement.

The actual DNS target is deployment-specific.

The operator must ensure that the ingress infrastructure serves a valid certificate for documents.example.org.

TLS requirements
Production custom domains must have:

Valid certificate chain.

Hostname-matching certificate.

TLS 1.2 or later, with TLS 1.3 preferred.

Secure reverse-proxy configuration.

Correct forwarded-host and forwarded-protocol handling.

No insecure HTTP fallback for authenticated operations.

I'd require the application to verify the externally visible HTTPS endpoint before activating the domain.

A DNS challenge alone does not prove that the intended application is serving traffic securely.

6. Custom-domain activation
I'd use explicit domain states.

requested
    ↓
pending_verification
    ↓
verified
    ↓
pending_tls
    ↓
ready
    ↓
active
Additional states:

suspended
verification_expired
tls_invalid
removed
A domain with invalid TLS should not be silently treated as healthy.

The application should alert the operator and organisation administrator.

For an existing active domain, temporary TLS failures should be handled as an operational incident, not by automatically transferring the hostname to another tenant.

7. Host-based tenant routing
Custom-domain routing must be resolved from an authoritative registry.

The server should not trust arbitrary Host or X-Forwarded-Host values.

The reverse proxy must be configured with explicit trusted proxy addresses and allowed forwarded headers.

Routing process
Incoming HTTPS request
        ↓
Trusted ingress validates hostname
        ↓
Application normalises hostname
        ↓
Lookup active domain assignment
        ↓
Resolve organisation
        ↓
Resolve dropbox or portal route
        ↓
Apply tenant authorisation and security policy
Unknown or inactive hostnames should receive a generic error.

They must not fall back to another organisation's portal.

Routing interface
public interface IOrganisationDomainResolver
{
    ValueTask<OrganisationDomain?> ResolveAsync(
        string hostname,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record OrganisationDomain(
    Guid OrganisationId,
    string Hostname,
    bool IsCanonical,
    long AssignmentVersion);

Domain resolution should occur before tenant-scoped data access.

The result must be checked against the requested dropbox's organisation.

A valid domain must never allow access to another organisation's dropbox simply because the URL contains its identifier.

8. URL design
I'd keep URLs short and avoid exposing internal database identifiers.

Suggested routes:

GET  /
GET  /d/{publicDropboxId}
GET  /s/{publicSubmissionReference}
GET  /status
However, the public submission reference must not itself grant access.

Status and return access must use the previously agreed capability-token and identity-verification model.

For one-way dropboxes, no public return or status route needs to be exposed.

Canonical URLs
Each organisation should have one canonical public origin.

For example:

https://documents.example.org
The canonical origin should be used when generating invitation or upload links.

I'd avoid automatic cross-domain redirects involving secret-bearing URLs.

If a custom domain changes, old links should either remain valid under a controlled compatibility policy or require reissuance.

Do not automatically append capability tokens to redirects.

9. Domain takeover prevention
Custom domains introduce a serious risk: a hostname can remain configured in DNS after its platform assignment has been removed.

This can lead to dangling-domain takeover scenarios.

I'd implement:

Ownership verification before activation.

Unique hostname assignment.

Reverification when ownership is disputed or materially changes.

Safe removal workflows.

Operator warnings about dangling DNS.

Domain assignment tombstones for a defined period.

No automatic assignment of a previously used hostname to another tenant.

Domain reassignment should require fresh verification and, where appropriate, platform approval.

The application cannot remove DNS records from an operator's domain. Its removal workflow must clearly instruct the operator to do so.

10. Browser security across custom domains
Custom domains create additional browser-origin boundaries.

I'd enforce:

Host-only session cookies.

Secure, HttpOnly and appropriate SameSite attributes.

No shared authentication cookies across unrelated custom domains.

Explicit CORS allowlists.

CSRF protection for cookie-authenticated operations.

Strict Content Security Policy.

Referrer-Policy: no-referrer.

No third-party scripts on encryption pages.

Controlled asset loading.

Strict origin validation for browser API calls.

The public portal should use short-lived, origin-bound sessions where feasible.
