Administrative authentication should preferably remain on the platform's trusted administrative origin.

I would not allow an organisation's custom upload domain to host the platform administration interface by default.

Important encryption boundary
Custom branding increases the importance of frontend supply-chain security.

A compromised custom-domain origin or injected JavaScript could compromise the client-side encryption process.

Therefore, custom branding must not permit executable code or arbitrary external assets.

For higher-assurance use cases, an independently distributed encryption client remains preferable.

11. Branding asset security
Logos and other assets should be handled separately from encrypted submission files.

I'd allow a small set of image formats, such as PNG, JPEG and WebP.

SVG should be excluded initially because of its additional sanitisation and scripting risks.

Branding assets should be:

Size-limited.

Validated by content, not only extension.

Re-encoded where practical.

Stored in a separate asset namespace.

Served with fixed content types.

Protected from HTML execution and content sniffing.

The platform can inspect branding assets because they are intentionally public presentation material.

This does not weaken the rule that submitted documents remain end-to-end encrypted.

12. Email identity and branding
Email sender identity is related to, but independent of, the custom portal domain.

An organisation may use:

Portal:
https://documents.example.org

Email sender:
documents@example.org
The platform should support a verified custom sender domain or address where the configured email provider permits it.

Verification should be separate from portal-domain verification.

Email security
Custom email identity should require:

Sender-domain ownership verification.

Provider-compatible SPF and DKIM configuration.

DMARC alignment where applicable.

No arbitrary sender impersonation.

Approved Reply-To addresses.

Safe template variables.

A custom portal domain should not automatically grant permission to send email from the same domain.

13. Domain administration UX
I'd provide an organisation-level Domains & Branding section.

Domains & Branding
Example
Primary portal domain

documents.example.org

Verified · TLS ready · Active

Fallback platform URL

intake.example.com/u/example-org

Branding

Primary colour

#274C77

Organisation logo configured

Illustrative administration screen.
The UI should provide a DNS verification record, current verification status, TLS readiness and canonical-domain controls.

Domain activation should require an explicit confirmation.

14. Database additions
organisation_branding
organisation_branding_versions
organisation_branding_assets
organisation_domains
organisation_domain_verifications
organisation_domain_assignments
organisation_domain_health_checks
organisation_email_identities
organisation_email_identity_verifications
Domain assignments and branding changes should be audited.

A domain reassignment must be transactional and concurrency-safe.

15. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/branding
PUT  /api/organisations/{id}/branding

POST /api/organisations/{id}/branding/assets
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/branding/preview

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/domains
POST /api/organisations/{id}/domains
POST /api/organisations/{id}/domains/{domainId}/verify
POST /api/organisations/{id}/domains/{domainId}/activate
POST /api/organisations/{id}/domains/{domainId}/deactivate
DELETE /api/organisations/{id}/domains/{domainId}

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/email-identities
POST /api/organisations/{id}/email-identities
POST /api/organisations/{id}/email-identities/{identityId}/verify
Domain activation and reassignment should require appropriate permissions, recent authentication and potentially platform approval.

16. Implementation priorities
I'd build this in the following order:

Organisation branding model and theme tokens.

Secure branding asset pipeline.

Public portal branding inheritance.

Domain registry and hostname normalisation.

DNS TXT verification.

Operator-managed TLS readiness checks.

Host-based tenant routing.

Canonical URL generation.

Domain removal and takeover safeguards.

Custom email sender verification.

The key design rule is that custom domains change where the portal is presented, not what the portal is authorised to do.

17. Next decision: public portal deployment
Custom domains create one remaining frontend deployment decision.

Should the public upload portal be served directly by the API host, or should it be a separately deployable static application?

Integrated portal only

The API host serves the public upload frontend. Simplest standalone deployment and fewer moving parts.


Separate static portal only

The public upload application is deployed independently and communicates with the API over HTTPS.


Both integrated and separately deployable

Standalone installations can serve the portal from the application host, while distributed installations may deploy the same frontend independently.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, the public upload portal should support both integrated hosting and separately deployable static hosting using the same frontend. Continue planning frontend architecture, static asset deployment, API communication, version compatibility, caching, CSP and encryption integrity.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.4
Frontend architecture · Integrated and static hosting · API communication · Version compatibility · Caching · CSP · Encryption integrity

Decision confirmed: The public upload portal will be built once and distributed in two supported forms:
1. Integrated: Served directly by the Secure Intake application host.
2. Standalone static: Published independently to any compatible static hosting environment.
Both use the same frontend source code, encryption implementation, upload workflow and API contracts.

The deployment model must not change the security guarantees or introduce separate frontend implementations.
1. Recommended frontend architecture
I'd use a lightweight TypeScript frontend with a dedicated cryptography module, rather than making the upload portal dependent on the administrative application's technology stack.

The backend remains .NET 10 Native AOT.

This keeps the public upload experience small and allows it to run on ordinary static hosting without requiring a .NET runtime.
Proposed project structure
secure-intake/
├── src/
│   ├── SecureIntake.Api/
│   ├── SecureIntake.Host/
│   ├── SecureIntake.Worker/
│   ├── SecureIntake.Cli/
│   ├── SecureIntake.Application/
│   ├── SecureIntake.Domain/
│   ├── SecureIntake.Contracts/
│   └── SecureIntake.Cryptography/
│
├── frontend/
│   ├── portal/
│   │   ├── src/
│   │   │   ├── app/
│   │   │   ├── components/
│   │   │   ├── pages/
│   │   │   ├── upload/
│   │   │   ├── crypto/
│   │   │   ├── api/
│   │   │   ├── branding/
│   │   │   └── security/
│   │   └── package.json
│   │
│   └── admin/
│
├── packages/
│   ├── api-client/
│   ├── crypto-protocol/
│   └── shared-types/
│
└── tests/
    ├── interoperability/
    ├── frontend-security/
    └── end-to-end/


The browser cryptography implementation must be interoperable with independently distributed reviewer and automation clients.

It should not depend on the server possessing private decryption keys.
2. Deployment topology
Integrated

One application host


ASP.NET Core

Static portal + API



Separate static

Static host or CDN


HTTPS API

Independent .NET host





Same frontend release artifact

Identical encryption code · Upload workflow · API client


I'd make same-origin deployment the preferred production configuration, even when the static assets and API run on different infrastructure.

For example, a reverse proxy can route:
https://documents.example.org/       → Static frontend
https://documents.example.org/api/    → Secure Intake API


This avoids unnecessary cross-origin authentication and CORS complexity.

A separately hosted portal may still use a different API origin, but that should be an explicit supported configuration.
3. Integrated hosting
For integrated deployment, the frontend is compiled into static assets during the release build.

The .NET host serves the compiled assets without requiring Node.js at runtime.

I'd support either:
- Embedded static resources in the executable.
- A versioned static asset directory distributed alongside the executable.
For Native AOT, I'd favour a packaged static asset directory initially because it simplifies updates, debugging and content-hash verification.
Example deployment
secure-intake/
├── secure-intake
├── appsettings.json
└── wwwroot/
    ├── index.html
    ├── assets/
    │   ├── app.a8f42c.js
    │   ├── crypto.9b73e1.js
    │   └── app.4c28ab.css
    └── release-manifest.json


The executable and frontend assets must be distributed as a compatible release set.

The host should verify the frontend release manifest during startup or deployment preflight.
4. Separate static hosting
The same compiled frontend can be deployed to:
- Cloudflare Pages or equivalent static hosting.
- Azure Static Web Apps.
- Amazon S3 with a CDN.
- Nginx or Caddy.
- Any compatible HTTPS static server.
The platform does not provision those services.

The operator uploads the release artifacts and configures DNS, TLS, caching and API routing.
Static build output
dist/
├── index.html
├── assets/
│   ├── app.a8f42c.js
│   ├── crypto.9b73e1.js
│   ├── app.4c28ab.css
│   └── vendor.2c99f0.js
├── release-manifest.json
└── integrity-manifest.json


The build must not contain database credentials, storage credentials, API client secrets or private encryption keys.

Any value included in static assets is public.
5. Runtime configuration
I'd avoid building a separate frontend for every organisation or custom domain.

Instead, use a common build that loads tenant presentation configuration from the API.
Bootstrap endpoint
GET /api/public/portal/bootstrap


The API resolves the organisation from the validated request hostname or an explicitly authorised platform-domain route.

Example response:
{
  "organisation": {
    "displayName": "Example Organisation",
    "logoUrl": "/assets/branding/logo.webp",
    "theme": {
      "primary": "#274C77",
      "appearance": "system"
    }
  },
  "portal": {
    "locale": "en-AU",
    "features": {
      "returnAccess": false
    }
  },
  "protocol": {
    "version": 1,
    "minimumClientVersion": "1.4.0"
  }
}


This endpoint must return only public presentation settings and non-secret protocol metadata.

Encryption recipient keys and approved suite details should be retrieved through a separate authenticated or capability-scoped protocol endpoint with explicit key authenticity controls.

Important: A bootstrap endpoint served by a compromised host cannot independently establish trust in the encryption keys it returns.
6. API communication
I'd use a generated TypeScript API client from the .NET API's OpenAPI contract.

That reduces contract drift between frontend and backend.
API client configuration
export interface ApiClientOptions {
  baseUrl: string;
  fetch?: typeof fetch;
}

export class SecureIntakeApiClient {
  constructor(private readonly options: ApiClientOptions) {}

  async getPortalBootstrap(signal?: AbortSignal) {
    const response = await (this.options.fetch ?? fetch)(
      new URL("/api/public/portal/bootstrap", this.options.baseUrl),
      {
        method: "GET",
        credentials: "same-origin",
        signal
      }
    );

    if (!response.ok) {
      throw new Error(`Portal bootstrap failed: ${response.status}`);
    }

    return response.json();
  }
}


For cross-origin deployments, authentication and credential behaviour must be explicitly designed rather than changing credentials to include indiscriminately.

I'd prefer short-lived upload capabilities held in memory over persistent browser storage.
7. Upload workflow
The browser upload pipeline remains:
1

Resolve portal

Load branding, dropbox policy and protocol compatibility



2

Verify access

Complete any PIN, email, OTP or OIDC requirements



3

Validate recipients

Check approved encryption suite and authenticated recipient keys



4

Encrypt locally

Generate file key and encrypt chunks in the browser



5

Upload ciphertext

Transfer encrypted chunks using scoped upload authorisation



6

Finalise submission

Verify manifest and ciphertext metadata



7

Issue receipt

Return reference and permitted status/return capabilities



The upload pipeline must be identical for integrated and separately hosted portals.
8. Version compatibility
Frontend and backend releases will not always be deployed simultaneously.

I'd define independent versions for:
Component	Versioning purpose
Application release	Overall product release
API contract	Request and response compatibility
Upload protocol	Session and finalisation semantics
Encryption protocol	Ciphertext and envelope compatibility
Frontend build	Static asset release
Branding schema	Presentation configuration

I would not use /v1 in API routes.

Instead, API compatibility can be expressed through headers and published capability metadata.
Example compatibility response
{
  "api": {
    "current": "2026-11-03",
    "minimumSupported": "2026-08-01"
  },
  "uploadProtocol": {
    "current": 2,
    "supported": [1, 2]
  },
  "encryptionProtocol": {
    "supported": [1]
  }
}


The dates and versions are illustrative.

The frontend should check compatibility before starting an upload.

An incompatible frontend must display a clear update requirement rather than attempting an upload with unsupported semantics.
Compatibility rule
A deployed API must support the active frontend release and any still-valid upload sessions created by older supported releases.

This may require a bounded compatibility window.
9. Caching strategy
Caching must distinguish immutable application assets from security-sensitive dynamic responses.
Resource	Cache policy
Hashed JavaScript/CSS assets	Long-lived, immutable
index.html	Revalidate on every navigation
Release manifest	No-cache or short revalidation
Branding assets	Versioned, cacheable
Portal bootstrap	Short-lived or no-store where sensitive
Encryption recipient keys	No-store unless explicitly protected by a verified cache protocol
Upload sessions	No-store
Authentication responses	No-store
Submission receipts	No-store

Example headers
For hashed assets:
Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable


For index.html:
Cache-Control: no-cache


For sensitive API responses:
Cache-Control: no-store


I'd avoid a service worker in the initial release.

Service workers introduce additional lifecycle and integrity concerns, especially when cryptographic JavaScript must be updated promptly.

Offline upload support is not required for the first release.
10. Frontend update behaviour
When a new frontend release is deployed:
1. New page loads receive the latest index.html.
2. The HTML references new content-hashed assets.
3. The browser downloads only assets whose URLs have changed.
4. Unchanged assets remain cached.
5. Existing open tabs continue running their loaded code until refreshed.
This means an upload started before deployment may continue using an older frontend.

The backend must either support that frontend for the session's lifetime or explicitly terminate the session safely.

I'd implement a release compatibility check before starting a new upload and before finalisation.

Do not forcibly reload the page while a file is being encrypted or uploaded.
11. Content Security Policy
The upload portal should use a restrictive CSP.

A starting point for a same-origin deployment:
Content-Security-Policy:
  default-src 'none';
  script-src 'self';
  style-src 'self';
  img-src 'self' data:;
  connect-src 'self';
  font-src 'self';
  object-src 'none';
  base-uri 'none';
  form-action 'self';
  frame-ancestors 'none';
  upgrade-insecure-requests


This is a policy template, not a final deployment-ready header.

For direct-to-storage uploads, connect-src must include the explicitly authorised storage endpoints.

For separately hosted frontends, it must include the configured API origin.

Avoid broad wildcards such as https: in connect-src.

If the frontend framework requires inline scripts or styles, I'd change the build configuration to avoid them rather than adding 'unsafe-inline'.

The portal should not permit arbitrary organisation-supplied scripts.
12. Additional browser security headers
I'd use:
X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff
Referrer-Policy: no-referrer
Permissions-Policy: camera=(), microphone=(), geolocation=()
Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin


HSTS should be enabled for production HTTPS origins, with includeSubDomains only where the operator controls and understands the entire affected domain hierarchy.

Cross-origin isolation headers such as COOP and COEP should be introduced only after validating compatibility with storage uploads, authentication redirects and supported browser cryptography.
13. Encryption integrity
This is the most important security consideration in the frontend architecture.

The server never receives plaintext files or private recipient keys.

However, the browser executes JavaScript delivered by the hosting environment.

A compromised static host, CDN, DNS configuration or deployment pipeline could replace that JavaScript with malicious code.

Therefore, client-side encryption does not automatically protect against a compromised frontend delivery environment.
Required controls
I'd implement:
- Reproducible or independently verifiable frontend builds where practical.
- Signed release manifests.
- Content-hashed assets.
- Dependency pinning and lockfile verification.
- Software bill of materials.
- CI security scanning.
- Strict CSP.
- No third-party runtime scripts.
- Independent cryptographic interoperability tests.
- Explicit recipient-key authentication.
- Release provenance and deployment verification.
Subresource Integrity can protect separately referenced scripts against unexpected modification, but it does not establish trust if an attacker can also replace the HTML and its integrity attributes.

Likewise, a signed manifest checked only by JavaScript from the same compromised origin does not establish independent trust.

For high-assurance installations, I'd support an independently distributed upload client or an independently trusted verification mechanism.
14. Encryption module design
I'd isolate cryptography behind a small, versioned interface.
export interface EncryptionRecipient {
  keyId: string;
  algorithm: string;
  publicKey: Uint8Array;
}

export interface EncryptionManifest {
  protocolVersion: number;
  suiteId: string;
  fileId: string;
  chunkCount: number;
  ciphertextLength: number;
  manifestDigest: Uint8Array;
}

export interface FileEncryptionResult {
  manifest: EncryptionManifest;
  encryptedChunks: AsyncIterable<Uint8Array>;
}

export interface FileEncryptor {
  encrypt(
    file: File,
    recipients: readonly EncryptionRecipient[],
    signal?: AbortSignal
  ): Promise<FileEncryptionResult>;
}


This is an application-level interface, not a cryptographic protocol specification.

The implementation must use reviewed constructions and vetted libraries.

I would not implement a custom hybrid post-quantum encryption construction without independent review.
Streaming and memory
Large files must be encrypted incrementally.

The frontend should not read an entire multi-gigabyte file into memory.

I'd use browser streams and workers where supported, with bounded buffers and backpressure.

A Web Worker can improve responsiveness, but it is not a security boundary against malicious same-origin JavaScript.
15. Direct-to-storage uploads
For large files, direct ciphertext upload to S3/R2-compatible storage is desirable.

The API should issue short-lived, tightly scoped upload authorisation.

The browser must never receive long-lived storage credentials.
Example flow
Browser
  │
  ├── Request upload session ──────► API
  │                                  │
  │◄──── Scoped upload authorisation ┤
  │
  ├── Encrypt chunks locally
  │
  ├── Upload ciphertext ───────────► Object storage
  │
  └── Finalise submission ─────────► API
                                     │
                                     └── Verify stored ciphertext


For cross-origin object storage, the operator must configure CORS to allow the approved portal origins and necessary methods and headers.

The API must verify object size, identity, upload session and authenticated manifest information before committing the file.

A client-supplied checksum alone is not sufficient evidence that the expected object was stored.
16. Static hosting and custom domains
The custom-domain registry remains authoritative.

For integrated hosting, the application resolves the incoming hostname directly.

For separate static hosting, the deployment operator must ensure the correct portal is served for the hostname.

The frontend should not infer organisation identity from an arbitrary client-supplied tenant ID.

The API must independently validate the hostname-to-organisation association.

Where the frontend and API use different origins, the API needs an explicit mapping between the trusted frontend origin and the organisation's verified domain.

CORS is not an authorisation mechanism.
17. Deployment validation
I'd extend the deployment preflight command.
secure-intake frontend verify \
  --manifest ./dist/release-manifest.json

secure-intake frontend compatibility \
  --api https://api.example.com

secure-intake frontend security-check \
  --url https://documents.example.org


These commands should verify:
- Release manifest and expected hashes.
- API compatibility.
- HTTPS and certificate validity.
- Security headers.
- CSP configuration.
- Expected static asset versions.
- Trusted API and storage origins.
- Custom-domain routing.
- Absence of known development-only configuration.
A deployment preflight check is evidence of the configuration at the time of testing, not a guarantee against later compromise.
18. Implementation priorities
I'd implement this in the following order:
1. TypeScript portal and shared API client.
2. Integrated static asset hosting.
3. Separate static build packaging.
4. Runtime portal bootstrap and branding.
5. Upload protocol compatibility negotiation.
6. Streaming encryption module and interoperability tests.
7. Direct-to-storage ciphertext uploads.
8. CSP and browser security policy.
9. Release manifests and integrity verification.
10. Deployment preflight and update compatibility tests.
The important architectural rule is that the same frontend artifact must work in both hosting modes without rebuilding it for each organisation.

19. Next decision: reviewer decryption client
The uploader portal is now defined, but authorised reviewers still need a client to retrieve ciphertext and decrypt it locally.

We should decide whether reviewer decryption belongs in the optional administration web application, a separate dedicated application, or both.

Web administration client only

Reviewers retrieve and decrypt authorised files directly within the organisation administration frontend.


Dedicated reviewer client only

Reviewers use an independently distributed desktop or CLI client for ciphertext retrieval and decryption.


Both browser and independently distributed clients

Browser decryption for convenience, with dedicated desktop/CLI clients for higher-assurance workflows and automation.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, reviewer decryption should support both browser-based decryption and independently distributed desktop/CLI clients. Continue planning reviewer authentication, private-key custody, ciphertext retrieval, decryption UX, desktop/CLI architecture and cryptographic interoperability.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.5
Reviewer authentication · Private-key custody · Ciphertext retrieval · Browser decryption · Desktop/CLI clients · Cryptographic interoperability

Decision confirmed: Secure File Intake will support both browser-based reviewer decryption and independently distributed desktop/CLI clients.

Both clients must use the same encrypted file format, recipient envelopes, authentication rules and API contracts.

The key difference is their trust model, not their access permissions.

A browser client is convenient for everyday review. An independently installed client offers stronger protection against a compromised web frontend, provided its own distribution and update mechanisms are trusted.
1. Reviewer architecture
Secure Intake API

Identity · RBAC · File metadata · Ciphertext · Envelopes · Audit


Browser reviewer

Optional administration frontend

Browser-held private key


Desktop / CLI

Independently installed application

OS keystore, encrypted key file or hardware-backed key




Common cryptographic protocol

Identical ciphertext · Manifest validation · Recipient envelopes


The API authorises access to encrypted material. The client independently determines whether it possesses the correct private key to decrypt it.

Administrative access does not imply decryption access.
2. Reviewer authentication
I'd support the existing built-in identity system and external OIDC.

Authentication should be consistent across browser, desktop and CLI, with different session handling appropriate to each client.
Client	Authentication
Browser	Secure session cookie, CSRF protection, MFA
Desktop	OIDC Authorization Code + PKCE through system browser
Interactive CLI	OIDC Authorization Code + PKCE, with device flow only where supported and authorised
Automation CLI	Scoped machine identity using OAuth2 client credentials or equivalent
Offline decryption	No live login required to decrypt a locally held file, if the private key is available

Offline decryption is an intentional capability.

Once a reviewer possesses ciphertext and the appropriate private key, the platform cannot cryptographically revoke that copy.
Reviewer access requirements
A ciphertext retrieval request must pass:
1. Authenticated identity.
2. Active organisation membership.
3. Resource-level permission.
4. Current organisation and file access policy.
5. Any required recent MFA or step-up authentication.
6. Tenant routing and storage authorisation.
The server should never use possession of a public key as proof that a user is authorised to retrieve a file.
3. Private-key custody
I'd support three reviewer key-custody options.
Custody mode	Storage	Intended use
Browser-managed	Browser-local encrypted key material	Convenient web review
Desktop-managed	Encrypted key file or OS-protected storage	Normal desktop review
Organisation-managed	External KMS/HSM or controlled key service	Managed enterprise and automation workflows

All three must follow the same public-key registration and recipient-envelope protocol.
Browser-managed keys
For browser use, private keys should be generated locally or imported from an approved encrypted key package.

Where supported, use non-extractable Web Crypto keys. However, support for the selected hybrid/PQ cryptographic suite must be verified before promising non-extractable storage.

If the cryptographic implementation requires extractable private-key bytes, protect them using an encrypted key container and an explicit unlock operation.

Do not claim that IndexedDB or browser key storage is equivalent to a hardware security module.
Desktop-managed keys
I'd support:
- Encrypted portable key files.
- Windows DPAPI/CNG-backed protection where compatible.
- macOS Keychain-backed protection where compatible.
- Linux Secret Service or equivalent, where available.
- PKCS#11 or vendor KMS integrations as optional advanced capabilities.
Hardware-backed custody must be evaluated per algorithm. Not every platform keystore or HSM supports the chosen post-quantum algorithms.
4. Key enrolment
A reviewer should enrol a key through a controlled workflow.
1

Authenticate

Reviewer signs in and completes required MFA



2

Generate or import key

Private key remains under reviewer or organisation control



3

Register public key

Submit suite, public key and proof of possession



4

Approve enrolment

Apply organisation key-registration policy



5

Activate recipient

Key becomes eligible for new encrypted submissions



6

Audit

Record enrolment, fingerprint, approval and policy version



Proof of possession must be defined per cryptographic suite.

For encryption-only keys, a generic signing challenge may not be appropriate. The protocol may instead require a reviewed challenge encapsulation/decryption exchange.

The server should not activate an unverified public key.
5. Ciphertext retrieval
The retrieval API should return a file descriptor and authorised download information.
Example
GET /api/organisations/{orgId}/files/{fileId}


{
  "fileId": "file_123",
  "submissionId": "sub_456",
  "encryption": {
    "protocolVersion": 1,
    "suiteId": "approved-suite-id",
    "manifestDigest": "base64url-encoded-digest"
  },
  "ciphertext": {
    "length": 10485760,
    "downloadSessionRequired": true
  },
  "recipientEnvelopes": [
    {
      "recipientKeyId": "key_789",
      "envelopeId": "env_123"
    }
  ]
}


The suite identifier is illustrative.

The reviewer client should explicitly request a download session.
POST /api/organisations/{orgId}/files/{fileId}/download-sessions


The server may return either:
- A short-lived, scoped object-storage URL.
- An authenticated API streaming endpoint.
The download URL must never be exposed to public uploaders.
Download safeguards
- Short expiry.
- Exact object scope.
- No arbitrary object keys.
- Authorisation at session creation.
- Range requests only where compatible with authenticated chunk verification.
- Bounded concurrent downloads.
- Usage and audit accounting.
- Revocation of platform-controlled access where possible.
A presigned URL already issued to a client may remain usable until expiry even after a permission change. Use short lifetimes and proxy streaming for workflows requiring tighter revocation.
6. Browser decryption UX
I'd keep decryption separate from ordinary submission browsing.

A reviewer can inspect permitted metadata without automatically unlocking private keys.
Submission SI-8K4P2M
Example reviewer interface

Encrypted

Encrypted document

10.0 MiB · 1 recipient envelope available



Your private key is locked


Unlock your decryption key

Your key is used locally. It is not sent to the Secure Intake server.

Unlock and decrypt


Illustrative UI only; no cryptographic operation is performed.
The reviewer should explicitly initiate decryption.

I would not automatically decrypt every file when a reviewer opens a submission.
Preview versus download
I'd distinguish:
- Decrypt and preview: For supported file types, using a constrained local preview component.
- Decrypt and save: Write plaintext to a reviewer-selected destination.
- Retrieve ciphertext: Download the encrypted package without decrypting.
Browser previewing introduces additional attack surfaces.

PDFs, images and office documents can contain malicious content or exploit vulnerabilities in parsers.

I'd initially support a restricted set of preview types and provide decrypted download for everything else.
7. Plaintext handling
The client should minimise plaintext persistence.

For browser decryption:
- Do not upload plaintext back to the API.
- Avoid persistent browser storage.
- Use bounded memory.
- Clear application-held buffers where practical.
- Revoke temporary object URLs.
- Avoid caching decrypted responses.
- Do not send plaintext filenames or contents to analytics.
- Avoid exposing plaintext through diagnostic logs.
JavaScript cannot guarantee complete memory erasure because of garbage collection and browser internals.

For desktop clients, decrypted files should be written only to explicitly selected locations.

Temporary files should be avoided or managed carefully.

The client should warn users that downloaded plaintext is outside the platform's cryptographic control.
8. Desktop client architecture
I'd implement the desktop client in .NET 10, sharing protocol models and cryptographic interoperability tests with the backend.

For the first release, I would favour a cross-platform CLI as the foundation.

A graphical desktop application can then reuse the same application services.
Suggested projects
src/
  SecureIntake.Client.Core/
  SecureIntake.Client.Crypto/
  SecureIntake.Client.Auth/
  SecureIntake.Client.Storage/
  SecureIntake.Client.Cli/
  SecureIntake.Client.Desktop/


The desktop application should not depend on the server's internal database models or infrastructure services.

It communicates through published API contracts.
Desktop technology
For the GUI, I'd evaluate Avalonia or another cross-platform .NET desktop framework.

The CLI can be built first and independently distributed.

The final GUI choice should account for macOS, Windows and Linux support, packaging, accessibility and Native AOT compatibility.
9. CLI design
I'd use a dedicated reviewer command namespace.
secure-intake-client auth login \
  --server https://intake.example.com

secure-intake-client keys list

secure-intake-client submissions list \
  --organisation org_123

secure-intake-client files download \
  --file file_456 \
  --output ./document.sif

secure-intake-client files decrypt \
  --input ./document.sif \
  --output ./document.pdf


The .sif extension is a proposed encrypted package format.

The actual file format should be formally specified before implementation.
Key management commands
secure-intake-client keys generate \
  --suite approved-suite-id

secure-intake-client keys register \
  --organisation org_123

secure-intake-client keys export-public \
  --output reviewer-public.json

secure-intake-client keys backup \
  --output reviewer-key-backup.sik


Private-key backup must require explicit confirmation and encryption.

The CLI should never print private keys to ordinary terminal output.
10. Authentication token custody
Desktop and CLI authentication tokens require separate handling from encryption private keys.

I'd store refresh tokens in an OS-protected credential store where supported.

Access tokens should be short-lived.

For headless automation, use dedicated machine credentials with narrow scopes.

Do not reuse a human reviewer's interactive session token for unattended processing.

A service principal may be authorised to retrieve ciphertext, but its ability to decrypt depends on its separately controlled private key.
11. Cryptographic interoperability
I'd establish one formal encrypted file format.

The browser, desktop client and automation clients must produce and consume compatible ciphertext.
Package structure
Conceptually:
Secure Intake Encrypted File
├── Format header
├── Protocol version
├── Encryption suite identifier
├── File identifier
├── Authenticated manifest
├── Recipient key envelopes
└── Encrypted chunks


The exact encoding should be deterministic or canonically specified.

I'd prefer a format with explicit length bounds, versioning and canonical serialization.

All security-relevant metadata must be authenticated or cryptographically bound to the encrypted content.
Interoperability matrix
Encrypting client	Decrypting client	Required
Browser	Browser	Yes
Browser	Desktop	Yes
Browser	CLI	Yes
Browser	Automation client	Yes
Desktop	Browser	If desktop encryption is supported
CLI	Desktop	If CLI encryption is supported

The initial product only requires public upload encryption and authorised reviewer decryption.

Desktop-side encryption can be added later without changing the inbound-only product boundary.
12. Test vectors
I'd publish protocol test vectors covering:
- Single recipient.
- Multiple recipients.
- Organisation recovery recipient.
- Empty file.
- Small file.
- Multi-chunk file.
- Large file.
- Corrupted chunk.
- Modified manifest.
- Invalid recipient envelope.
- Unsupported suite.
- Duplicate nonce detection.
- Truncated ciphertext.
- Wrong private key.
- Key rotation and historical envelopes.
Each supported client must pass the same test vectors.

I'd also require browser-to-CLI and CLI-to-browser interoperability tests in CI.
13. Key revocation and access changes
Revocation has two distinct effects.

Platform access revocation: The API stops authorising future ciphertext retrieval.

Cryptographic key revocation: The key is no longer used for new encryption, and the platform may restrict future access to its envelopes.

Neither can revoke plaintext or ciphertext already downloaded.

If a private key is compromised, rewrapping the same file key for a new recipient does not restore confidentiality.

The platform should identify affected files and support full re-encryption through an authorised client where necessary.
14. Organisation-managed automation
Automation clients should be treated as cryptographic recipients, not privileged server internals.

For example, an organisation might run its own document-processing service.

That service:
1. Authenticates with a scoped machine identity.
2. Retrieves authorised ciphertext.
3. Obtains its private key from organisation-controlled custody.
4. Decrypts outside the Secure Intake server.
5. Processes the plaintext in its own environment.
The Secure Intake platform does not receive the plaintext processing results unless the organisation explicitly sends permitted metadata through a separate integration.

This preserves the inbound-only model.
15. Audit events
I'd add:
reviewer.key.generated
reviewer.key.registered
reviewer.key.activated
reviewer.key.revoked
reviewer.ciphertext_access.authorised
reviewer.ciphertext_access.denied
reviewer.download_session.created
reviewer.download_session.expired
reviewer.key_recovery.requested
reviewer.key_recovery.approved


The server cannot reliably know whether an external client successfully decrypted a file.

A client may optionally report decryption success or failure, but those reports are client assertions, not independently verified server facts.

The audit UI must make that distinction clear.
16. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-keys
POST /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-keys
POST /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-keys/{keyId}/activate
POST /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-keys/{keyId}/revoke

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/submissions
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/files/{fileId}
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/files/{fileId}/envelopes

POST /api/organisations/{id}/files/{fileId}/download-sessions

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-access-policies
PUT  /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-access-policies


Key registration and revocation should require appropriate permissions and step-up authentication.
17. Implementation priorities
I'd implement reviewer clients in this order:
1. Formal encrypted file format.
2. Cryptographic test vectors.
3. Shared API contracts.
4. CLI authentication and ciphertext retrieval.
5. CLI decryption.
6. Reviewer key generation and registration.
7. Browser key custody and decryption.
8. Browser preview and decrypted download.
9. Desktop GUI.
10. Organisation-managed KMS/HSM integrations.
This order gives the project an independently distributed reference client before relying on browser-based decryption.

18. Next decision: private-key portability
The platform supports browser, desktop and CLI decryption. We now need to decide how easily reviewers can move their private keys between devices.

This affects convenience, recovery and the security of private-key custody.

Device-bound keys by default

Each device has its own key pair. Private keys are not exported except through separately authorised recovery procedures.


Portable encrypted keys

Reviewers can export and import encrypted private-key packages between browser, desktop and CLI clients.


Policy-controlled portability

Organisations choose device-bound, exportable encrypted or externally managed keys. Exports require explicit permissions and audit.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, private-key portability should use organisation policy-controlled portability supporting device-bound, encrypted exportable and externally managed keys. Continue planning key export/import, device enrolment, key backup, recovery, revocation and cross-client portability.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.6
Key portability · Device enrolment · Encrypted exports · Backup · Recovery · Revocation · Cross-client compatibility

Decision confirmed: Each organisation controls how its reviewers' private keys are stored, exported, backed up and used across devices.

The platform will support three key-custody models:
1. Device-bound: Private keys remain on the device and cannot be exported through supported application workflows.
2. Encrypted portable: Private keys may be exported into a protected, interoperable package.
3. Externally managed: Private-key operations are delegated to an organisation-controlled KMS, HSM or compatible cryptographic provider.
The policy must be enforced by the clients as well as the server. However, software-only controls cannot guarantee that a private key is non-exportable if the user or device administrator controls the execution environment.
1. Key custody architecture
Organisation key policy

Permitted custody · Export permissions · Backup requirements · Recovery rules


Device-bound

Local or hardware-protected key


Portable

Encrypted key package


External

Organisation-controlled key service




Common encryption protocol

Public-key registration · Recipient envelopes · Ciphertext decryption


Key custody should be a property of the individual key, governed by organisation policy, rather than a permanent property of the reviewer account.

A reviewer may have multiple keys with different custody arrangements.
2. Organisation key policy
I'd introduce a versioned policy with the following controls.
Setting	Supported values
Allowed custody	Device-bound, portable, external
Default custody	One of the allowed modes
Private-key export	Disabled, permitted, approval required
Private-key import	Disabled, permitted, approval required
Maximum active devices	Configurable
MFA for key enrolment	Required by default
MFA for export	Required
Independent export approval	Optional or mandatory
Key backup	Optional, required, prohibited
Recovery	Disabled, organisation key, external custody
Device attestation	Optional, where supported
Key expiry	Configurable
Revocation authority	Security Officer / authorised administrator

A policy change should affect new operations immediately, but it cannot retroactively make an already exported key non-exportable.

Likewise, a policy requiring device-bound keys should not silently delete existing portable keys.

Instead, the organisation should have a migration and retirement workflow.
Example policy
{
  "policyVersion": 3,
  "allowedCustodyModes": [
    "device_bound",
    "encrypted_portable",
    "external"
  ],
  "defaultCustodyMode": "device_bound",
  "exports": {
    "enabled": true,
    "requiresMfa": true,
    "requiresIndependentApproval": true
  },
  "imports": {
    "enabled": true,
    "requiresMfa": true
  },
  "devices": {
    "maximumActive": 5
  },
  "backup": {
    "requiredForPortableKeys": true
  }
}


The server must record the policy version under which each sensitive operation was authorised.
3. Device enrolment
Device enrolment should be distinct from user authentication and key registration.

A user can authenticate on a device without that device automatically becoming authorised for decryption.

I'd maintain a device registry:
reviewer_devices
  id
  organisation_id
  reviewer_identity_id
  display_name
  device_type
  client_type
  client_version
  public_device_key
  attestation_status
  enrolled_at
  last_seen_at
  revoked_at


Device names, operating systems and client versions are useful operational metadata, but should not be treated as trustworthy security evidence unless verified.
Enrolment workflow
1

Authenticate

Reviewer signs in using the supported identity provider and MFA



2

Register device

Client creates a device identifier and suitable device-bound credential



3

Evaluate policy

Check device limits, allowed clients and attestation requirements



4

Generate or import key

Create a new recipient key or import an authorised portable key



5

Prove key possession

Complete the suite-specific cryptographic challenge



6

Activate

Record device, key assignment and approvals



For device-bound keys, I'd recommend a separate key pair per device.

This improves compromise containment and makes device retirement clearer.

For portable keys, the same private key may exist on several devices, but that limits the effectiveness of device-specific cryptographic revocation.
4. Encrypted key export
Portable keys should use a formally specified encrypted package format.

I'd propose .sik for Secure Intake Key.

The package should be interoperable between supported browser, desktop and CLI implementations.
Conceptual package
Secure Intake Key Package (.sik)
├── Format identifier
├── Package version
├── Encryption suite
├── Key identifier
├── Public-key fingerprint
├── Key metadata
├── Key-encryption parameters
├── Salt and nonce
├── Encrypted private-key material
└── Authentication tag


The private-key payload must be authenticated and encrypted.

Metadata that affects interpretation must also be authenticated.
Passphrase protection
For passphrase-based export, I'd use a reviewed password-based key derivation mechanism such as Argon2id, followed by an approved authenticated encryption algorithm.

Parameters must be versioned and bounded to prevent malicious packages from exhausting client resources.

I'd also support wrapping a portable package using an organisation-approved public recovery or transfer key where the selected cryptographic suite permits it.

Do not invent a new key-wrapping construction.
5. Export authorisation
Key export is a sensitive operation.

I'd require:
- Active reviewer authentication.
- Recent MFA.
- Organisation export permission.
- Policy validation.
- Explicit key selection.
- Destination confirmation.
- Reason where required.
- Independent approval if configured.
- Audit event.
The server can authorise an export workflow, but it must not receive the plaintext private key.

The client performs the export locally.
Export authorisation token
I'd issue a short-lived, one-time authorisation for a specific key and operation.

Conceptually:
{
  "operation": "reviewer_key.export",
  "organisationId": "org_123",
  "reviewerId": "usr_456",
  "keyId": "key_789",
  "approvalId": "approval_123",
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-03T12:00:00Z"
}


The actual authorisation should be cryptographically protected and bound to the authenticated session.

It should not contain private-key material.

Limitation: If a key is already exportable in software, a compromised client may copy it without invoking the authorised export workflow. Server-side export controls are governance controls, not absolute prevention of local key extraction.
6. Key import
Import must verify the package before activating the key.

Checks should include:
1. Supported package version.
2. Supported cryptographic suite.
3. Valid package structure and bounded parameters.
4. Successful authenticated decryption.
5. Public/private key consistency.
6. Expected key fingerprint.
7. Organisation policy.
8. Duplicate key detection.
9. Device registration and permissions.
A portable key imported onto another device should retain its cryptographic key identity.

However, the platform should create a separate device-key association.

This allows administrators to see which devices are known to hold the same key, although it cannot prove that no unregistered copies exist.
7. Key backup
I'd distinguish three backup strategies.
Strategy	Description	Limitation
Encrypted local backup	Reviewer exports an encrypted .sik package	Reviewer must protect backup and passphrase
Organisation-controlled backup	Encrypted key package stored in approved external custody	Organisation must manage access and recovery
Device-bound with no export	No private-key backup	Device loss may permanently lose that key

The platform should not offer a server-side plaintext private-key backup service.

For portable backups, the server may store an opaque encrypted backup package only if explicitly enabled and approved by the organisation.

However, I'd keep that feature out of the first release.

An organisation-managed external backup destination provides a clearer custody boundary.
8. Recovery model
The existing organisation recovery policy remains authoritative.

Reviewer key recovery and file recovery are different operations.
Reviewer key recovery
Restores access to a particular private key, where a protected backup or external custody mechanism exists.
File recovery
Uses a separately authorised organisation recovery recipient to recover the file's encryption key and decrypt the file.

File recovery does not necessarily restore the reviewer's original private key.
Recovery scenarios
Scenario	Outcome
Reviewer loses device; portable backup exists	Import backup on replacement device
Reviewer loses device; external custody exists	Re-enrol through external key provider
Reviewer loses device-bound key	Register new key; historical access requires another authorised recipient or recovery
All recipient keys lost; organisation recovery exists	Recover files through approved recovery process
All keys lost; recovery disabled	Files may be permanently unrecoverable

I'd make these consequences explicit during organisation onboarding.
9. Revocation
Revocation should operate at multiple levels.
Revocation	Effect
Device revocation	Blocks future authenticated operations from that device where enforceable
Session revocation	Invalidates platform sessions and tokens
Reviewer key revocation	Prevents key use for new recipient envelopes
Reviewer membership removal	Blocks future platform authorisation
Organisation recovery-key revocation	Prevents future recovery envelopes using that key
Storage credential revocation	Blocks affected storage access

Revocation does not delete copies of private keys or plaintext already outside the platform.
Device-bound advantage
With separate keys per device, an organisation can stop using a compromised device's key without necessarily retiring every other device's key.

With one portable key shared across devices, compromise of one copy compromises the shared key.

For that reason, I'd recommend device-bound keys as the default custody mode.
10. Key rotation
Key rotation should create a new key identity.

It should not overwrite the old public key.
key_001  active
    ↓
key_001  retiring
key_002  active
    ↓
key_001  retired
key_002  active


New submissions use the active key set.

Existing encrypted files retain their historical recipient envelopes unless an authorised client performs a rewrap or full re-encryption operation.

The platform should track which files are associated with each recipient key.

This enables impact analysis after compromise or retirement.
11. Cross-client portability
The .sik format should be supported consistently.
Source	Destination	Portable key
Browser	Desktop	Supported
Browser	CLI	Supported
Desktop	Browser	Supported, subject to policy and suite
Desktop	CLI	Supported
CLI	Desktop	Supported
External KMS/HSM	Browser	Not assumed
Device-bound OS key	Another device	Not assumed

Hardware-backed keys may be non-exportable by design.

For those keys, cross-device access should use new key enrolment rather than private-key copying.
Browser limitations
A browser may not support every external key provider or cryptographic algorithm.

The platform should advertise client capabilities and reject unsupported import or decryption operations clearly.

It must not silently convert a device-bound or externally managed key into a portable software key.
12. Key inventory and administration
I'd provide a dedicated Keys & Devices administration area.
Reviewer Keys & Devices
Example

MacBook Pro

Device-bound · Key RK-2048

Active


Windows workstation

Portable encrypted key · RK-1024

Active


Previous laptop

Device-bound · Key RK-0812

Revoked


Illustrative inventory; device names and key identifiers are examples.
The UI should clearly distinguish:
- Key status.
- Device status.
- Custody mode.
- Exportability.
- Backup status, where verifiable.
- Last platform use.
- Cryptographic suite.
- Associated recipient groups.
- Historical file coverage.
A reported backup should not be presented as verified unless a restore test or equivalent validation has occurred.
13. API additions
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/key-policies
PUT  /api/organisations/{id}/key-policies

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-devices
POST /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-devices
POST /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-devices/{deviceId}/revoke

POST /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-keys/{keyId}/export-authorisations
POST /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-keys/{keyId}/import-registrations

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-keys/{keyId}/usage
POST /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-keys/{keyId}/rotate
POST /api/organisations/{id}/reviewer-keys/{keyId}/revoke


The API authorises and audits key-management operations.

It does not perform private-key export or import on behalf of the client.
14. Database additions
organisation_key_policies
organisation_key_policy_versions
reviewer_devices
reviewer_device_credentials
reviewer_device_key_assignments
reviewer_key_export_authorisations
reviewer_key_import_registrations
reviewer_key_backup_attestations
reviewer_key_recovery_operations
reviewer_key_rotation_operations
reviewer_key_revocation_events


I'd retain immutable historical key records for audit and file-envelope references.

Private-key material should not appear in these tables.
15. Security considerations
Several boundaries need explicit documentation.

Device-bound does not always mean hardware-bound. A software key stored locally may still be extractable by malware or a privileged local user.

Encrypted export does not guarantee safe custody. Weak passphrases, compromised endpoints and copied backups remain risks.

Key revocation is not retrospective secrecy. Previously obtained ciphertext and private keys may remain usable.

External custody does not automatically guarantee post-quantum support. HSM and KMS capabilities must be verified against the selected cryptographic suite.

Browser and desktop clients have different threat models. An independently installed client can reduce exposure to compromised web-delivered JavaScript, but introduces software distribution and update security requirements.
16. Implementation priorities
I'd implement key portability in this order:
1. Versioned key-custody policy.
2. Device registry and enrolment.
3. Per-device recipient key generation.
4. Encrypted .sik package specification.
5. Desktop/CLI export and import.
6. Browser import and export.
7. Export authorisation and approval.
8. Backup verification workflows.
9. Device and key revocation.
10. External KMS/HSM adapters.
The default should be device-bound keys, with portable exports and external custody enabled through organisation policy.

17. Next decision: reviewer access assignment
We now need to decide how encrypted submissions are assigned to reviewers.

This affects recipient selection during upload, team membership changes, reviewer onboarding and historical file access.

Individual reviewer recipients

Each submission encrypts its file keys for explicitly assigned reviewer public keys.


Organisation/team recipient keys

Submissions encrypt for organisation-controlled team keys, with membership managed separately.


Hybrid individual and team recipients

Dropboxes can use individual reviewer keys, organisation/team keys, or both, with explicit rules for historical access and key rotation.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, reviewer assignment should use a hybrid of individual reviewer keys and organisation/team recipient keys. Continue planning recipient selection, team membership, access grants, historical submissions, reassignment, group key rotation and cryptographic access boundaries.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.7
Hybrid reviewer assignment · Recipient selection · Team membership · Historical access · Reassignment · Group-key rotation

Decision confirmed: A dropbox may encrypt submissions for individual reviewers, organisation-controlled team recipients, or both.

I'd implement this using two independent mechanisms:
- Authorisation: Determines who may view submissions, retrieve ciphertext and perform reviewer operations.
- Cryptographic access: Determines which private keys can decrypt each file.
Both must be satisfied for a reviewer to retrieve and decrypt a file through the platform.

The distinction is essential. Adding a reviewer to a team must not silently imply that the reviewer can decrypt every historical file belonging to that team.
1. Recipient architecture
Dropbox recipient policy

Defines eligible individual, team and recovery recipients


Server-approved recipient snapshot

Specific public keys · Key versions · Encryption suites · Policy version


Browser encrypts file key

One independently authenticated recipient envelope per selected key


Reviewer

Individual key


Team

Group key


Recovery

Custody key




The uploader's client performs encryption. The server supplies the authorised recipient policy and public keys, but never receives the file's plaintext data-encryption key.
2. Recipient assignment modes
I'd support four dropbox-level policies.
Mode	Recipients	Use case
Individual	Selected reviewer keys	Confidential case-specific submissions
Team	Organisation-controlled team keys	Shared review queues
Hybrid	Individual and team keys	Flexible team-based workflows
Organisation	Organisation-controlled intake key	Centralised intake and subsequent assignment

The organisation mode is effectively a special case of team custody, but I'd expose it separately because its operational implications differ.
Recommended default
Hybrid mode, with a designated intake team as the primary recipient.

This avoids requiring the organisation to know which individual reviewer will handle a submission before it arrives.

Individual reviewer envelopes can be included where policy requires them.
3. Recipient policy configuration
Each dropbox should have a versioned recipient policy.

Example:
{
  "dropboxId": "dbx_123",
  "policyVersion": 4,
  "mode": "hybrid",
  "teamRecipients": [
    {
      "teamId": "team_finance",
      "required": true
    }
  ],
  "individualRecipients": [
    {
      "reviewerId": "usr_456",
      "required": false
    }
  ],
  "recoveryRecipient": {
    "enabled": true,
    "required": true
  },
  "historicalAccess": "explicit_rewrap"
}


This configuration is illustrative.

The recipient policy should be resolved into actual public-key identifiers when the upload session begins.

The resulting snapshot must be immutable for that upload session.

If a key is revoked before finalisation, the server must revalidate the snapshot and either reject finalisation or require a new encryption session.

It must not silently substitute another public key after the client has encrypted the file.
4. Recipient snapshot
The server should return a signed or otherwise authenticated recipient manifest.

Conceptually:
{
  "uploadSessionId": "upl_123",
  "organisationId": "org_123",
  "dropboxId": "dbx_123",
  "recipientPolicyVersion": 4,
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-03T12:00:00Z",
  "recipients": [
    {
      "recipientType": "team",
      "recipientId": "team_finance",
      "keyId": "key_team_004",
      "suiteId": "approved-suite-id",
      "publicKeyFingerprint": "sha256:..."
    },
    {
      "recipientType": "individual",
      "recipientId": "usr_456",
      "keyId": "key_user_008",
      "suiteId": "approved-suite-id",
      "publicKeyFingerprint": "sha256:..."
    }
  ]
}


The client must verify the authenticity and validity of the recipient information.

A signature alone is not sufficient if its verification key is obtained solely from the same potentially compromised origin. Higher-assurance clients should have an independently established trust anchor.

The authenticated file manifest should bind the recipient policy version, recipient key identifiers and encryption-suite selection to the encrypted submission.
5. Team membership
Team membership and team-key possession must be managed separately.

A reviewer can belong to a team without automatically receiving its private key.

I'd model:
teams
team_memberships
team_roles
team_recipient_keys
team_key_custody_assignments
team_key_access_grants


A team membership record grants organisational permissions.

A key access grant records the authorised mechanism through which the reviewer may use the team's cryptographic key.
Example
Reviewer	Team member	Ciphertext access	Team key available	Can decrypt
Alice	Yes	Yes	Yes	Yes
Ben	Yes	Yes	No	No
Charlie	No	No	Yes, previously copied	Not through platform; potentially offline
Dana	Yes	No	Yes	Not through platform

This illustrates why membership, retrieval permission and key custody must remain distinct.
6. Team-key custody
A shared team key introduces a substantial security decision.

I'd support two custody models.
A. Organisation-controlled team key
The team private key is held in an organisation-controlled KMS, HSM or secure service.

Reviewers obtain access through an authorised client or controlled cryptographic operation.

This is preferable where centralised revocation and custody are important.

However, the external service must support the selected encryption suite.
B. Distributed team key
The team private key is provisioned to approved reviewer devices using encrypted key packages.

This supports offline and browser decryption but increases exposure.

Once a reviewer has received an exportable team private key, removing that reviewer from the team cannot guarantee that the key has been destroyed.

For higher-security organisations, I'd recommend organisation-controlled custody rather than distributing one shared private key widely.
7. Alternative: individual envelopes for team members
A team does not necessarily need a shared private key.

The platform can expand a team into its eligible individual recipient keys when an upload starts.

For example:
Finance Team
├── Alice → key_A
├── Ben   → key_B
└── Dana  → key_D


The uploader encrypts the file key separately for each selected reviewer key.
Comparison
Property	Shared team key	Individual member envelopes
Number of envelopes	Usually fewer	Grows with team size
New member historical access	Possible if team key provided	Requires explicit rewrap
Removed member	Shared key may remain usable	Existing individual envelope remains usable
Compromise isolation	Shared key affects team files	Individual key affects files addressed to it
Offline decryption	Supported if key is available	Supported
Administration	Central key custody	More recipient management

I'd support both.

For ordinary teams, individual member envelopes are the safer default because they reduce the blast radius of a single private-key compromise.

For large or frequently changing teams, organisation-controlled team keys may be operationally preferable.
8. Historical submissions
Historical access must be explicit.

I'd support three policies:
Policy	Behaviour
No historical access	New members only receive access to future submissions
Explicit rewrap	Authorised client adds new recipient envelopes to selected historical files
Organisation custody	Authorised members may access historical files through the organisation-controlled team key

The default should be explicit rewrap.

This preserves a clear audit trail and prevents membership changes from automatically granting access to older encrypted submissions.
Important distinction
If a new member receives an existing shared team private key, that member may be able to decrypt historical ciphertext encrypted for that key.

An application setting labelled "no historical access" cannot prevent this cryptographically.

Therefore, strict historical access separation requires individual recipient envelopes or separate team key epochs with controlled custody.
9. Reviewer reassignment
Reassignment should be a workflow, not merely a database update.

Suppose Alice originally handled a submission and it is reassigned to Ben.
1

Request reassignment

Authorised manager selects the new reviewer



2

Validate permissions

Check submission policy, reviewer status and key eligibility



3

Determine key coverage

Check whether Ben already has a valid recipient envelope



4

Authorised rewrap

If required, an authorised client decrypts the file key and wraps it for Ben



5

Commit assignment

Update workflow assignment and envelope metadata atomically where possible



6

Audit and notify

Record the change and notify affected reviewers



The server cannot independently rewrap a file key because it does not possess the plaintext file key.

If no authorised client can decrypt the existing envelope, reassignment may require the organisation's recovery process.
10. Rewrap versus re-encryption
These operations must remain distinct.

Rewrap: Decrypt the existing file's data-encryption key in an authorised client and encrypt that same key for another recipient. The ciphertext remains unchanged.

Full re-encryption: Decrypt the file in an authorised client and encrypt its contents using a new data-encryption key, producing new ciphertext and recipient envelopes.

Rewrapping is suitable for adding legitimate recipients.

It is not sufficient to restore confidentiality after an old recipient private key or data-encryption key has been compromised.

Full re-encryption may be necessary in that case, although it cannot undo prior disclosure.
11. Group-key rotation
Team keys should have explicit versions or epochs.
Finance Team
├── Epoch 1 → key_team_001 → retired
├── Epoch 2 → key_team_002 → retiring
└── Epoch 3 → key_team_003 → active


New uploads must use the current active key epoch.

Historical submissions retain their original envelopes unless migrated.
Rotation triggers
- Scheduled key rotation.
- Reviewer departure.
- Suspected compromise.
- Custody-provider migration.
- Cryptographic suite migration.
- Organisation policy change.
I'd require an impact assessment before retiring a team key.

The system should identify historical files that still depend on that key.
12. Team member removal
Removing a member should trigger several independent actions:
1. Remove team membership.
2. Revoke active sessions and platform grants where appropriate.
3. Revoke device or external key-service access.
4. Stop using that reviewer's individual key for future submissions.
5. Evaluate whether shared team keys require rotation.
6. Assess historical file exposure.
7. Audit the removal.
If the reviewer possessed a distributed team private key, rotation should generally be required for future submissions.

Existing ciphertext encrypted under the old key remains potentially decryptable by that former reviewer if they retained a copy.
13. Access grant model
I'd distinguish:
submission_assignments
submission_access_grants
file_recipient_envelopes
team_key_access_grants


An assignment determines responsibility for reviewing a submission.

An access grant determines platform retrieval permissions.

A recipient envelope determines cryptographic capability.

These records are related but not interchangeable.
Example
{
  "submissionId": "sub_123",
  "assignedTeamId": "team_finance",
  "assignedReviewerId": "usr_456",
  "accessPolicy": {
    "allowTeamReviewers": true,
    "allowAssignedReviewer": true
  },
  "encryption": {
    "recipientPolicyVersion": 4,
    "keyEpoch": 3
  }
}


14. Access evaluation
I'd implement an explicit authorisation service.
public interface ISubmissionAccessEvaluator
{
    ValueTask<SubmissionAccessDecision> EvaluateAsync(
        SubmissionAccessRequest request,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record SubmissionAccessRequest(
    Guid OrganisationId,
    Guid SubmissionId,
    Guid ActorId,
    SubmissionAction Action);

public sealed record SubmissionAccessDecision(
    bool Allowed,
    string ReasonCode);


The evaluator checks organisation membership, scoped permissions, submission assignment, current policy and any required step-up authentication.

It should not assume that the presence of an envelope proves authorisation.

The cryptographic client independently verifies envelope integrity and key compatibility.
15. Administration interface
I'd provide a Review Teams & Recipients area.
Finance Review Team
Example

Members

8 reviewers

Recipient strategy

Individual envelopes

Current key epoch

3

Historical access

Explicit rewrap



Recipient coverage
Files accessible to active reviewers

92%




Files requiring rewrap

8%





Illustrative figures, not actual tenant data.
The interface should clearly distinguish assignment, retrieval permission and cryptographic access.

A reviewer should not be shown as fully able to access a submission merely because they belong to its assigned team.
16. Database additions
review_teams
review_team_memberships
review_team_roles
review_team_recipient_policies
review_team_key_epochs
review_team_key_custody_assignments

dropbox_recipient_policies
dropbox_recipient_policy_versions
upload_recipient_snapshots

submission_assignments
submission_access_grants
submission_reassignment_operations
