Recipient snapshots and historical key associations should be retained for audit and compromise-impact analysis.
17. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/review-teams
POST /api/organisations/{id}/review-teams
PUT  /api/organisations/{id}/review-teams/{teamId}

POST /api/organisations/{id}/review-teams/{teamId}/members
DELETE /api/organisations/{id}/review-teams/{teamId}/members/{memberId}

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/review-teams/{teamId}/recipient-policy
PUT  /api/organisations/{id}/review-teams/{teamId}/recipient-policy

POST /api/organisations/{id}/review-teams/{teamId}/rotate-key

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/assignments
POST /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/reassign

POST /api/organisations/{id}/files/{fileId}/rewrap-operations
POST /api/organisations/{id}/files/{fileId}/reencryption-operations


Rewrap and re-encryption endpoints coordinate authorisation, metadata and audit. The actual cryptographic operation happens in an authorised client.
18. Implementation priorities
I'd implement hybrid recipient assignment in this order:
1. Team and membership model.
2. Versioned dropbox recipient policies.
3. Individual recipient expansion.
4. Immutable upload recipient snapshots.
5. Cryptographic envelope validation.
6. Submission assignments and access grants.
7. Reassignment workflow.
8. Client-side rewrap protocol.
9. Team-key custody and epochs.
10. Full re-encryption workflow.
For the first release, I'd prioritise individual reviewer envelopes with team-based assignment.

That delivers most of the operational flexibility while avoiding the additional custody risks of widely distributed shared team private keys.

19. Next decision: submission review workflow
We now have the cryptographic access model. The next step is deciding how reviewers process submissions after receiving them.

Simple review statuses

Received, In Review, Completed and Closed, with reviewer assignment and internal notes.


Configurable review workflows

Organisation-defined review stages, assignment rules, approval requirements, internal notes and escalation, while retaining standard public-facing statuses.


External workflow systems

Secure Intake handles submissions and encryption; external applications manage most review processes through APIs and webhooks.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, submission review should use configurable organisation-defined review workflows with assignments, approvals, internal notes and escalation. Continue planning workflow states, assignment rules, approvals, internal notes, escalations, public status mapping and workflow automation.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.8
Configurable review workflows · State transitions · Assignments · Approvals · Internal notes · Escalations · Public status mapping · Automation

Decision confirmed: Organisations can define their own submission review workflows, including stages, assignment rules, approvals, internal notes, escalations and automation.

I'd implement this as a versioned workflow engine within the existing modular monolith, rather than embedding workflow rules throughout the submission API.

The engine should remain optional for API consumers. An organisation can use the built-in review workflow, or integrate an external system through the existing APIs and webhooks.

The platform remains an inbound-only secure file-intake system, not a general document exchange or messaging platform.
1. Workflow architecture
Encrypted submission received

Submission and ciphertext committed


Workflow instance

Versioned definition · Current state · Assignment · Due dates


Assignment

Reviewer or team


Approvals

Human decisions


Escalations

Deadlines and alerts




Durable transition + audit + outbox

Consistent state changes and at-least-once notifications


The workflow engine must not decrypt files or require access to plaintext.

Its decisions are based on submission metadata, authorised reviewer actions and explicitly supplied workflow information.
2. Workflow states
Each organisation should be able to define its own internal states.

I'd provide a default workflow:
Received


Awaiting Assignment


In Review


More Information Required


Pending Approval




Completed


Closed


This is a default template, not a mandatory state sequence. Organisations can add, remove or rename internal stages within defined platform constraints.
State properties
{
  "key": "pending_approval",
  "displayName": "Pending Approval",
  "category": "active",
  "terminal": false,
  "publicStatus": "in_review",
  "requiresAssignment": true
}


State keys should be immutable within a workflow version.

Display names may be localised.
3. Workflow definitions and versioning
I'd treat every published workflow definition as immutable.
Workflow: Standard Document Review
├── Draft v4
├── Published v3
├── Published v2
└── Published v1


New submissions use the current published version.

Existing submissions remain on the version under which they started unless explicitly migrated.

This prevents a workflow edit from silently changing the meaning of an in-progress submission.
Example definition
{
  "key": "document_review",
  "version": 3,
  "initialState": "received",
  "states": [
    {
      "key": "received",
      "category": "active",
      "publicStatus": "received"
    },
    {
      "key": "in_review",
      "category": "active",
      "publicStatus": "in_review"
    },
    {
      "key": "pending_approval",
      "category": "active",
      "publicStatus": "in_review"
    },
    {
      "key": "completed",
      "category": "terminal",
      "publicStatus": "completed"
    }
  ],
  "transitions": [
    {
      "key": "start_review",
      "from": "received",
      "to": "in_review",
      "requiredPermission": "submissions.review"
    },
    {
      "key": "request_approval",
      "from": "in_review",
      "to": "pending_approval",
      "requiredPermission": "submissions.request_approval"
    }
  ]
}


The actual schema should be validated against a published JSON Schema.

I would not support arbitrary executable code inside workflow definitions.
4. Transition engine
Every workflow transition should pass through a single application service.
public interface IWorkflowTransitionService
{
    Task<WorkflowTransitionResult> ExecuteAsync(
        WorkflowTransitionRequest request,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record WorkflowTransitionRequest(
    Guid OrganisationId,
    Guid SubmissionId,
    string TransitionKey,
    Guid ActorId,
    long ExpectedVersion,
    string? Reason,
    string IdempotencyKey);

public sealed record WorkflowTransitionResult(
    Guid WorkflowInstanceId,
    string PreviousState,
    string CurrentState,
    long Version);


The transition service should:
1. Resolve the tenant and workflow instance.
2. Validate the expected instance version.
3. Verify actor permissions and transition guards.
4. Check approvals, holds and other relevant constraints.
5. Commit the new state and audit record.
6. Write notification and automation events to the durable outbox.
Use optimistic concurrency to prevent simultaneous reviewers from overwriting each other's changes.

An idempotency key should make retries safe.
5. Assignment rules
Assignments should support both teams and individual reviewers.

I'd provide these rule types:
Rule	Behaviour
Fixed team	Assign to a configured review team
Fixed reviewer	Assign to a designated reviewer
Round robin	Distribute across eligible reviewers
Least active	Prefer reviewers with fewer active assignments
Metadata-based	Match on approved submission metadata
Manual	Leave in an unassigned queue
Escalation assignment	Reassign when a deadline or condition is met

Rules must operate only on metadata available to the server.

Because submitted files are encrypted, the workflow engine cannot inspect their contents.
Example assignment rule
{
  "key": "finance_assignment",
  "trigger": "submission.received",
  "strategy": "least_active",
  "eligibleTeamId": "team_finance",
  "fallback": "manual_queue"
}


For fairness, round-robin and least-active allocation should use transactional coordination.

A reviewer should not be assigned simply because their account is active. They must also be eligible under the applicable access and recipient-key policies.
6. Assignment versus decryption access
The v2.7 cryptographic boundary remains unchanged.

Assigning a submission does not automatically grant the ability to decrypt it.

For each assignment, the platform should calculate:
- Workflow assignment eligibility.
- Ciphertext retrieval permission.
- Available recipient envelopes.
- Whether a client-side rewrap is required.
- Whether organisation recovery is necessary.
I'd expose this as a recipient-coverage status.
{
  "submissionId": "sub_123",
  "assignedReviewerId": "usr_456",
  "retrievalAuthorised": true,
  "recipientEnvelopeAvailable": false,
  "cryptographicActionRequired": "rewrap"
}


The server can establish envelope availability but cannot prove that a reviewer still possesses the corresponding private key.

If a rewrap is required, the assignment may be recorded as pending cryptographic access rather than immediately ready for review.
7. Approval stages
Workflow approvals are separate from the existing platform security approval engine, although both can share infrastructure.

I'd distinguish:

Business review approval: An organisation-defined decision such as accepting a submission or completing a review.

Security-sensitive approval: A mandatory controlled operation such as recovery-key use, deletion or legal-hold release.

Organisations may configure business approvals, but cannot bypass mandatory platform security approvals.
Supported approval rules
Rule	Example
Single approver	One authorised manager
Multiple approvers	Two independent reviewers
Role-based	Organisation Security Officer
Team-based	Any eligible member of a specified team
Sequential	Reviewer then manager
Unanimous	All specified approvers
Quorum	Two of three eligible approvers

Approval policies should define whether the submitter of an approval request may approve their own request.

For sensitive operations, self-approval must remain prohibited.
Approval record
{
  "approvalId": "apr_123",
  "workflowInstanceId": "wf_456",
  "policyVersion": 2,
  "requiredApprovals": 2,
  "allowSelfApproval": false,
  "status": "pending",
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-05T12:00:00Z"
}


The approval engine must revalidate permissions and workflow state before applying the final decision.
8. Internal notes
Internal notes should be available to authorised reviewers and administrators.

They must never appear in public submission status responses.

I'd support:
- Plain-text notes.
- Limited Markdown formatting.
- Author and timestamp.
- Immutable note revisions.
- Visibility restricted by role and submission access.
- Audit of creation and modification.
- Optional mentions of authorised reviewers.
Encryption consideration
Internal notes are different from submitted files.

There are two possible models:
1. Server-readable internal notes, protected by database encryption and RBAC.
2. End-to-end encrypted internal notes, accessible only to cryptographic recipients.
For consistency with the platform's security positioning, I'd recommend E2EE internal notes by default, with any server-readable mode explicitly declared as a lower-confidentiality option.

E2EE notes introduce limitations: server-side full-text search, content-based automation and moderation are unavailable.

The workflow engine can still use note metadata such as author, timestamp and note count.
9. Note editing and retention
I'd avoid destructive editing of internal notes.

Instead, support append-only revisions:
Note 123
├── Revision 1 — created
├── Revision 2 — corrected
└── Revision 3 — redacted


The UI displays the current revision while authorised auditors can inspect permitted historical metadata and revisions.

Encrypted note revisions must remain covered by retention and legal-hold policies.

A redaction operation should not be represented as guaranteed cryptographic erasure if earlier ciphertext or keys remain available.
10. Escalations
Escalations should be policy-driven and executed by durable background jobs.

I'd support:
- Time since submission.
- Time in current state.
- Time since assignment.
- Approval deadline.
- Reviewer inactivity.
- Repeated transition failures.
- Missing cryptographic access.
- Explicit manual escalation.
Example escalation policy
{
  "key": "review_overdue",
  "trigger": {
    "type": "state_duration",
    "state": "in_review",
    "durationHours": 48
  },
  "actions": [
    {
      "type": "notify",
      "recipient": "team_manager"
    },
    {
      "type": "set_priority",
      "value": "high"
    }
  ]
}


Escalation rules should have deduplication and cooldown controls.

A failed notification must not repeatedly create duplicate escalation records.
11. Public status mapping
Internal workflow states should map to a small, stable set of public statuses.

I'd retain the existing public vocabulary:
Public status	Meaning
Received	Submission accepted
In review	Organisation is processing it
More information required	Additional inbound information requested
Completed	Review completed
Closed	Submission workflow closed

Organisations can customise internal states but should not invent arbitrary public status codes.

This preserves API compatibility and prevents accidental disclosure of internal processes.
Example mapping
Internal state	Public status
Awaiting Assignment	Received
Assigned	In review
Pending Manager Approval	In review
Legal Review	In review
Awaiting Additional Documents	More information required
Approved	Completed
Rejected	Completed
Archived	Closed

The public response should not reveal internal reviewer identities, escalation details, security flags or confidential notes.
12. More-information requests
Because the product is inbound-only, a request for additional information must not become a two-way messaging feature.

I'd support a controlled request that permits the uploader to add another submission revision.

For example:
{
  "requestId": "req_123",
  "submissionId": "sub_456",
  "requestType": "additional_files",
  "messageTemplate": "additional_documents_required",
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-10T12:00:00Z"
}


The uploader may return through the existing scoped return-access mechanism, complete required verification and upload additional encrypted files.

The organisation does not send files back to the uploader.

Free-form communication should remain outside the platform or be added only through a separate, explicit product decision.
13. Workflow automation
I'd implement a constrained rules engine rather than arbitrary scripts.

Supported triggers:
submission.received
submission.revision.added
submission.assigned
workflow.state.changed
workflow.approval.completed
workflow.deadline.reached
workflow.escalation.triggered


Supported actions:
assign_team
assign_reviewer
set_priority
set_due_date
request_approval
transition_state
send_notification
emit_webhook
create_internal_task


No workflow rule should be able to decrypt submitted files or export private keys.
Example rule
{
  "name": "Auto-assign new submissions",
  "trigger": "submission.received",
  "conditions": [
    {
      "field": "dropbox.category",
      "operator": "equals",
      "value": "finance"
    }
  ],
  "actions": [
    {
      "type": "assign_team",
      "teamId": "team_finance"
    },
    {
      "type": "set_priority",
      "value": "normal"
    }
  ]
}


Conditions should use allowlisted fields and operators.

I would not support arbitrary SQL, JavaScript, C# expressions or HTTP calls inside workflow definitions.

External integrations use the existing webhook framework.
14. Automation safety
Automation can create loops.

For example:
State changed
   ↓
Rule changes state
   ↓
State changed
   ↓
Rule changes state


I'd enforce:
- Maximum automation depth.
- Per-event execution limits.
- Idempotency.
- Loop detection.
- Rule cooldowns.
- Explicit transition permissions.
- Durable execution history.
- Dead-letter handling.
- Tenant-level quotas.
Automation must never bypass required approvals.

If a transition requires human approval, a rule may request the approval but cannot manufacture the decision.
15. Workflow administration
I'd provide a desktop-first workflow editor.
Document Review Workflow
Version 3 · Published

Active

1

Received

Automatic assignment



2

In Review

Assigned reviewer required



3

Pending Approval

Two approvers



4

Completed

Terminal state




Published workflow

4 states · 5 transitions

Illustrative workflow configuration.
The editor should provide:
- Draft and published versions.
- State and transition configuration.
- Assignment rules.
- Approval policies.
- Escalation rules.
- Public status mapping.
- Validation and simulation.
- Audit history.
Workflow publishing should validate that every reachable nonterminal state has a valid path to completion or closure, unless intentionally configured as a waiting state with an escalation or administrative escape route.
16. Database additions
workflow_definitions
workflow_definition_versions
workflow_states
workflow_transitions

workflow_transition_history

workflow_assignment_rules
workflow_approval_policies
workflow_approval_requests
workflow_approval_decisions

submission_internal_notes
submission_internal_note_revisions

workflow_escalation_policies
workflow_escalation_events
workflow_automation_rules
workflow_automation_executions
workflow_deadlines


Workflow instances and their history must remain tenant-scoped.

For dedicated tenant databases, the workflow records reside in the corresponding tenant data plane.
17. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/workflows
POST /api/organisations/{id}/workflows
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/workflows/{workflowId}
POST /api/organisations/{id}/workflows/{workflowId}/publish

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/workflow
POST /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/transitions

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/notes
POST /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/notes

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/workflow-approvals
POST /api/organisations/{id}/workflow-approvals/{approvalId}/approve
POST /api/organisations/{id}/workflow-approvals/{approvalId}/reject

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/workflow-automations
POST /api/organisations/{id}/workflow-automations


The API should expose permitted transitions for the current actor, so clients do not have to duplicate workflow authorisation logic.
18. Implementation priorities
I'd implement configurable workflows in this order:
1. Workflow definition schema and validator.
2. Versioned workflow publishing.
3. Workflow instances and transition engine.
4. Default review workflow.
5. Team and reviewer assignment integration.
6. Internal notes.
7. Approval stages.
8. Deadline and escalation workers.
9. Public status mapping.
10. Constrained automation rules and simulation.
The central design rule is that workflow state, reviewer assignment, ciphertext retrieval permission and cryptographic access remain separate concerns.

A workflow can decide that a reviewer should process a submission, but it cannot grant cryptographic access without the required recipient key or an authorised client-side rewrap.

19. Next decision: submission metadata and custom forms
Configurable workflows raise another requirement: organisations may want to collect structured information alongside uploaded documents.

This could include reference numbers, categories, descriptions, dates and custom fields.

The question is whether those fields are server-readable for routing and search, or encrypted like the submitted documents.

Server-readable structured metadata

Custom form fields can be searched, filtered and used in workflow automation, protected by RBAC and database security.


End-to-end encrypted form fields

Custom fields are encrypted for recipients, maximising confidentiality but limiting server-side search and automation.


Field-level confidentiality

Organisations classify each field as server-readable or end-to-end encrypted. Only approved server-readable fields may drive workflow automation.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, submission metadata and custom forms should use field-level confidentiality with server-readable and end-to-end encrypted fields. Continue planning custom form schemas, validation, field-level encryption, indexing, workflow integration, data retention and privacy.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v2.9
Custom forms · Field-level confidentiality · Schema validation · Encryption · Indexing · Workflow integration · Retention · Privacy

Decision confirmed: Organisations can create custom submission forms containing both server-readable fields and end-to-end encrypted fields.

The platform must enforce this distinction throughout the submission lifecycle, including API responses, database storage, search, workflow automation, notifications, audit and exports.

The governing rule is:

A field classified as end-to-end encrypted must never require the Secure Intake server to access its plaintext value.
1. Form architecture
I'd implement a versioned form-definition system associated with each dropbox.

A dropbox can use a simple file-upload form or a more complex structured intake form.
Published form definition

Field types · Validation · Confidentiality · Visibility · Schema version


Uploader completes form

Client validates fields and separates confidentiality classes


Server-readable

Validated values · Controlled indexing · Workflow rules


End-to-end encrypted

Ciphertext · Recipient envelopes · Client-side validation




Submission committed

Immutable schema version · Authenticated metadata · Audit · Retention


The form renderer should use the same frontend build in integrated and separately hosted portal deployments.

No organisation-provided JavaScript should be executed.
2. Field confidentiality classes
I'd define two primary classes.
Class	Server sees value	Searchable	Workflow conditions	Reviewer decryption
server_readable	Yes	If explicitly indexed	Yes, if allowlisted	Not required
end_to_end_encrypted	No	No plaintext indexing	No content-based conditions	Required

A third concept, public presentation metadata, can exist for form labels, help text and field configuration.

That is schema metadata, not a separate submission-value confidentiality class.
Important privacy distinction
Server-readable does not mean public.

Server-readable values remain tenant-scoped and protected by RBAC, database controls, retention policies and audit.

However, the operator-controlled server can technically access those values.

This must be clear to organisations configuring their forms.
3. Supported field types
I'd support a constrained set of built-in field types.
Field type	Server-readable	E2EE
Short text	Yes	Yes
Long text	Yes	Yes
Number	Yes	Yes
Date	Yes	Yes
Boolean	Yes	Yes
Single select	Yes	Yes
Multi-select	Yes	Yes
Email	Yes	Yes
Telephone	Yes	Yes
Reference number	Yes	Yes
File attachment	No plaintext	Yes, existing file encryption
Static text / heading	Not applicable	Not applicable

File attachments should continue using the existing encrypted-file pipeline rather than introducing a second encryption system.

I'd exclude arbitrary HTML, scripts, custom field plugins and executable validation rules from the first release.
4. Form schema
I'd define a platform-specific schema rather than exposing unrestricted JSON Schema functionality directly to organisation administrators.

JSON Schema can still be used internally for validation.
Example
{
  "formKey": "customer_documents",
  "version": 3,
  "title": "Customer Document Submission",
  "fields": [
    {
      "key": "reference_number",
      "type": "text",
      "label": "Reference number",
      "required": true,
      "confidentiality": "server_readable",
      "validation": {
        "minLength": 3,
        "maxLength": 40
      },
      "indexing": {
        "enabled": true,
        "mode": "exact"
      }
    },
    {
      "key": "description",
      "type": "textarea",
      "label": "Description",
      "required": false,
      "confidentiality": "end_to_end_encrypted",
      "validation": {
        "maxLength": 5000
      }
    },
    {
      "key": "document_category",
      "type": "select",
      "label": "Document category",
      "required": true,
      "confidentiality": "server_readable",
      "options": [
        "identity",
        "financial",
        "other"
      ],
      "indexing": {
        "enabled": true,
        "mode": "exact"
      }
    }
  ]
}


Field keys must be stable identifiers, not derived from display labels.

The published schema must be immutable.

New form versions may add or modify fields, but historical submissions retain their original schema version.
5. Form builder
I'd provide a desktop-first form builder in the administration UI.
Customer Document Form
Draft version 4

Example

Reference number
Server-readable


Required · Exact-match indexing

Description
E2EE



Optional · Not searchable by server

Document category
Server-readable

Select category

Required · Available to workflow rules


Illustrative form-builder preview. Controls are not editable in this example.
Confidentiality should be prominent in the editor.

I'd require confirmation when changing a field from E2EE to server-readable in a new version.

That change must not decrypt or reclassify historical values.
6. Validation model
Validation should occur at two levels.
Client validation
The public portal validates all fields before submission.

For E2EE fields, this is the only place where plaintext validation can occur within the Secure Intake platform.

Supported checks include:
- Required values.
- Length limits.
- Numeric bounds.
- Date ranges.
- Regular-expression patterns from a restricted, safe subset.
- Allowed select options.
- Cross-field conditions supported by the form renderer.
Server validation
The server validates server-readable fields normally.

For E2EE fields, it validates only the encrypted representation and permitted metadata.

That includes:
- Expected field identifiers.
- Required encrypted field presence.
- Encryption suite.
- Recipient-envelope coverage.
- Ciphertext size limits.
- Authenticated manifest structure.
- Schema version.
- Submission ownership.
The server cannot verify whether an encrypted field actually contains a valid email address, date or other plaintext value.

This is an inherent limitation of end-to-end encryption.
7. Conditional fields
I'd support conditional visibility using a constrained declarative rule model.

Example:
{
  "field": "financial_details",
  "visibleWhen": {
    "field": "document_category",
    "operator": "equals",
    "value": "financial"
  }
}


Conditions can reference both confidentiality classes within the browser.

However, the server can only independently evaluate conditions involving server-readable fields.

If a required field's visibility depends on an E2EE value, the server cannot verify the condition.

I'd therefore restrict security-sensitive server-enforced requirements to conditions the server can independently evaluate.

The form builder should identify these limitations before publishing.
8. Field-level encryption
I'd use the same approved cryptographic suite and recipient-key infrastructure as encrypted file submissions.

However, I would not encrypt each individual field using a completely independent, unrelated protocol.

Instead, I'd define an encrypted form payload containing the E2EE fields.

Conceptually:
Encrypted Form Payload
├── Format version
├── Organisation ID
├── Submission ID
├── Form definition ID and version
├── Field-value map
├── Authenticated metadata
└── Recipient envelopes


The client serialises E2EE values into a canonical representation, encrypts the payload locally and wraps its data-encryption key for the approved recipients.

This avoids unnecessary recipient envelopes for every individual field.
Why one encrypted payload?
It reduces:
- Cryptographic overhead.
- Key-management complexity.
- Envelope proliferation.
- Database fragmentation.
- Interoperability testing requirements.
It also makes it easier to authenticate the complete confidential form response.

For extremely large encrypted text fields, chunked encryption could be supported later, but ordinary form values should remain bounded.
9. Cryptographic binding
The encrypted form payload must be bound to the correct submission and schema.

I'd authenticate:
organisation_id
dropbox_id
submission_id
form_definition_id
form_version
encrypted_payload_version
recipient_policy_version


This prevents a valid encrypted payload from being silently transplanted into another submission or interpreted under an incompatible schema.

Server-readable values should also be included in a canonical submission commitment, so the reviewer client can detect unexpected changes to the combined submission record.

A commitment must be designed carefully: a plain hash of low-entropy sensitive values can leak information through guessing.

I'd use an authenticated manifest or an appropriately keyed commitment rather than treating ordinary unsalted hashes as confidentiality protection.
10. Storage model
I'd separate schema definitions from submitted values.
form_definitions
form_definition_versions
form_fields
form_field_options

submission_form_responses
submission_form_readable_values
submission_form_encrypted_payloads
submission_form_revisions


The server-readable values table contains typed, validated values.

The encrypted payload table contains ciphertext, encryption metadata and recipient-envelope references.
Example conceptual record
{
  "submissionId": "sub_123",
  "formVersion": 3,
  "readableValues": {
    "reference_number": "REF-2026-001",
    "document_category": "financial"
  },
  "encryptedPayload": {
    "payloadId": "ep_456",
    "protocolVersion": 1,
    "ciphertextLength": 2048,
    "recipientEnvelopeCount": 3
  }
}


The API must never substitute decrypted E2EE values into the readable-values response.
11. Indexing and search
Indexing should be explicit per server-readable field.

I'd support:
Index type	Appropriate fields
Exact match	Reference numbers, category codes
Range	Dates, numeric values
Prefix	Selected text identifiers
Full-text	Explicitly approved server-readable text
No index	Default for sensitive fields

Indexing should not be enabled automatically for every custom field.

Indexes increase storage, can expose additional information through operational access and may complicate retention.
Database strategy
For PostgreSQL, I'd use typed projections for approved searchable fields rather than relying entirely on arbitrary JSONB queries.

For standalone SQLite, the same logical model should be implemented with SQLite-compatible indexes.

The form schema compiler should produce an index plan that the operator can inspect.

Index creation should be handled through controlled migrations or schema-management jobs, not arbitrary user-supplied SQL.
12. Search API
I'd expose a constrained search endpoint.
POST /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/search


Example:
{
  "filters": [
    {
      "field": "reference_number",
      "operator": "equals",
      "value": "REF-2026-001"
    },
    {
      "field": "document_category",
      "operator": "equals",
      "value": "financial"
    }
  ],
  "pageSize": 50
}


The server validates each filter against the published schema and its indexing permissions.

An attempt to search an E2EE field should return a clear unsupported-operation error.

The API should not silently search encrypted values using deterministic encryption or unreviewed blind-index schemes.
13. Workflow integration
The v2.8 workflow engine may use approved server-readable fields.

For example:
{
  "trigger": "submission.received",
  "conditions": [
    {
      "field": "document_category",
      "operator": "equals",
      "value": "financial"
    }
  ],
  "actions": [
    {
      "type": "assign_team",
      "teamId": "team_finance"
    }
  ]
}


E2EE values must not be available to workflow conditions, notification templates, webhook payloads or server-side assignment rules.
Controlled derived metadata
An organisation may choose to collect a separate server-readable category alongside an E2EE description.

For example:
- document_category: server-readable.
- confidential_description: E2EE.
This allows useful automation without requiring the server to inspect the confidential description.

The form builder should encourage this pattern.
14. Reviewer experience
Reviewers should see server-readable fields immediately, subject to authorisation.

E2EE fields should remain locked until the reviewer explicitly decrypts them.
Submission SI-8K4P2M
In review

Reference number

REF-2026-001

Document category

Financial

Confidential description
Encrypted


Requires key

Unlock an authorised reviewer key to decrypt this field locally.


Illustrative reviewer interface.
The desktop and CLI clients should expose the same logical separation.
15. Data retention
Form values must follow the submission's retention policy.

I'd treat the following as one retention unit by default:
- Server-readable field values.
- Encrypted form payloads.
- Attached encrypted files.
- Recipient envelopes.
- Submission revisions.
Audit records and legally required tombstones may have separate retention rules.
Field-level retention
I'd defer arbitrary per-field retention periods initially.

They complicate schema history, encrypted payload composition and legal-hold handling.

If field-level retention becomes necessary later, encrypted fields may need separate encryption units to permit selective deletion.

The initial design should document that limitation.
16. Privacy and data minimisation
I'd make the form builder privacy-aware.

When an administrator adds a field, the UI should ask whether the server genuinely needs to read its value.

For example:
Information	Suggested classification
Internal reference number	Server-readable
Document category	Server-readable
Processing priority	Server-readable
Detailed personal circumstances	E2EE
Financial account information	E2EE
Medical information	E2EE
Supporting documents	E2EE

These are recommendations, not fixed classifications.

An organisation may have legitimate operational reasons to use a different classification, subject to deployment policy.
Metadata leakage
Even with E2EE values, the server may observe:
- Field presence.
- Ciphertext size.
- Submission timing.
- Organisation and dropbox.
- Recipient key identifiers.
- Number of attached files.
- Workflow state.
- Server-readable values.
The product must not claim that E2EE hides all metadata.

Where useful, encrypted payload padding can reduce some size leakage, at the cost of storage and bandwidth.
17. Schema changes and historical submissions
Published form versions are immutable.

A new version may:
- Add fields.
- Remove fields from future forms.
- Change labels.
- Change validation.
- Change select options.
- Change confidentiality classifications.
Historical submissions retain their original schema and encryption interpretation.

Changing a field from E2EE to server-readable requires a new schema version and applies only to new responses.

The platform must never decrypt historical values to populate new server-readable columns.
18. Form response revisions
The existing append-only submission revision model should apply to forms.

For example:
Submission SI-8K4P2M
├── Revision 1
│   ├── Form version 3
│   ├── Readable values
│   ├── Encrypted payload
│   └── Files A, B
└── Revision 2
    ├── Form version 3
    ├── Updated readable values
    ├── New encrypted payload
    └── File C


A revision should be an immutable snapshot or an explicitly defined patch with authenticated lineage.

I'd favour immutable snapshots for the first release because they are easier to audit and verify.
19. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/forms
POST /api/organisations/{id}/forms
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/forms/{formId}
POST /api/organisations/{id}/forms/{formId}/publish

GET  /api/public/dropboxes/{dropboxId}/form
POST /api/public/upload-sessions/{sessionId}/form-response

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/form
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/form/encrypted-payload

POST /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/search


Public endpoints must remain scoped to valid dropbox or upload-session capabilities.

The form definition endpoint should expose only published, public-safe schema information.
20. Implementation priorities
I'd implement field-level confidentiality in this order:
1. Form definition schema and validator.
2. Versioned form publishing.
3. Public form renderer.
4. Server-readable field validation and storage.
5. Encrypted form payload protocol.
6. Recipient-envelope integration.
7. Reviewer decryption support.
8. Controlled indexing and search.
9. Workflow field allowlisting.
10. Retention, revisions and privacy validation.
The key architectural principle is that server-readable metadata is an explicit operational choice, while E2EE fields remain cryptographically inaccessible to the platform.

21. Next decision: form templates and reusable field definitions
Organisations may create many dropboxes with similar forms.

We should decide whether forms are defined independently per dropbox or can be reused across an organisation.

Independent forms per dropbox

Every dropbox owns its own form definition and versions. Simpler isolation, but more duplication.


Reusable organisation form templates

Organisations maintain versioned form templates that can be assigned to multiple dropboxes, with controlled overrides and independent publication.


Platform and organisation template library

Includes built-in platform templates and organisation-managed templates, with explicit version pinning and inheritance.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, form templates should use reusable organisation form templates with version pinning and controlled overrides. Continue planning form template inheritance, publishing, dropbox assignment, schema migration, reusable fields and validation.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.0
Reusable organisation form templates · Version pinning · Controlled overrides · Publishing · Dropbox assignment · Schema migration · Validation

Decision confirmed: Organisations can create reusable, versioned form templates and assign them to multiple dropboxes. Each dropbox pins a specific published template version and may apply controlled presentation overrides.

I'd implement this using immutable published templates and compiled dropbox form snapshots, rather than resolving multiple levels of inheritance dynamically for every submission.

This ensures a submission always has a precise, reproducible schema.
1. Form template architecture
Organisation form template

Reusable fields · Validation · Confidentiality classifications


Published immutable version

Template ID · Version · Content digest


Finance Dropbox

Pinned to template v3

Custom heading and help text


Customer Support Dropbox

Pinned to template v2

Custom heading and branding




Compiled dropbox form snapshots

Stable schema · Fixed confidentiality · Validated overrides


The template is reusable, but each dropbox has an independently pinned effective schema.

Publishing a new template version must not silently change existing dropboxes.
2. Template ownership and scope
Templates should belong to exactly one organisation.

A template created by Organisation A must not be directly assignable to Organisation B.

I'd support:
Scope	Supported
Organisation-owned templates	Yes
Reuse across organisation dropboxes	Yes
Cross-organisation sharing	No, initially
Built-in example templates	Yes, copied into an organisation
Template export/import	Later, with schema validation
Arbitrary nested template inheritance	No

Avoiding nested inheritance prevents complicated resolution rules and unexpected confidentiality changes.

An organisation can copy a template to create a new independent template when significant structural differences are required.
3. Template lifecycle
I'd use explicit states:
Draft
  ↓
Validated
  ↓
Published
  ↓
Deprecated
  ↓
Archived


A template can have multiple published versions.

For example:
Customer Documents
├── v1 — Published, deprecated
├── v2 — Published
├── v3 — Published
└── v4 — Draft


Published versions are immutable.

Deprecating a version prevents new assignments if policy requires, but does not invalidate historical submissions or necessarily break existing pinned dropboxes.

Archiving a template must not delete versions referenced by submissions, legal holds or audit records.
4. Template schema
I'd separate template identity from template version content.
Example
{
  "templateId": "tpl_customer_documents",
  "version": 3,
  "name": "Customer Documents",
  "fields": [
    {
      "id": "fld_reference",
      "key": "reference_number",
      "type": "text",
      "required": true,
      "confidentiality": "server_readable",
      "validation": {
        "maxLength": 40
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "fld_description",
      "key": "description",
      "type": "textarea",
      "required": false,
      "confidentiality": "end_to_end_encrypted",
      "validation": {
        "maxLength": 5000
      }
    }
  ]
}


Field IDs should remain stable across compatible template versions.

Field keys should not be reused for unrelated meanings.

Changing a field's type or confidentiality may require a new field identity, depending on compatibility rules.
5. Reusable field definitions
I'd support a small organisation-level field catalogue.

This is useful for commonly repeated fields such as:
- Customer reference number.
- Case category.
- Submission date.
- Contact email.
- Confidential description.
- Internal processing reference.
A reusable field definition should specify its core semantics:
{
  "fieldDefinitionId": "field_customer_reference",
  "key": "customer_reference",
  "type": "text",
  "confidentiality": "server_readable",
  "validation": {
    "maxLength": 40
  }
}


Templates reference a specific version of a reusable field definition.

They must not dynamically inherit the latest version.
Why version pinning matters
Suppose an administrator changes a reusable field from server-readable to E2EE.

That change must not automatically alter every template using the field.

Instead, the new field version becomes available for deliberate adoption.

This is particularly important for workflow rules and search indexes.
6. Controlled overrides
Dropbox overrides should be deliberately limited.

I'd allow:
Property	Override
Form heading	Yes
Introductory text	Yes
Help text	Yes
Placeholder text	Yes
Field display order	Yes, within constraints
Optional field visibility	Yes, if permitted by template
Branding	Yes
Field type	No
Confidentiality classification	No
Required field status	No, initially
Validation rules	No
Indexing configuration	No
Workflow metadata classification	No

The guiding principle is that dropbox overrides can change presentation but not data meaning or security guarantees.

If a dropbox needs different validation or confidentiality, create a new template version or fork the template.
7. Dropbox assignment
Each dropbox should have an active form assignment.
dropbox_form_assignments
  id
  organisation_id
  dropbox_id
  template_id
  template_version_id
  override_version_id
  compiled_snapshot_id
  activated_at
  deactivated_at


The assignment must reference a published template version belonging to the same organisation.

A dropbox may have a pending assignment for a future cutover.

I'd allow a dropbox to operate without a custom form, using the platform's standard upload experience.
8. Compiled form snapshots
When a template version is assigned to a dropbox, the platform should compile an effective form snapshot.

The compiler:
1. Loads the immutable template version.
2. Resolves pinned reusable field definitions.
3. Applies permitted presentation overrides.
4. Validates the resulting schema.
5. Computes a canonical digest.
6. Stores the immutable effective snapshot.
7. Activates it for new upload sessions.
The compiled snapshot is what the public portal receives.

It should not need to dynamically resolve inheritance during an upload.
Snapshot example
{
  "snapshotId": "snap_123",
  "templateVersionId": "tplv_456",
  "overrideVersionId": "ovr_789",
  "schemaDigest": "sha256:...",
  "fields": [
    {
      "id": "fld_reference",
      "key": "reference_number",
      "label": "Your customer reference",
      "type": "text",
      "required": true,
      "confidentiality": "server_readable"
    }
  ]
}


The digest must be computed over a canonical representation.

A simple JSON serialisation without canonicalisation is insufficient because property ordering and representation differences can change hashes.
9. Publishing workflow
I'd make template publication a controlled operation.
1

Edit draft

Administrator modifies fields, validation and presentation



2

Validate

Check schema correctness, confidentiality and compatibility



3

Compare

Generate a diff against the previous published version



4

Preview

Render the form using the actual public portal component



5

Publish

Create immutable version and content digest



6

Assign

Explicitly update selected dropboxes to the new version



Publishing and assignment are separate actions.

That allows an organisation to publish a template update without immediately deploying it to every dropbox.
10. Template compatibility analysis
Before publication, I'd generate a compatibility report.
Change	Compatibility	Action
Change label	Compatible	Publish
Change help text	Compatible	Publish
Add optional field	Usually compatible	Publish
Add required field	Potentially breaking	New version; review integrations
Remove field	Breaking for consumers	Migration assessment
Change field type	Breaking	Prefer new field identity
Change confidentiality	Security-significant	Explicit review
Change select options	Potentially breaking	Review existing rules
Change indexing	Operational impact	Index migration plan
Change validation	Potentially breaking	Review client/API compatibility

Compatibility is contextual.

A change may be safe for new submissions but break external integrations that assume a particular field exists.

The platform should report both form-rendering compatibility and API/workflow compatibility.
11. Dropbox upgrade workflow
A dropbox should not automatically follow the latest template version.

I'd use explicit upgrades:
Dropbox A
  Current: Template v2
  Available: Template v3
       ↓
  Preview differences
       ↓
  Validate workflow dependencies
       ↓
  Compile new snapshot
       ↓
  Schedule or activate


Existing upload sessions must remain pinned to the snapshot they started with.

A template upgrade should not change a form halfway through an upload.

The server must continue accepting valid sessions created under an older snapshot until their configured expiry, subject to security revocation and compatibility policy.
12. Schema migration
I'd distinguish three migration types.
A. Template migration
Creates a new template version.

Does not modify existing submissions.
B. Dropbox assignment migration
Changes which compiled snapshot is used for new upload sessions.

Does not modify historical submissions.
C. Historical submission migration
Transforms previously stored responses.

This should not be automatic.

For server-readable fields, a controlled migration may be possible.

For E2EE fields, the server cannot transform plaintext values.

Any historical E2EE migration requiring plaintext must occur in an authorised client.

I'd defer automatic historical E2EE schema migration from the initial release.
13. Historical form rendering
A reviewer must be able to open a historical submission using the exact schema version under which it was submitted.

For example:
Submission SI-1001
  Form snapshot: snap_v2
  Template: Customer Documents v2

Submission SI-1002
  Form snapshot: snap_v3
  Template: Customer Documents v3


The reviewer UI should not reinterpret the older response using the current template.

Historical schema snapshots must remain available for the duration of their referenced data and applicable audit obligations.
14. Workflow dependency validation
The workflow engine may reference server-readable form fields.

For example:
{
  "field": "document_category",
  "operator": "equals",
  "value": "financial"
}


Before upgrading a dropbox, the platform should identify workflow rules that depend on fields being changed or removed.

I'd implement a dependency graph:
Form field
├── Workflow condition
├── Assignment rule
├── Search index
├── Notification template
├── Webhook projection
└── External API contract


The compiler should reject an assignment that would invalidate mandatory workflow rules.

For optional dependencies, it may allow the upgrade with explicit warnings and a documented fallback.

E2EE fields must never become available to server-side workflow conditions through template inheritance.
15. Reusable field versioning
I'd use immutable reusable field versions.
Customer Reference
├── v1 — text, max 20
├── v2 — text, max 40
└── v3 — text, max 40, updated label


A template pins a specific field version.

If an organisation publishes a new field version, existing templates remain unchanged.

I'd avoid a system where updating one shared field silently modifies dozens of published forms.
16. Validation engine
I'd implement a common form-schema validator in the application layer.
public interface IFormSchemaValidator
{
    FormValidationResult Validate(
        FormDefinition definition);
}

public sealed record FormValidationResult(
    bool IsValid,
    IReadOnlyList<FormValidationIssue> Issues);

public sealed record FormValidationIssue(
    string Code,
    string Path,
    string Message,
    ValidationSeverity Severity);

public enum ValidationSeverity
{
    Information,
    Warning,
    Error
}


The validator should check:
- Unique field keys and identifiers.
- Supported field types.
- Valid confidentiality classifications.
- Bounded validation constraints.
- Safe conditional expressions.
- No circular field dependencies.
- Valid reusable field references.
- Compatible override rules.
- Indexing permissions.
- Workflow field compatibility.
- Maximum form complexity.
The public TypeScript renderer should use a generated representation of the same schema contract.

It should not maintain an unrelated interpretation of field validation rules.
17. Form complexity limits
I'd introduce deployment and organisation limits.

For example:
Limit	Illustrative default
Fields per form	100
Conditional rules	100
Maximum conditional depth	5
Select options per field	250
Maximum label length	200 characters
Maximum help-text length	2,000 characters
Maximum published versions	Configurable retention
Maximum active templates	Organisation quota

These limits should be configurable within deployment-level hard limits.

The platform should reject pathological schemas that could cause excessive client processing or server validation costs.
18. Administration interface
I'd provide a Form Templates area.
Customer Documents
Published v3

Fields

12

Server-readable

5

End-to-end encrypted

7

Assigned dropboxes

4



Dropbox assignments
Finance Documents

Pinned to v3

Current

Customer Support

Pinned to v2

Upgrade available

Legal Intake

Pinned to v3

Current


Illustrative administration screen.
The interface should clearly show which dropboxes use each template version.

A bulk upgrade action should produce a per-dropbox compatibility report before applying changes.
19. Database additions
organisation_form_templates
organisation_form_template_versions

organisation_field_definitions
organisation_field_definition_versions

form_template_field_references
form_template_validation_reports

dropbox_form_assignments
dropbox_form_override_versions
dropbox_compiled_form_snapshots

form_template_publication_events
form_template_upgrade_operations
form_template_dependency_references


Published template versions and compiled snapshots should be immutable.

Use content digests and audit records to detect unexpected changes.
20. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/form-templates
POST /api/organisations/{id}/form-templates
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/form-templates/{templateId}

POST /api/organisations/{id}/form-templates/{templateId}/validate
POST /api/organisations/{id}/form-templates/{templateId}/publish

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/field-definitions
POST /api/organisations/{id}/field-definitions

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/dropboxes/{dropboxId}/form-assignment
PUT  /api/organisations/{id}/dropboxes/{dropboxId}/form-assignment

POST /api/organisations/{id}/dropboxes/{dropboxId}/form-preview
POST /api/organisations/{id}/dropboxes/{dropboxId}/form-upgrade-check


All endpoints must enforce organisation ownership and scoped permissions.

Publishing and bulk assignment changes should require appropriate administrative permissions and audit.
21. Implementation priorities
I'd implement reusable form templates in this order:
1. Organisation template registry.
2. Immutable template versions.
3. Reusable field definitions.
4. Template schema validation.
5. Dropbox version pinning.
6. Controlled presentation overrides.
7. Compiled effective form snapshots.
8. Public portal rendering.
9. Compatibility and dependency analysis.
10. Upgrade preview and bulk assignment.
The central rule is that reusability must not create implicit schema changes.

A published form template is immutable, a dropbox explicitly pins its effective version, and every submission retains the exact schema used when it was created.

22. Next decision: public upload link access
The platform supports anonymous, verified-email, one-time-code and OIDC uploader identity modes. We should now define how public upload links are issued, restricted and revoked.

Shared dropbox links

One reusable public link per dropbox, with optional access challenges and expiry.


Individual invitation links

Each uploader receives a scoped, expiring invitation with optional identity binding and submission limits.


Shared links and individual invitations

Dropboxes support both reusable public links and individually issued invitations, with configurable identity, expiry, usage limits and revocation.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, public upload access should use both shared dropbox links and individual scoped invitation links. Continue planning link issuance, token security, identity binding, expiration, usage limits, revocation, abuse protection and uploader UX.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.1
Public upload links · Individual invitations · Token security · Identity binding · Expiration · Usage limits · Revocation · Abuse prevention

Decision confirmed: Each dropbox supports both reusable shared upload links and individually issued invitations, with independently configurable access policies.

The recommended architecture is a unified Upload Access Grant system. Shared links and individual invitations are different grant types, but use the same authentication, authorisation, quota, audit and upload-session infrastructure.

This avoids building two competing security models.
1. Upload access architecture
Dropbox

Form · Encryption recipients · Upload limits · Access policy


Shared link

Reusable · Public or restricted · Multiple uploaders


Invitation

Individual · Scoped · Expiring · Optional identity binding




Upload Access Grant evaluation

Token · Identity · Challenges · Expiry · Usage · Rate limits


Short-lived upload session

Pinned form snapshot · Recipient snapshot · Reserved quota · Ciphertext upload


A public link must not itself grant access to existing encrypted submissions.

Upload access, return access and reviewer ciphertext retrieval remain separate capabilities.
2. Access grant types
I'd introduce the following grant types.
Grant type	Purpose	Typical constraints
shared_link	Reusable public intake	Expiry, rate limits, submission quotas
invitation	Named or individually scoped intake	Identity binding, expiry, use count
api_issued	Integration-created upload capability	Scoped service identity, TTL, quotas
internal_test	Synthetic testing	Development/test environments only

api_issued can use the same underlying grant model without becoming a separate public-facing feature.

Every grant belongs to exactly one organisation and one dropbox.
3. Shared dropbox links
A shared link should represent an independently managed access grant, not simply the dropbox's public identifier.

This allows an organisation to create several links for the same dropbox.

For example:
Finance Dropbox
├── Website upload link
├── Customer portal link
├── Temporary campaign link
└── Partner upload link


Each link can have different restrictions and can be revoked independently.
Shared link configuration
{
  "name": "Website document upload",
  "type": "shared_link",
  "dropboxId": "dbx_123",
  "identityMode": "anonymous",
  "expiresAt": null,
  "limits": {
    "maxCompletedSubmissions": null,
    "maxFilesPerSubmission": 10,
    "maxBytesPerSubmission": 104857600
  },
  "challenges": {
    "pinRequired": false
  }
}


The limits above are illustrative and remain subject to organisation and deployment hard limits.

A shared link can be anonymous, but the organisation should be able to require verified email, one-time code or OIDC before an upload session is issued.
4. Individual invitations
Invitations should be independently issued, revocable and auditable.

I'd support:
Property	Behaviour
Recipient	Email address or external identity
Identity binding	Optional or required
Expiry	Required
Maximum completed submissions	Configurable
Maximum concurrent sessions	Configurable
Dropbox	Exactly one
Form version	Current at redemption or explicitly pinned
Recipient encryption policy	Dropbox policy, never caller-controlled
Revocation	Immediate for future authorisation
Delivery	Email, API response or administrator-managed distribution

An invitation should not require creating a permanent uploader account.
Example invitation
{
  "dropboxId": "dbx_123",
  "recipient": {
    "type": "email",
    "value": "customer@example.com"
  },
  "identity": {
    "mode": "verified_email",
    "required": true
  },
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-10T12:00:00Z",
  "maxCompletedSubmissions": 1,
  "maxConcurrentSessions": 1
}


For an email-bound invitation, the server should require proof of control of the intended email address.

Possessing a forwarded invitation URL alone must not satisfy that requirement.
5. Token design
I'd use opaque, high-entropy, server-verifiable bearer tokens, rather than JWTs embedded directly into public upload links.

Opaque tokens simplify immediate revocation and avoid exposing claims through URL contents.
Proposed token format
siu_<public-grant-id>.<random-secret>


The identifier is a non-sensitive lookup handle. The secret should contain at least 256 bits of cryptographically secure randomness.

The database stores only a keyed digest of the secret, using a server-held lookup key, not the raw token.

A keyed digest is appropriate for high-entropy random tokens. Password hashing algorithms such as Argon2id remain appropriate for human-chosen PINs and passwords.
Example C# token generation
using System.Security.Cryptography;

public static class UploadTokenGenerator
{
    public static string GenerateSecret()
    {
        Span<byte> bytes = stackalloc byte[32];
        RandomNumberGenerator.Fill(bytes);

        return Convert.ToBase64String(bytes)
            .TrimEnd('=')
            .Replace('+', '-')
            .Replace('/', '_');
    }

    public static byte[] ComputeDigest(
        ReadOnlySpan<byte> lookupKey,
        string secret)
    {
        return HMACSHA256.HashData(
            lookupKey,
            System.Text.Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes(secret));
    }
}


The lookup key should be managed through the existing secret-resolution infrastructure.

Token comparison must use constant-time comparison where applicable.

Token creation responses should return the secret only once.
6. Public URL structure
I'd support platform-hosted and custom-domain URLs.

Examples:
https://intake.example.com/u/abc123#siu_...


https://documents.example.org/upload/abc123#siu_...


The URL fragment is preferable to a query parameter for bearer-token transport because browsers do not send fragments in ordinary HTTP requests.

The portal JavaScript reads the fragment and redeems the capability through a secure API request.

It should then remove the secret from the visible URL using history.replaceState.

This does not eliminate exposure through browser history, malicious extensions, copied links or compromised frontend JavaScript.

The public portal must not load third-party scripts or analytics that could access the fragment.
Important distinction
The route identifier is not the secret.

A dropbox can have a human-friendly or opaque public route while the access grant remains a separate capability.
7. Token redemption
I'd implement a redemption endpoint.
POST /api/public/upload-grants/redeem


Example request:
{
  "grantId": "grt_123",
  "secret": "<opaque-token-secret>"
}


The server validates the token and returns a limited access-session descriptor.

It should not immediately create a storage upload session.

Instead, the uploader may first need to complete identity verification or another challenge.
Redemption states
Token presented
      ↓
Token valid?
      ↓
Identity required?
      ↓
Challenge required?
      ↓
Access authorised
      ↓
Upload session created


The grant secret should not be repeatedly transmitted after successful redemption.

Use a short-lived, narrowly scoped session credential or secure cookie as appropriate.
8. Identity binding
The platform already supports four uploader identity modes.

I'd apply them consistently to shared links and invitations.
Mode	Verification
Anonymous	No verified personal identity
Verified email	Email challenge and successful verification
One-time code	Possession of a separately issued code
OIDC	Validated external issuer and subject

A PIN challenge is not equivalent to verified identity.

An anonymous uploader who knows the correct PIN remains anonymous.

For OIDC-bound invitations, identity must be matched using the trusted issuer and subject, not email address alone.
Binding example
{
  "identityBinding": {
    "type": "oidc",
    "issuer": "https://identity.example.com",
    "subject": "user-12345"
  }
}


Identity verification should be scoped to the grant and its permitted lifetime.
9. Invitation delivery
The existing notification subsystem should handle invitation emails.

Invitation emails should contain:
- Organisation display name.
- Purpose of the upload.
- Expiry date.
- Secure upload link.
- Appropriate support information.
They must not include encryption private keys, plaintext file content or unnecessary sensitive details.

Invitation delivery should use the configured verified sender identity.

A successful email-provider response does not prove the intended person received or opened the invitation.
10. Expiration
I'd support expiration at several independent levels.
Resource	Expiration
Shared link	Optional
Invitation	Required
Redemption challenge	Short-lived
Authenticated access session	Short-lived
Upload session	Configurable bounded lifetime
Storage upload URL	Very short-lived
Return-access token	Separate policy

Expiration must be enforced server-side.

The client may display countdowns, but its clock must not be authoritative.
Expiration semantics
I'd distinguish:
- Grant expires: No new redemption or upload sessions.
- Access session expires: Uploader must reauthenticate or redeem again.
- Upload session expires: Upload can no longer continue.
- Invitation revoked: Future authorisation fails.
An organisation should be able to choose whether grant expiry immediately terminates existing upload sessions or allows already-authorised sessions to finish within a short grace period.

For the production default, I'd favour immediate termination on explicit revocation and bounded completion on ordinary expiry.
11. Usage limits
Usage limits need transactional enforcement.

I'd support:
Limit	Scope
Maximum completed submissions	Grant
Maximum active upload sessions	Grant
Maximum total uploaded bytes	Grant
Maximum files per submission	Dropbox/grant
Maximum bytes per submission	Dropbox/grant
Maximum redemptions	Invitation
Maximum attempts	Identity challenge
Rate limits	IP, grant, organisation and deployment

Reservation model
When creating an upload session, reserve the required capacity.

When a submission is successfully finalised, consume the completed-submission allowance.

When a session expires or is cancelled, release its reservation only after cleanup is reconciled.

This prevents parallel requests from exceeding limits.

For PostgreSQL, use transactional row locking or atomic conditional updates.

For SQLite, use the corresponding atomic write transaction.
12. One-time invitations
A one-time invitation should mean one successfully completed submission, not necessarily one HTTP request or one attempted redemption.

I'd track:
issued
redeemed
upload_in_progress
consumed
expired
revoked


A failed or interrupted upload should not permanently consume the invitation unless the configured policy explicitly requires it.

However, repeated failed attempts must be bounded to prevent abuse.
Concurrency control
For a one-submission invitation, the server should permit only one active reserved submission slot at a time.

A second simultaneous upload-session request should fail or resume the existing session where securely supported.
13. Revocation
Revocation should be available through the admin UI, API and CLI.

I'd support:
- Revoke one shared link.
- Revoke one invitation.
- Revoke all grants for a dropbox.
- Revoke grants issued by a compromised API client.
- Suspend a dropbox.
- Suspend public access for an organisation.
Revocation should invalidate future grant redemption and access-session authorisation.

Active upload sessions should be cancelled or fenced according to the revocation policy.

Already issued presigned storage URLs may remain usable until expiry.

The server must therefore reject finalisation of revoked sessions and clean up any resulting orphaned ciphertext.
14. Abuse protection
Public upload endpoints are exposed to untrusted users.

I'd implement layered controls.
Control	Purpose
IP and network rate limits	Reduce automated abuse
Grant-level rate limits	Protect individual links
Challenge attempt limits	Prevent OTP/PIN guessing
Maximum upload sizes	Prevent resource exhaustion
Storage reservations	Prevent quota bypass
Concurrent session limits	Prevent upload flooding
Temporary upload expiry	Clean abandoned ciphertext
Suspicious activity signals	Detect repeated failures
Optional CAPTCHA	Add friction when necessary
Organisation suspension	Stop abusive tenants

CAPTCHA should not be mandatory for every upload.

I'd make it an optional, configurable challenge for higher-risk or publicly advertised dropboxes.
Privacy consideration
Abuse signals should have defined retention periods.

Do not indefinitely retain IP addresses, user-agent strings or other identifying telemetry merely because they were collected during upload.
15. Upload-session creation
The grant authorises the creation of a session.

The upload session itself should contain a snapshot of relevant policies.
{
  "uploadSessionId": "upl_123",
  "grantId": "grt_456",
  "dropboxId": "dbx_789",
  "formSnapshotId": "snap_123",
  "recipientSnapshotId": "rcp_456",
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-03T13:00:00Z",
  "limits": {
    "maxFiles": 10,
    "maxBytes": 104857600
  }
}


The session must be bound to the authorised uploader identity where identity verification is required.

The server must revalidate the session before finalisation.

It must also verify uploaded ciphertext objects and manifests rather than trusting client-declared upload success.
16. Uploader experience
I'd keep the public upload experience simple.

Secure Document Upload
Example organisation portal


Submit documents securely. Files are encrypted on your device before upload.
Upload invitation validated

Email verification completed


Reference number

Documents
Choose files to encrypt and upload

Maximum 10 files · 100 MiB total


Encrypt and submit

Illustrative UI only. Controls are not connected to an upload service.
The portal should not expose unnecessary access-control terminology.

For example, an uploader does not need to understand grants, recipient envelopes or storage reservations.

They need clear instructions, upload progress, expiration information and a receipt.
17. Receipt and return access
After successful finalisation, the platform should issue a submission receipt.

Example:
{
  "submissionReference": "SI-8K4P2M",
  "receivedAt": "2026-11-03T12:30:00Z",
  "publicStatus": "received",
  "returnAccess": {
    "enabled": true,
    "mode": "status_only"
  }
}


Return access must use a separate capability from the original upload grant.

An invitation that allows one submission should not automatically allow the uploader to access unrelated submissions created through the same dropbox.

The receipt may be downloaded locally or delivered through the notification system, subject to policy.
18. Audit events
I'd add:
upload_grant.created
upload_grant.updated
upload_grant.revoked
upload_grant.expired

upload_grant.redemption.succeeded
upload_grant.redemption.failed

upload_invitation.issued
upload_invitation.delivered
upload_invitation.consumed

upload_identity.verified
upload_identity.challenge.failed

upload_session.created
upload_session.expired
upload_session.cancelled

upload_abuse.rate_limit_triggered
upload_abuse.grant_suspended


Audit records must not contain raw bearer tokens, OTPs, PINs or other credentials.

For anonymous links, audit should distinguish an anonymous capability holder from a verified identity.
19. Database additions
upload_access_grants
upload_access_grant_secrets
upload_access_grant_policies
upload_access_grant_usage

upload_invitations
upload_invitation_recipients
upload_invitation_delivery_events

upload_grant_redemptions
upload_access_sessions
upload_identity_challenges

upload_grant_reservations
upload_grant_revocations
upload_abuse_signals


All records must remain tenant-scoped.

Grant secrets should be stored only as keyed digests.

Use database constraints and atomic transactions to enforce usage limits.
20. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/dropboxes/{dropboxId}/upload-grants
POST /api/organisations/{id}/dropboxes/{dropboxId}/upload-grants

POST /api/organisations/{id}/upload-grants/{grantId}/revoke
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/upload-grants/{grantId}/usage

POST /api/organisations/{id}/dropboxes/{dropboxId}/invitations
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/invitations
POST /api/organisations/{id}/invitations/{invitationId}/revoke

POST /api/public/upload-grants/redeem
POST /api/public/upload-access-sessions/{sessionId}/verify
POST /api/public/upload-access-sessions/{sessionId}/upload-sessions


Administrative endpoints require scoped permissions and audit.

Public endpoints must apply rate limiting before expensive database or cryptographic operations wherever practical.
21. Implementation priorities
I'd implement public upload access in this order:
1. Unified upload grant model.
2. Secure token generation and digest storage.
3. Shared links.
4. Grant redemption.
5. Short-lived access sessions.
6. Individual invitations.
7. Email and OIDC identity binding.
8. Atomic usage reservations.
9. Revocation and expiry.
10. Abuse controls and operational monitoring.
The central principle is that a link grants only the minimum authority needed to initiate a controlled inbound submission.

It does not provide access to reviewer data, historical submissions or decrypted content.

22. Next decision: uploader submission experience
We should now decide whether uploaders can save an unfinished submission and return later.

This affects browser-local encryption, temporary ciphertext storage, session expiry, quota reservations and return-access security.

Single-session uploads

Uploaders must complete their submission in one session. Simpler implementation and fewer retained temporary objects.


Resumable uploads with encrypted drafts

Uploaders can resume interrupted ciphertext uploads and return to unfinished submissions within a defined expiry period, without storing plaintext on the server.


Long-lived submission drafts

Uploaders can save incomplete forms and files for days or weeks, with configurable draft retention and stronger return authentication.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, uploader submission experience should use resumable uploads with encrypted drafts and bounded expiry. Continue planning draft storage, resumable encrypted uploads, browser recovery, upload session state, expiry, cleanup, quota reservations and uploader UX.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.2
Encrypted drafts · Resumable uploads · Browser recovery · Upload state · Expiration · Cleanup · Quota reservations · Uploader experience

Decision confirmed: Uploaders can resume interrupted submissions within a configurable, bounded period. The server stores only encrypted file content and encrypted confidential form values, never plaintext drafts.

I'd implement this using durable server-side upload sessions, resumable ciphertext chunks and optional browser-local encrypted draft state.

The design must distinguish two operations:
- Resume upload: Continue transferring ciphertext already encrypted by the browser.
- Resume draft: Reopen an unfinished form, modify fields or add files, then complete the submission.
These have different security and browser-storage requirements.
1. Architecture
Uploader starts submission

Access grant · Identity checks · Pinned form and recipient policies


Durable draft session

Expiry · Quota reservation · Upload manifest · Resume credential


Browser

Local encrypted form state · File references · Encryption context


Server / storage

Encrypted chunks · Upload progress · Reservations




Finalisation

Verify ciphertext · Commit submission · Issue receipt · Release unused reservations


The existing encryption protocol, recipient snapshots and access-grant policies remain authoritative.
2. Draft types
I'd support two draft-storage modes.
Mode	Server stores	Browser stores	Capability
Upload-only recovery	Ciphertext, manifest, progress	Resume credentials and local file references	Continue interrupted upload
Encrypted form draft	Ciphertext, manifest, encrypted form state	Draft-unlock material and optional local encrypted state	Reopen unfinished form

The second mode should be optional at the organisation or dropbox level.

Neither mode should require the server to hold plaintext form values or file contents.
Important browser limitation
A browser cannot reliably reopen an arbitrary local file after a restart without user interaction or a previously granted persistent file handle.

Therefore, upload recovery should work even when the original file is no longer accessible locally, provided the complete ciphertext has already reached server storage.

If only part of the file was uploaded, the browser may need the uploader to select the original file again.
3. Draft lifecycle
I'd introduce explicit states:
1

Created

Access validated and draft session allocated



2

Preparing

Form state captured; file encryption begins



3

Uploading

Encrypted chunks transferred



4

Paused

Upload interrupted or intentionally paused



5

Ready

Required ciphertext and metadata uploaded



6

Finalising

Server verifies and commits submission



7

Completed

Immutable submission created and receipt issued



Cancelled, expired and failed-cleanup are separate terminal or operational states.


State transitions must be transactional and idempotent.

A session in finalising must not be finalised twice if the client retries after a network timeout.
4. Session persistence
I'd store draft sessions in the tenant data plane.
upload_draft_sessions
  id
  organisation_id
  dropbox_id
  access_grant_id
  uploader_identity_id
  form_snapshot_id
  recipient_snapshot_id
  status
  created_at
  last_activity_at
  expires_at
  maximum_expires_at
  version


A draft session must be pinned to the exact form and recipient snapshots selected when it began.

Updating the dropbox template or recipient policy should not silently mutate an existing draft.

Security-sensitive changes, such as key revocation, must be revalidated before finalisation.
5. Resumable ciphertext uploads
I'd use fixed-size encrypted chunks with independently authenticated encryption.

The browser should be able to reproduce a particular encrypted chunk without re-encrypting the entire file.

This requires a carefully specified protocol with deterministic chunk identification and strict nonce uniqueness.

The implementation must use a reviewed construction and must never reuse a nonce with the same encryption key for different plaintext.
Upload flow
File selected
    ↓
Create file upload record
    ↓
Encrypt chunk locally
    ↓
Upload ciphertext chunk
    ↓
Server confirms durable receipt
    ↓
Repeat until complete
    ↓
Verify manifest
    ↓
Mark file ready


The server should track confirmed ciphertext chunks, not merely bytes the client claims to have sent.
6. Chunk manifest
Each encrypted file should have a manifest describing its encrypted chunks.

Example:
{
  "fileId": "file_123",
  "protocolVersion": 1,
  "chunkSize": 4194304,
  "chunkCount": 25,
  "ciphertextLength": 104861600,
  "chunks": [
    {
      "index": 0,
      "ciphertextLength": 4194320,
      "digest": "sha256:..."
    }
  ]
}


The values are illustrative.

The manifest must be authenticated and cryptographically bound to the encrypted file.

The server can verify ciphertext digests and object sizes without decrypting the contents.

The client must verify authenticated chunk contents during decryption.

A ciphertext hash alone does not provide plaintext authenticity.
7. Resume protocol
I'd expose a session status endpoint.
GET /api/public/upload-sessions/{sessionId}/status


Example response:
{
  "sessionId": "upl_123",
  "status": "uploading",
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-04T12:00:00Z",
  "files": [
    {
      "fileId": "file_456",
      "confirmedChunks": [0, 1, 2, 3, 4],
      "totalChunks": 25
    }
  ]
}


The response must be authorised using the uploader's scoped resume capability and any required identity binding.

The client compares server-confirmed chunks with its local manifest and uploads only missing ciphertext.

For large files, the API should support compact ranges or paginated chunk-status responses rather than returning enormous arrays.
8. Resume credentials
Resume access must use a separate capability from the original upload grant.

I'd issue an opaque, high-entropy resume token bound to:
- One draft session.
- One organisation and dropbox.
- The original uploader identity where required.
- A bounded expiration.
- Permitted draft operations.
