The resume token must not grant access to other submissions.

It must not allow downloading reviewer ciphertext or decrypting stored files.
Token storage
For same-browser recovery, a scoped secure cookie may be used.

For explicit cross-browser recovery, a separate resume link can be issued, subject to stronger identity verification.

I would not automatically put long-lived resume tokens in localStorage.

If a resume link is issued, use the same fragment-based secret handling described in v3.1.
9. Browser-local draft state
I'd use IndexedDB for optional local encrypted draft state.

The browser may store:
draft_id
form_snapshot_id
encrypted_form_state
file_metadata
file_handle_reference
upload_manifest
confirmed_chunk_cache
last_saved_at


Sensitive form values should not be stored as plaintext.
Draft encryption key
I'd generate a separate draft-state encryption key.

This is not the same as the file data-encryption key.

The draft key should be protected by a user-controlled unlock mechanism or an appropriately protected browser-local key.

For cross-device recovery, a separate encrypted recovery package or passphrase-derived key may be necessary.

Browser-local storage can be deleted by the browser, operating system or user.

The UI must not promise that local drafts are permanent.
10. Cross-device recovery
I'd support two levels.
Same-device recovery
The browser restores local encrypted draft state and resumes upload using its existing session credential.
Different-device recovery
The uploader opens a resume link, completes any required identity verification and retrieves the server-stored encrypted draft.

To edit E2EE fields, the new device must possess the draft decryption secret.

A resume token alone should not automatically grant plaintext access to confidential draft values.
File recovery limitation
If a file is only partially uploaded and the new device does not possess the original file or remaining ciphertext, the uploader must provide the file again.

The platform cannot reconstruct missing plaintext from incomplete ciphertext.
11. Draft expiration
I'd use bounded expiration with an absolute maximum lifetime.

For example:
Setting	Illustrative default
Initial draft lifetime	24 hours
Maximum lifetime	7 days
Inactivity timeout	24 hours
Upload URL lifetime	10 minutes
Resume capability lifetime	No longer than draft
Cleanup grace period	1 hour

These should be configurable within deployment hard limits.
Sliding expiration
I'd allow activity to extend a draft's expiry only up to its absolute maximum.

For example:
Created: 3 November
Initial expiry: 4 November
Maximum expiry: 10 November

Activity on 4 November
    ↓
Expiry extended
    ↓
Never beyond 10 November


The server clock is authoritative.

An explicit revocation or organisation suspension may terminate the draft earlier.
12. Cleanup
Cleanup must be durable and idempotent.

I'd use the existing database-backed worker infrastructure.
Cleanup workflow
Draft expired
    ↓
Mark expiration pending
    ↓
Fence new uploads and finalisation
    ↓
Enumerate temporary ciphertext objects
    ↓
Delete objects from storage
    ↓
Verify deletion or record provider limitations
    ↓
Release reserved quota
    ↓
Mark cleanup complete


If storage deletion fails, the worker retries with backoff.

The draft should remain inaccessible to the uploader after expiration even if physical ciphertext cleanup is delayed.
BYO storage
For organisation-managed storage, deletion may fail because credentials have been revoked or the provider is unavailable.

The platform must record unresolved cleanup obligations.

It must not falsely report physical deletion as completed.
13. Quota reservations
Drafts consume resources before becoming completed submissions.

I'd account for:
Resource	Reservation
Active draft sessions	At session creation
Expected upload bytes	Before upload
Confirmed temporary ciphertext	During upload
Object count	Per file/chunk
Concurrent upload slots	While active
Completed submissions	At finalisation

Quota calculations should include temporary ciphertext.

Otherwise, an attacker could exhaust storage using abandoned drafts that never become completed submissions.
Reservation example
{
  "draftId": "upl_123",
  "reservedBytes": 104857600,
  "confirmedTemporaryBytes": 62914560,
  "reservedObjects": 25,
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-04T12:00:00Z"
}


Reservation changes must be atomic.

If a user removes a file from a draft, quota should not be released until the corresponding temporary objects have been removed or reconciled.
14. Upload progress UX
I'd provide clear, file-level progress and recovery information.
Secure Upload
Example resumable submission

In progress

Financial statement.pdf

72%



Encrypted locally · Uploading ciphertext



Identity document.jpg
Uploaded

Ciphertext received and verified



You can resume this upload

Your unfinished submission expires on 4 November 2026 at 12:00 PM.

Pause upload

Continue


Illustrative interface; progress values are examples.
The UI should distinguish:
- Encrypting.
- Uploading.
- Waiting for connection.
- Paused.
- Verifying.
- Ready to submit.
- Completed.
It should not show 100% completion merely because all bytes were transferred.

Finalisation and server verification are separate steps.
15. Offline behaviour
I'd support interrupted connectivity, but not promise full offline operation.

If the connection drops, the browser can continue preparing encrypted chunks where local resources permit.

It cannot confirm server receipt or complete finalisation while offline.

The UI should preserve recoverable state and resume when connectivity returns.

I'd avoid a service worker in the initial release, consistent with v2.4.

Background uploads after the browser is closed should not be promised.
16. Encryption and resume safety
Resumable encryption introduces important cryptographic constraints.

The protocol must ensure:
- Unique nonce use for every encryption operation under a key.
- Stable chunk identities.
- Authenticated chunk ordering.
- Detection of missing or duplicated chunks.
- Detection of truncated ciphertext.
- Authenticated file metadata.
- Safe recovery after interrupted encryption.
- No accidental reuse of encryption state across different files.
I'd avoid deriving nonces from mutable counters that can reset after a browser crash.

The reviewed protocol should define deterministic nonce derivation from a unique file context and immutable chunk index, or another construction with equivalent safety.

If the original file changes between sessions, the client must not continue encrypting it under the same file encryption context.

Instead, create a new file version and encryption context.
17. API endpoints
POST /api/public/upload-access-sessions/{id}/drafts

GET  /api/public/upload-drafts/{draftId}
GET  /api/public/upload-drafts/{draftId}/status

POST /api/public/upload-drafts/{draftId}/resume
POST /api/public/upload-drafts/{draftId}/pause
POST /api/public/upload-drafts/{draftId}/cancel

POST /api/public/upload-drafts/{draftId}/files
GET  /api/public/upload-drafts/{draftId}/files/{fileId}/chunks
POST /api/public/upload-drafts/{draftId}/files/{fileId}/chunks

POST /api/public/upload-drafts/{draftId}/finalise


All draft endpoints must require scoped access-session authorisation.

The API should never accept arbitrary storage object keys from the client.
18. Database additions
upload_draft_sessions
upload_draft_session_versions

upload_draft_files
upload_draft_file_manifests
upload_draft_chunks

upload_draft_resume_credentials
upload_draft_identity_bindings

upload_draft_quota_reservations
upload_draft_cleanup_operations
upload_draft_cleanup_attempts

upload_draft_events


Draft records should be tenant-scoped and stored in the appropriate tenant database.

Cleanup jobs must remain compatible with shared and dedicated tenant data planes.
19. Operational metrics
I'd add:
secureintake_upload_drafts_active
secureintake_upload_drafts_expired
secureintake_upload_drafts_resumed
secureintake_upload_drafts_completed

secureintake_upload_bytes_temporary
secureintake_upload_bytes_confirmed

secureintake_upload_cleanup_pending
secureintake_upload_cleanup_failed

secureintake_upload_resume_failures
secureintake_upload_finalisation_duration


Metrics should avoid uploader-identifying labels.

Operational dashboards should show temporary-storage usage and cleanup backlog.
20. Implementation priorities
I'd implement resumable uploads in this order:
1. Durable upload draft state machine.
2. Chunked ciphertext upload protocol.
3. Server-confirmed chunk tracking.
4. Idempotent finalisation.
5. Scoped resume capabilities.
6. Same-browser recovery.
7. Draft expiry and cleanup.
8. Quota reservation integration.
9. Encrypted form draft persistence.
10. Cross-browser recovery.
I'd ship same-browser recovery before cross-device draft editing.

Cross-device recovery is substantially more complex because it requires portable draft decryption material and cannot assume the original local files remain available.

21. Next decision: uploader identity and reusable contacts
We now have shared links, invitations and resumable submissions. The next question is whether uploaders should be able to maintain a reusable identity across submissions.

Stateless uploaders

Each upload is independent. Identity is verified per invitation or submission, with no reusable uploader profile.


Optional reusable uploader identities

Anonymous and one-off submissions remain supported. Organisations may enable reusable uploader identities for repeat submissions and streamlined verification.


Reusable uploader accounts

Uploaders create or link an account before submitting, enabling ongoing submission history and identity management.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, uploader identity should use optional reusable uploader identities alongside anonymous and one-off submissions. Continue planning uploader identity lifecycle, verified contacts, cross-submission linking, account privacy, authentication, invitations and organisation isolation.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.3
Optional uploader identities · Verified contacts · Cross-submission linking · Authentication · Invitation binding · Privacy · Tenant isolation

Decision confirmed: Uploaders may submit anonymously, verify their identity for a single submission, or use an optional reusable identity for future submissions.

I'd implement this as a dedicated Uploader Identity subsystem, separate from reviewer and administrator identities.

The fundamental rule is:

An uploader identity establishes who is submitting information. It does not automatically grant access to previous submissions, uploaded files or other organisations.
1. Identity architecture
Public upload access

Shared link · Invitation · API-issued capability


Anonymous

No account


One-off

Verified for one grant


Reusable

Persistent identity




Organisation-scoped identity binding

Verified contacts · Invitation eligibility · Explicit submission links


Submission access

Separate grant · Return capability · Public status · Append permission


Reusable identity should improve convenience without weakening the existing capability-based security model.
2. Identity modes
I'd define three modes for uploaders.
Mode	Persistent account	Contact verification	Cross-submission history
Anonymous	No	Not required	No
One-off verified	No	Per grant/session	No automatic linking
Reusable identity	Optional	Stored verification evidence	Explicitly authorised submissions only

A verified email address does not establish legal identity.

An OIDC identity establishes control of an identity-provider account, subject to that provider's assurance level.

The platform should not describe either as government identity verification.
3. Reusable identity scope
I'd separate authentication identity from organisation relationships.
Uploader Authentication Identity
  ├── Organisation A relationship
  │     ├── Verified contact
  │     └── Submission grants
  │
  └── Organisation B relationship
        ├── Separate contact policy
        └── Separate submission grants


A person may use the same authentication credential across organisations, but organisations must not gain visibility into one another's relationship with that person.
Recommended model
Use a platform-level opaque authentication subject internally, with a distinct organisation-scoped uploader identifier.

For example:
Global authentication subject: auth_123

Organisation A uploader: upl_A_456
Organisation B uploader: upl_B_789


The organisation-facing API exposes only the organisation-scoped identifier.

It must never expose a global identifier that enables cross-organisation correlation.
4. Identity lifecycle
I'd support:
Created
   ↓
Pending Verification
   ↓
Active
   ↓
Suspended
   ↓
Closed


A reusable identity can also be marked as pending deletion.

Deletion is a separate workflow because submission records may be subject to retention requirements or legal holds.
Lifecycle actions
Action	Result
Create	Establish authentication subject
Verify contact	Add verified contact evidence
Link organisation	Create scoped uploader relationship
Suspend	Block new authenticated actions
Revoke sessions	Invalidate active credentials
Close	Disable identity and remove optional profile data
Request deletion	Evaluate retention and legal obligations

Closing an account must not silently delete an organisation's legally retained submissions.
5. Authentication
I'd support built-in authentication and external OIDC, consistent with the existing deployment authentication architecture.

For reusable uploaders:
Method	Support
Email one-time code	Yes
Passkeys	Yes, where supported
External OIDC	Yes
Password	Optional
TOTP	Optional or policy-required
Recovery codes	For applicable built-in accounts

I'd recommend email OTP or passkeys as the default for reusable uploader accounts.

A permanent password should not be mandatory.
Session handling
Browser authentication should use secure, host-only cookies, appropriate CSRF protection and bounded sessions.

Custom upload domains complicate cross-origin authentication.

A central authentication origin may be used, but it must not rely on broad cross-domain cookies.

Use an explicit OIDC-style redirect or short-lived authorisation exchange where necessary.
6. Verified contacts
An uploader can have multiple verified contact methods.
uploader_contacts
  id
  uploader_identity_id
  type
  normalised_value
  verification_status
  verified_at
  verification_method
  verification_expires_at
  revoked_at


I'd support email first, with telephone verification as an optional future capability.
Email verification
The system should:
1. Issue a short-lived verification challenge.
2. Rate-limit challenge creation and attempts.
3. Store only a digest of the verification secret.
4. Verify atomically and mark the challenge consumed.
5. Record verification evidence and its expiry.
6. Reverify when required by organisation policy.
Verification status must not be permanent by assumption.

An email address can change ownership or become inaccessible.
7. Contact privacy
Contact details are personal information.

I'd store them using appropriate encryption at rest and restrict access through scoped permissions.

For exact-match lookup, a keyed digest of a normalised contact value may be maintained separately.

This allows matching without exposing the contact value through ordinary database indexes.

However, the application server can still access the value when needed for email delivery or account management.

That is not end-to-end encryption.
Cross-organisation privacy
Organisation A should not be able to search whether an email address belongs to an uploader in Organisation B.

Global contact lookup must not be exposed through tenant APIs.

Any internal deduplication must avoid creating a user-enumeration endpoint.
8. Invitation binding
Invitations can be bound to a reusable uploader identity.

I'd support:
Binding	Behaviour
None	Valid bearer invitation, subject to configured challenges
Email	Requires verification of specified email
OIDC subject	Requires exact issuer/subject match
Reusable uploader identity	Requires authentication as specified identity
One-off verified contact	Requires verification without account creation

An invitation should not automatically create a reusable account.

If an uploader has no account, they may complete the invitation as a one-off verified uploader.
Example
{
  "invitationId": "inv_123",
  "dropboxId": "dbx_456",
  "identityBinding": {
    "type": "verified_email",
    "value": "customer@example.com"
  },
  "allowOneOffVerification": true,
  "allowReusableIdentity": true,
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-10T12:00:00Z"
}


The invitation's restrictions remain authoritative even if the uploader authenticates with a reusable account.
9. Cross-submission linking
I'd make linking explicit.

A reusable identity may be associated with multiple submissions, but the association must be authorised.
Uploader Identity
   ├── Submission A — linked
   ├── Submission B — linked
   └── Submission C — not linked


A submission created anonymously should not automatically become visible to an account merely because the account later verifies the same email address.
Linking requirements
For an existing submission, linking should require proof of the original submission capability, an authorised organisation-controlled process, or another explicitly approved recovery mechanism.

Email equality alone is insufficient.

A return-access token may support linking only if its scope and assurance satisfy the applicable policy.
10. Submission history
A reusable uploader may have a submission history view, but it must be deliberately limited.

I'd support:
- Submission reference.
- Submission date.
- Organisation display name.
- Public status.
- Permitted return actions.
- Expiry or closure information.
It must not expose internal workflow states, reviewer identities, confidential notes or encrypted files.
Example
My Submissions
Example

SI-8K4P2M

Example Finance · 3 Nov 2026

In review



SI-6R9Q1A

Example Legal · 29 Oct 2026

Completed



SI-2T7B5C

Example Finance · 21 Oct 2026

Closed


Illustrative account history. Only explicitly linked submissions appear.
For stronger organisation isolation, the default view could show submissions only for the currently selected organisation.

A cross-organisation view should require an explicit user opt-in and should be generated only from relationships the uploader is authorised to see.
11. Organisation isolation
The platform must prevent cross-tenant data disclosure.

I'd enforce:
Layer	Isolation
Authentication	Global opaque subject, where applicable
Organisation relationship	Tenant-scoped uploader ID
Submission linkage	Tenant-scoped authorisation
Database	Tenant routing and RLS where applicable
API	Organisation context resolved server-side
Search	No cross-tenant uploader lookup
Audit	Organisation-scoped events
Notifications	Organisation-approved contact usage

The same email address appearing in multiple organisations does not merge those organisations' records.
12. Account privacy controls
I'd provide uploader-facing controls for:
- Viewing verified contacts.
- Adding or removing contact methods.
- Managing passkeys and sessions.
- Revoking devices.
- Closing the reusable identity.
- Requesting deletion of optional profile information.
- Reviewing organisation relationships where appropriate.
An uploader must not be able to delete legally retained submissions simply by closing their account.

The UI should distinguish account closure from submission deletion.
13. Identity recovery
Recovery should depend on the authentication method.

For email OTP accounts, recovery may be possible through continued control of the verified email address.

For passkeys, multiple registered passkeys should be supported.

For lost access to all credentials, a controlled recovery process may be necessary.

I'd avoid allowing support staff to bypass identity verification based only on a name or email address.

Recovery events must be audited.
Security boundary
Recovering an uploader account does not recover reviewer encryption keys.

Uploader identities and reviewer cryptographic identities remain separate systems.
14. Account enumeration protection
Public identity endpoints must not disclose whether a particular email address has an account.

For example, the passwordless sign-in flow should use a neutral response:
If this address is eligible, we'll send instructions.

Internally, the system should rate-limit by account, contact digest, IP and other appropriate signals.

Timing and response differences should be minimised.
15. Reusable identity and draft recovery
Reusable identities can simplify the v3.2 draft-resume workflow.

For example:
Uploader signs in
    ↓
Lists authorised unfinished drafts
    ↓
Selects draft
    ↓
Completes identity checks if required
    ↓
Retrieves encrypted draft
    ↓
Unlocks local draft encryption material


Authentication alone must not automatically provide the plaintext encryption secret for E2EE draft fields.

If the draft was encrypted using a device-local key, cross-device recovery may remain unavailable without a portable recovery package.
16. API design
I'd separate public uploader account endpoints from organisation administration endpoints.
Uploader account APIs
GET  /api/uploader/me
GET  /api/uploader/me/contacts
POST /api/uploader/me/contacts
POST /api/uploader/me/contacts/{contactId}/verify

GET  /api/uploader/me/sessions
DELETE /api/uploader/me/sessions/{sessionId}

GET  /api/uploader/me/submissions
GET  /api/uploader/me/drafts

POST /api/uploader/me/submissions/link
POST /api/uploader/me/close


Organisation administration APIs
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/uploaders
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/uploaders/{uploaderId}

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/uploaders/{uploaderId}/submissions

POST /api/organisations/{id}/uploaders/{uploaderId}/suspend
POST /api/organisations/{id}/uploaders/{uploaderId}/restore


Organisation administrators must not be able to access a user's credentials or sessions belonging to unrelated organisations.
17. Database additions
uploader_authentication_identities
uploader_authentication_credentials
uploader_authentication_sessions

uploader_contacts
uploader_contact_verifications

organisation_uploader_identities
organisation_uploader_identity_links

submission_uploader_links
uploader_submission_link_requests

uploader_account_recovery_operations
uploader_account_closure_operations

uploader_identity_audit_events


The authentication identity registry may reside in the control plane.

Organisation relationships and submission links should reside in the appropriate tenant data plane.

Avoid cross-database foreign keys; use stable opaque identifiers and explicit consistency workflows.
18. C# identity model
I'd keep authentication subjects distinct from tenant uploader identities.
public sealed record UploaderAuthenticationSubject(
    Guid Id,
    string AuthenticationProvider,
    string ProviderSubject);

public sealed record OrganisationUploaderIdentity(
    Guid Id,
    Guid OrganisationId,
    Guid AuthenticationSubjectId,
    UploaderIdentityStatus Status);

public enum UploaderIdentityStatus
{
    PendingVerification,
    Active,
    Suspended,
    Closed
}


For anonymous and one-off submissions, AuthenticationSubjectId should be nullable or represented through a separate identity-binding type.

Do not create fake permanent accounts merely to satisfy a non-null database relationship.
19. Audit events
I'd add:
uploader.identity.created
uploader.identity.verified
uploader.identity.suspended
uploader.identity.closed

uploader.contact.added
uploader.contact.verified
uploader.contact.removed

uploader.session.created
uploader.session.revoked

uploader.organisation.linked
uploader.organisation.unlinked

uploader.submission.linked
uploader.submission.link.rejected

uploader.account.recovery.requested
uploader.account.recovery.completed


Audit records should use opaque identifiers and avoid unnecessarily copying personal contact details.
20. Implementation priorities
I'd implement optional reusable uploader identities in this order:
1. Separate uploader identity domain model.
2. Email OTP authentication.
3. Organisation-scoped uploader relationships.
4. Verified contact management.
5. Invitation binding.
6. Explicit submission linking.
7. Submission history view.
8. Draft recovery integration.
9. Passkeys and external OIDC.
10. Account closure and privacy workflows.
The key design rule is that authentication convenience must not become implicit authorisation.

A reusable account may prove who the uploader is, but each submission remains protected by its own organisation scope and access policy.

21. Next decision: uploader contact and notification preferences
We now have optional reusable identities and verified contacts. The next question is how uploaders should receive submission receipts, status changes and requests for additional information.

Email-only notifications

Receipts, status updates and additional-information requests are delivered by email when a verified address is available.


Configurable uploader notifications

Organisations configure allowed notification types; uploaders control optional preferences. Mandatory security messages remain enabled.


Minimal notifications

Only essential receipts and security notices are sent. Uploaders check submission status through their return-access capability.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, uploader notifications should use configurable organisation-approved notifications with uploader preferences and mandatory security notices. Continue planning notification types, preferences, delivery, privacy, templates, verification and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.4
Uploader notifications · Organisation policies · Personal preferences · Mandatory security notices · Delivery · Privacy · Templates · Verification · Audit

Decision confirmed: Organisations control which uploader notifications are available, while uploaders can manage their preferences for optional notifications. Mandatory security and identity-related notices cannot be disabled through ordinary notification preferences.

This extends the notification infrastructure defined in v2.2 rather than introducing a separate delivery system.

The recommended design is a policy-driven notification engine with three independent layers:
1. Deployment policy determines which notification capabilities are available.
2. Organisation policy determines which optional notifications may be sent.
3. Uploader preferences determine which permitted optional notifications the uploader receives.
Mandatory notices bypass ordinary opt-out preferences, but must still satisfy privacy, recipient-verification and delivery-security requirements.
1. Notification architecture
Submission, identity or security event

Durable event recorded within the appropriate transaction


Notification policy resolver

Deployment rules · Organisation settings · Uploader preferences


Recipient and privacy validation

Verified contact · Disclosure policy · Template version · Suppression checks


Email delivery

SMTP or configured provider


Portal notifications

Visible after authenticated or capability-scoped access




Delivery tracking and audit

Queued · Sent · Failed · Suppressed · Acknowledged where supported


The notification subsystem must never need to decrypt submitted files or E2EE form values.
2. Notification categories
I'd define four categories.
Category	Examples	Uploader opt-out
Security	Account recovery, credential changes, suspicious sign-in	No
Identity and access	OTP, email verification, invitation access changes	No, when required for the operation
Transactional	Submission receipt, failed finalisation, draft expiration	Depends on essentiality
Optional status	In review, more information required, completed	Yes

A notification being transactional does not automatically make it mandatory.

For example, a submission receipt may be essential to the configured submission process, while routine progress emails may be optional.

Mandatory classification should be defined by platform policy and constrained organisation configuration—not by arbitrary administrator-controlled labels.
3. Notification event catalogue
I'd establish a stable, versioned catalogue.
Event	Default classification	Email	Portal
submission.received	Transactional	Configurable	Yes
submission.revision.added	Transactional	Configurable	Yes
submission.status.changed	Optional	Opt-in/configurable	Yes
submission.more_information_required	Action-required	Configurable	Yes
submission.completed	Optional	Configurable	Yes
submission.closed	Optional	Configurable	Yes
upload.draft.expiring	Optional	Configurable	Yes
upload.draft.expired	Transactional	Configurable	Yes
uploader.contact.verification	Mandatory for verification	Yes	Not sufficient alone
uploader.account.security_changed	Mandatory	Yes, where appropriate	Yes
uploader.account.recovery	Mandatory	Yes	Yes
upload.invitation.issued	Access-related	Yes	Where applicable

An event may generate different notification types depending on the recipient and organisation policy.

For example, a workflow transition to an internal "Legal Review" state should not generate a public notification unless its mapped public status actually changes.
4. Policy hierarchy
I'd apply the following precedence.
Deployment policy
Highest authority

Defines available channels, mandatory security categories, disclosure restrictions and delivery hard limits.

Organisation policy

Enables optional notification types, chooses approved templates and defines defaults.

Uploader preferences

Opt in or out of permitted optional notifications and select verified delivery contacts.

Event-specific eligibility

Checks recipient identity, submission access, contact verification, expiry and current security state.

An uploader preference cannot enable a notification that the organisation prohibits.

An organisation cannot disable platform-required security notices.

However, if no verified or safe delivery destination exists, the platform must record the delivery limitation rather than sending sensitive information to an unverified address.
5. Organisation notification policy
Each organisation should have a versioned notification policy.
{
  "policyVersion": 3,
  "defaultLocale": "en-AU",
  "channels": {
    "email": true,
    "portal": true
  },
  "notifications": {
    "submission.received": {
      "enabled": true,
      "mandatory": false,
      "defaultPreference": true
    },
    "submission.status.changed": {
      "enabled": true,
      "mandatory": false,
      "defaultPreference": false
    },
    "submission.more_information_required": {
      "enabled": true,
      "mandatory": false,
      "defaultPreference": true
    }
  }
}


The mandatory property in an organisation-facing API should be read-only, derived from platform policy.

An organisation administrator must not be able to mark arbitrary messages as mandatory to bypass uploader preferences.
6. Uploader preferences
Reusable uploader identities should support persistent notification preferences.

One-off uploaders should be able to choose notification preferences for the individual submission without creating an account.

Anonymous uploaders without a verified contact can still receive notifications through a scoped return-access portal, where enabled.
Example preference model
{
  "submissionUpdates": true,
  "completionNotices": true,
  "draftReminders": false,
  "moreInformationRequests": true,
  "preferredChannel": "email",
  "preferredLocale": "en-AU"
}


Preferences should be scoped appropriately.

For example, a reusable uploader may disable routine status emails for Organisation A while keeping them enabled for Organisation B.

I'd support organisation-level defaults with optional submission-level overrides.
7. Preference resolution
A deterministic resolver should determine whether a notification is eligible.
public interface IUploaderNotificationPolicyResolver
{
    ValueTask<NotificationDecision> ResolveAsync(
        NotificationContext context,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record NotificationContext(
    Guid OrganisationId,
    Guid? UploaderIdentityId,
    Guid? SubmissionId,
    string NotificationType,
    string Channel);

public sealed record NotificationDecision(
    bool Allowed,
    bool Mandatory,
    string ReasonCode,
    Guid PolicyVersionId);


The resolver must use the relevant policy version and current recipient eligibility.

For queued optional notifications, preferences should be rechecked immediately before delivery so that a recent opt-out is respected.

Mandatory security notices should use the security policy applicable to the event, with current destination-safety checks.
8. Contact verification
Email delivery should use verified contacts wherever possible.

I'd distinguish:
Contact state	Allowed use
Unverified	Verification challenge only
Verified	Eligible for approved notifications
Verification expired	Reverification required where policy specifies
Revoked	No further delivery
Suppressed	No ordinary delivery
Bounced	Delivery blocked pending correction
Compromised	Security workflow determines safe alternatives

A previously verified address may become unsuitable for sensitive account-recovery notifications.

For example, if the user reports that their email account is compromised, the system should not blindly send recovery credentials to that same address.
One-off verified uploaders
Verification should be bound to the relevant submission or invitation.

It must not create a reusable global identity implicitly.
9. Portal notifications
Portal notifications are useful when email is unavailable or undesirable.

I'd support a minimal notification centre for reusable uploaders.
Notifications
Example


Submission received
New


SI-8K4P2M · 3 Nov 2026




Additional information requested
Action required


SI-6R9Q1A · 2 Nov 2026




Security settings updated


1 Nov 2026


Illustrative uploader notification centre.
For anonymous or one-off uploaders, portal notifications must be accessible only through the existing submission-specific return capability.

A notification centre must not become a way to enumerate submissions belonging to the same email address.
10. Email privacy
Email is not an end-to-end encrypted delivery channel in this design.

Therefore, notification emails should contain minimal information.

I'd allow:
- Organisation display name.
- Generic event description.
- Submission reference where policy permits.
- Date and time.
- Safe link to the authenticated portal.
- Support contact information.
I'd prohibit:
- Uploaded filenames by default.
- E2EE form values.
- Internal notes.
- Internal workflow states.
- Reviewer names.
- Recipient key identifiers.
- Confidential document descriptions.
- Public download links.
Example email
Your secure submission has been received.

Reference: SI-8K4P2M

Your documents were successfully submitted to Example Organisation.

You can check the status of your submission using your secure return-access link.

For security, this email does not contain your uploaded documents or confidential submission details.

Example Organisation




The actual return-access link should be generated from the applicable capability policy.

It must not be invented or embedded in an unrelated notification.
11. Template system
I'd extend the existing versioned notification templates.

Templates should be scoped by:
notification type
channel
locale
organisation
template version


The rendering engine should use an allowlist of approved variables.
Example
{
  "templateKey": "submission.received",
  "channel": "email",
  "locale": "en-AU",
  "version": 2,
  "variables": [
    "organisation.display_name",
    "submission.public_reference",
    "submission.received_at",
    "portal.return_url"
  ]
}


Custom templates must not contain arbitrary JavaScript or executable expressions.

For HTML email, use sanitised templates and safe variable encoding.

I'd support organisation branding within controlled constraints, consistent with v2.3.
12. Template privacy classification
Each template variable should have a disclosure classification.
Classification	Example	External email
Public-safe	Organisation name	Allowed
Transactional	Submission reference	Policy-controlled
Personal	Verified contact name	Restricted
Confidential	E2EE form value	Prohibited
Internal	Reviewer notes	Prohibited
Security secret	OTP, reset credential	Only through dedicated, purpose-bound security templates

OTP and recovery credentials are exceptional security messages, not ordinary template variables.

The general-purpose template editor should never have access to those secrets.
13. Localisation
I'd support notification localisation using the same general localisation strategy as the frontend.

For example:
en-AU
en-GB
de-DE
ja-JP


The organisation chooses its supported locales.

The uploader may select a preferred locale.

Fallback order:
Uploader preference
    ↓
Organisation default
    ↓
Deployment default


Only platform-owned notification text should be translated automatically through maintained templates.

User-generated content and E2EE values should not be translated.
14. Delivery processing
Notifications should use the durable outbox and worker infrastructure already established.
Delivery lifecycle
Pending
   ↓
Eligible
   ↓
Queued
   ↓
Sending
   ↓
Sent


Alternative outcomes:
Suppressed
Failed
Retry scheduled
Dead-lettered
Cancelled


The system should distinguish a message accepted by an email provider from a message actually delivered to an inbox.

A provider acceptance response is not proof of receipt or reading.
Retry behaviour
I'd retry transient errors such as:
- Provider timeouts.
- HTTP 429.
- HTTP 5xx.
- Temporary SMTP failures.
Permanent failures should move to suppression or dead-letter handling as appropriate.

Retries must be bounded and use stable idempotency identifiers.

Email providers may still produce duplicates; exactly-once email delivery cannot be guaranteed.
15. Notification deduplication
Multiple internal workflow changes should not necessarily generate multiple public emails.

For example:
Assigned
    ↓
Manager review
    ↓
Legal review
    ↓
Pending approval


All four internal states may map to the same public status: in_review.

I'd send a public status-change notification only when the effective public status changes, unless a distinct action-required event is generated.

This reduces unnecessary notifications and avoids leaking internal workflow activity.
16. Additional-information requests
These notifications need special handling because the platform remains inbound-only.

A notification may tell the uploader that additional information is required.

The uploader follows a scoped return-access link and uploads a new encrypted submission revision.

The platform does not send documents back to the uploader.
Example
Additional information is required to continue processing your secure submission.

Reference: SI-8K4P2M

Please open your secure submission page to review the request and provide the additional information.

For security, confidential details are not included in this email.

Example Organisation




The request details should be constrained to approved, uploader-visible instructions.

Internal reviewer notes must not be copied into these messages.
17. Security notices
Mandatory security notices should cover events such as:
Event	Notification
Password changed	Notify existing verified contact
Passkey added or removed	Notify verified contact
Recovery initiated	Notify eligible existing contact
Recovery completed	Notify eligible existing contact
Verified contact changed	Notify old and new contacts where safe
Suspicious sign-in	Risk-based security notice
Account suspended	Notify when safe and appropriate
Active sessions revoked	Security notification where configured

Security notices should be generated by dedicated security workflows.

An organisation administrator should not be able to suppress mandatory security events through ordinary notification preferences.

However, delivery to a compromised or unverified destination must be handled safely.
18. Notification preferences UI
I'd provide a simple uploader-facing preferences page.
Notification Preferences
Preview

Choose which optional email notifications you receive.
[x] Submission status updates

When the public status of your submission changes.



[x] Completion notices

When your submission has been completed.



[x] Additional information requests

When further information is requested.



[ ] Draft reminders

When an unfinished upload is approaching expiry.



Security notifications

Essential account and security notices cannot be disabled.

Required

Copy preference configurationInteractive local preview; preferences are not saved to a server.
The real UI should show only options enabled by the organisation's notification policy.
19. Database additions
I'd extend the v2.2 notification tables with uploader-specific policy and preference records.
organisation_uploader_notification_policies
organisation_uploader_notification_policy_versions

uploader_notification_preferences
uploader_submission_notification_preferences

uploader_notification_inbox
uploader_notification_read_states

notification_recipient_snapshots
notification_privacy_classifications
notification_suppression_reasons


The existing notification event, delivery, attempt and template tables should be reused.

Avoid duplicating the delivery infrastructure for uploaders.
20. API endpoints
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/uploader-notification-policy
PUT  /api/organisations/{id}/uploader-notification-policy

GET  /api/uploader/me/notification-preferences
PUT  /api/uploader/me/notification-preferences

GET  /api/uploader/me/notifications
POST /api/uploader/me/notifications/{notificationId}/read

GET  /api/public/submissions/{submissionId}/notifications


The final public endpoint must require a valid submission-specific return capability.

It must not expose notifications through the submission identifier alone.

Organisation administrators may manage notification policy and templates, but must not gain access to another organisation's uploader preferences or private contact data.
21. Audit events
I'd add:
uploader.notification.preference_changed
uploader.notification.policy_changed

uploader.notification.queued
uploader.notification.sent
uploader.notification.failed
uploader.notification.suppressed

uploader.notification.template_published
uploader.notification.template_changed

uploader.notification.contact_rejected
uploader.notification.security_notice_issued


Audit should capture policy version, template version, recipient reference, delivery outcome and reason codes.

It should not store raw OTPs, return-access tokens, confidential form values or rendered email bodies by default.
22. Implementation priorities
I'd implement uploader notifications in this order:
1. Notification event catalogue and classification.
2. Organisation notification policy.
3. Verified recipient resolution.
4. Email templates and privacy allowlists.
5. Submission receipt notifications.
6. Additional-information request notifications.
7. Uploader preferences.
8. Public status-change deduplication.
9. Portal notification centre.
10. Security notice handling and delivery monitoring.
The core design principle is that organisations control available notification capabilities, uploaders control optional delivery preferences, and the platform enforces mandatory security and confidentiality boundaries.

23. Next decision: submission receipts and verification
We should now decide how much independently verifiable evidence the uploader receives after completing a submission.

Basic submission receipt

Reference number, timestamp and status, available in the portal or email.


Cryptographically signed receipts

Receipts include submission identifiers, server-verified ciphertext commitments, timestamps and a digital signature that can be verified independently.


Signed receipts with independent timestamp evidence

Signed receipts plus optional external timestamping or transparency evidence for higher-assurance deployments.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, submission receipts should use cryptographically signed submission receipts with independently verifiable ciphertext commitments. Continue planning receipt format, signing keys, verification, file commitments, timestamp handling, download, retention and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.5
Signed receipts · Ciphertext commitments · Signing keys · Independent verification · Timestamping · Downloads · Retention · Audit

Decision confirmed: Every successfully finalised submission receives a cryptographically signed receipt that can be independently verified without contacting the Secure Intake server or possessing a decryption key.

I'd implement this as a versioned, portable receipt format using asymmetric digital signatures, with explicit commitments to the ciphertext and submission manifest.

The receipt establishes that a particular Secure Intake deployment acknowledged a particular encrypted submission at a stated time.

It does not prove the plaintext contents, the uploader's real-world identity, that the server's clock was correct, or that the organisation subsequently processed the submission.
1. Receipt architecture
Uploader finalises submission

Ciphertext objects · Form response · Recipient envelopes


Server verifies stored ciphertext

Object existence · Length · Digest · Manifest consistency


Canonical submission commitment

Immutable manifest · File hashes · Encrypted form payload · Metadata commitment


Receipt signing service

Deployment signing identity · Signature · Key identifier


Portable receipt

Download · Retain · Share


Independent verifier

Signature · Trusted public key · Manifest digests




The receipt system belongs to the submission finalisation pipeline, not the notification subsystem. Email and portal notifications merely deliver or reference the resulting receipt.
2. Receipt format
I'd use two representations:
Format	Purpose
.sir.json	Canonical machine-verifiable signed receipt
.pdf	Optional human-readable presentation

The JSON receipt is authoritative.

A PDF is a convenience document and must not be treated as independently verified merely because it displays a reference number or signature image.

The PDF should include the receipt identifier, signing-key fingerprint, verification instructions and, where appropriate, an embedded copy of the signed JSON receipt.
Proposed receipt envelope
Use a standard signature construction such as a constrained JWS JSON Serialization profile, with explicit canonicalisation and protocol versioning.

Avoid creating a proprietary digital-signature algorithm.

Example conceptual receipt:
{
  "format": "secure-intake-receipt",
  "version": 1,
  "payload": {
    "receiptId": "rcpt_01J...",
    "submissionReference": "SI-8K4P2M",
    "organisationId": "org_123",
    "dropboxId": "dbx_456",
    "receivedAt": "2026-11-03T12:30:00Z",
    "commitment": {
      "algorithm": "SHA-256",
      "manifestDigest": "base64url-encoded-digest",
      "fileCount": 2
    }
  },
  "signature": {
    "algorithm": "ES256",
    "keyId": "receipt-key-2026-01",
    "value": "base64url-encoded-signature"
  }
}


This is an illustrative logical structure, not yet the final JWS wire format.

The actual specification must define the exact signed bytes, encoding, algorithm identifiers and signature representation.

No field should be added to the receipt without deciding whether it is part of the signed payload.
3. What the receipt proves
A verifier should be able to establish:
- The receipt has not been altered since signing.
- It was signed by the holder of the corresponding private key.
- The receipt identifies a particular submission and organisation.
- The signing system asserted receipt at the recorded timestamp.
- The receipt commits to a specific immutable submission manifest.
- Supplied ciphertext can be compared against the committed digests.
The verifier cannot establish from the receipt alone:
- Whether the uploader was truthful.
- Whether the uploaded plaintext was valid or lawful.
- Whether the encrypted files contain particular documents.
- Whether the organisation actually reviewed the submission.
- Whether the ciphertext remains available.
- Whether the server's timestamp is independently trustworthy.
This distinction should be explicit in the product documentation and verifier output.
4. Ciphertext commitments
I'd commit to a complete Submission Evidence Manifest, not just a single combined hash of file bytes.

The manifest should cover:
Component	Commitment
Submission identifier	Exact identifier
Organisation and dropbox	Stable scoped identifiers
Form snapshot	Immutable schema digest
Server-readable fields	Authenticated representation or protected commitment
E2EE form response	Ciphertext digest
Files	Ciphertext digests and lengths
File manifests	Digest, protocol and chunk information
Recipient envelopes	Digest of immutable envelope set
Recipient policy	Pinned version and key references
Revision number	Exact submitted revision
Finalisation event	Receipt identifier and timestamp binding

A reviewer or auditor can then determine whether the evidence matches the receipt without requiring the server to decrypt it.
Manifest structure
{
  "manifestVersion": 1,
  "submissionId": "sub_123",
  "formSnapshotDigest": "sha256:...",
  "files": [
    {
      "fileId": "file_001",
      "ciphertextLength": 104861600,
      "ciphertextDigest": "sha256:...",
      "encryptedManifestDigest": "sha256:..."
    }
  ],
  "encryptedFormPayload": {
    "ciphertextLength": 2048,
    "ciphertextDigest": "sha256:..."
  },
  "recipientEnvelopeSetDigest": "sha256:..."
}


The receipt signs a digest of the canonical evidence manifest.

The manifest itself must remain available for verification or be bundled with the downloadable receipt.

For portability, I'd include the necessary non-sensitive manifest information directly in the receipt package.
5. Large files and chunked uploads
The v3.2 upload protocol already divides encrypted files into independently authenticated chunks.

For signed receipts, I'd define two related commitments.

Chunk commitment: A digest for each confirmed ciphertext chunk.

File commitment: A digest of a canonical file manifest containing the ordered chunk digests, lengths and cryptographic context.

This allows verification without concatenating a multi-gigabyte ciphertext file into memory.

A verifier can stream through chunks and calculate the required digests.

A manifest commitment is not automatically the same as SHA-256 of the concatenated ciphertext bytes. The receipt format must identify which construction it uses.

I'd make the canonical file-manifest digest authoritative and optionally include a whole-ciphertext digest when operationally useful.
6. Signing keys and custody
Receipt signing keys are platform signing keys, not E2EE recipient keys.

They serve different purposes:
Key	Purpose	Custodian
File recipient key	Decrypt submitted files	Authorised reviewer or organisation
Draft encryption key	Recover unfinished confidential drafts	Uploader
Audit checkpoint key	Sign audit-chain checkpoints	Deployment security infrastructure
Receipt signing key	Attest to accepted submission evidence	Deployment or delegated organisation signing service

I'd support two receipt-signing models:
- Deployment-managed signing: One signing authority for the deployment, with organisation identity included in each signed receipt. Recommended initial implementation.
- Organisation-managed signing: Each organisation configures a dedicated signing key, potentially held in an external KMS/HSM. Optional advanced capability.
For a standalone deployment, a protected local private key should be supported. For distributed deployments, use a configured external signing service or securely managed key store.

The application should never generate a new signing identity silently on every restart.
Signing algorithm
I'd use ES256 (ECDSA P-256 with SHA-256) as the initial interoperability profile, provided the selected key provider and Native AOT build support the required signing operations.

The receipt protocol should allow future approved algorithms through explicit versioned profiles.

Do not allow clients to select arbitrary signing algorithms or downgrade verification.
7. Trusting the public key
A valid signature proves possession of a private key. It does not independently establish who controls that key.

This is the most important trust distinction in receipt verification.

I'd publish a signed-key registry through a well-known endpoint:
GET /.well-known/secure-intake/receipt-keys.json


Each key record should contain:
{
  "keyId": "receipt-key-2026-01",
  "algorithm": "ES256",
  "status": "active",
  "validFrom": "2026-11-01T00:00:00Z",
  "validUntil": null,
  "publicKeyFingerprint": "sha256:...",
  "publicKey": {
    "kty": "EC",
    "crv": "P-256",
    "x": "...",
    "y": "..."
  }
}


For offline verification, the user must be able to obtain the trusted public key independently.

I'd support:
- Explicit public-key fingerprint pinning.
- A locally installed trust registry.
- A public-key file supplied through a trusted channel.
- Optional certificate-backed signing identities later.
The verifier must not automatically trust a public key merely because it is embedded in the receipt.

That would make self-signed forged receipts appear authentic.
8. Key rotation and revocation
Signing-key rotation must preserve the ability to verify historical receipts.

I'd use the following key states:
Pending
   ↓
Active
   ↓
Retired
   ↓
Revoked / Compromised


A retired key cannot sign new receipts but remains available for verifying historical ones.

A compromised key must be explicitly marked as such.

The verifier should distinguish:
- Signature mathematically valid.
- Key trusted for the relevant period.
- Key subsequently revoked or compromised.
- Historical trust status uncertain.
If a key is compromised, an attacker may be able to forge receipts with plausible historical timestamps. Without independently anchored time evidence, the verifier cannot reliably distinguish all such forgeries from genuine historical receipts.

Key rotation alone does not resolve that problem.

For v3.5, I'd retain signed key-history records and audit checkpoints, while leaving independent timestamp authorities as an optional later enhancement.
9. Timestamp handling
The receipt should contain:
Timestamp	Meaning
receivedAt	Server recorded successful submission finalisation
signedAt	Signing service created the receipt
keyValidFrom	Start of signing key's authorised period
keyValidUntil	End of signing key's authorised period, if applicable

All protocol timestamps should use UTC with an unambiguous ISO 8601 representation.

The UI can display them in the user's local timezone.

I'd avoid using the initial upload-session creation time as the receipt timestamp. A submission is not received until finalisation succeeds.
Time authority
For the initial release, timestamps are server assertions.

The deployment should monitor clock synchronisation and reject or flag significant clock anomalies.

The receipt should not claim an independently certified timestamp unless an external timestamping or equivalent evidence service was actually used.
10. Finalisation and signing consistency
There is an important distributed-systems issue here: database commit and external signing cannot always be made atomic.

I'd use a two-stage finalisation process.
Ciphertext verified
       ↓
Transactional commit
  - Submission finalised
  - Evidence manifest immutable
  - Receipt signing job created
       ↓
Signing worker
  - Signs canonical receipt payload
  - Stores signed receipt
  - Marks receipt issued
       ↓
Receipt available to uploader


A submission can therefore be successfully received while its signed receipt is temporarily pending.

This is preferable to losing an accepted submission because a remote KMS or signing provider is temporarily unavailable.

The signing job must be idempotent. It should operate on the immutable manifest digest and never recompute evidence from mutable live records.

If signing is retried, the system should preserve one authoritative issued receipt per submission revision, rather than creating conflicting receipts.
Submission states
I'd distinguish:
- received_receipt_pending
- received_receipt_issued
- receipt_signing_failed — operational condition, not failed submission
The public submission status can remain Received while the receipt is pending.
11. Independent verification
I'd provide a standalone verifier that requires no API access.

It should be available as:
1. A .NET 10 CLI executable.
2. An optional desktop verifier.
3. An optional browser-based verifier operating locally.
The CLI is the priority because it is straightforward to automate and independently distribute.
Proposed commands
secureintake receipt verify \
  --receipt submission.sir.json \
  --trusted-key organisation-public-key.json


To verify the receipt against an exported evidence package:
secureintake receipt verify \
  --receipt submission.sir.json \
  --trusted-key organisation-public-key.json \
  --evidence ./submission-evidence/


The first command verifies the signature and the receipt's embedded commitment.

The second additionally verifies supplied evidence against the signed commitment.

These are different assurance levels and must be reported separately.
Example verification output
Receipt verification

Signature
Valid

Signing key

Trusted locally

Manifest commitment
Matched

Ciphertext evidence
Not supplied


Submission reference

SI-8K4P2M

Recorded receipt time

3 Nov 2026, 12:30 UTC

The receipt signature and manifest commitment are valid. The original ciphertext was not supplied, so its contents have not been independently compared.
Illustrative verification result, not an actual verified receipt.
Verification result codes
I'd standardise machine-readable results:
VALID_SIGNATURE_TRUSTED_KEY
VALID_SIGNATURE_UNTRUSTED_KEY
INVALID_SIGNATURE
UNSUPPORTED_RECEIPT_VERSION
UNSUPPORTED_SIGNATURE_ALGORITHM
SIGNING_KEY_REVOKED
SIGNING_KEY_STATUS_UNKNOWN
MANIFEST_DIGEST_MISMATCH
CIPHERTEXT_DIGEST_MISMATCH
EVIDENCE_NOT_SUPPLIED


A valid signature with an untrusted key must not produce a general "Verified" success result.
12. C# verification contracts
I'd separate cryptographic verification from trust evaluation and evidence verification.
public interface IReceiptVerifier
{
    ValueTask<ReceiptVerificationResult> VerifyAsync(
        ReadOnlyMemory<byte> receipt,
        IReceiptTrustStore trustStore,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public interface IReceiptTrustStore
{
    ValueTask<TrustedReceiptKey?> FindAsync(
        string issuer,
        string keyId,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record TrustedReceiptKey(
    string Issuer,
    string KeyId,
    string Algorithm,
    byte[] SubjectPublicKeyInfo,
    ReceiptKeyTrustStatus Status);

public enum ReceiptKeyTrustStatus
{
    Trusted,
    Retired,
    Revoked,
    Unknown
}

public sealed record ReceiptVerificationResult(
    bool SignatureValid,
    bool KeyTrusted,
    bool ManifestValid,
    bool? CiphertextEvidenceValid,
    IReadOnlyList<string> Findings);


CiphertextEvidenceValid is nullable because the user may verify a receipt without providing the original ciphertext.

For Native AOT, use source-generated JSON serialisation and avoid reflection-based plugin loading.

The implementation must also enforce receipt size, nesting depth, field-count and supported-algorithm limits.
13. Receipt download and distribution
A signed receipt should be available immediately after issuance through the uploader's authorised submission session.

I'd support:
Delivery method	Behaviour
Direct JSON download	Default, independently verifiable
PDF receipt	Optional readable presentation
Email notification	Advises that the receipt is ready
Return-access portal	Download while the capability remains valid
Reusable uploader account	Download for explicitly linked submissions
Organisation API	Authorised retrieval for records management

Receipt retrieval must not require access to the submitted files.

However, receipt URLs must still be protected because receipts may disclose submission references, timestamps and organisational relationships.
Recommended file naming
SI-8K4P2M-receipt.sir.json
SI-8K4P2M-receipt.pdf


The JSON receipt should remain portable. Its signature must not depend on the download filename.

I'd also provide a verification command in the download instructions.
14. Receipt privacy
Signed receipts create a long-lived, shareable record.

That makes data minimisation important.

I'd exclude the following from the default receipt:
- Uploader email address and telephone number.
- Original filenames.
- Server-readable form answers.
- E2EE field labels and values unless strictly necessary.
- IP addresses and device information.
- Internal workflow states.
- Reviewer identities.
- Storage object paths.
- Recipient public keys.
The receipt should use opaque identifiers and cryptographic commitments.
Commitment privacy
Even a cryptographic hash can leak information when it represents a low-entropy value.

For example, directly hashing a short category code or date of birth may allow guessing.

I'd therefore commit to server-readable form data through a canonical, nonce-bound private evidence record, rather than publishing raw hashes of individual field values.

The nonce must be retained with the private evidence package if later verification of those values is required.

The public receipt can contain the digest of that evidence record without exposing its fields.
15. Receipt retention
I'd treat signed receipts as a distinct evidence category.

A receipt may need to survive longer than the ciphertext it acknowledges.
Proposed policy
Resource	Retention
Signed receipt	Organisation-defined evidence retention
Submission manifest	At least while receipt evidence verification is supported
Ciphertext	Submission retention policy
Signing public keys	At least as long as receipts may be verified
Signing private keys	Only while authorised for signing; securely retire
Key history and revocation records	At least as long as receipt trust must be assessed
Receipt issuance audit	Audit retention policy

Deleting the ciphertext must not automatically invalidate the mathematical signature on the receipt.

It does, however, prevent a verifier from comparing the original ciphertext unless an authorised copy exists elsewhere.
Legal holds
A legal hold on a submission should preserve its associated receipt and required verification evidence.

An organisation must not be allowed to delete the only retained evidence manifest while claiming that full evidence verification remains available.
Backup considerations
Receipt deletion from the live database does not guarantee immediate removal from backups.

Retention documentation must describe backup expiry and restoration behaviour accurately.
16. Audit integration
Receipt issuance should produce durable audit events.
receipt.signing.requested
receipt.signing.completed
receipt.signing.failed

receipt.issued
receipt.downloaded
receipt.reissued

receipt.signing_key.created
receipt.signing_key.activated
receipt.signing_key.retired
receipt.signing_key.revoked

receipt.verification.requested
receipt.evidence.exported


Independent offline verification does not generate a server-side audit event, because it does not contact the server.

The system must not imply that it can monitor offline receipt verification.
Reissuing receipts
I'd prohibit silently replacing an existing signed receipt.

If correction is necessary, issue a new signed receipt that references the previous receipt identifier and includes a correction reason.

The original remains part of the evidence history, subject to retention policy.
17. Database additions
CREATE TABLE submission_receipts (
    id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
    organisation_id UUID NOT NULL,
    submission_id UUID NOT NULL,
    submission_revision INTEGER NOT NULL,
    signing_key_id UUID NOT NULL,
    evidence_manifest_digest BYTEA NOT NULL,
    receipt_digest BYTEA,
    status VARCHAR(32) NOT NULL,
    received_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
    signed_at TIMESTAMPTZ,
    issued_at TIMESTAMPTZ,
    created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

    UNIQUE (organisation_id, submission_id, submission_revision)
);


I'd also introduce:
submission_evidence_manifests
submission_evidence_manifest_files

receipt_signing_keys
receipt_signing_key_history
receipt_signing_operations

receipt_corrections
receipt_download_events
receipt_evidence_exports


The signing key registry may live in the deployment control plane, while receipt and evidence records belong to the relevant tenant data plane.

Cross-plane references must use stable opaque identifiers rather than database foreign keys.
18. API endpoints
GET  /api/public/submissions/{submissionId}/receipt
GET  /api/public/submissions/{submissionId}/receipt.pdf

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/receipts
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/receipts/{receiptId}

POST /api/organisations/{id}/receipts/{receiptId}/evidence-export

GET  /.well-known/secure-intake/receipt-keys.json


Public submission receipt endpoints require valid submission-specific return authorisation.

I'd avoid a public receipt lookup based solely on a predictable submission reference.

Independent verification should happen locally, although an optional online verification service could be added later.
19. Security requirements
The receipt implementation should explicitly test:
- Altered signed payloads.
- Changed file digests.
- Reordered file manifests.
- Removed or duplicated chunks.
- Incorrect organisation identifiers.
- Wrong signing keys.
- Unknown or revoked keys.
- Unsupported signature algorithms.
- Malformed canonicalisation.
- Truncated receipts.
- Cross-tenant receipt substitution.
- Concurrent finalisation attempts.
- Signing-provider outages.
- Signing-key rotation during issuance.
- Verification of historical receipts after key retirement.
Cryptographic interoperability tests should use fixed, independently generated test vectors.

A receipt verifier should not depend on the main Secure Intake API or its database to determine whether a signature is mathematically valid.
20. Implementation priorities
I'd deliver v3.5 in this order:
1. Canonical submission evidence manifest specification.
2. Ciphertext chunk and file commitment format.
3. Versioned receipt payload specification.
4. Receipt signing-key registry and custody abstraction.
5. Durable signing workflow.
6. JSON receipt download.
7. Independent .NET CLI verifier.
8. Trust-store and key-history verification.
9. PDF presentation.
10. Retention, correction and evidence-export workflows.
The critical distinction is between receipt authenticity, signer trust and evidence integrity. They must be reported separately rather than collapsed into a single success indicator.

21. Next decision: submission evidence exports
The next logical feature is how organisations export encrypted submissions for archiving, legal review, migration or independent verification.

Individual file and receipt exports

Authorised users download encrypted files, manifests and receipts separately. Minimal export infrastructure.


Portable encrypted evidence packages

A versioned package containing ciphertext, authenticated manifests, recipient envelopes, form data and signed receipts, verifiable offline without server access.


Portable packages with managed archive workflows

Adds scheduled evidence exports, archive destinations, approval-controlled release and independent integrity reconciliation.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, submission evidence exports should use portable encrypted evidence packages with offline verification. Continue planning evidence package formats, streaming exports, encryption envelopes, independent verification, authorisation, retention, migration and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.6
Portable evidence packages · Streaming exports · Encryption envelopes · Offline verification · Authorisation · Retention · Migration · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will support portable, encrypted evidence packages that can be exported, archived, transferred and independently verified without requiring access to the original deployment.

I'd introduce a versioned Secure Intake Evidence Package (.siep) format.

The package is a transport and preservation format, not a new encryption scheme. It reuses the existing encrypted files, confidential form payloads, recipient envelopes, authenticated manifests and signed submission receipts.

Most importantly, exporting a package must not require the Secure Intake server to decrypt any submission content.
1. Evidence package architecture
Finalised submission

Ciphertext · Form snapshot · Recipient envelopes · Signed receipt


Authorised export operation

Permission checks · Approval policy · Immutable evidence snapshot


Streaming package writer

Manifest · Receipt · Ciphertext streams · Envelope references


Portable .siep package

Immutable evidence · Integrity commitments · No plaintext files


Offline verification

Trust · Signature · Hashes · Completeness


Authorised decryption

Reviewer-controlled keys · Local plaintext




Export, verification and decryption are separate capabilities.

A user may be authorised to export encrypted evidence without possessing the private keys needed to decrypt it.
2. Package format
I'd use a constrained, streaming-friendly container based on ZIP64, with a formally specified internal layout.

ZIP64 provides interoperability and supports large files. However, we should prohibit archive-level encryption, arbitrary compression methods, path traversal and ambiguous duplicate entries.

The encrypted file content is already ciphertext, so compression offers little benefit.
Proposed .siep structure
submission-SI-8K4P2M.siep
│
├── package.json
├── package-signature.jws
│
├── evidence/
│   ├── submission-manifest.json
│   ├── form-snapshot.json
│   ├── readable-fields.enc.json
│   └── recipient-envelope-manifest.json
│
├── receipts/
│   └── submission-revision-1.sir.json
│
├── encrypted/
│   ├── form-response.bin
│   └── files/
│       ├── file-001/
│       │   ├── manifest.json
│       │   ├── chunk-000000.bin
│       │   └── chunk-000001.bin
│       └── file-002/
│           ├── manifest.json
│           └── chunk-000000.bin
│
├── envelopes/
│   ├── file-001.json
│   ├── file-002.json
│   └── form-response.json
│
└── trust/
    ├── signing-key.json
    └── key-history.json


The trust directory contains public verification material, not private keys.

The verifier must not automatically trust those included keys. It must compare them against an independently trusted key or trust registry.
Why ZIP64?
- Mature tooling and libraries.
- Streaming read and write support.
- Large-file support.
- No requirement to load the entire package into RAM.
- Simple inspection with standard archive tools.
- Straightforward integration into .NET 10.
The .siep specification should define a strict ZIP64 profile rather than accepting arbitrary ZIP files.
3. Package manifest
The package manifest describes the archive and its contents.

Example:
{
  "format": "secure-intake-evidence-package",
  "version": 1,
  "packageId": "pkg_01J...",
  "organisationId": "org_123",
  "submissionId": "sub_456",
  "submissionReference": "SI-8K4P2M",
  "submissionRevision": 1,
  "createdAt": "2026-11-03T13:00:00Z",
  "receiptId": "rcpt_789",
  "evidenceManifestDigest": "sha256:...",
  "entries": [
    {
      "path": "evidence/submission-manifest.json",
      "length": 4096,
      "digest": "sha256:..."
    },
    {
      "path": "encrypted/files/file-001/chunk-000000.bin",
      "length": 4194320,
      "digest": "sha256:..."
    }
  ]
}


All paths must be normalised and relative.

The specification must reject duplicate paths, traversal sequences, symbolic links, unexpected entry types and unsupported compression.

The manifest must be canonicalised before signing.
4. Two independent signatures
I'd preserve two different cryptographic attestations.
Signature	Issuer	Purpose
Submission receipt	Original Secure Intake signing authority	Confirms acceptance of the committed submission
Export package signature	Exporting deployment's authorised signing authority	Confirms which package entries were assembled for export

The package signature does not replace the submission receipt.

This matters because a submission may be exported years after it was received, potentially by a different deployment following migration.
Signature boundaries
The original receipt signs the submission evidence commitment.

The package signature covers the package manifest and its entry digests.

An attacker who modifies ciphertext should fail package integrity verification and, where the original receipt commits to that ciphertext, submission evidence verification.

An attacker who removes a package entry should fail package completeness verification.
5. Package confidentiality
The package must never contain plaintext E2EE file content or plaintext E2EE form values.

However, there is a separate issue with server-readable metadata.

In v2.9, fields such as reference numbers and document categories may be server-readable.

Those values should not automatically be exported in plaintext.

I'd define two evidence export profiles.
Profile	Contents
minimal	Ciphertext, required manifests, envelopes, signed receipts and minimal public-safe metadata
complete	Same evidence plus server-readable submission values, protected inside an encrypted metadata payload

The default should be minimal.

For a complete export, the server may encrypt server-readable metadata specifically for the export recipients.

This is export-time encryption, not historical E2EE. The server had access to those values before export.

That distinction must be preserved in the package metadata.
Export metadata encryption
I'd use a separate export metadata DEK and wrap it for explicitly approved recipient public keys.

This must not modify the original file recipient envelopes.

The package manifest should record which metadata components are export-encrypted and which remain visible.
6. Recipient envelopes
The package must preserve the original recipient-envelope set associated with the submission revision.

That includes:
- Recipient key identifiers.
- Cryptographic suite identifiers.
- Envelope ciphertext.
- Key epoch references.
- Recipient policy version.
- Envelope creation metadata.
- Authenticated envelope-set digest.
It must not contain private keys, recovery passwords or unwrapped DEKs.
Historical access
Exporting an envelope does not guarantee that a reviewer can decrypt it.

The reviewer still needs the corresponding private key.

A retired or deleted key may make the package permanently undecryptable.

The export preflight should report recipient-key availability where the platform has reliable inventory information, without pretending it can prove possession of private keys held outside the platform.
Adding new recipients
I'd prohibit the server from adding new decryption recipients during export.

If new recipients require access, an authorised client must perform the existing v2.7 rewrap workflow.

That operation creates a new authenticated envelope-set version and appropriate audit evidence.

The original evidence package must preserve the original submission commitment rather than silently rewriting history.
7. Streaming export design
Large evidence packages must not be assembled in memory.

I'd implement a streaming writer that reads ciphertext from the configured storage backend and writes it directly into the package stream.
Storage backend

S3 · R2 · Local filesystem

Export worker

Bounded buffers

Package output

ZIP64 stream



Each ciphertext entry is streamed, hashed and checked against the immutable submission evidence manifest.

I'd target constant memory usage relative to file size, subject to bounded per-entry buffers and metadata overhead.
.NET interface
public interface IEvidencePackageExporter
{
    Task<ExportResult> ExportAsync(
        EvidenceExportRequest request,
        Stream destination,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record EvidenceExportRequest(
    Guid OrganisationId,
    Guid SubmissionId,
    int SubmissionRevision,
    EvidenceExportProfile Profile,
    Guid RequestedBy,
    string IdempotencyKey);

public enum EvidenceExportProfile
{
    Minimal,
    Complete
}

public sealed record ExportResult(
    Guid PackageId,
    long BytesWritten,
    byte[] PackageDigest,
    DateTimeOffset CompletedAt);


The exporter must not accept arbitrary storage object paths from API callers.

It should resolve immutable object references through the authorised tenant storage abstraction.
8. Export consistency
A submission may have several revisions, recipient rewrap operations or retention changes.

The export must therefore operate against an immutable evidence snapshot.

I'd use this sequence:
Authorise export
       ↓
Select submission revision
       ↓
Resolve immutable evidence manifest
       ↓
Create export snapshot
       ↓
Pin required object references
       ↓
Stream and verify objects
       ↓
Create package manifest
       ↓
Sign package
       ↓
Publish export artifact


The export snapshot must include the exact envelope-set version being exported.

It should not silently switch to a newer envelope set halfway through processing.
Storage consistency
For object storage, use immutable object versions or content-addressed objects where supported.

If an expected ciphertext object changes or disappears during export, the operation must fail.

Do not substitute a replacement object merely because its filename or storage key matches.
9. Export job lifecycle
I'd support both direct and asynchronous exports.

Small packages can be streamed directly when deployment policy allows.

Large packages should use a durable export job.
Requested
    ↓
Authorising
    ↓
Preparing
    ↓
Exporting
    ↓
Verifying
    ↓
Signing
    ↓
Ready
    ↓
Expired / Deleted


Failure states include:
- authorisation_failed
- approval_required
- source_unavailable
- integrity_failed
- signing_failed
- cancelled
A failed export must never be published as a valid package.
10. Export authorisation
Evidence export is more sensitive than ordinary submission viewing.

I'd define a dedicated permission:
submissions.evidence.export


Additional permissions:
submissions.evidence.export.complete
submissions.evidence.export.bulk
submissions.evidence.export.approve
submissions.evidence.export.audit


Export authorisation must be independent of decryption authorisation.
Recommended controls
Operation	Default control
Single minimal export	Authorised reviewer or designated exporter
Complete export	Elevated permission
Bulk export	Elevated permission and approval
Export including sensitive server-readable metadata	Explicit policy
Export to external destination	Approval where configured
