Export under legal hold	Retention/security policy evaluation

For high-assurance organisations, the existing sensitive-operation approval engine should support two-person approval, fresh MFA and no self-approval.

An export approval should bind to an immutable manifest of exactly what will be exported.
11. Offline verification
I'd extend the v3.5 verifier with package support.
CLI commands
secureintake evidence verify \
  --package submission-SI-8K4P2M.siep \
  --trusted-key receipt-public-key.json \
  --trusted-export-key export-public-key.json


The verifier should perform:
1. ZIP64 structural validation.
2. Package manifest validation.
3. Entry count and path validation.
4. Entry length and digest verification.
5. Package signature verification.
6. Signing-key trust evaluation.
7. Submission receipt signature verification.
8. Submission evidence manifest verification.
9. Ciphertext commitment verification.
10. Recipient-envelope integrity verification.
It must not require a network connection.
Verification results
Evidence package verification

Package structure
Valid

Package signature
Valid

Submission receipt
Valid

Ciphertext commitments
Matched

Trusted signing keys
Matched

Plaintext verification
Not performed


All included evidence matches the signed commitments. This does not establish the plaintext contents or the uploader's real-world identity.
Illustrative successful verification, not a verified real package.
The verifier should return machine-readable JSON results for automation.
12. Verification versus decryption
I'd keep the verifier and decryptor as separate commands.
secureintake evidence verify \
  --package submission.siep \
  --trusted-key receipt-public-key.json


secureintake evidence decrypt \
  --package submission.siep \
  --key reviewer.sik \
  --output ./decrypted/


The second command must require appropriate private-key access.

Before decryption, the client should verify the package's integrity and authenticated encryption metadata.

The decryptor should never overwrite existing files without explicit confirmation.

Output filenames must be sanitised and treated as untrusted data.

Decrypted output should be written only to a user-selected destination with appropriate filesystem permissions.
13. Archive safety
ZIP64 introduces archive-processing risks.

I'd explicitly prohibit:
- Absolute paths.
- ../ traversal.
- Symbolic links.
- Duplicate entry names.
- Unsupported compression.
- Unbounded metadata lengths.
- Excessive entry counts.
- Nested archives interpreted automatically.
- Executable extraction hooks.
- Arbitrary external references.
The verifier should use bounded memory and configurable resource limits.

The exporter should write only known, validated entry types.

This is particularly important because .siep files may be supplied by untrusted third parties for verification.
14. Retention
Evidence packages are derived copies of retained submission data.

They need separate lifecycle controls.

I'd distinguish:
Resource	Retention
Original submission ciphertext	Submission policy
Signed submission receipt	Evidence retention policy
Generated export package	Export retention policy
Export job metadata	Operational retention
Export audit records	Audit retention policy
Independently downloaded package	Outside platform control

Temporary export retention
For server-generated packages, I'd use a short default retention period, such as 24 hours, configurable within deployment limits.

After expiration:
- Download access is revoked.
- Temporary package objects are deleted.
- Cleanup is verified or retried.
- Export metadata and audit remain according to policy.
Deleting the server copy cannot delete packages already downloaded by authorised users.

The UI and documentation should make this explicit.
15. Migration between deployments
Portable packages should support future migration and disaster recovery.

However, importing an evidence package into another Secure Intake deployment is not the same as creating a new submission.

I'd distinguish:
Operation	Behaviour
Verify	Validate package offline
Archive	Store package as external evidence
Import evidence	Register package and its provenance
Restore submission	Reconstruct an eligible submission record under controlled migration
Re-encrypt	Client-side cryptographic transformation

Migration principles
A destination deployment must not rewrite the original receipt.

It may issue a separate signed import attestation recording when and where the package was accepted.

The original submission reference, issuer and receipt remain part of the evidence history.

The destination should assign its own internal record identifiers while preserving the original identifiers as provenance.
Recipient keys
Migration does not automatically transfer private keys.

If the destination's reviewers do not possess the original recipient private keys, they cannot decrypt the imported ciphertext.

A migration requiring new recipients must use the authorised rewrap or re-encryption process.
16. Import provenance
I'd define an import attestation containing:
{
  "importId": "imp_123",
  "sourcePackageDigest": "sha256:...",
  "sourceReceiptId": "rcpt_456",
  "destinationOrganisationId": "org_789",
  "importedAt": "2026-11-03T15:00:00Z",
  "verificationResult": "valid",
  "importMode": "archive"
}


The destination deployment signs its own attestation.

This creates a chain of provenance without altering the original evidence.

The importer must not infer that the source organisation and destination organisation are the same legal entity merely because their display names match.
17. Audit events
I'd introduce:
evidence.export.requested
evidence.export.approved
evidence.export.denied
evidence.export.started
evidence.export.completed
evidence.export.failed
evidence.export.downloaded
evidence.export.expired
evidence.export.deleted

evidence.import.requested
evidence.import.verified
evidence.import.completed
evidence.import.rejected

evidence.package.signature.created
evidence.package.integrity_failed


Audit records should include:
- Actor identity.
- Organisation.
- Submission and revision.
- Export profile.
- Approval reference.
- Package digest.
- Signing key identifier.
- Destination classification.
- Outcome and timestamp.
They must not contain plaintext E2EE values, private keys or decrypted file contents.
18. Database additions
evidence_export_jobs
evidence_export_job_items
evidence_export_snapshots
evidence_export_artifacts

evidence_export_approvals
evidence_export_download_grants
evidence_export_cleanup_operations

evidence_import_jobs
evidence_import_attestations
evidence_import_provenance

evidence_package_signing_keys
evidence_package_signing_operations


These tables should integrate with the existing tenant routing, audit and durable worker infrastructure.

Export artifacts should use the configured storage abstraction, with separate storage prefixes or buckets where operationally appropriate.
19. API endpoints
POST /api/organisations/{id}/submissions/{submissionId}/evidence-exports

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-exports/{exportId}
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-exports/{exportId}/download

POST /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-exports/{exportId}/cancel

POST /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-imports
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-imports/{importId}


Export creation should accept an idempotency key.

Download access should use short-lived, narrowly scoped capabilities.

The API must revalidate export eligibility before issuing a download capability, particularly if permissions or organisation status have changed since package creation.
20. Implementation priorities
I'd implement v3.6 in this order:
1. Formal .siep format specification.
2. Canonical package manifest.
3. ZIP64 streaming writer.
4. Ciphertext digest verification during export.
5. Package signing.
6. Offline CLI verifier.
7. Export authorisation and approval integration.
8. Temporary artifact storage and cleanup.
9. Encrypted server-readable metadata export.
10. Import and provenance support.
The central principle: A .siep package is a self-contained, cryptographically verifiable record of encrypted submission evidence. Its authenticity and integrity can be checked independently, while plaintext access remains controlled by the original recipient encryption keys.

21. Next decision: evidence import and disaster recovery
We now have portable evidence packages. The next architectural decision is how a Secure Intake deployment should consume them.

Archive-only import

Imported packages are independently verified and stored as read-only external evidence. They do not become native submissions.


Controlled evidence import and native restoration

Supports read-only archive imports and authorised restoration of compatible submissions, preserving original receipts, provenance and encryption boundaries.


Full cross-deployment migration

Adds bulk migration of submissions, workflows, organisation configuration, key references and audit history, with reconciliation and cutover tooling.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, evidence import and disaster recovery should use controlled evidence import and native submission restoration. Continue planning import verification, provenance, native restoration, trust boundaries, key availability, disaster recovery, reconciliation and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.7
Controlled evidence import · Native restoration · Cryptographic provenance · Trust boundaries · Key availability · Disaster recovery · Reconciliation · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will support two import modes:
1. Evidence archive: Verify and retain a .siep package as read-only evidence without creating a live submission.
2. Native restoration: Restore a compatible submission into the destination deployment, preserving its original cryptographic evidence, receipt and provenance.
Native restoration must be a controlled administrative operation. It must never silently treat imported evidence as a newly received submission.

The implementation should reuse the v3.6 package format, v3.5 signed receipts, existing E2EE architecture, tenant isolation and sensitive-operation approval engine.
1. Import architecture
Incoming .siep package

Ciphertext · Manifests · Envelopes · Signed receipt · Public-key evidence


Quarantine and offline verification

Archive safety · Digests · Signatures · Trust registry · Completeness


Import planning

Tenant mapping · Duplicate detection · Key availability · Compatibility · Approval


Archive-only

Immutable evidence record · No workflow execution


Native restoration

Restored submission · Isolated workflow · Provenance retained




Destination attestation and reconciliation

Signed import record · Object verification · Audit · Operational status


A restored submission is a new destination-side record linked to original evidence. It is not a rewriting of the original submission's history.
2. Import modes
Capability	Archive-only	Native restoration
Verify signatures and hashes	Yes	Yes
Retain original package	Yes	Yes
Create native submission	No	Yes
Preserve original receipt	Yes	Yes
Access via reviewer UI	Evidence viewer	Restored submission view
Trigger normal workflow	No	Not automatically
Decrypt with existing recipient keys	If available	If available
Generate destination attestation	Yes	Yes
Delete or alter source evidence	Never	Never

Both modes require an explicit destination organisation.

The importer must not infer the destination organisation from an untrusted package field.
3. Trust boundaries
There are three separate trust decisions.
Evidence integrity

Does the package match its signed manifests and receipts?



Issuer trust

Does the destination trust the signing authority that issued the original receipt?



Destination authorisation

Is this evidence permitted to be imported into the selected organisation, and under what conditions?



A mathematically valid signature is not sufficient to authorise import.

A package can be authentic but belong to another organisation or contain evidence the destination is not entitled to hold.
Trust policy
I'd define:
{
  "trustedIssuers": [
    {
      "issuerId": "issuer_original",
      "keyFingerprints": ["sha256:..."],
      "allowedSourceOrganisations": ["org_original"],
      "allowArchiveImport": true,
      "allowNativeRestoration": false
    }
  ],
  "requireValidReceipt": true,
  "requireValidPackageSignature": true,
  "allowUnknownIssuer": false,
  "requireApprovalForNativeRestoration": true
}


Unknown issuers may be quarantined for investigation, but must not be automatically promoted to trusted status.

The public keys included in .siep are evidence material, not trust anchors.
4. Import verification
The importer must verify the package before creating native records.

I'd use the same verification library as the standalone CLI, ensuring that online import and offline verification apply the same rules.
Verification stages
Stage	Validation
Container	Valid constrained ZIP64 profile
Entries	No traversal, duplicates or unsupported types
Package manifest	Canonical structure, required entries, limits
Package signature	Cryptographic validity
Source trust	Trusted signing key and issuer policy
Submission receipt	Valid original signature
Evidence manifest	Matches receipt commitment
Ciphertext	Every required object matches its committed digest
Envelopes	Original envelope-set digest matches
Schema	Supported form and cryptographic protocol versions
Tenant	Explicitly authorised source-to-destination mapping

Verification should produce a signed or audit-bound report.

The report must identify exactly which checks passed, failed or could not be completed.

Native restoration requires complete integrity verification. A partially verified package may be retained in quarantine for investigation but should not become a native submission.
5. Import lifecycle
I'd define the following states.
1

Uploaded

Package received into quarantine



2

Verifying

Structure, signatures, trust and ciphertext commitments



3

Verified

Package passes required integrity checks



4

Planning

Destination mapping, compatibility and key assessment



5

Awaiting approval

Required authorisation and sensitive-operation controls



6

Restoring

Ciphertext and metadata committed into destination storage



7

Reconciling

Object digests, records, provenance and quotas checked



8

Completed

Archive or restored submission made available



Terminal and exceptional states should include rejected, cancelled, failed, quarantined and cleanup_pending.

A failed restoration must not leave a partially visible native submission.
6. Provenance model
Every import needs a provenance chain.

For example:
Original deployment
  Submission SUB-A
  Receipt RCPT-A
       ↓
Export package PKG-A
       ↓
Destination deployment
  Import IMP-B
  Native submission SUB-B
  Import attestation ATT-B


The destination record must preserve:
- Original issuer identity.
- Original organisation and dropbox identifiers.
- Original submission identifier and reference.
- Original receipt and its digest.
- Export package digest.
- Import verification report digest.
- Destination organisation and submission identifiers.
- Import actor and approval reference.
- Import time.
- Restoration mode.
Example provenance record
{
  "source": {
    "issuerId": "issuer_original",
    "organisationId": "org_original",
    "submissionId": "sub_original",
    "receiptId": "rcpt_original",
    "packageDigest": "sha256:..."
  },
  "destination": {
    "organisationId": "org_destination",
    "submissionId": "sub_restored",
    "importId": "imp_123"
  },
  "importedAt": "2026-11-03T15:00:00Z",
  "mode": "native_restoration"
}


The destination must never replace the original receipt with a newly generated submission receipt.

Instead, it issues a separate signed import attestation.
7. Native restoration
Native restoration is not equivalent to creating a fresh submission through a public dropbox.

The restored record should have an explicit origin:
public enum SubmissionOrigin
{
    PublicUpload,
    ApiUpload,
    ImportedEvidence,
    DisasterRecovery
}


I'd introduce a dedicated restoration service.
public interface ISubmissionRestorationService
{
    Task<RestorationResult> RestoreAsync(
        RestorationRequest request,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record RestorationRequest(
    Guid ImportId,
    Guid DestinationOrganisationId,
    Guid RequestedBy,
    Guid ApprovedOperationId,
    string IdempotencyKey);

public sealed record RestorationResult(
    Guid DestinationSubmissionId,
    Guid ImportAttestationId,
    RestorationStatus Status);

public enum RestorationStatus
{
    Restoring,
    Reconciling,
    Completed,
    Failed
}


The service must never call the normal public-upload finalisation endpoint.

It should use a separate, auditable restoration pipeline.
Restored submission state
I'd add a restoration-specific state:
Restored — Pending Review


This is not an ordinary public workflow state.

The destination organisation can subsequently assign a compatible workflow through an explicit administrative action.

No automatic notifications, webhook deliveries, assignment rules or approval requests should fire merely because historical evidence was restored.
8. Workflow compatibility
The source organisation's workflow definition may not exist on the destination.

I'd preserve the original workflow history as evidence, where it was included in the export package, but not execute it.

The destination can choose:
Strategy	Behaviour
Archive only	No destination workflow
Assign default workflow	Explicit administrative activation
Map compatible workflow	Validate state and transition mapping
Keep pending	No processing until configuration is resolved

Workflow mapping must be versioned and reviewed.

An imported submission must not silently inherit a workflow whose semantics differ from the original.
Internal notes and approvals
The current .siep package specification primarily covers submission evidence.

It does not automatically include all internal notes, workflow approvals, escalation history or operational audit records.

Those would require a separately defined evidence-export extension.

The importer must not invent missing workflow history.
9. Key availability
Cryptographic integrity and decryptability are independent.

A package may verify perfectly while no current reviewer possesses the necessary private key.

I'd classify key availability as follows.
State	Meaning
recipient_key_known	Public recipient key is recognised
recipient_key_unknown	Recipient key is not registered locally
private_key_available	Authorised client has demonstrated usable private-key possession
rewrap_required	Destination reviewer lacks an applicable envelope
recovery_required	Organisation recovery process may be needed
unrecoverable	No usable decryption path is known

The server must not claim that a private key is available merely because its public key exists in the database.
Key possession verification
An authorised reviewer client may perform a cryptographic proof-of-possession challenge.

That can establish control of a corresponding private key, but it does not by itself prove that every file decrypts successfully.

For high-assurance restoration, the client can perform local authenticated decryption of a sample or the complete evidence set and report the result.

The server must not receive the plaintext.
10. Recipient reassignment after restoration
The existing v2.7 hybrid recipient model remains authoritative.

If destination reviewers lack access to the original envelopes:
1. Identify an authorised holder of an existing recipient private key.
2. Obtain the required approval.
3. Perform client-side DEK rewrapping.
4. Generate a new authenticated envelope-set version.
5. Record the relationship to the original envelope set.
6. Preserve the original receipt and evidence manifest unchanged.
The new envelope set is a post-import access modification, not a correction to the original submission.

If no authorised private key or recovery mechanism exists, the destination cannot recover the plaintext.
11. Disaster recovery scenarios
I'd distinguish three scenarios.

A. Same-deployment recovery

Restore database and object storage from coordinated backups, retaining deployment identity, key registry and tenant configuration.




B. New-deployment restoration

Import verified .siep packages into a newly provisioned deployment and preserve original provenance.




C. Selective evidence recovery

Restore individual submissions from archived packages without restoring the entire source database.



Important: .siep packages are not a complete replacement for database and configuration backups.

They preserve submission evidence, but may not contain account credentials, organisation settings, workflow configuration, operational queues, notification state or the complete audit history.

For full disaster recovery, the deployment still needs coordinated backups of its control plane, tenant databases, storage and signing-key metadata.
12. Recovery objectives
I'd make recovery objectives operator-configurable.

For example:
Objective	Illustrative target
RPO	15 minutes
RTO	4 hours
Evidence package verification	Before restoration
Key availability check	Before operational activation
Reconciliation	Required before completion

These are planning targets, not guarantees.

Actual recovery performance depends on backup frequency, storage volume, infrastructure and key-custody arrangements.

A deployment with lost signing keys may still verify historical receipts using trusted public keys, but cannot issue new receipts under the lost signing identity.
13. Import reconciliation
Reconciliation should verify both logical records and physical storage.
Required checks
Check	Expected result
Imported file count	Matches package manifest
Ciphertext object count	Matches expected storage representation
Ciphertext digests	Match original commitments
Envelope-set digest	Matches selected evidence version
Original receipt	Preserved byte-for-byte
Destination submission	Correct tenant and origin
Retention policy	Explicitly assigned
Legal hold	Preserved or explicitly mapped where applicable
Storage quota	Correctly accounted
Import attestation	Signed and persisted

Reconciliation must be repeatable.

I'd implement a durable worker that can resume after interruption.
Storage writes
Imported ciphertext should initially be written to staging storage.

After digest verification, objects are promoted or registered as immutable destination objects.

If promotion fails, the import remains incomplete and the staging objects are cleaned up through the existing durable cleanup mechanism.
14. Duplicate detection
A package may be imported more than once.

I'd detect duplicates using a combination of:
- Source issuer.
- Original submission identifier.
- Original revision.
- Original receipt digest.
- Package digest.
The package digest alone is insufficient because the same submission evidence may be packaged more than once with different export metadata.
Duplicate policy
I'd support:
reject
link_existing
create_separate_archive_copy


For native restoration, the default should be reject unless the operation is explicitly an idempotent retry of the same restoration.

A destination must not silently create multiple native submissions for the same original evidence.
15. Retention and legal holds
The destination must not blindly inherit source retention settings.

Retention requirements may differ between organisations or jurisdictions.

I'd require an explicit destination retention policy during import.

The importer should preserve source retention metadata as provenance but apply the destination's authorised policy.
Legal holds
If the package contains evidence of an active source legal hold, native restoration should require a policy decision.

It should not silently discard that information.

For a disaster recovery operation within the same legal organisation, the default should preserve equivalent holds.

For a transfer between different organisations, legal-hold handling requires explicit approval and documented responsibility.
16. Import permissions
I'd introduce:
evidence.import.create
evidence.import.verify
evidence.import.archive
evidence.import.restore
evidence.import.approve
evidence.import.manage_trust
evidence.import.audit


Native restoration should require elevated permission.

Managing trusted issuers should be a separate security-administration capability.

A user who can upload an evidence package must not automatically be able to trust its signing key.
Approval model
I'd use the existing sensitive-operation approval engine.

For native restoration, approval should bind to an immutable import plan containing:
- Package digest.
- Source issuer and organisation.
- Destination organisation.
- Import mode.
- Selected submission revisions.
- Retention policy.
- Key availability assessment.
- Intended workflow state.
Any material change invalidates the approval and requires reevaluation.
17. Import planning API
I'd make import a multi-step operation rather than a single POST that immediately restores records.
POST /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-imports

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-imports/{importId}

POST /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-imports/{importId}/verify

POST /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-imports/{importId}/plan

POST /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-imports/{importId}/approve

POST /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-imports/{importId}/execute

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/evidence-imports/{importId}/reconciliation


The execution endpoint must require a previously verified, approved and unchanged plan.

All state transitions should use optimistic concurrency and idempotency keys.
18. Import planning example
{
  "importId": "imp_123",
  "packageDigest": "sha256:...",
  "mode": "native_restoration",
  "destinationOrganisationId": "org_destination",
  "source": {

  },

    "retentionPolicyId": "ret_456",
    "preserveOriginalReceipt": true,
    "issueImportAttestation": true
  },
  "requiresApproval": true
}


The plan must be stored immutably once submitted for approval.
19. Database additions
I'd add:
evidence_import_jobs

I'd also extend submissions with:

These fields should be immutable once the restoration is completed, except through explicitly authorised correction workflows.
20. Audit events
I'd add:
evidence.import.created
evidence.import.package_uploaded
evidence.import.verification_started
evidence.import.verification_completed
evidence.import.verification_failed

evidence.import.plan_created

evidence.import.attestation_issued
evidence.import.trust_policy_changed


Audit records should include the source package digest, destination organisation, actor, approval reference, verification outcome and restoration identifiers.

They must not contain private keys or decrypted submission content.

Offline verification performed outside the deployment cannot be recorded by the server unless the verifier explicitly submits a report.
21. Operational UI
I'd provide a desktop-oriented import workflow for administrators.
Evidence Import

Illustrative administrative interface; no import has been performed.
I would restrict native restoration and trust-policy management to desktop administration, consistent with the existing high-risk admin UX policy.
22. Implementation priorities
I'd implement v3.7 in this order:
1. Quarantined package ingestion.
2. Shared offline/online verification library.
3. Trusted issuer policy and key-history validation.
4. Archive-only import.
5. Immutable import plans.
6. Approval-controlled native restoration.
7. Ciphertext staging and object reconciliation.
8. Signed destination import attestations.
9. Recipient-key availability assessment.
10. Disaster recovery runbooks and restoration testing.
The architectural boundary: Imported evidence can be verified and restored, but it must never acquire a new, misleading origin. The original receipt remains authoritative for the original submission; the destination attestation establishes what happened during import.

23. Next decision: disaster recovery and backup strategy
Controlled evidence restoration is now defined. The next decision is how deployments protect their operational databases, configuration, signing-key references and storage between evidence exports.

Operator-managed backups only

Secure Intake provides backup and restore commands, while operators schedule and store backups using their own infrastructure.


Integrated backup orchestration with external storage

Built-in scheduling, backup manifests, verification and restoration workflows, using operator-configured storage destinations and encryption keys.


Integrated backups with multi-region disaster recovery

Adds coordinated cross-region replication, standby deployment support, recovery drills and automated failover controls.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, disaster recovery and backups should use operator-managed backups only. Continue planning database and object-storage consistency, encrypted backups, signing-key custody, scheduling, restore validation, retention, RPO/RTO, recovery drills and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.8
Operator-managed backups · Database consistency · Object storage · Encryption · Signing keys · Recovery validation · Retention · RPO/RTO · Recovery drills

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will not operate a backup scheduler, backup-storage service or automatic disaster-recovery system.

Instead, the application will provide backup-aware operational controls, consistency checkpoints, restore validation and recovery commands. The deployment operator remains responsible for scheduling, encrypting, retaining, replicating and restoring backups.

This keeps the platform self-hostable without requiring a particular cloud provider, backup vendor or storage service.

The distinction is important:
- Secure Intake guarantees application-level consistency checks and recovery-safe behaviour within its documented boundaries.
- The operator is responsible for the backup infrastructure and its recoverability.
1. Backup architecture
Running Secure Intake deployment

Control plane · Tenant databases · Ciphertext storage · Key references · Audit


Application consistency checkpoint

Backup readiness · Object inventory · Schema versions · Checkpoint manifest


Operator-managed backup tools

Database snapshots/PITR · Object versioning · Encrypted copies · Key custody


Operator-controlled backup destination

Local/offsite storage · Immutable backups · Retention · Replication


Secure Intake recovery validation

Database/schema · Ciphertext inventory · Key registry · Audit continuity · Recovery report


Secure Intake must not assume that a database backup alone constitutes a complete recoverable deployment.
2. What must be backed up?
I'd classify deployment data into six categories.
Component	Backup responsibility	Recovery significance
Control-plane database	Operator	Organisations, routing, configuration, identity references
Tenant databases	Operator	Submissions, workflows, envelopes, receipts, audit
Ciphertext object storage	Operator	Encrypted files and form payloads
Deployment configuration	Operator	Storage profiles, auth settings, routing, integration settings
Signing keys and secret custody	Operator/key custodian	New receipt issuance, service operation, trust continuity
Trust registries and audit checkpoints	Operator	Historical verification and tamper detection

The backup must also preserve database migration history and application/protocol version information.
Private decryption keys
The platform generally does not possess reviewer private keys.

Therefore, backing up Secure Intake does not back up those keys.

Organisation-controlled key custody, portable .sik backups and external KMS/HSM recovery remain separate responsibilities.

A perfectly restored database and object store may still contain permanently undecryptable submissions if the corresponding private keys have been lost.
3. Database and object-storage consistency
This is the most important technical issue.

A submission may involve:
1. Database session creation.
2. Ciphertext object uploads.
3. Database confirmation of objects.
4. Submission finalisation.
5. Signed receipt issuance.
6. Audit and notification events.
Those operations are not necessarily contained in one distributed transaction.

If the database is restored to 12:00 and object storage to 11:55, the database may reference ciphertext that no longer exists.

Conversely, restoring storage to 12:05 may leave ciphertext objects that the restored database does not reference.

I'd design for both cases.
Consistency rules
- Finalised submissions reference immutable ciphertext objects.
- Object identifiers are never reused for different ciphertext.
- Object deletion is delayed until retention and reconciliation conditions are satisfied.
- Database state records expected object digests and lengths.
- Recovery validation compares database references with actual storage objects.
- Unreferenced objects are quarantined or reported, not automatically deleted during recovery.
- Missing referenced objects are treated as recovery failures, not silently ignored.
The preferred recovery relationship is that restored storage contains every object referenced by the restored database.

Extra unreferenced ciphertext can be reconciled. Missing referenced ciphertext may be unrecoverable without another backup.
4. Consistency checkpoints
I'd introduce an application-generated Recovery Consistency Manifest.

This is not a database or storage backup. It records the application state needed to assess a backup set.

Example:
{
  "format": "secure-intake-recovery-checkpoint",
  "version": 1,
  "checkpointId": "chk_01J...",
  "deploymentId": "dep_123",
  "createdAt": "2026-11-03T12:00:00Z",
  "applicationVersion": "3.8.0",
  "schemaVersions": {
    "controlPlane": 18,
    "tenantData": 27
  },
  "tenants": [
    {
      "organisationId": "org_123",
      "databaseProfileId": "dbprofile_1",
      "storageProfileId": "storage_1"
    }
  ],
  "auditCheckpointId": "audit_chk_456"
}


A checkpoint manifest should also record the required storage consistency boundary, database recovery positions and object inventory commitments where available.
Important limitation
A checkpoint created before a database backup does not automatically make a later database and object-storage backup consistent.

The operator must follow a documented sequence or use coordinated snapshots/PITR and storage-version retention.
5. Backup coordination modes
I'd support three operational modes.
Mode	Behaviour	Appropriate deployment
Quiesced backup	Temporarily stop writes, establish checkpoint, back up data	Small standalone deployments
Online coordinated backup	Continue operation with WAL/PITR and immutable object versions	Production PostgreSQL deployments
Storage-first protected backup	Ensure required object versions are preserved before restoring database state	Distributed deployments

Quiesced backup
For SQLite and local filesystem deployments, I'd recommend a maintenance-mode checkpoint.
Enter maintenance mode
        ↓
Stop new uploads and finalisation
        ↓
Drain or fence active writers
        ↓
Create database-consistent snapshot
        ↓
Capture matching object-store state
        ↓
Record recovery manifest
        ↓
Resume normal operation


The operator should use SQLite's supported backup mechanism or a coordinated filesystem snapshot, not simply copy an active database file.
Online coordinated backup
For PostgreSQL and S3-compatible storage:
- Use PostgreSQL backup/PITR tooling.
- Retain immutable ciphertext object versions.
- Preserve objects required by the selected database recovery point.
- Record database recovery positions and storage inventory boundaries.
- Validate the restored relationship before reopening the deployment.
The application can expose checkpoint information and recovery validation, but the operator remains responsible for making the backup copies.
6. Backup commands
I'd provide commands through the Secure Intake executable.
secureintake maintenance enter --reason "scheduled-backup"

secureintake backup checkpoint create \
  --output ./recovery-checkpoint.json

secureintake backup preflight \
  --checkpoint ./recovery-checkpoint.json

secureintake maintenance exit


These commands do not create database or object-storage backups.

They coordinate application state and produce verification metadata.
Restore validation
secureintake restore validate \
  --checkpoint ./recovery-checkpoint.json


For detailed object verification:
secureintake restore reconcile \
  --checkpoint ./recovery-checkpoint.json \
  --verify-objects


The commands should work with the deployment's configured database and storage providers.
7. Maintenance mode and fencing
Maintenance mode must be more than a UI flag.

I'd implement durable deployment-level write fencing.

During a quiesced backup:
- New public upload sessions are rejected.
- Existing sessions are paused or allowed to drain according to policy.
- Submission finalisation is blocked after the fence.
- Background workers stop mutating protected state.
- Scheduled cleanup and retention deletion are suspended.
- Administrative writes are blocked except approved maintenance operations.
For multi-node deployments, the fence must be enforced through shared authoritative state.

A node that loses contact with the authoritative fence state must fail closed for protected writes.
Presigned upload URLs
Already issued storage upload URLs may remain usable until expiry.

The backup procedure must account for that.

For strict quiescence, wait for issued URLs to expire or use provider-supported mechanisms to prevent further staging writes.

Newly uploaded but unfinalised ciphertext must not be treated as committed submission evidence.
8. Encrypted backups
The operator should encrypt all backups independently of the application's E2EE file encryption.

Why?

Because the databases contain information that is not end-to-end encrypted, including:
- Organisation configuration.
- Server-readable form fields.
- Contact information.
- Submission metadata.
- Workflow records.
- Audit data.
- Authentication and integration references.
Even though file payloads are ciphertext, database backups still contain sensitive information.
Recommended encryption model
Use authenticated backup encryption with keys controlled by the operator.

Support external KMS/HSM, age-compatible tools or established backup solutions as appropriate.

Secure Intake should document supported restore procedures without mandating one backup encryption vendor.

The application must not store backup decryption passwords inside the same backup set.
9. Signing-key custody
Receipt signing keys and audit checkpoint signing keys need explicit recovery plans.

I'd classify keys as follows.
Key type	Recovery requirement
Receipt signing private key	Needed to continue signing under the same identity
Receipt signing public key	Needed for historical verification
Audit checkpoint private key	Needed for continued checkpoint signing under that identity
Audit checkpoint public key	Needed for historical chain verification
Deployment secret keys	Needed for service continuity and decrypting server-managed secrets
Reviewer private keys	Managed outside platform backup

Key-loss scenarios
If a receipt signing private key is lost, the deployment may activate a new signing key and continue operating.

Historical receipts remain verifiable if their public keys and trust history survive.

If the private key is suspected compromised, rotation alone is insufficient; the key must be marked compromised and historical trust assessed.

If a server-managed secret-encryption key is lost, some stored integration credentials or encrypted metadata may become inaccessible.

The restore preflight should identify missing key dependencies before production activation.
10. Backup scheduling
Scheduling remains entirely operator-managed.

I'd document example strategies rather than embed a scheduler.
Illustrative PostgreSQL production strategy
Component	Example frequency
PostgreSQL base backup	Daily
WAL archiving	Continuous
Object storage replication/backup	Continuous or frequent
Configuration backup	On change and daily
Signing-key metadata	On change
Recovery checkpoint	Aligned with backup process
Restore drill	Quarterly

These are example operating targets, not application defaults or guarantees.

The operator may use cron, systemd timers, Kubernetes CronJobs, cloud backup services or external backup platforms.
Example shell wrapper
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

CHECKPOINT_DIR="/var/backups/secureintake/checkpoints"
mkdir -p "$CHECKPOINT_DIR"

TIMESTAMP="$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ)"
CHECKPOINT="$CHECKPOINT_DIR/$TIMESTAMP.json"

secureintake backup checkpoint create \
  --output "$CHECKPOINT"

secureintake backup preflight \
  --checkpoint "$CHECKPOINT"

# Invoke the operator's database and storage backup tools here.
# The operator must coordinate the actual consistency boundary.


This is a checkpoint/preflight skeleton, not a complete backup procedure. A quiesced backup must additionally establish and release the write fence safely; an online backup must use a documented PITR/object-version protocol.
11. Restore validation
A restored deployment should start in recovery mode, not immediately serve public traffic.

I'd define:
Restored
   ↓
Recovery validation
   ↓
Reconciliation
   ↓
Security review
   ↓
Operational approval
   ↓
Active


Validation checks
Area	Required validation
Database	Integrity and schema compatibility
Tenant routing	Correct organisation/database mapping
Storage	Referenced objects exist
Ciphertext	Digests match committed manifests
Signing keys	Required keys and trust history available
Receipts	Signatures remain verifiable
Audit	Chain and checkpoint continuity
Retention	Holds and deletion state preserved
Jobs	No unsafe duplicate processing
Webhooks	Delivery replay policy reviewed
Identity	Sessions and credentials handled safely
Configuration	Secrets, domains and endpoints validated

The deployment should remain fenced until mandatory validation passes or an authorised recovery exception is recorded.
12. Job and outbox recovery
Restoring a database can cause previously completed operations to reappear as pending.

For example, a webhook might have been delivered after the backup point but before the outage.

After restore, the database may show it as undelivered.

The existing at-least-once delivery architecture must account for this.
Recovery behaviour
- Reset stale worker leases.
- Reconcile incomplete operations.
- Preserve idempotency keys.
- Avoid regenerating conflicting signed receipts.
- Recheck external side effects before replay where possible.
- Require explicit policy for high-impact webhook replay.
- Record recovery-originated retries.
Exactly-once delivery to external systems cannot be guaranteed.

Consumers should use stable event identifiers for deduplication.
13. Recovery point objective (RPO)
RPO is the maximum acceptable amount of data loss measured in time.

For example, an RPO of 15 minutes means the recovery design aims to lose no more than 15 minutes of committed changes.

The application cannot guarantee that objective without suitable operator-managed backups.
RPO considerations
- Database WAL archive frequency.
- Object storage replication lag.
- Backup completion time.
- Key/configuration backup freshness.
- External identity-provider availability.
- Storage version retention.
A database recovery point is only useful if all referenced ciphertext objects are recoverable.
14. Recovery time objective (RTO)
RTO is the target time to restore service.

It depends on:
- Database restore duration.
- Object-storage restoration.
- Secret and key availability.
- Infrastructure provisioning.
- Tenant routing restoration.
- Reconciliation time.
- Security validation.
- Operational approval.
I'd make RPO/RTO documentation part of deployment configuration, but not pretend the application enforces those targets.

Example:
{
  "recoveryObjectives": {
    "rpoMinutes": 15,
    "rtoMinutes": 240
  }
}


These are declared operator targets used for reporting and validation, not automatic recovery guarantees.
15. Retention and immutable backups
Backup retention is operator-controlled.

I'd document separate policies for:
Backup class	Purpose
Short-term	Operational recovery
Medium-term	Accidental deletion and corruption recovery
Long-term	Compliance and archival requirements
Immutable/offsite	Ransomware and administrative compromise resilience

The operator should protect backup storage from the credentials used by the production application.

A compromised Secure Intake runtime should not automatically have permission to delete or overwrite all backup copies.
Retention caveat
Deleted submission data may remain in backups until the relevant backup expires.

The platform must document that limitation and avoid claiming immediate physical erasure across all backup media.
16. Recovery drills
I'd require documented restore testing as part of the production deployment guidance.

A recovery drill should verify more than whether PostgreSQL starts.
Drill checklist
Recovery drill checklist
0/12



[ ] Restore control-plane database
[ ] Restore tenant database
[ ] Restore ciphertext objects
[ ] Validate tenant routing
[ ] Verify sample signed receipts
[ ] Reconcile ciphertext digests
[ ] Validate signing-key trust history
[ ] Check legal holds and retention
[ ] Review pending jobs and webhook replay
[ ] Confirm reviewer decryption from an authorised client
[ ] Record achieved RPO and RTO
[ ] Approve recovery report
 Copy checklist
A drill report should record the actual recovery point, elapsed restoration time, verification failures and unresolved risks.
17. Recovery audit events
I'd add:
recovery.checkpoint.created
recovery.checkpoint.validated

recovery.maintenance.entered
recovery.maintenance.exited

recovery.validation.started
recovery.validation.completed
recovery.validation.failed

recovery.reconciliation.started
recovery.reconciliation.completed
recovery.reconciliation.failed

recovery.activation.requested
recovery.activation.approved
recovery.activation.completed

recovery.drill.started
recovery.drill.completed


Secure Intake cannot directly audit external backup-tool operations unless the operator explicitly supplies their results.

For example, the application may record that a recovery checkpoint was created, but it must not claim that an offsite backup succeeded without external evidence.
Audit continuity after restoration
Restoring an older database may remove newer audit records from the live database.

Externally stored signed audit checkpoints can reveal a rollback or discontinuity.

The recovery process should preserve and compare those checkpoints, then record a new recovery epoch rather than silently continuing the previous audit chain as though no rollback occurred.
18. Recovery epoch
I'd introduce a deployment recovery epoch.
deployment_id
recovery_epoch
recovered_from_checkpoint
activated_at
approved_by


Every successful disaster recovery activation increments the epoch.

New audit records, operational events and receipt-signing metadata should identify the current epoch where relevant.

This helps distinguish operations performed before and after a rollback or recovery.

It also supports forensic analysis when external systems have observed events that the restored database no longer contains.
19. Database additions
I'd keep the schema minimal because backup scheduling is external.
recovery_checkpoints
recovery_checkpoint_tenants
recovery_checkpoint_storage_profiles

deployment_recovery_epochs
recovery_validation_runs
recovery_validation_findings

recovery_reconciliation_runs
recovery_reconciliation_findings

recovery_activation_approvals
recovery_drill_reports


These records are for consistency, validation and audit—not backup orchestration.

The operator's backup catalog remains outside Secure Intake.
20. Operational API
I'd expose administrative endpoints only where useful.
POST /api/platform/recovery/checkpoints
GET  /api/platform/recovery/checkpoints/{id}

POST /api/platform/recovery/validate
GET  /api/platform/recovery/validations/{id}

POST /api/platform/recovery/reconcile
GET  /api/platform/recovery/reconciliations/{id}

POST /api/platform/recovery/activate


Recovery activation should require elevated permission and fresh MFA, with independent approval where configured.

For severe recovery scenarios where the normal authentication service is unavailable, a separate operator-controlled break-glass CLI procedure may be required.

That procedure must not bypass audit creation or allow unrestricted plaintext access.
21. Implementation priorities
I'd implement v3.8 in this order:
1. Immutable ciphertext and object-reference recovery guarantees.
2. Recovery consistency checkpoint format.
3. Maintenance-mode write fencing.
4. SQLite/local filesystem backup guidance.
5. PostgreSQL PITR/object-storage consistency guidance.
6. Restore validation CLI.
7. Ciphertext reconciliation.
8. Signing-key and trust-history validation.
9. Recovery epochs and audit continuity.
10. Recovery drill tooling and reporting.
The important boundary is that Secure Intake makes backups recoverable and verifiable through application-aware tooling, but does not schedule, store, replicate or manage the backups themselves.

22. Next decision: deployment configuration and secrets
With backup ownership established, the next architectural area is how operators configure standalone and distributed deployments, particularly database connections, storage credentials, signing keys and external integrations.

Configuration files and environment variables

Simple self-hosted operation using validated configuration files and environment variables, with secrets supplied externally.


Layered configuration with pluggable secret providers

Validated configuration files, environment overrides and optional external secret providers, including local encrypted secrets and cloud KMS integrations.


Centralised configuration control plane

Adds remotely managed deployment settings, configuration rollouts, secret-provider policies and multi-node configuration coordination.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, deployment configuration should use layered configuration with pluggable secret providers. Continue planning configuration precedence, secret resolution, validation, rotation, deployment profiles, Native AOT compatibility, environment isolation, CLI tooling and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v3.9
Layered configuration · Pluggable secrets · Validation · Rotation · Deployment profiles · .NET 10 Native AOT · Environment isolation · CLI · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will use layered configuration with pluggable secret providers. Operators can run the platform using local configuration and environment variables, or integrate external secret stores without changing application code.

The system remains independently deployable, with no mandatory cloud provider, configuration server or external secrets service.

I'd separate configuration into three categories:
- Deployment configuration: Infrastructure, endpoints, database connections, storage profiles, authentication and service behaviour.
- Secret references: Credentials and cryptographic material resolved through approved providers.
- Runtime organisation configuration: Tenant policies, dropboxes, workflows and other application settings stored in the database.
These must have different ownership, validation and audit rules.
1. Configuration architecture
Configuration files

Base · Environment · Deployment


Environment / CLI

Explicit operator overrides




Configuration composition and validation

Known schema · Precedence · Deployment profile · Cross-field constraints


Secret reference resolver

Environment · File · Encrypted local store · External provider


Validated immutable runtime snapshot

API · Workers · Signing · Storage · Authentication


No service component should read arbitrary configuration files or environment variables directly after startup. It should consume validated, typed configuration or narrowly scoped secret handles.
2. Configuration precedence
I'd define a deterministic precedence order.

Highest priority first
Priority	Source	Purpose
1	Explicit CLI overrides	One-off operational commands
2	SECUREINTAKE__ environment variables	Deployment overrides
3	Deployment-specific configuration	Host/container settings
4	Environment-specific configuration	Production, development, etc.
5	Base configuration	Shared defaults
6	Built-in defaults	Safe documented defaults

Higher-priority sources override lower-priority values only where the setting is explicitly overridable.

A production security requirement cannot be weakened merely by supplying a higher-priority environment variable.
Example files
config/
├── appsettings.json
├── appsettings.Development.json
├── appsettings.Production.json
└── deployment.json


The exact environment must be selected explicitly by the operator.

Production deployments must not silently fall back to development settings.
Example override
export SECUREINTAKE__Storage__DefaultProfile="primary-s3"
export SECUREINTAKE__Api__PublicBaseUrl="https://intake.example.com"


These values should be parsed into typed settings rather than read throughout the codebase using Environment.GetEnvironmentVariable.
3. Secret references
Secrets should be represented by references rather than embedded values.

Example:
{
  "database": {
    "provider": "postgresql",
    "connectionString": "secret://env/SECUREINTAKE_DB_CONNECTION"
  },
  "storage": {
    "provider": "s3",
    "accessKeyId": "secret://env/SECUREINTAKE_S3_ACCESS_KEY",
    "secretAccessKey": "secret://env/SECUREINTAKE_S3_SECRET_KEY"
  },
  "receiptSigning": {
    "provider": "external",
    "keyReference": "key://azure/secure-intake-receipts"
  }
}


I'd distinguish two reference types:
- secret:// resolves confidential bytes or strings.
- key:// references a cryptographic operation provider that may never expose private key material.
A KMS signing key should be used through a signing interface, not retrieved as raw private-key bytes.
4. Secret providers
I'd implement a small provider abstraction.
Provider	Use case	Initial release
Environment	Containers and simple deployments	Yes
Protected file	Docker secrets, mounted files, systemd credentials	Yes
Local encrypted store	Standalone deployments	Yes
Azure Key Vault	Azure-hosted deployments	Optional module
AWS Secrets Manager/KMS	AWS-hosted deployments	Optional module
HashiCorp Vault	Independent deployments	Optional module
PKCS#11/HSM	Advanced signing/key custody	Later

The core platform must not require any of these external providers.
Interface
public interface ISecretProvider
{
    string Scheme { get; }

    ValueTask<SecretValue> ResolveAsync(
        SecretReference reference,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record SecretReference(
    string Scheme,
    string Identifier,
    string? Version);

public sealed class SecretValue : IDisposable
{
    private byte[]? _bytes;

    public SecretValue(byte[] bytes) => _bytes = bytes;

    public ReadOnlyMemory<byte> Value =>
        _bytes ?? throw new ObjectDisposedException(nameof(SecretValue));

    public void Dispose()
    {
        if (_bytes is null)
            return;

        CryptographicOperations.ZeroMemory(_bytes);
        _bytes = null;
    }
}


This limits accidental secret lifetime, although managed-runtime copies, provider SDKs and operating-system memory behaviour mean zeroisation cannot be guaranteed universally.

The implementation should avoid unnecessary string conversion for binary secrets.
5. Native AOT compatibility
Because Secure Intake targets .NET 10 Native AOT, provider discovery must not depend on runtime assembly scanning or arbitrary dynamic loading.

I'd use explicit compile-time registration.
var builder = WebApplication.CreateSlimBuilder(args);

builder.Services.AddSecureIntakeConfiguration();

builder.Services.AddEnvironmentSecretProvider();
builder.Services.AddFileSecretProvider();

#if AZURE_SECRETS
builder.Services.AddAzureKeyVaultSecretProvider();
#endif

#if HASHICORP_VAULT
builder.Services.AddVaultSecretProvider();
#endif

var app = builder.Build();

app.MapSecureIntakeEndpoints();

app.Run();


Provider modules are included at build time, not downloaded and dynamically loaded into the running Native AOT binary.

This preserves trimming compatibility and makes the supported provider set auditable.
JSON source generation
[JsonSerializable(typeof(SecureIntakeConfiguration))]
[JsonSerializable(typeof(DeploymentConfiguration))]
[JsonSerializable(typeof(StorageConfiguration))]
[JsonSerializable(typeof(SecretReference))]
internal partial class ConfigurationJsonContext : JsonSerializerContext;


Configuration parsing, validation and provider SDK dependencies must be covered by Native AOT publish tests.
6. Configuration schema
I'd use strongly typed configuration objects.
public sealed record SecureIntakeConfiguration
{
    public required DeploymentConfiguration Deployment { get; init; }
    public required ApiConfiguration Api { get; init; }
    public required DatabaseConfiguration Database { get; init; }
    public required StorageConfiguration Storage { get; init; }
    public required SecurityConfiguration Security { get; init; }
}

public sealed record DeploymentConfiguration
{
    public required string DeploymentId { get; init; }
    public required DeploymentProfile Profile { get; init; }
    public required string Environment { get; init; }
}

public enum DeploymentProfile
{
    Development,
    Production,
    Hardened
}


The configuration validator should reject unknown security-sensitive properties rather than silently ignoring them.

For backwards compatibility, deprecated properties should generate explicit warnings and have defined removal versions.
7. Deployment profiles
I'd retain the three deployment profiles established earlier.
Profile	Purpose	Behaviour
Development	Local testing	Explicitly relaxed development-only controls
Production	Normal deployment	Secure defaults, strict validation
Hardened	High-assurance deployment	Stronger key custody, audit and access requirements

Profiles are policy baselines, not substitutes for individual configuration.
Production requirements
At minimum:
- HTTPS on public origins.
- No development credentials.
- No default signing keys.
- Explicit trusted hosts.
- Approved secret providers.
- No plaintext secrets in configuration files.
- Strict CORS/CSP settings.
- Valid database and storage connectivity.
- Working receipt-signing capability.
- Configured audit checkpoint signing.
- No insecure authentication fallback.
Hardened additions
I'd require configurable controls such as external signing-key custody, stronger administrative MFA, restricted export operations and explicit security approval for high-risk configuration changes.

The hardened profile must fail startup when required controls are absent.
8. Environment isolation
I'd make deployment identity explicit and immutable after initial provisioning.
{
  "deployment": {
    "deploymentId": "dep_prod_01",
    "environment": "Production",
    "profile": "Production"
  }
}


Production and UAT must use separate:
- Databases.
- Storage buckets or prefixes with independent credentials.
- Signing keys.
- Authentication client registrations.
- Webhook credentials.
- Email sender configuration.
- Secret-provider namespaces.
- Audit checkpoint identities.
A UAT deployment must not accidentally send production notifications or access production ciphertext.
Environment binding
I'd bind important resources to the deployment identity.

For example, database metadata should record its expected deployment ID.

At startup, Secure Intake compares configured deployment identity with the database's recorded identity.

A mismatch blocks startup unless an authorised restoration or migration procedure explicitly permits it.

This prevents accidentally connecting a development instance to a production database.
9. Configuration validation
I'd perform validation in three stages.
Stage 1 — Structural validation
Checks required properties, types, enum values, URI syntax, duplicate identifiers and unsupported configuration keys.
Stage 2 — Security validation
Checks TLS requirements, allowed algorithms, secret-reference schemes, signing-key policy, host routing, authentication settings and deployment-profile constraints.
Stage 3 — Operational preflight
Checks database connectivity, storage permissions, secret resolution, signing operations and external service availability where required.

I'd separate static validation from live preflight so operators can validate configuration before infrastructure is fully available.
Example output
Secure Intake configuration validation

[PASS] Configuration schema
[PASS] Deployment identity
[PASS] Production security profile
[PASS] Database configuration
[PASS] Storage profile definitions
[PASS] Secret references
[PASS] Receipt signing configuration
[WARN] SMTP connectivity not tested
[PASS] Audit checkpoint signing

Result: VALID WITH WARNINGS


The output must never print secret values.
10. Configuration CLI
I'd provide:
secureintake config validate
secureintake config show --redacted
secureintake config explain Database.ConnectionString
secureintake config diff --against ./previous-config.json

secureintake secrets test
secureintake secrets providers

secureintake preflight


config explain
This command should show where a setting came from.

Example:
Setting: Storage.DefaultProfile
Value: primary-s3
Source: Environment variable
Variable: SECUREINTAKE__Storage__DefaultProfile
Effective profile: Production
Restart required: No


For secrets:
Setting: Database.ConnectionString
Value: [REDACTED]
Source: deployment.json
Reference: secret://env/SECUREINTAKE_DB_CONNECTION
Resolved: Yes


The command should never reveal the resolved connection string.
11. Secret rotation
Rotation must be supported without requiring manual database edits.

I'd classify secrets by rotation behaviour.
Secret	Rotation approach
Database credentials	Provider-supported credential rollover, reconnect
S3/R2 credentials	Overlapping credentials, then revoke old
SMTP credentials	Replace and reconnect
Webhook signing secrets	Versioned overlap and controlled retirement
Receipt signing keys	Formal key lifecycle, historical verification retained
Audit signing keys	Versioned signing epochs and trust continuity
Server-side encryption keys	Explicit key-version and re-encryption workflow

Not every secret can be hot-reloaded safely.
Rotation state machine
Prepared
   ↓
Activated
   ↓
Overlap
   ↓
Old credential retired
   ↓
Completed


Where a provider supports only immediate replacement, the rotation workflow must document the availability impact.
12. Runtime configuration reload
I'd divide configuration into three classes.
Class	Example	Reload behaviour
Dynamic	Rate limits, notification defaults	Validated reload
Reconnectable	SMTP or storage credentials	Controlled client replacement
Restart-required	Database provider, deployment identity, cryptographic protocol baseline	Restart

A configuration reload must be atomic from the application's perspective.

I'd use immutable configuration snapshots.

Each active request or job should observe one coherent configuration generation, not a mixture of old and new settings.
Generation tracking
public sealed record RuntimeConfigurationSnapshot(
    long Generation,
    DateTimeOffset ActivatedAt,
    SecureIntakeConfiguration Configuration);


Secret versions should be tracked separately from ordinary configuration generation where appropriate.

A long-running export job must not unexpectedly switch storage profiles or signing-key identity midway through execution.
13. Secret resolution failure
I'd distinguish between startup-critical and runtime-optional secrets.
Startup-critical
Examples:
- Primary database credential.
- Required receipt-signing provider.
- Required audit-signing provider.
- Required storage credentials.
Failure should block startup or prevent the relevant service role from becoming ready.
Runtime-optional
Examples:
- Optional email provider.
- Disabled external integration.
- Nonessential webhook destination credentials.
Failure may disable the affected feature and produce a health warning.

The system must not silently fall back to insecure defaults.
14. Local encrypted secret store
For standalone deployments, I'd provide an optional encrypted local secret store.
secureintake secrets init --provider local
secureintake secrets set Database.ConnectionString
secureintake secrets list
secureintake secrets rotate Database.ConnectionString


The store should use established authenticated encryption and a versioned format.

The master key must come from outside the encrypted store, such as an operator-managed file, environment-injected secret or OS-backed key facility.

Encrypting secrets with a key stored beside them with identical access controls offers limited protection.
Backup implications
The operator must back up:
- Encrypted secret-store data.
- Required master-key recovery material.
- Secret-version metadata.
- Provider configuration.
Losing the master key can make the store unrecoverable.
15. Configuration drift
For distributed deployments, different nodes must not silently run incompatible security configurations.

I'd record a non-secret configuration fingerprint for each node.
node_id
deployment_id
configuration_generation
configuration_fingerprint
application_version
activated_at


The fingerprint should be derived from a canonical, redacted representation that excludes raw secrets and other sensitive values. Secret versions may be represented by opaque version identifiers.
Compatibility checks
Nodes must agree on critical settings such as:
- Deployment identity.
- Database routing.
- Cryptographic protocol policy.
- Trusted hosts.
- Receipt-signing policy.
- Audit checkpoint configuration.
- Supported API and storage protocol versions.
Noncritical settings may differ by service role.

For example, an API node and a worker node may have different listening ports.
16. Multi-node configuration coordination
Because the selected architecture does not include a centralised configuration control plane, I'd use deployment tooling to distribute configuration.

Examples include:
- Docker Compose environment files and secrets.
- Kubernetes ConfigMaps and Secrets.
- systemd credentials.
- Ansible or other configuration-management tools.
- Cloud deployment pipelines.
Secure Intake should detect incompatible node configurations and fail readiness checks rather than attempt to become its own configuration orchestration platform.

This keeps the application modular and avoids duplicating infrastructure tooling.
17. Audit requirements
Configuration changes should be auditable when they occur through application-controlled operations.

I'd add:
configuration.validated
configuration.activated
configuration.reload_requested
configuration.reload_completed
configuration.reload_failed

secret.reference.changed
secret.rotation.started
secret.rotation.completed
secret.rotation.failed

deployment.profile.changed
deployment.identity_mismatch_detected
deployment.configuration_drift_detected


Audit records should include:
- Actor or deployment automation identity.
- Configuration generation.
- Changed setting paths.
- Old and new non-secret fingerprints.
- Validation result.
- Activation time.
- Affected service roles.
They must not contain secret values.
External changes
If an operator edits a file or rotates a credential directly in an external vault, Secure Intake may not know who performed that action.

The application can audit when it detects and activates the change.

The external configuration or secret provider remains responsible for auditing the original administrative action.
18. Database additions
I'd avoid storing deployment configuration in the application database unnecessarily.

Only operational metadata needs persistence.
deployment_configuration_generations
deployment_node_configuration_states
deployment_configuration_validation_runs

deployment_secret_rotation_events
deployment_configuration_audit_events


Organisation-level runtime settings remain in the existing tenant configuration tables.

The database must not become a hidden second source of truth for deployment infrastructure settings.
19. Operational UI
I'd provide a read-only deployment configuration view for authorised platform administrators.
Deployment Configuration
Valid

Environment

Production


Profile

Production


Configuration generation

42


Secret providers

3 configured




Database credentials
Resolved

Storage credentials
Resolved

Receipt signing key
Available

Node configuration consistency
Matched

Illustrative read-only administrative view. No secret values are exposed.
High-risk configuration changes should remain operator-controlled through deployment tooling and the CLI, rather than being editable through an ordinary web administration page.
20. Implementation priorities
I'd implement v3.9 in this order:
1. Typed configuration schema and source-generated serialisation.
2. Deterministic configuration precedence.
3. Secret reference parser and provider abstraction.
4. Environment and protected-file providers.
5. Production/hardened validation policies.
6. Configuration CLI and preflight.
7. Local encrypted secret store.
8. Configuration generation and drift detection.
9. Secret rotation and controlled reload.
10. Optional external secret-provider modules.
The core design rule is that configuration defines how Secure Intake operates, secret providers supply confidential material, and the application validates both without becoming responsible for managing the operator's infrastructure.

21. Next decision: deployment packaging and updates
With configuration and secret management defined, the next architectural area is how Secure Intake is packaged, upgraded and rolled back across standalone and distributed deployments.

Container-first distribution

OCI images and Docker Compose as the primary distribution, with Kubernetes manifests for distributed deployments.


Native binaries and containers

Native AOT binaries for Linux, macOS and Windows, plus OCI images, Compose examples and Kubernetes deployment guidance. Operator-controlled updates.


Native binaries and containers with managed update tooling

Adds built-in update orchestration, staged rollout controls, automated compatibility checks and application-managed rollback workflows.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, deployment packaging should use native AOT binaries and containers with operator-controlled updates. Continue planning release artifacts, platform targets, versioning, database migrations, rolling upgrades, rollback constraints, supply-chain signing, SBOMs and deployment verification.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v4.0
Native AOT binaries · OCI containers · Operator-controlled upgrades · Schema migrations · Rolling deployments · Rollback · Supply-chain integrity

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will ship as native executables and OCI container images. Operators control installation, upgrades, rollback and deployment orchestration.

The application will provide release verification, migration tooling, compatibility checks and deployment health reporting, but will not automatically download or install updates.

This applies to standalone deployments and distributed API/worker deployments.
1. Distribution architecture
I'd use one versioned release containing multiple deployment artifacts built from the same source revision.
Tagged source release

Pinned dependencies · Reproducible build inputs · Release manifest


CI build and verification

Native AOT · Unit/integration tests · Protocol compatibility · Security checks


Native executables

Linux · Windows · macOS


OCI images

API · Worker · Combined host




Signed release manifest and SBOM

Artifact digests · Provenance · Build metadata · Dependency inventory


Operator verification and deployment

Verify · Preflight · Migrate · Deploy · Validate


All artifacts for a release must advertise the same application version and compatible protocol/schema ranges.
2. Platform targets
I'd establish the following initial target matrix.
Platform	Architecture	Artifact
Linux	x64	Native binary + OCI
Linux	ARM64	Native binary + OCI
Windows	x64	Native executable
Windows	ARM64	Later, subject to CI validation
macOS	Apple Silicon ARM64	Native binary
macOS	Intel x64	Native binary

The exact support matrix should be based on successful automated Native AOT builds and tests, not merely on the existence of a .NET runtime identifier.

Linux is the recommended production server platform.

Windows and macOS binaries remain useful for self-hosted installations, development and administration.
Native AOT publish
Example:
dotnet publish src/SecureIntake.Host \
  -c Release \
  -r linux-x64 \
  -p:PublishAot=true \
  -p:SelfContained=true \
  -p:StripSymbols=true \
  -o ./artifacts/linux-x64


Native AOT cross-compilation is not universally supported. The release pipeline should build on appropriate target-compatible runners with the required native toolchains.

The build must treat trimming and AOT warnings as release-blocking unless an exception is explicitly reviewed.
3. Executable design
I'd retain a single host executable with explicit service roles.
secureintake serve --role all
secureintake serve --role api
secureintake serve --role worker

secureintake config validate
secureintake preflight

secureintake database status
secureintake database migrate

secureintake restore validate
secureintake evidence verify


For standalone installations:
secureintake serve --role all


For distributed deployments:
secureintake serve --role api
secureintake serve --role worker


The roles share domain and infrastructure code, but activate only the services required for that process.

The release should also include a separately runnable offline verifier, because verification must not require production credentials or database connectivity.
4. Release artifacts
I'd publish a consistent artifact set.
secureintake-4.0.0/
├── binaries/
│   ├── secureintake-linux-x64.tar.gz
│   ├── secureintake-linux-arm64.tar.gz
│   ├── secureintake-win-x64.zip
│   ├── secureintake-osx-arm64.tar.gz
│   └── secureintake-osx-x64.tar.gz
│
├── containers/
│   └── image-digests.json
│
├── deployment/
│   ├── compose.yaml
│   ├── compose.production.example.yaml
│   └── kubernetes/
│
├── schemas/
│   ├── configuration.schema.json
│   └── release-manifest.schema.json
│
├── security/
│   ├── sbom.spdx.json
│   ├── provenance.intoto.jsonl
│   └── release-manifest.sig
│
├── release-manifest.json
├── checksums.sha256
└── RELEASE-NOTES.md


These are proposed artifact names, not files that have already been generated.

The release manifest should identify the exact artifact digest for every supported platform.
5. OCI image strategy
I'd publish a multi-architecture OCI image for Linux x64 and ARM64.

Example image references:
registry.example.com/secureintake:4.0.0
registry.example.com/secureintake:4.0
registry.example.com/secureintake@sha256:...


For production deployments, I'd recommend pinning by immutable digest.

Version tags may be provided for convenience, but must never be treated as cryptographic evidence of image identity.
Container layout
I'd use a minimal runtime image containing:
- Native AOT executable.
- Required native libraries.
- Trusted CA certificates.
- Static web assets.
- Version metadata.
Run as a non-root user with a read-only root filesystem where practical.

Writable directories should be explicit mounts for temporary files, local storage and operational state.

No compiler, SDK or shell is required in the production runtime image unless a documented operational need justifies it.
6. Versioning
I'd use Semantic Versioning for the application release, but maintain separate compatibility versions for persistent formats and protocols.
Version	Purpose
Application version	Release compatibility and change management
Database schema version	Persistent storage compatibility
Public API contract version	Client compatibility
Encryption protocol version	Ciphertext interoperability
Receipt format version	Signed receipt verification
.siep format version	Portable evidence package compatibility
Configuration schema version	Operator configuration compatibility
Workflow/form schema versions	Tenant configuration compatibility

An application release must not automatically imply a new encryption protocol or evidence-package format.
Example version report
{
  "applicationVersion": "4.0.0",
  "buildCommit": "abcdef123456",
  "databaseSchema": {
    "controlPlane": 21,
    "tenantData": 31
  },
  "protocols": {
    "api": "2026-11",
    "encryption": [1],
    "receipt": [1],
    "evidencePackage": [1]
  },
  "configurationSchema": 4
}


The API remains free of mandatory /v1 URL prefixes.

Protocol compatibility can be negotiated through documented headers, content types or capability metadata where necessary.
7. Compatibility manifest
Each release should publish a machine-readable compatibility manifest.

For example:
{
  "release": "4.0.0",
  "database": {
    "minimumSupportedSchema": 29,
    "maximumSupportedSchema": 31,
    "targetSchema": 31
  },
  "rollingUpgrade": {
    "supportedFrom": ["3.9.2"],
    "mixedVersionMinutes": 30
  },
  "rollback": {
    "binaryRollbackSupportedTo": ["3.9.2"],
    "databaseRollbackSupported": false
  }
}


These values are illustrative. The actual compatibility ranges must be generated from tested release metadata.

I'd make the application refuse to start against a database schema outside its declared compatibility range.
8. Database migration model
Database migrations should be:
- Versioned.
- Ordered.
- Checksummed.
- Idempotent where practical.
- Protected by a migration lock.
- Explicitly executed by the operator.
- Tested against supported database providers.
- Audited.
I'd continue using built-in migration tooling rather than requiring an external migration framework at runtime.
Migration commands
secureintake database status

secureintake database migrate --plan

secureintake database migrate --apply

secureintake database verify


--plan should show exactly which migrations would run.

--apply should require confirmation or an explicit noninteractive operator flag.

Production API and worker processes must not automatically apply schema migrations on startup.
9. Expand-and-contract migrations
Rolling upgrades require backward-compatible schema changes.

I'd adopt expand-and-contract as the standard approach.
Phase A — Expand
Compatible

Add new tables, nullable columns and compatible indexes. Old and new application versions can continue operating.

Phase B — Deploy

Roll out the new application while preserving compatibility with the previous supported version.

Phase C — Backfill

Migrate existing data through resumable, bounded jobs where required.

Phase D — Contract

Remove obsolete fields only in a later release after the old application version is no longer supported.
