A migration that renames or drops a column required by the currently running application cannot be considered rolling-upgrade compatible.
SQLite consideration
SQLite standalone deployments should generally use maintenance-mode upgrades.

Even if an individual migration is transactional, the complete application upgrade may not support simultaneous old and new processes.
10. Migration failure handling
A migration failure must not leave the application silently running with a partially upgraded schema.

I'd record:
migration_id
migration_checksum
started_at
completed_at
status
application_release
operator_identity


Where the database supports transactional DDL, migrations should use transactions.

For operations that cannot be performed transactionally, the migration must define resumable steps and explicit failure recovery.

A failed migration should block deployment readiness until the schema is reconciled.
11. Rolling upgrades
For distributed PostgreSQL deployments, I'd support rolling upgrades only when the compatibility manifest explicitly permits them.
Recommended sequence
1

Verify release

Check signatures, digests, compatibility and configuration



2

Preflight

Confirm backup readiness, schema and storage health



3

Expand schema

Apply backward-compatible migrations



4

Upgrade workers

Drain old workers and activate compatible new workers



5

Upgrade API nodes

Replace nodes gradually behind the load balancer



6

Validate

Check health, receipts, uploads, queues and audit



7

Complete

Record release activation and enable new capabilities



This sequence is conditional on tested API/worker compatibility. Some releases may require worker-first, API-first or full maintenance-mode deployment.

The release manifest must specify the supported order.
12. Worker compatibility
Durable jobs can survive application upgrades.

Therefore, job payloads must be versioned.

Example:
{
  "jobType": "receipt.sign",
  "payloadVersion": 2,
  "payload": {
    "submissionId": "sub_123",
    "receiptId": "rcpt_456"
  }
}


New workers must be able to process supported older payload versions.

Old workers must not claim jobs using a payload version they cannot understand.

I'd include a job compatibility predicate in the leasing query or job dispatcher.
Worker draining
Before replacement:
1. Stop claiming new jobs.
2. Allow active jobs to finish within a configured grace period.
3. Release or expire unfinished leases safely.
4. Start replacement workers.
5. Resume processing.
Jobs must remain idempotent and recoverable after process termination.
13. API compatibility
The browser portal, reviewer clients and CLI may be updated independently of the API.

I'd publish an API capability document.
GET /.well-known/secure-intake/capabilities


Example:
{
  "applicationVersion": "4.0.0",
  "apiContract": "2026-11",
  "supportedEncryptionProtocols": [1],
  "supportedReceiptFormats": [1],
  "supportedEvidencePackages": [1],
  "minimumReviewerClient": "3.8.0"
}


Clients should reject unsupported cryptographic protocol combinations rather than silently downgrade.

The portal's static assets should use content-hashed filenames and immutable caching, while the HTML entry point and bootstrap configuration should be revalidated.

This avoids unnecessarily downloading unchanged assets on every deployment.
14. Rollback constraints
Rollback must be treated as a compatibility operation, not simply replacing an executable.

I'd distinguish:
Rollback type	Supported when
Binary rollback	Previous binary supports current schema and protocol state
Container rollback	Previous image supports current schema and persisted jobs
Configuration rollback	Previous configuration remains valid and secure
Database rollback	Explicitly supported migration path or restored backup
Cryptographic protocol rollback	Only if no unsupported ciphertext/protocol state has been created
Full recovery	Operator restores a coordinated backup set

Database rollback should not be assumed safe.

Once new data has been written using a newer schema, reverting the database schema may lose or corrupt information.
Default rollback policy
I'd support binary rollback within an explicitly tested compatibility window.

For incompatible schema changes, require a forward-fix or operator-managed recovery from coordinated backups.
15. Irreversible operations
Some upgrades may introduce changes that cannot be safely reversed.

Examples:
- New encryption protocol.
- New receipt signature profile.
- New evidence package format.
- New persistent job payload.
- Destructive database migration.
- Key rotation with retirement of old signing capability.
The upgrade preflight must identify irreversible steps.

Example:
Upgrade: 3.9.2 -> 4.0.0

[PASS] Configuration compatible
[PASS] Database migration plan valid
[PASS] Storage profiles compatible
[PASS] Signing keys available
[WARN] New job payload version introduced
[WARN] Binary rollback window limited
[PASS] No destructive schema migrations

Result: UPGRADE PERMITTED


The operator should be required to acknowledge explicitly identified irreversible changes.
16. Supply-chain signing
I'd sign release artifacts and publish verifiable build provenance.

Recommended approach:
- OCI image signatures.
- Signed release manifest.
- Cryptographic checksums for binaries and archives.
- Build provenance attestations.
- Published signing-key identity and rotation policy.
Sigstore-compatible signing is a reasonable implementation choice, provided the project also supports offline verification using pinned trust material.

A signature is only meaningful when the operator trusts the signing identity independently.
Release verification
secureintake release verify \
  --manifest release-manifest.json \
  --signature release-manifest.sig \
  --trusted-key release-public-key.pem


For a native binary:
secureintake release verify \
  --artifact secureintake-linux-x64.tar.gz \
  --manifest release-manifest.json \
  --signature release-manifest.sig \
  --trusted-key release-public-key.pem


The verifier should validate the manifest signature, artifact digest and declared release identity.

A compromised download server must not be able to substitute both an artifact and an untrusted signing key without detection.
17. Software Bill of Materials
Each release should include an SBOM.

I'd support SPDX JSON as the primary format, with CycloneDX as an optional additional output.

The SBOM should cover:
- .NET packages.
- Native libraries.
- Cryptographic dependencies.
- Container base components.
- Bundled frontend dependencies.
- Build-time tools where relevant.
The SBOM should identify exact component versions and dependency relationships.
Vulnerability reporting
I'd publish a security advisory process and support mapping advisories to affected releases.

Operators should be able to determine whether their installed version contains a vulnerable dependency without installing a new release.
18. Build provenance
I'd record:
source_repository
source_commit
release_tag
build_workflow
build_runner_identity
toolchain_versions
dependency_lock_digest
artifact_digest
build_timestamp


Build provenance should be signed or otherwise cryptographically attested.

Reproducible builds are desirable, but byte-for-byte reproducibility must be demonstrated rather than assumed.

Native AOT builds may vary across toolchains, platforms and native linkers unless the build environment is tightly controlled.
19. Deployment verification
After an operator deploys a release, Secure Intake should provide a deployment validation command.
secureintake deployment verify


I'd check:
Area	Validation
Application	Expected release version
Database	Supported schema version
Configuration	Valid and consistent
Storage	Required access and object integrity checks
Signing	Receipt and audit signing operational
Workers	Compatible job processing
API	Health and protocol capability
Portal	Static asset manifest matches release
Audit	Checkpoint chain operational
Cluster	Nodes run compatible versions

The command should produce a structured report and nonzero exit code on critical failures.
20. Health and readiness
I'd separate liveness, readiness and operational health.
GET /health/live
GET /health/ready
GET /health/status


/health/live answers whether the process is alive.

/health/ready answers whether it can safely receive its assigned traffic or work.

/health/status provides detailed authorised diagnostics.

Sensitive health information must not be exposed publicly.
During upgrades
An API node should fail readiness when:
- Its database schema is incompatible.
- Required signing capability is unavailable.
- Its configuration is invalid.
- A deployment fence prevents public operations.
- Its release is outside the supported cluster compatibility window.
A worker should fail readiness if it cannot safely claim jobs.
21. Deployment activation record
I'd introduce a deployment activation record.
{
  "deploymentId": "dep_prod_01",
  "release": "4.0.0",
  "artifactDigest": "sha256:...",
  "configurationGeneration": 42,
  "databaseSchema": 31,
  "activatedAt": "2026-11-03T16:00:00Z",
  "activatedBy": "operator_123",
  "verificationStatus": "passed"
}


This provides an auditable history of which release was active and when.

It should not be confused with cryptographic proof that every running node was updated; that requires node-level reporting and reconciliation.
22. Database additions
I'd keep deployment metadata small.
deployment_release_activations
deployment_release_nodes
deployment_upgrade_plans

database_migration_history
database_migration_execution_steps

deployment_verification_runs
deployment_verification_findings

deployment_rollback_events


The application should not become a package manager or deployment orchestrator.

These records exist for compatibility, safety and audit.
23. Audit events
I'd add:
release.verified
release.verification_failed

deployment.upgrade.planned
deployment.upgrade.started
deployment.upgrade.completed
deployment.upgrade.failed

database.migration.started
database.migration.completed
database.migration.failed

deployment.rollback.started
deployment.rollback.completed
deployment.rollback.failed

deployment.verification.completed
deployment.verification.failed


Events should include the release digest, schema versions, configuration generation, operator identity and result.

They must not include deployment secrets.
24. Operator update procedure
I'd document a standard update process.
# 1. Verify the downloaded release.
secureintake release verify \
  --artifact secureintake-linux-x64.tar.gz \
  --manifest release-manifest.json \
  --signature release-manifest.sig \
  --trusted-key release-public-key.pem

# 2. Validate the existing deployment.
secureintake preflight

# 3. Review migration requirements.
secureintake database migrate --plan

# 4. Operator creates and verifies coordinated backups.
#    Backup tooling remains external to Secure Intake.

# 5. Apply supported migrations.
secureintake database migrate --apply

# 6. Operator deploys the new binary or container image.

# 7. Validate the deployed release.
secureintake deployment verify


For standalone SQLite installations, this should normally run under maintenance mode with a controlled stop/start.

For distributed PostgreSQL installations, use the release-specific rolling-upgrade plan.
25. Implementation priorities
I'd implement v4.0 in this order:
1. Release manifest and compatibility schema.
2. Native AOT build matrix.
3. Multi-architecture OCI images.
4. Signed release artifacts and checksums.
5. SBOM and build provenance generation.
6. Explicit database migration CLI.
7. Expand-and-contract migration policy.
8. Versioned worker payloads and compatibility checks.
9. Deployment verification and readiness.
10. Upgrade and rollback documentation.
The core principle: Secure Intake supplies verifiable, compatible release artifacts and safe upgrade tooling. Operators remain responsible for deciding when and how those artifacts are deployed.

26. Next decision: operational monitoring and diagnostics
With packaging and upgrades defined, the next area is how operators observe system health, background jobs, storage, security events and performance without exposing confidential submission content.

Built-in health and structured logs

JSON logs, health endpoints, operational metrics and CLI diagnostics without external monitoring dependencies.


OpenTelemetry-first observability

Structured logs, metrics and traces with OpenTelemetry export, privacy controls, health checks and optional integration with Prometheus, Grafana and other monitoring platforms.


Integrated operations dashboard

Adds a built-in administrative monitoring dashboard for jobs, storage, queues, security events and service health, alongside OpenTelemetry export.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, operational monitoring should use an integrated operations dashboard alongside OpenTelemetry. Continue planning instrumentation, telemetry privacy, health checks, job monitoring, security alerts, performance metrics, retention, dashboards and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v4.1
Integrated operations dashboard · OpenTelemetry · Health checks · Background jobs · Security alerts · Performance · Retention · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will include a built-in operations dashboard alongside OpenTelemetry-based observability.

The dashboard will work in standalone deployments without requiring Grafana, Prometheus, Elasticsearch or another monitoring platform. Operators can additionally export telemetry to their preferred monitoring infrastructure.

The architecture should distinguish three types of operational information:
- Telemetry: Logs, metrics and traces describing system behaviour.
- Operational state: Durable jobs, storage health, queue backlogs, service status and security findings.
- Audit evidence: Authoritative records of security-sensitive actions, configuration changes and submission lifecycle events.
These have different reliability, privacy and retention requirements.

The dashboard must not become a second audit system, and telemetry must never expose encrypted submission content or private keys.
1. Observability architecture
API nodes

Requests · Authentication · Uploads · Errors


Workers

Jobs · Receipts · Exports · Cleanup




Instrumentation and privacy enforcement

OpenTelemetry API · Typed event attributes · Redaction · Cardinality limits


Built-in operations

Database state · Aggregates · Alerts · Diagnostics


OTel exporters

OTLP · Metrics · Logs · Traces




Independent audit subsystem

Durable transactional records · Hash chain · Signed checkpoints


The built-in dashboard should read durable operational state and bounded aggregates. It should not depend on collecting every trace or retaining every application log.
2. Deployment modes
Capability	Standalone	Distributed
Built-in dashboard	Yes	Yes
Structured JSON logs	Yes	Yes
OpenTelemetry metrics	Yes	Yes
Distributed traces	Optional	Yes
External OTLP collector	Optional	Optional
Durable job monitoring	SQLite	PostgreSQL
Alert evaluation	Combined host	Leased worker
Operational history	Bounded local storage	Bounded shared storage
Audit	Existing durable audit system	Existing durable audit system

The dashboard must remain usable when external telemetry exporters are unavailable.

Conversely, the API and workers should continue operating if the dashboard UI is disabled.
3. Instrumentation
I'd use standard .NET instrumentation through System.Diagnostics.ActivitySource, System.Diagnostics.Metrics and OpenTelemetry.
Instrumentation namespaces
SecureIntake.Api
SecureIntake.Authentication
SecureIntake.Uploads
SecureIntake.Storage
SecureIntake.Cryptography
SecureIntake.Submissions
SecureIntake.Receipts
SecureIntake.Evidence
SecureIntake.Workflows
SecureIntake.Notifications
SecureIntake.Workers
SecureIntake.Security
SecureIntake.Recovery


Each namespace has documented metrics, activity names and permitted attributes.
.NET 10 example
using System.Diagnostics;
using System.Diagnostics.Metrics;

public static class IntakeTelemetry
{
    public const string Name = "SecureIntake.Submissions";

    public static readonly ActivitySource Activities = new(Name);

    private static readonly Meter Meter = new(Name);

    public static readonly Counter<long> SubmissionsReceived =
        Meter.CreateCounter<long>(
            "secureintake.submissions.received",
            unit: "{submission}");

    public static readonly Histogram<double> FinalisationDuration =
        Meter.CreateHistogram<double>(
            "secureintake.submissions.finalisation.duration",
            unit: "s");

    public static readonly Counter<long> FinalisationFailures =
        Meter.CreateCounter<long>(
            "secureintake.submissions.finalisation.failures",
            unit: "{failure}");
}


Usage:
using var activity =
    IntakeTelemetry.Activities.StartActivity("submission.finalise");

var started = Stopwatch.GetTimestamp();

try
{
    await submissionService.FinaliseAsync(
        submissionId,
        cancellationToken);

    IntakeTelemetry.SubmissionsReceived.Add(1);
}
catch (SubmissionFinalisationException ex)
{
    IntakeTelemetry.FinalisationFailures.Add(
        1,
        new KeyValuePair<string, object?>(
            "reason", ex.ReasonCode));

    throw;
}
finally
{
    IntakeTelemetry.FinalisationDuration.Record(
        Stopwatch.GetElapsedTime(started).TotalSeconds);
}


The exception reason should be a bounded, predefined code—not an arbitrary exception message.
4. Telemetry privacy policy
I'd implement a strict telemetry attribute allowlist.
Data	Metrics	Logs	Traces
Operation name	Yes	Yes	Yes
Status/error category	Yes	Yes	Yes
Duration and byte counts	Yes	Yes	Yes
Service/node identifier	Yes	Yes	Yes
Organisation identifier	No by default	Restricted	Restricted
Submission identifier	No	Restricted	Restricted
Uploader email/IP	No	Security-only when necessary	No
Original filenames	No	No	No
Form answers	No	No	No
Ciphertext or plaintext	No	No	No
Access tokens/private keys	No	No	No

The rule applies equally to built-in telemetry and external exporters.

I'd prohibit automatic request-body, response-body and authentication-header capture.

Query strings must be stripped or sanitised because they can contain credentials or sensitive parameters.
Cardinality controls
Metrics must use bounded labels such as:
operation
result
provider_type
service_role
error_category
deployment_profile


Do not use submission IDs, user IDs, email addresses, storage object keys or arbitrary URLs as metric labels.

That prevents excessive metric cardinality and reduces privacy exposure.
5. Integrated operations dashboard
The dashboard should be available through the existing administrative web interface, with separate platform and organisation scopes.

The platform dashboard shows deployment-wide health.

An organisation dashboard shows only that organisation's permitted operational information.

I'd avoid exposing platform infrastructure topology to ordinary organisation administrators.
Dashboard overview
Operations Overview
Illustrative production deployment

Healthy

API availability
99.98%
Last 24 hours

Pending jobs
18
Across all workers

Submission finalisation
420 ms
p95 latency

Storage integrity
100%
Of objects checked



Service status

4 components

API nodes
Healthy

Background workers
Healthy

Object storage
Healthy

Notification delivery
Degraded

Example values only. No live deployment data is connected.
The dashboard should make the scope of each measurement explicit. For example, “100% of objects checked” is not the same as proving every object in storage is intact.
6. Dashboard sections
I'd use six primary operational areas.
Section	Contents
Overview	Health, traffic, failures, latency, alerts
Jobs & Queues	Backlog, retries, leases, dead-letter jobs
Storage	Connectivity, quotas, integrity, orphaned objects
Security	Authentication failures, suspicious activity, key issues
Integrations	SMTP, webhooks, OIDC, secret providers
Diagnostics	Node status, versions, configuration drift, recovery

The audit interface remains separate but can be linked from relevant operational events.
Mobile access
Read-only overview and basic alert acknowledgement can be mobile-enabled.

Job replay, security policy changes, key operations, recovery activation and other high-impact actions should remain desktop-only.
7. Health checks
I'd retain the v4.0 endpoints:
GET /health/live
GET /health/ready
GET /health/status


Liveness
/health/live answers whether the process is running.

It should not fail merely because an external SMTP service is unavailable.
Readiness
/health/ready answers whether the node can safely perform its assigned role.

An API node should fail readiness when it cannot access its authoritative database or when its schema/configuration is incompatible.

A worker should fail readiness when it cannot safely claim and process its supported jobs.
Detailed health
/health/status should require appropriate authorisation.

Example:
{
  "status": "degraded",
  "components": {
    "database": "healthy",
    "storage": "healthy",
    "receiptSigning": "healthy",
    "notifications": "degraded"
  },
  "configurationGeneration": 42,
  "applicationVersion": "4.1.0"
}


Health probes should use bounded timeouts and avoid expensive full scans.
8. Job and queue monitoring
The existing durable worker system is an authoritative source for job state.

I'd expose:
- Pending jobs by type.
- Oldest pending job age.
- Active leases.
- Expired leases.
- Retry counts.
- Dead-letter jobs.
- Processing duration.
- Success/failure rates.
- Worker compatibility issues.
- Jobs blocked by configuration or unavailable keys.
Job monitoring example
Background Jobs
Job type	Pending	Failed	Oldest
Receipt signing	2	0	12s
Webhook delivery	9	3	8m
Retention cleanup	5	0	2m
Evidence export	2	0	35s

Illustrative operational data.
A failed job must not automatically imply that a submission was lost.

The UI should distinguish transient failures, terminal failures and jobs awaiting operator intervention.
9. Job administration
I'd allow authorised operators to inspect and manage jobs without direct database access.

Supported operations:
Action	Behaviour
Inspect	View status, attempts, safe error details and dependencies
Retry	Requeue a retryable failed job
Pause type	Stop claiming a particular job category
Resume type	Resume eligible processing
Cancel	Request cancellation where supported
Release lease	Recover an abandoned lease after fencing checks
Dead-letter	Quarantine a permanently failing job
Reconcile	Compare durable job state with the underlying operation

Job replay is potentially dangerous.

For example, replaying a webhook may produce a duplicate external notification. Replaying a receipt-signing operation must not generate a conflicting receipt.

I'd require each job handler to declare its replay policy:
public enum JobReplayPolicy
{
    Idempotent,
    ReconcileBeforeReplay,
    ApprovalRequired,
    NotReplayable
}


Operators must not be able to bypass those rules through the dashboard.
10. Security alerts
I'd distinguish security alerts from ordinary operational warnings.
Initial security alert catalogue
Alert	Default severity
Receipt-signing key compromised	Critical
Audit-chain checkpoint mismatch	Critical
Cross-tenant isolation violation detected	Critical
Ciphertext digest mismatch	Critical
Recovery/deployment identity mismatch	Critical
Unauthorised administrative access attempt	High
Repeated privileged authentication failures	High
Unexpected signing-key rotation	High
Suspicious bulk evidence exports	High
Secret-provider authentication failure	High
Repeated uploader verification failures	Medium
Abnormal upload activity	Medium
Configuration drift	Medium
Elevated API error rate	Warning

These are default classifications; alert severity should be based on verified signals rather than arbitrary thresholds alone.

An alert about suspicious behaviour is not proof of malicious activity.
Alert lifecycle
Detected
   ↓
Open
   ↓
Acknowledged
   ↓
Investigating
   ↓
Resolved
   ↓
Closed


I'd also support suppressed and false_positive outcomes with explicit reasons.

Acknowledging an alert does not resolve the underlying condition.
Notification channels
Use the existing notification infrastructure for operator alerts, with separate routing from uploader notifications.

Initially support:
- Administrative dashboard.
- Email.
- Signed outgoing webhooks.
- OpenTelemetry export.
No mandatory PagerDuty, Slack or other third-party integration.
11. Alert rule engine
I'd implement a constrained rule engine rather than arbitrary scripts.

Example:
{
  "ruleId": "receipt-signing-backlog",
  "enabled": true,
  "signal": "receipt.signing.oldest_pending_seconds",
  "condition": {
    "operator": "greater_than",
    "value": 300
  },
  "evaluationWindowSeconds": 120,
  "severity": "high",
  "cooldownSeconds": 900
}


Rules should support threshold, sustained-condition, absence-of-signal and explicit security-event matching.

The evaluator must have:
- Bounded rule complexity.
- Deduplication.
- Cooldowns.
- Recovery detection.
- Durable alert state.
- Suppression windows for approved maintenance.
- Protection against notification loops.
For distributed deployments, only one worker should hold the active evaluation lease for a given rule scope.

A missing evaluator heartbeat should itself become an operational warning.
12. Performance metrics
I'd instrument the following initial metrics.
Area	Metrics
API	Requests/sec, error rate, p50/p95/p99 latency
Uploads	Active sessions, bytes/sec, failures, resumptions
Finalisation	Duration, verification failures, pending receipts
Storage	Operation latency, failures, quota usage
Database	Query latency, connection-pool saturation, transaction failures
Workers	Throughput, backlog age, retries, lease expiry
Signing	Signing latency, failures, provider availability
Evidence exports	Throughput, package size, verification failures
Authentication	Failure categories, challenge latency
Notifications	Queue age, delivery attempts, provider acceptance

For encrypted uploads, report ciphertext throughput separately from logical plaintext size if both are available and safe to expose.

Do not claim that provider acceptance means an email was delivered to the recipient.
Example time-series dashboard
API request latency
Illustrative p50 and p95 latency over a 60-minute window; milliseconds.


p50p95

0 ms85 ms170 ms255 ms340 ms10:0010:1010:2010:3010:4010:5011:00














Latency measurements should be tagged by bounded operation categories, not individual user or submission identifiers.
13. OpenTelemetry configuration
I'd use OpenTelemetry SDK registration with explicit instrumentation and exporter configuration.
using OpenTelemetry.Metrics;
using OpenTelemetry.Resources;
using OpenTelemetry.Trace;

builder.Services.AddOpenTelemetry()
    .ConfigureResource(resource => resource
        .AddService(
            serviceName: "secureintake-api",
            serviceVersion: releaseVersion))
    .WithMetrics(metrics => metrics
        .AddMeter(
            "SecureIntake.Api",
            "SecureIntake.Submissions",
            "SecureIntake.Workers")
        .AddAspNetCoreInstrumentation()
        .AddRuntimeInstrumentation()
        .AddOtlpExporter())
    .WithTracing(tracing => tracing
        .AddSource(
            "SecureIntake.Api",
            "SecureIntake.Submissions",
            "SecureIntake.Workers")
        .AddAspNetCoreInstrumentation()
        .AddHttpClientInstrumentation()
        .AddOtlpExporter());


The relevant OpenTelemetry packages and their dependencies must pass the Native AOT publish/test matrix. Unsupported instrumentation should be excluded or replaced with explicit instrumentation.
Example configuration
{
  "observability": {
    "enabled": true,
    "tracing": {
      "enabled": true,
      "samplingRatio": 0.05
    },
    "metrics": {
      "enabled": true
    },
    "otlp": {
      "enabled": false,
      "endpoint": "http://otel-collector:4317"
    }
  }
}


A disabled exporter should not cause startup failure.

When an exporter is enabled but unavailable, telemetry should use bounded buffering and controlled dropping rather than exhausting application memory or blocking submission processing.
14. Built-in operational history
The dashboard needs historical trends without requiring an external metrics database.

I'd implement bounded aggregation.

For example:
Resolution	Suggested retention
1 minute	24 hours
5 minutes	7 days
1 hour	90 days
1 day	12 months

These are configurable defaults, not compliance retention requirements.

Aggregates should include counts, sums, durations and histogram distributions where supported.

Averages alone are insufficient for useful latency analysis.
Storage model
operational_metric_aggregates
operational_health_snapshots
operational_alert_rules
operational_alert_instances
operational_alert_transitions
operational_node_heartbeats
operational_diagnostic_runs


For standalone SQLite deployments, the aggregation process should have strict storage and retention limits.

For PostgreSQL deployments, use partitioning or bounded deletion where operationally justified.

Do not store raw traces indefinitely in the application database.
15. Telemetry retention
I'd separate retention by data category.
Category	Owner	Default approach
Application logs	Operator	Configurable external log retention
Distributed traces	Operator	Short-lived, sampled
Built-in metric aggregates	Secure Intake	Bounded retention
Alert history	Secure Intake	Operational retention policy
Security findings	Secure Intake	Security retention policy
Audit records	Secure Intake	Independent audit retention policy

Deleting telemetry must not delete authoritative audit evidence.

Conversely, retaining an audit event does not require retaining its original trace.
16. Diagnostic bundles
I'd provide an operator command to create a sanitised support bundle.
secureintake diagnostics collect \
  --output ./secureintake-diagnostics.zip


It may include:
- Application and schema versions.
- Configuration fingerprints.
- Redacted configuration summary.
- Health-check results.
- Worker queue aggregates.
- Storage connectivity results.
- Recent safe error categories.
- Migration history.
- Relevant non-sensitive logs.
It must exclude credentials, private keys, authentication tokens, uploaded content, confidential form values and unrestricted database dumps.

Diagnostic bundles should require an explicit scope and be reviewed before external sharing.

I'd support:
secureintake diagnostics inspect \
  --bundle ./secureintake-diagnostics.zip


This allows operators to see exactly what the bundle contains.
17. Dashboard access control
The operations dashboard should use the existing platform and organisation RBAC model.

I'd introduce permissions such as:
operations.dashboard.view
operations.health.view
operations.jobs.view
operations.jobs.manage
operations.alerts.view
operations.alerts.acknowledge
operations.alerts.manage
operations.diagnostics.export
operations.security.view


Platform operators may see deployment-wide metrics.

Organisation administrators see only their organisation's permitted information.

An organisation administrator should not be able to infer another tenant's activity from global submission counts, storage metrics or security findings.

For shared infrastructure, I'd avoid exposing highly granular cross-tenant resource usage.
18. Operational APIs
GET  /api/platform/operations/overview
GET  /api/platform/operations/metrics
GET  /api/platform/operations/nodes

GET  /api/platform/operations/jobs
GET  /api/platform/operations/jobs/{id}
POST /api/platform/operations/jobs/{id}/retry

GET  /api/platform/operations/alerts
GET  /api/platform/operations/alerts/{id}
POST /api/platform/operations/alerts/{id}/acknowledge

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/operations/overview
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/operations/alerts


Metric queries should enforce bounded date ranges, result limits and authorised scopes.

The API should not expose arbitrary SQL, raw telemetry queries or unrestricted trace search.
19. Operational audit
I'd add audit events for actions taken through the dashboard:
operations.alert.acknowledged
operations.alert.resolved
operations.alert.suppressed

operations.job.retry_requested
operations.job.cancel_requested
operations.job.lease_released

operations.alert_rule.created
operations.alert_rule.updated
operations.alert_rule.disabled

operations.diagnostics.generated
operations.diagnostics.downloaded


Routine metric collection and ordinary health probes should not create audit events on every invocation.

Security-sensitive administrative actions must generate durable audit records using the existing transactional audit/outbox architecture.

Telemetry failures must not be allowed to silently disable mandatory security auditing.
20. Alerting and failure isolation
Observability must not become a new availability dependency.

I'd enforce the following:
- External OTLP outages cannot block API requests.
- Full telemetry buffers drop telemetry according to policy, not submission data.
- Dashboard query failures do not interrupt background workers.
- Metric aggregation jobs run at lower priority than submission processing.
- Security alert state is persisted independently of email/webhook delivery.
- Mandatory audit persistence follows the existing fail-closed policy for security-sensitive transactions.
- A failure of the alert evaluator is visible through a separate heartbeat/health signal.
For security-critical alerts, the application should record the finding durably before attempting notification delivery.
21. Implementation priorities
I'd deliver v4.1 in this order:
1. Typed instrumentation catalogue and telemetry privacy policy.
2. OpenTelemetry metrics and traces.
3. Structured JSON logging and sanitisation.
4. Durable health snapshots and node heartbeats.
5. Bounded operational metric aggregation.
6. Integrated overview dashboard.
7. Job and queue monitoring.
8. Security alert catalogue and rule evaluator.
9. Controlled job administration.
10. Diagnostic bundles, retention and operational audit.
The main architectural distinction is that the integrated dashboard provides operational visibility, OpenTelemetry provides external observability, and the audit subsystem remains the authoritative record of security-sensitive activity.

22. Next decision: administrative access and privileged operations
The dashboard introduces privileged actions such as job replay, alert suppression and diagnostics export. We should now define how administrative access is secured across standalone and distributed deployments.

Role-based access with MFA

Existing platform and organisation roles, privileged MFA, session management and permission checks.


Role-based access with just-in-time elevation

Adds temporary privileged sessions, fresh MFA, scoped elevation, approval for sensitive operations and detailed audit.


External privileged-access integration

Adds integration with external PAM systems, privileged session policies and enterprise access governance.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, administrative access should use role-based access with just-in-time privilege elevation and approval-controlled sensitive operations. Continue planning privileged sessions, MFA, elevation grants, approval enforcement, emergency access, session expiry, API tokens, admin UI restrictions and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v4.2
Privileged access · Just-in-time elevation · MFA · Independent approvals · Emergency access · API security · Administrative UI · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will use role-based access control (RBAC), just-in-time (JIT) privilege elevation and independent approval for sensitive operations.

This builds on the existing platform and organisation roles without introducing a mandatory external privileged-access management system.

The central rule is:

An administrator's permanent role establishes what they may request. A temporary elevation grant establishes what they may perform. An operation-specific approval establishes whether a sensitive action may proceed.

These are three separate authorisation decisions.
1. Privileged access architecture
Authenticated administrator

OIDC or built-in identity · Base roles · Session


Request temporary elevation

Requested permission · Organisation/resource scope · Reason · Duration


Fresh MFA and eligibility checks

Base role · Device/session assurance · Risk policy · Revocation


JIT grant

Temporary scoped permission


Independent approval

Required for sensitive operations




Authorisation at execution

Role + grant + approval + current policy + immutable target


Execute and audit

Durable decision · Fenced execution · Outcome · Signed audit checkpoint


A JIT grant does not automatically approve an operation. An approval does not automatically grant a user permission to execute it.
2. Permanent roles versus elevated permissions
I'd retain the existing role model.
Platform roles
Role	Base responsibility
Owner	Deployment governance and ownership
Admin	Platform administration
Security Admin	Security policies, key governance and privileged access
Operator	Service operations and diagnostics
Auditor	Read-only audit and compliance review

Organisation roles
Role	Base responsibility
Owner	Organisation governance
Admin	Organisation administration
Dropbox Manager	Dropbox and form configuration
Reviewer	Submission review
Security Officer	Security and sensitive-operation approval
Auditor	Read-only evidence and audit access
API Client	Scoped machine operations

The role system should support multiple memberships, but permissions must be evaluated within the correct scope.

A platform Operator does not automatically gain permission to decrypt organisation submissions.

A platform Owner does not gain cryptographic access to E2EE content.
Permission classification
I'd divide permissions into three levels.
Level	Example	Requirement
Standard	View dashboard, manage assigned submissions	Base role
Elevated	Retry jobs, export diagnostics, change integrations	Base role + JIT grant
Sensitive	Change trust anchors, bulk export, activate recovery	Base role + JIT + independent approval

Some sensitive operations may additionally require two or more approvers.
3. Just-in-time elevation grants
A grant should be narrow, temporary and revocable.

Example:
{
  "grantId": "elev_01J...",
  "principalId": "usr_123",
  "organisationId": "org_456",
  "permissions": [
    "operations.jobs.manage"
  ],
  "resourceScope": {
    "type": "job_category",
    "id": "webhook.delivery"
  },
  "reason": "Investigating failed webhook deliveries",
  "issuedAt": "2026-11-03T08:00:00Z",
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-03T08:30:00Z",
  "status": "active"
}


The grant should never contain wildcard access to every organisation unless the operation genuinely requires deployment-wide scope and policy explicitly permits it.
Recommended durations
Elevation	Suggested maximum
Operational job management	30 minutes
Integration configuration	15 minutes
Sensitive evidence export	10 minutes
Security configuration	10 minutes
Recovery activation	Single operation
Break-glass access	15 minutes, renewable only under policy

These are proposed configurable ceilings.

A grant must not be extended silently because an administrator remains active in the UI.
4. Elevation lifecycle
Requested
    ↓
Eligibility checked
    ↓
MFA required
    ↓
Active
    ↓
Expired / Revoked / Consumed


For some permission categories, the elevation request itself may require approval.

I'd support:
- automatic_after_mfa for low-risk operational elevation.
- approval_required for higher-risk elevation.
- operation_bound for one specific sensitive action.
An elevation request must include a reason. The reason is recorded for audit, but free-text reasons must never be used as an authorisation rule.
5. MFA requirements
I'd prioritise phishing-resistant authentication for privileged access.
Authentication methods
Method	Privileged use
WebAuthn/FIDO2 security key	Preferred
Platform passkey	Preferred where assurance policy permits
OIDC with verified MFA assurance	Supported
TOTP	Fallback where allowed
Email OTP	Not sufficient for high-risk elevation
SMS OTP	Not sufficient for high-risk elevation

For OIDC, the platform must not assume MFA occurred merely because authentication succeeded.

It should evaluate trusted issuer configuration, authentication context (acr), authentication methods (amr) where reliable, and authentication time (auth_time) where available.
Fresh authentication
I'd require recent MFA for elevation.

Example policy:
{
  "privilegedAuthentication": {
    "maxAuthenticationAgeSeconds": 300,
    "requirePhishingResistantMfa": true,
    "allowTotpFallback": false
  }
}


The authentication timestamp must come from a trusted authentication event, not a client-supplied value.
6. Privileged sessions
I'd separate the normal login session from the privileged elevation context.

A user can remain signed in after their elevation expires.
Session structure
Normal session
  ├── Identity
  ├── Base roles
  ├── Organisation memberships
  └── Normal expiry

Elevation context
  ├── Grant ID
  ├── Permission scope
  ├── MFA assurance
  ├── Issued time
  ├── Expiry
  └── Revocation state


For browser clients, I'd prefer a server-side privileged session reference bound to the authenticated session.

Do not store a long-lived privileged bearer token in localStorage.

Use secure, HttpOnly, SameSite cookies where applicable, with CSRF protection on state-changing requests.
Session invalidation
Elevation must be revoked when:
- The user signs out.
- The base session is revoked.
- The account is suspended.
- Relevant role membership is removed.
- MFA assurance is invalidated.
- The grant expires.
- The grant is explicitly revoked.
- The deployment enters a security state requiring privilege reset.
Revocation must be checked against authoritative state for sensitive operations.
7. Approval-controlled operations
I'd extend the existing approval engine from v2.8 rather than creating a second approval system.

Each sensitive operation should create an immutable operation request.

Example:
{
  "operationId": "op_123",
  "operationType": "evidence.export.bulk",
  "requestedBy": "usr_123",
  "organisationId": "org_456",
  "targetManifestDigest": "sha256:...",
  "parametersDigest": "sha256:...",
  "requiredApprovals": 2,
  "expiresAt": "2026-11-03T09:00:00Z"
}


Approvers should see the actual operation details, not merely an opaque hash.

The hash binds their approval to the exact approved target and parameters.
Approval rules
- Requesters cannot approve their own operations.
- Each approval must come from a distinct authorised human.
- Approvers must have the appropriate role and fresh MFA.
- An approval cannot be transferred to another operation.
- Material parameter changes invalidate prior approvals.
- Approval expiry is enforced.
- Revocation is checked before execution.
- Approval does not bypass legal holds, tenant boundaries or cryptographic restrictions.
For quorum approval, the policy should define the exact required roles and number of independent approvers.
8. Approval enforcement at execution
A common security mistake is checking approvals when an operation is requested but not when it actually runs.

I'd require a final authorisation check immediately before execution.

The execution service should verify:
Authenticated principal
        AND
Required base permission
        AND
Active scoped JIT grant
        AND
Valid operation-specific approvals
        AND
Unchanged target manifest
        AND
Current security policy permits action
        AND
No active revocation or blocking hold


For asynchronous operations, the worker must receive an immutable operation reference—not a copied boolean such as approved: true.

The worker should re-evaluate the operation's current authorisation state before irreversible actions.
Race conditions
Authorisation and operation claiming must be coordinated transactionally where possible.

For long-running operations, use a durable execution lease and fencing token. Cancellation or revocation must stop subsequent protected steps; already completed irreversible external actions cannot always be undone.
9. C# authorisation contracts
I'd introduce a dedicated privileged-operation service.
public interface IPrivilegedOperationAuthorizer
{
    ValueTask<PrivilegedAuthorizationResult> AuthorizeAsync(
        PrivilegedOperationContext context,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record PrivilegedOperationContext(
    Guid PrincipalId,
    Guid SessionId,
    Guid? OrganisationId,
    string Permission,
    Guid? ElevationGrantId,
    Guid? ApprovedOperationId,
    string TargetManifestDigest);

public sealed record PrivilegedAuthorizationResult(
    bool Allowed,
    string DecisionCode,
    Guid? ElevationGrantId,
    Guid? ApprovedOperationId);


The service should use authoritative database state, not client-provided role claims alone.

For high-risk operations, I would centralise execution through a command handler that requires successful privileged authorisation.
public sealed class ExecuteSensitiveOperationHandler(
    IPrivilegedOperationAuthorizer authorizer,
    ISensitiveOperationRepository repository)
{
    public async Task ExecuteAsync(
        SensitiveOperation command,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken)
    {
        var decision = await authorizer.AuthorizeAsync(
            command.AuthorizationContext,
            cancellationToken);

        if (!decision.Allowed)
            throw new UnauthorizedAccessException(
                decision.DecisionCode);

        await repository.ExecuteWithFencingAsync(
            command,
            cancellationToken);
    }
}


The repository must still enforce the operation's concurrency and immutable-target checks. A successful earlier authorisation result is not permission to ignore later revocation or state changes.
10. Emergency access
I'd provide an explicit break-glass procedure for situations where normal administrative authentication is unavailable.

Examples:
- OIDC provider outage.
- Loss of all ordinary platform administrators.
- Critical configuration failure.
- Recovery activation after disaster restoration.
Break-glass access must not be a hidden permanent superuser account with a default password.
Recommended model
Use an operator-provisioned, offline recovery credential with strong custody controls.

For hardened deployments, require multiple custodians or threshold approval where feasible.

The emergency credential should be able to authenticate to a restricted recovery interface or CLI, not automatically grant unrestricted access to all tenant data.
Emergency capabilities
I'd permit narrowly defined actions:
- Restore administrative identity-provider configuration.
- Inspect deployment health.
- Validate recovery state.
- Activate a recovered deployment.
- Rotate compromised platform credentials.
- Restore trusted signing-key configuration.
I'd prohibit:
- Decrypting E2EE submissions without recipient keys.
- Silently changing original receipts.
- Disabling audit logging.
- Bypassing tenant isolation.
- Deleting evidence without retention checks.
Break-glass lifecycle
Emergency declared
        ↓
Recovery credential presented
        ↓
Credential and custody policy verified
        ↓
Restricted emergency session created
        ↓
Permitted recovery actions executed
        ↓
Emergency session terminated
        ↓
Mandatory post-incident review


Emergency actions must generate audit records and should trigger security notifications through available independent channels.

If the primary database is unavailable, the recovery CLI should maintain a protected, append-only local emergency journal and import/reconcile it into the audit system after recovery. Its integrity and custody limitations must be documented.
11. API tokens and machine identities
Human JIT elevation and machine-to-machine access should be treated differently.

I'd avoid issuing long-lived API tokens that inherit all permissions of a human administrator.
Token types
Token	Purpose
User access token	Normal authenticated API access
Privileged user session	Short-lived JIT operations
Service account token	Scoped automation
Operation capability	One approved action
Emergency credential	Restricted recovery access

Service accounts should have explicit owners, scopes, expiry and rotation policies.

They should not be able to request human-style JIT elevation unless a dedicated machine-operation policy explicitly supports it.
Sensitive automation
For automated sensitive operations, I'd use pre-approved policy-bound service capabilities or operation-specific approvals.

For example, a scheduled evidence export can be authorised under a narrowly defined standing policy with limits on scope, destination and volume.

A service account must not be able to approve its own unrestricted exports.
12. Token security
I'd support OAuth2 client credentials for service-to-service access where an identity provider is configured.

For built-in service tokens, use opaque high-entropy credentials stored only as keyed hashes or otherwise appropriately protected verifiers.

Recommended controls:
- Short expiry where practical.
- Narrow scopes.
- Organisation binding.
- Optional mTLS or proof-of-possession binding.
- Rotation with overlap.
- Immediate revocation.
- Last-used metadata.
- Rate limits.
- No token values in logs.
- No tokens in query strings.
A token's claimed scope is not sufficient by itself. The resource server must also evaluate current service-account status, organisation state and operation policy.
13. Admin UI restrictions
I'd make privileged access visible but not intrusive.

Ordinary administrative pages should remain usable without repeated elevation prompts.

Elevation is requested only when the user attempts an action requiring it.
Example privileged operation UI
Privileged Operation
Elevation required

Requested action

Retry failed webhook deliveries

Scope

Organisation · Webhook delivery jobs

Requested duration

15 minutes

Required authentication

Fresh passkey verification

Security control

This permission allows retrying failed delivery jobs. Duplicate external deliveries may occur. All actions are audited.

Illustrative interface; no privilege elevation has been requested.
The UI should clearly display active elevation scope and remaining validity.

It must not imply that a user has unrestricted administrative privileges merely because one elevation grant is active.
Desktop restrictions
I'd keep these operations desktop-only:
- Security policy changes.
- Trusted issuer/key management.
- Bulk evidence exports.
- Native evidence restoration.
- Recovery activation.
- Break-glass administration.
- High-risk job replay.
- Privileged service-account creation.
Basic monitoring, alert acknowledgement and routine submission review can remain available on mobile.

The API must enforce the same permissions regardless of client. A desktop-only UI restriction is a usability and risk-reduction measure, not an authorisation boundary.
14. Privileged session management UI
I'd add a dedicated session-management page.
Active Elevations
Example

Permission	Scope	Remaining
Jobs manage	Webhook delivery	12m
Diagnostics export	Platform	4m

Administrators can voluntarily end their own elevations. Security administrators can revoke grants within their authorised scope.
Illustrative session data.
15. Privileged operation APIs
POST /api/admin/elevations
GET  /api/admin/elevations
DELETE /api/admin/elevations/{grantId}

POST /api/admin/operations
GET  /api/admin/operations/{operationId}

POST /api/admin/operations/{operationId}/approve
POST /api/admin/operations/{operationId}/reject
POST /api/admin/operations/{operationId}/execute

GET  /api/admin/privileged-sessions
DELETE /api/admin/privileged-sessions/{sessionId}


Organisation-scoped equivalents should use the existing organisation route structure.

All state-changing endpoints require CSRF protection where cookie authentication is used, idempotency controls where appropriate and strict authorisation.
16. Database additions
I'd introduce:
privileged_elevation_requests
privileged_elevation_grants
privileged_elevation_revocations

privileged_operation_requests
privileged_operation_approvals
privileged_operation_executions

privileged_session_assurance
privileged_session_events

emergency_access_credentials
emergency_access_sessions
emergency_access_reviews

service_accounts
service_account_credentials
service_account_permission_grants


Existing approval tables from v2.8 should be extended or generalised where practical rather than duplicated.
Grant constraints
Each elevation grant should have:
grant_id
principal_id
session_id
organisation_id
permission
resource_scope
issued_at
expires_at
revoked_at
mfa_event_id
policy_version
reason


The grant's effective permission must never exceed the principal's eligible role permissions and the applicable security policy.
17. Audit events
I'd add:
privilege.elevation.requested
privilege.elevation.granted
privilege.elevation.denied
privilege.elevation.expired
privilege.elevation.revoked

privilege.operation.requested
privilege.operation.approved
privilege.operation.rejected
privilege.operation.executed
privilege.operation.failed

privilege.session.created
privilege.session.terminated

privilege.emergency.activated
privilege.emergency.action_executed
privilege.emergency.terminated
privilege.emergency.review_completed

service_account.created
service_account.permission_changed
service_account.credential_rotated
service_account.revoked


Audit records should capture the actor, scope, policy version, MFA assurance, approval references, target digest and outcome.

Do not record raw authentication assertions, passkey secrets, API tokens or recovery credentials.
18. Security invariants
I'd make these non-negotiable and cover them with automated tests.
1. A base role alone cannot execute an elevation-required operation.
2. An expired or revoked elevation cannot authorise a new action.
3. A user cannot approve their own sensitive operation.
4. An approval cannot be reused for different parameters.
5. An administrator cannot elevate into another organisation without an eligible membership and explicit scope.
6. A service account cannot bypass human approval policy merely because it has an API token.
7. Break-glass access cannot disable mandatory audit or decrypt E2EE content.
8. A privileged browser session cannot be silently extended by normal activity.
9. Worker execution rechecks authorisation against the immutable operation.
10. Tenant isolation and cryptographic access boundaries remain effective regardless of administrative privilege.
19. Implementation priorities
I'd implement v4.2 in this order:
1. Permission classification and privileged-operation catalogue.
2. Fresh MFA assurance validation.
3. Session-bound JIT elevation grants.
4. Central privileged authorisation service.
5. Integration with the existing approval engine.
6. Immutable operation manifests and execution fencing.
7. Privileged admin UI.
8. Service-account and API-token restrictions.
9. Emergency-access CLI and recovery journal.
10. Audit, security alerts and adversarial testing.
The important distinction is that privilege elevation grants temporary authority, approval permits a specific sensitive action, and the execution service independently enforces both at the moment the action occurs.

20. Next decision: administrative policy management
With privileged sessions and approvals defined, the next area is how organisations configure and inherit security policies without weakening deployment-level controls.

Fixed deployment policies

Operators configure security requirements centrally. Organisations can view but cannot customise privileged-access policies.


Hierarchical policies with organisation overrides

Deployment security baselines apply to all tenants. Organisations may strengthen permitted settings, with versioned policy evaluation, preview and audit.


Hierarchical policies with conditional access

Adds device assurance, network restrictions, risk-based access conditions and more advanced policy evaluation.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, administrative security policy management should use hierarchical deployment and organisation security policies with strengthening-only overrides. Continue planning policy inheritance, versioning, conflict resolution, MFA baselines, approval rules, enforcement, policy simulation, administration, rollout and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v4.3
Hierarchical security policies · Strengthening-only overrides · Versioning · MFA · Approvals · Enforcement · Simulation · Rollout · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will use hierarchical security policies with deployment-level baselines and organisation-level overrides that may only strengthen security.

This extends v4.2's JIT privilege elevation and approval-controlled operations without introducing a general-purpose policy scripting engine.

The governing rule is:

An organisation may impose stricter controls than the deployment baseline, but it cannot weaken, disable or bypass a deployment requirement.

Policy evaluation must happen server-side and remain consistent across API nodes, background workers, CLI operations and administrative interfaces.
1. Policy hierarchy
I'd use three policy layers, with one additional scope for resource-specific restrictions.
Deployment security baseline

Mandatory controls · Operator-owned · Cannot be weakened


Organisation security policy

Strengthening-only overrides · Organisation-owned


Resource-specific restrictions

Dropbox · Evidence export · Recovery · Privileged operation


Effective security policy

Resolved · Validated · Versioned · Enforced


The resource layer is not an unrestricted fourth configuration system. It contains only controls explicitly designated as resource-scoped.
Ownership
Layer	Owner	May weaken parent?
Deployment baseline	Platform operator	Not applicable
Organisation policy	Authorised organisation security administrator	No
Resource restrictions	Authorised resource/security administrator	No
Operation-specific approval	Authorised independent approvers	No

The application must never accept a lower-level override simply because its JSON structure is valid.
2. Policy categories
I'd begin with a deliberately limited policy catalogue.
Category	Example controls
Authentication	MFA assurance, freshness, permitted factors
Sessions	Maximum lifetime, idle timeout, elevation duration
Privileged access	Eligible permissions, elevation requirements
Approvals	Quorum, approver roles, independence, expiry
Evidence	Export restrictions, import/restoration approvals
Key governance	Rotation approval, recovery controls
Operations	Job replay, diagnostics export, recovery activation
Audit	Required event coverage, checkpoint frequency
Access restrictions	Allowed client types, administrative UI restrictions

Tenant data retention and legal holds remain in their established policy systems. They may impose additional restrictions, but must not be casually merged into administrative access policy.
3. Strengthening-only semantics
This is where the design needs precision.

Not every configuration field can be combined using max() or min().

I'd assign each policy field an explicit restriction-comparison strategy.
Policy type	Stronger means	Example
Minimum requirement	Higher	MFA assurance level
Maximum allowance	Lower	Elevation lifetime
Allowed set	Smaller subset	Permitted authentication factors
Required set	Larger superset	Mandatory approval roles
Boolean requirement	true dominates	Fresh MFA required
Boolean permission	false dominates	Bulk export permitted
Approval quorum	Higher, subject to feasibility	Two approvals instead of one
Exact constraint	Explicit compatibility rule	Trusted issuer policy

For example:
Deployment max elevation: 30 minutes
Organisation max elevation: 15 minutes
Effective maximum: 15 minutes


And:
Deployment requires fresh MFA: true
Organisation requires fresh MFA: false
Result: invalid override


Critical distinction
A stronger policy must be at least as restrictive for every relevant authorisation decision, not merely look numerically stronger.

For complex conditions, if the engine cannot prove that an override is strengthening-only, it should reject it.
4. Policy schema
I'd use a typed, versioned document with known fields.
{
  "policySchemaVersion": 1,
  "policyId": "policy_org_123",
  "scope": {
    "type": "organisation",
    "id": "org_123"
  },
  "authentication": {
    "requireFreshMfaForElevation": true,
    "maxMfaAgeSeconds": 180,
    "allowedPrivilegedFactors": [
      "fido2_security_key",
      "platform_passkey"
    ]
  },
  "privilegedAccess": {
    "maxElevationSeconds": 900,
    "allowBulkEvidenceExport": false
  },
  "approvals": {
    "minimumIndependentApprovers": 2,
    "maxApprovalAgeSeconds": 600
  }
}


This represents an organisation policy overlay, not the complete effective policy.

I'd use omission to mean inherit.

Explicit null should be rejected for security-critical fields unless the schema defines a precise meaning.

Unknown policy fields must be rejected, not silently ignored.
5. Effective policy resolution
The engine should calculate a complete effective policy from:
Deployment baseline
        +
Organisation strengthening
        +
Resource restrictions
        =
Effective policy


However, the actual implementation should be typed composition rather than generic JSON merging.
C# contracts
public interface ISecurityPolicyResolver
{
    ValueTask<EffectiveSecurityPolicy> ResolveAsync(
        SecurityPolicyContext context,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record SecurityPolicyContext(
    Guid? OrganisationId,
    string Operation,
    string? ResourceType,
    Guid? ResourceId);

public sealed record EffectiveSecurityPolicy(
    string Fingerprint,
    long DeploymentVersion,
    long? OrganisationVersion,
    long? ResourceVersion,
    AuthenticationPolicy Authentication,
    PrivilegedAccessPolicy PrivilegedAccess,
    ApprovalPolicy Approvals);


The effective policy should include a fingerprint and all contributing version identifiers.

This allows audit records to identify the exact policy used for a decision.
6. Conflict resolution
I'd distinguish three situations.
Valid strengthening
Deployment: minimum 1 independent approver
Organisation: minimum 2 independent approvers

Result: 2 approvers required


Invalid weakening
Deployment: phishing-resistant MFA required
Organisation: TOTP permitted as sufficient

Result: reject organisation override


Incompatible restrictions
Deployment: approvers must be Security Officers
Organisation: approvers must be Owners only


A naïve merge could produce an impossible approval requirement.

I'd reject the policy as unsatisfiable unless a defined intersection or combined-role interpretation produces eligible approvers.

The engine should not silently select one rule over another.
Unsatisfiable policies
A policy can be secure but operationally unusable.

For example, requiring three independent Security Officers when the organisation has only one.

I'd allow such a draft to be saved with validation findings, but block publication by default.

For certain deliberate lockout policies, publication should require a separate platform-governed exception process—not a silent bypass of the deployment baseline.
7. MFA baselines
I'd make MFA assurance a structured policy rather than a simple boolean.
Proposed assurance levels
Level	Requirement
Standard	Authenticated session
Elevated	Recent MFA from an accepted factor
Phishing-resistant	Verified WebAuthn/FIDO2 or trusted equivalent
Recovery	Restricted emergency authentication procedure

The recovery level is a separate operating mode, not a universally stronger MFA level.

An emergency credential must not satisfy ordinary privileged MFA requirements merely because it is powerful.
Example baseline
{
  "authentication": {
    "minimumPrivilegedAssurance": "phishing_resistant",
    "maxMfaAgeSeconds": 300,
    "maxSessionIdleSeconds": 1800,
    "maxSessionAbsoluteSeconds": 28800
  }
}


An organisation could reduce the MFA age to 120 seconds or the idle timeout to 900 seconds.

It could not increase those limits beyond the deployment maximum.
8. Approval policy inheritance
Approvals require more than a numeric quorum.

I'd model an approval policy as a collection of constraints:
public sealed record ApprovalPolicy(
    int MinimumIndependentApprovers,
    TimeSpan MaximumApprovalAge,
    bool ProhibitSelfApproval,
    bool RequireFreshMfa,
    IReadOnlySet<string> RequiredApproverRoles,
    bool RequireDistinctPrincipals,
    bool RequireDistinctApprovalSessions);


I'd also support operation-specific policies.

For example:
Operation	Deployment minimum	Organisation strengthening
Retry ordinary job	JIT elevation	Require approval
Export one evidence package	JIT + 1 approver	Require 2 approvers
Bulk evidence export	JIT + 2 approvers	Disable entirely
Change signing-key trust	JIT + 2 approvers	Require Security Officer and Owner
Activate recovery	Restricted recovery procedure + approval	Add additional independent custodian

For role requirements, the policy should define whether the roles are alternatives or separate mandatory approval slots.

Simply combining role names into one set is insufficient.
Approval validity
The operation-specific approval record should bind to:
- Immutable target manifest.
- Requested operation.
- Effective policy fingerprint.
- Approval-policy version.
- Approver identity and assurance.
- Expiry.
- Required quorum and role slots.
If the effective policy changes before execution, the engine must re-evaluate the approvals.

Previously collected approvals may remain usable only if they still satisfy the new policy and the operation target has not changed.

A policy change must never grandfather an operation into weaker authorisation.
9. Policy lifecycle and versioning
I'd use immutable published versions.
Draft
  ↓
Validated
  ↓
Pending approval
  ↓
Published
  ↓
Active
  ↓
Superseded


A published policy version cannot be edited in place.

Any change creates a new draft with a new version number.
Policy record
public enum SecurityPolicyStatus
{
    Draft,
    PendingApproval,
    Published,
    Active,
    Superseded,
    Rejected
}

public sealed record SecurityPolicyVersion(
    Guid PolicyId,
    long Version,
    SecurityPolicyStatus Status,
    string DocumentDigest,
    string SchemaVersion,
    Guid CreatedBy,
    DateTimeOffset CreatedAt,
    DateTimeOffset? ActivatedAt);


The policy fingerprint must cover the canonical effective policy, not just the organisation's override document.

For example, changing the deployment baseline may change an organisation's effective policy even when its own override remains unchanged.
10. Deployment baseline changes
A deployment baseline update can affect every organisation.

I'd require an impact analysis before activation.

The analysis should identify:
- Organisations whose effective policies change.
- Existing grants that will become invalid.
- Pending approvals that need re-evaluation.
- Service accounts affected by new restrictions.
- Administrative sessions requiring renewed MFA.
- Organisations that may no longer have enough eligible approvers.
- Any operation types that become prohibited.
Activation rule
A stricter deployment baseline takes precedence immediately at the authoritative policy boundary once activated.

Existing sessions and grants must not retain permissions that the new baseline prohibits.

For distributed deployments, policy activation should use a monotonic generation and a shared authoritative record. Nodes that cannot establish they have a compatible active policy generation must fail closed for protected operations.
11. Policy simulation
I'd include a policy simulator in the administrative interface and CLI.

This is particularly useful because a strengthening-only policy can accidentally make normal operations impossible.
Example simulation
Security Policy Simulator
Illustrative

Operation
Export one evidence package

Bulk evidence export


Authentication
Fresh phishing-resistant MFA

Fresh TOTP


Independent approvals
0

1

2

3



Simulated result: Denied

Bulk evidence export is disabled by the example organisation policy.
Demonstration only. This is not connected to the actual policy engine.
Simulation API
POST /api/platform/security-policies/simulate

POST /api/organisations/{id}/security-policies/simulate


The simulator should return a structured decision trace containing safe reason codes and the effective policy versions.

It must not issue a real authorisation grant or execute the requested operation.
12. Policy administration
I'd separate policy editing, publishing and approval.
Permission	Purpose
security.policy.view	Read effective policy
security.policy.draft	Create and edit drafts
security.policy.simulate	Run policy simulations
security.policy.submit	Submit for publication
security.policy.approve	Independently approve publication
security.policy.publish	Activate an approved version
security.policy.audit	Review policy history

Organisation administrators should be able to view the deployment baseline applicable to them, without necessarily seeing sensitive platform infrastructure configuration.

Publishing security-policy changes should be a sensitive operation under v4.2.

A requester cannot approve their own policy publication.
13. Policy rollout
I'd support two activation modes.

Immediate activation: For urgent security changes, including revoked authentication methods or newly prohibited operations.

Scheduled activation: For planned changes that may affect administrators, approval staffing or operational workflows.

The application does not need a general-purpose rollout orchestrator. A durable activation record and worker are sufficient.
Rollout safeguards
Before activation:
1. Validate the policy document.
2. Prove strengthening-only inheritance.
3. Simulate affected operation categories.
4. Identify administrative lockout risks.
5. Collect required approvals.
6. Commit the new policy generation.
7. Invalidate incompatible grants and cached decisions.
8. Record activation and audit outcomes.
Policy caches may improve performance, but must not allow stale authorisation for sensitive operations.

I'd use short-lived or generation-aware caches for ordinary permission evaluation and authoritative checks for high-risk operations.
14. Enforcement integration
All entry points should use the same policy engine.
public interface ISecurityPolicyEvaluator
{
    ValueTask<PolicyDecision> EvaluateAsync(
        PolicyEvaluationRequest request,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record PolicyEvaluationRequest(
    Guid PrincipalId,
    Guid SessionId,
    Guid? OrganisationId,
    string Operation,
    string? ResourceType,
    Guid? ResourceId,
    string? TargetManifestDigest);

public sealed record PolicyDecision(
    bool Allowed,
    string DecisionCode,
    string EffectivePolicyFingerprint,
    IReadOnlyList<string> Requirements);


The evaluator should be called by the privileged-operation authoriser from v4.2.

It must not replace ordinary RBAC, tenant isolation, legal-hold checks or cryptographic recipient validation.

The effective authorisation result is the intersection of all applicable controls.
Policy engine safety
I'd avoid arbitrary expression languages, embedded JavaScript or user-defined SQL in the initial implementation.

A typed policy catalogue is easier to validate, test, audit and maintain under Native AOT.
15. Emergency access and policy recovery
Strengthening-only policies can create accidental administrative lockouts.

I'd handle this through the previously defined restricted break-glass procedure.

Emergency recovery must not simply disable the deployment baseline.

Permitted actions should be limited to recovering administrative access, correcting an invalid policy activation or restoring an approved prior configuration under explicit emergency controls.

The emergency action should preserve the original policy version, record the correction and require post-incident review.

A rollback to a previous policy is not automatically permitted if that previous policy would violate the current deployment baseline.
16. Database additions
I'd add or extend:
security_policy_definitions
security_policy_versions
security_policy_activation_records

security_policy_effective_snapshots
security_policy_validation_runs
security_policy_simulation_runs

security_policy_approval_requests
security_policy_impact_assessments
security_policy_rollback_events


Effective snapshots may be cached or materialised for performance, but the published policy documents and their activation history remain authoritative.

Each policy version should retain its canonical digest and schema version.
17. Audit events
I'd introduce:
security.policy.draft_created
security.policy.draft_updated
security.policy.validation_completed
security.policy.validation_failed

security.policy.simulated
security.policy.publication_requested
security.policy.publication_approved
security.policy.publication_rejected

security.policy.activated
security.policy.superseded
security.policy.rollback_requested
security.policy.rollback_completed

security.policy.override_rejected
security.policy.conflict_detected
security.policy.effective_policy_changed


For every published policy change, record:
- Actor and organisation scope.
- Previous and new policy version.
- Canonical document digest.
- Effective policy fingerprint.
- Approval references.
- Validation and impact-assessment results.
- Activation time and deployment generation.
Do not place authentication secrets or sensitive user data in policy audit records.
18. Implementation priorities
I'd implement v4.3 in this order:
1. Typed policy catalogue and restriction-comparison rules.
2. Deployment baseline resolver.
3. Organisation strengthening-only overrides.
4. Effective policy fingerprinting and immutable versions.
5. MFA and approval-policy composition.
6. Integration with v4.2 privileged authorisation.
7. Policy validation and simulation.
8. Publication approvals and impact analysis.
9. Distributed activation and cache invalidation.
10. Administrative UI, recovery safeguards and audit.
The architectural boundary: Deployment policy establishes the minimum security requirements. Organisation policy can make them stricter. Every execution is evaluated against the current effective policy, not merely the policy that existed when the user signed in or requested approval.

19. Next decision: cryptographic key governance
The security-policy hierarchy is now established. The next area is how signing keys, organisation recipient keys and recovery keys are governed throughout their lifecycle.

Operator-managed key custody

Local protected keys and existing portable client-key mechanisms, with manual rotation and recovery procedures.


Hybrid key governance with external custody support

Built-in key lifecycle, policies and audit, with local protected keys, organisation-held E2EE keys and optional external KMS/HSM integrations.


External key custody required for production

Production signing and recovery operations require an external KMS/HSM or equivalent custody system; local key handling remains development-only.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, cryptographic key governance should use hybrid key governance with external custody support. Continue planning signing-key lifecycle, organisation recipient keys, rotation, revocation, compromise handling, recovery custody, KMS/HSM integration, key ceremonies, trust registries, authorisation and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v4.4
Hybrid key governance · Signing keys · E2EE recipients · Rotation · Revocation · Recovery · KMS/HSM · Trust registries · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will provide built-in cryptographic key governance while supporting local protected keys, organisation-controlled E2EE keys and optional external KMS/HSM custody.

External KMS/HSM services will not be mandatory for production. The deployment's security profile and organisation policies may nevertheless require them for particular operations.

The most important boundary remains unchanged:

Secure Intake can manage key identities, public keys, authorisation, rotation and audit without possessing the private keys used to decrypt end-to-end encrypted submissions.
1. Key architecture
I'd divide cryptographic keys into five separate domains.
Domain	Private-key custodian	Purpose
Receipt signing	Deployment or external KMS/HSM	Prove issuance of submission receipts
Evidence package signing	Deployment/export authority or external KMS/HSM	Authenticate exported package manifests
Audit checkpoint signing	Deployment or external KMS/HSM	Authenticate audit-chain checkpoints
Organisation/reviewer E2EE	Reviewers, organisation custody or external cryptographic client	Decrypt authorised submission content
Recovery keys	Organisation-designated custodians	Recover E2EE access when permitted

These domains must not share private keys.

The existing deployment secret-encryption keys form a sixth, separate operational category. They protect server-managed secrets and are not E2EE recipient keys.
Trust and custody diagram
Secure Intake key governance

Registry · Policy · Lifecycle · Approval · Trust · Audit


Platform signing

Local protected key or external signer
Server may sign


E2EE decryption

Reviewer client or organisation custody
Server cannot decrypt




Versioned trust and key history

Fingerprint · Key epoch · Status · Provenance · Revocation · Signed events


An external KMS is not automatically an E2EE solution. If the Secure Intake server can invoke a KMS decrypt operation and receive plaintext DEKs, that custody arrangement breaks the intended server-blind E2EE boundary.

For organisation-managed E2EE, decryption must remain within an independently controlled client, HSM boundary or trusted organisation-side service that does not expose plaintext to Secure Intake.
2. Key identity model
Every registered key should have a stable identity independent of its provider.

I'd define:
public enum CryptographicKeyPurpose
{
    ReceiptSigning,
    EvidencePackageSigning,
    AuditCheckpointSigning,
    ReviewerEncryption,
    TeamEncryption,
    OrganisationEncryption,
    RecoveryEncryption,
    ServerSecretEncryption
}

public enum CryptographicKeyStatus
{
    Pending,
    Active,
    Retiring,
    Retired,
    Revoked,
    Compromised,
    Destroyed
}

public sealed record CryptographicKeyDescriptor(
    Guid KeyId,
    CryptographicKeyPurpose Purpose,
    Guid? OrganisationId,
    string AlgorithmSuite,
    string PublicKeyFingerprint,
    string CustodyProvider,
    string? ProviderKeyReference,
    long Epoch,
    CryptographicKeyStatus Status,
    DateTimeOffset CreatedAt,
    DateTimeOffset? ActivatedAt,
    DateTimeOffset? RetiredAt);
