For symmetric server-secret keys, there is no public-key fingerprint. Those keys should use a separate descriptor containing an opaque key-version identifier rather than forcing them into an asymmetric-key model.
Key identity invariants
- A key ID is never reassigned to different key material.
- A public-key fingerprint is derived from a canonical, algorithm-specific encoding.
- The algorithm suite is immutable for a key.
- Key purpose is immutable.
- Organisation ownership is immutable.
- Provider references are never treated as proof of key identity without verification.
- Historical key records remain available for verification after retirement.
3. Signing-key lifecycle
I'd use the following lifecycle for receipt, evidence-package and audit-checkpoint signing keys.
1

Pending

Generated or registered; awaiting validation and activation



2

Active

Permitted to sign new records



3

Retiring

Replacement active; controlled overlap if policy permits



4

Retired

No new signatures; historical verification supported



5

Revoked

Signing prohibited; trust treatment depends on reason and effective time



6

Compromised

Historical signatures require compromise-aware assessment



This is the normal progression, not the only possible transition. A key can move directly from active to compromised, for example.

destroyed is a custody disposition that must not erase the historical public-key and lifecycle records needed for verification.
4. Signing-key rotation
Rotation should be a controlled operation with independent approval where required.
Planned rotation
1. Generate or register a replacement key.
2. Verify its public identity and permitted algorithms.
3. Test a signing operation against a non-production challenge.
4. Publish the new public key and its pending status.
5. Obtain required approvals.
6. Activate the new signing epoch.
7. Stop issuing new signatures with the old key.
8. Retain the old public key and trust history.
9. Retire or destroy old private material according to custody policy.
The signing service should select the active key through a versioned signing policy.

An asynchronous signing job must not silently switch signing identities midway through an operation.
Signing job pinning
public sealed record SigningRequest(
    Guid OperationId,
    Guid KeyId,
    long KeyEpoch,
    string AlgorithmSuite,
    ReadOnlyMemory<byte> CanonicalPayload);


If the selected key is no longer permitted to sign, the job must be re-authorised or fail according to its operation policy.

It must not silently downgrade to another algorithm or choose an arbitrary available key.
5. KMS/HSM signing abstraction
I'd distinguish local signing from remote signing without exposing private-key bytes to the application layer.
public interface ISigningKeyProvider
{
    string ProviderId { get; }

    ValueTask<SigningKeyCapabilities> GetCapabilitiesAsync(
        string keyReference,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);

    ValueTask<SignatureResult> SignAsync(
        SigningOperation request,
        CancellationToken cancellationToken = default);
}

public sealed record SigningOperation(
    string KeyReference,
    string AlgorithmSuite,
    ReadOnlyMemory<byte> Payload,
    Guid OperationId);

public sealed record SignatureResult(
    string KeyReference,
    string AlgorithmSuite,
    byte[] Signature);


The caller must verify that the returned signature matches the expected public key and algorithm.

The provider must not be able to silently substitute a different signing key.
Provider modules
I'd support:
Provider	Initial direction
Local protected key	Built-in
File-backed key with operator-controlled protection	Built-in
Azure Key Vault / Managed HSM	Optional
AWS KMS	Optional
PKCS#11 HSM	Optional
Other provider	Explicit compile-time module

As established in v3.9, provider registration must remain Native AOT compatible, with no arbitrary runtime plugin loading.
6. Local signing-key custody
For standalone deployments, I'd permit locally protected signing keys.

However, a production local-key configuration should require:
- Operator-controlled filesystem permissions.
- Encryption at rest for private-key material.
- A master key stored separately from the encrypted key file.
- No private keys in JSON configuration.
- No private keys in logs or diagnostics.
- Controlled key export and backup procedures.
- Explicit key rotation and recovery documentation.
The application should not claim that encrypted local key files offer HSM-equivalent protection.

A compromised host with access to both encrypted keys and the unlock material may still be able to sign.
7. Organisation and reviewer E2EE keys
The v2.7 hybrid recipient model remains authoritative.

A submission may be encrypted to:
- Individual reviewer public keys.
- Team recipient keys.
- Organisation recipient keys.
- Approved recovery public keys.
The upload manifest records the exact recipient-key snapshot used at encryption time.
Recipient key record
public sealed record RecipientKeyRegistration(
    Guid KeyId,
    Guid OrganisationId,
    Guid? PrincipalId,
    Guid? TeamId,
    string AlgorithmSuite,
    byte[] PublicKey,
    string Fingerprint,
    long Epoch,
    DateTimeOffset RegisteredAt);


The registration process must prove possession of the corresponding private key where practical.

For externally held organisation keys, the custody service should provide an authenticated key-attestation or proof-of-possession mechanism appropriate to the provider.

A public key submitted by an administrator must not automatically be trusted merely because the administrator has permission to edit key settings.
8. Key enrolment and trust
I'd require explicit key enrolment.
Reviewer enrolment
Generate key in trusted client
        ↓
Submit public key and device metadata
        ↓
Prove private-key possession
        ↓
Apply organisation key policy
        ↓
Approve if required
        ↓
Activate recipient key


For a browser-generated key, the security model remains dependent on the integrity of the browser origin and delivered JavaScript.

High-assurance organisations should be able to require independently distributed reviewer clients.
Key substitution protection
A malicious or compromised server could attempt to replace recipient public keys during upload.

Therefore, the client must validate the recipient manifest against an independently trusted key identity or signed key directory where the assurance policy requires it.

A signature verified using a key supplied solely by the same untrusted server does not solve this problem.
9. Recipient key rotation
Recipient key rotation is more complex than signing-key rotation.

A new recipient key does not automatically decrypt files encrypted to the old key.

I'd distinguish three operations.
Operation	Result
Rotate for future submissions	New uploads use new public key
Rewrap historical DEKs	Existing authorised client adds new recipient envelope
Re-encrypt historical content	New ciphertext generated under a new DEK/protocol context

The default should be rotate for future submissions, with explicit historical rewrap where required.
Key epochs
Each recipient-key rotation increments its epoch.
Reviewer key epoch 1
        ↓
Reviewer key epoch 2
        ↓
Reviewer key epoch 3


A submission retains the exact epoch used when its envelopes were created.

Historical access must not be granted merely because a user currently belongs to a team.
10. Revocation
Revocation must be immediate for future authorisation decisions, but its cryptographic effect is limited.

I'd distinguish:

Access revocation: Prevents new server-side ciphertext retrieval and new authorised operations.

Recipient-key revocation: Prevents the key from being selected for future uploads.

Envelope replacement: Changes which recipients can decrypt a newly published envelope-set version.

Full re-encryption: Creates new ciphertext to reduce future exposure from a compromised DEK or recipient key.

None of these can erase ciphertext or plaintext already copied by an authorised recipient.
Revocation policy
When a reviewer loses access:
1. Revoke their application permissions.
2. Revoke active sessions and grants.
3. Disable the recipient key for future uploads.
4. Identify historical submissions encrypted to that key.
5. Determine whether rewrap or re-encryption is required.
6. Audit the decision and resulting operations.
For shared team keys, revoking one member is especially difficult because the member may retain the shared private key.

This reinforces the earlier preference for individual recipient envelopes over broadly shared private keys.
11. Compromise handling
I'd define separate compromise procedures by key purpose.
Compromised key	Required response
Receipt signing	Stop signing, publish compromise status, assess historical receipts
Evidence package signing	Stop exports using key, review affected packages
Audit checkpoint signing	Establish new trust epoch and assess checkpoint continuity
Reviewer E2EE	Revoke access, rotate, assess historical exposure
Organisation/team E2EE	Rotate custody, identify affected submissions, consider re-encryption
Recovery E2EE	Suspend recovery capability, rotate and assess affected envelopes
Server secret encryption	Rotate affected secrets and encryption keys

A compromised signing key cannot be made historically trustworthy simply by rotating it.

If a key was compromised before a claimed receipt time, the signature may not establish genuine issuance.

Independent timestamp evidence and externally anchored audit checkpoints can improve assessment, but must be evaluated according to their own trust assumptions.
12. Compromise event model
I'd persist a structured compromise record.
{
  "incidentId": "keyincident_123",
  "keyId": "key_456",
  "status": "confirmed_compromise",
  "detectedAt": "2026-11-03T09:00:00Z",
  "suspectedCompromiseFrom": null,
  "actions": [
    "disable_signing",
    "activate_replacement",
    "publish_trust_update"
  ],
  "reviewRequired": true
}


A compromise start time may be unknown.

The system must not invent one.

When historical verification depends on an unknown compromise window, the result should be explicitly indeterminate rather than automatically trusted.
13. Recovery-key custody
Recovery keys must be governed independently from normal reviewer keys.

I'd support the existing recovery modes:
Mode	Behaviour
disabled	No organisation recovery recipient
organisation_key	Recovery public key included in approved upload manifests
external_custody	Recovery private key held by an independent organisation-controlled service or custodian

Recovery is only possible if the original encryption process included an appropriate recovery envelope, or another authorised recipient can rewrap the DEK.

Enabling recovery later does not retroactively create access to historical submissions.
Recovery authorisation
I'd require an operation-specific recovery request containing:
- Organisation and submission scope.
- Reason for recovery.
- Recovery key and epoch.
- Requester identity.
- Independent approvals.
- Effective security-policy fingerprint.
- Expiry and permitted output destination.
The Secure Intake server may authorise ciphertext retrieval and record recovery activity, but it must not receive the recovered DEK or plaintext.

For external custody, the trusted organisation-side recovery client performs the decryption.
14. Recovery ceremonies
I'd introduce formal key ceremonies for high-impact operations.

Examples include:
- Creating an organisation recovery key.
- Replacing a compromised recovery key.
- Activating a new receipt-signing authority.
- Transferring external KMS custody.
- Destroying retired private-key material.
Ceremony record
public sealed record KeyCeremony(
    Guid CeremonyId,
    Guid KeyId,
    string CeremonyType,
    Guid? OrganisationId,
    string ProcedureVersion,
    string EvidenceDigest,
    DateTimeOffset StartedAt,
    DateTimeOffset? CompletedAt);


A ceremony should capture who authorised the operation, which public key was involved, which custody provider was used and whether required verification succeeded.

The ceremony record must not contain private-key material, recovery shares or unlock passwords.

For multi-custodian recovery, a threshold cryptographic scheme may be supported later, but it should not be invented internally. Use a reviewed implementation with a clearly defined custody and reconstruction process.
15. Trust registries
I'd maintain separate trust registries for different cryptographic purposes.
Registry	Contains
Receipt issuer trust	Receipt-signing public keys and lifecycle history
Evidence exporter trust	Package-signing public keys
Audit checkpoint trust	Audit-signing public keys
Organisation recipient directory	Approved E2EE recipient public keys
Recovery-key directory	Approved recovery public keys and epochs

These registries must not be interchangeable.

A trusted receipt-signing key is not automatically trusted to sign recipient-key directories.
Public verification endpoint
Retain the existing receipt-key discovery endpoint:
GET /.well-known/secure-intake/receipt-keys.json


I'd additionally support:
GET /.well-known/secure-intake/evidence-signing-keys.json
GET /.well-known/secure-intake/audit-keys.json


Organisation recipient-key directories should use authenticated, organisation-scoped endpoints unless their publication is explicitly intended.
Registry integrity
A registry update should have:
- Monotonic sequence or epoch.
- Canonical document digest.
- Previous-registry commitment.
- Signing authority.
- Effective time.
- Key status and revocation history.
For offline verification, the verifier needs an independently established trust anchor.

Downloading a registry and accepting its included keys without prior trust does not establish authenticity.
16. Key governance APIs
I'd expose lifecycle operations through explicit endpoints.
GET  /api/platform/cryptographic-keys
POST /api/platform/cryptographic-keys
GET  /api/platform/cryptographic-keys/{keyId}

POST /api/platform/cryptographic-keys/{keyId}/activate
POST /api/platform/cryptographic-keys/{keyId}/retire
POST /api/platform/cryptographic-keys/{keyId}/revoke
POST /api/platform/cryptographic-keys/{keyId}/report-compromise

GET  /api/organisations/{id}/recipient-keys
POST /api/organisations/{id}/recipient-keys
POST /api/organisations/{id}/recipient-keys/{keyId}/rotate

POST /api/organisations/{id}/key-recovery-requests
GET  /api/organisations/{id}/key-recovery-requests/{requestId}


Registering a public key and authorising its activation should be separate operations where the effective policy requires approval.

There should be no generic endpoint that returns stored private signing keys.
17. Key governance CLI
I'd provide operator-oriented commands.
# List key identities and lifecycle states.
secureintake keys list

# Inspect public metadata.
secureintake keys inspect --key-id <key-id>

# Verify a registered public key.
secureintake keys verify --key-id <key-id>

# Prepare a signing-key rotation.
secureintake keys rotate plan \
  --purpose receipt-signing

# Inspect trusted issuer history.
secureintake trust verify \
  --registry ./receipt-keys.json \
  --trusted-key ./trust-anchor.pem


Key creation, activation, revocation and compromise handling should invoke the same policy and approval services as the administrative API.

The CLI must not provide a privileged bypass merely because it runs locally.

A separate restricted recovery procedure remains available for genuine deployment emergencies.
18. Key governance dashboard
I'd add a desktop-only Cryptographic Keys administration page.
Cryptographic Keys
Example

Purpose	Custody	Status
Receipt signing	External KMS	Active
Evidence signing	Local protected	Active
Audit checkpoints	External KMS	Active
Organisation recovery	External custody	Pending


Selected key — Receipt signing
Algorithm

ES256

Epoch

3

Private-key export

Unavailable

Rotation approval

Required


Illustrative data only. Key operations are subject to JIT elevation and approval.
The UI should show key purpose, algorithm, fingerprint, epoch, custody provider, status, trust history and relevant incidents.

It must never show private-key material.
19. Database additions
I'd introduce the following logical tables, reusing existing recipient-key records where appropriate.
cryptographic_key_descriptors
cryptographic_key_epochs
cryptographic_key_lifecycle_events
cryptographic_key_provider_bindings

cryptographic_key_rotation_operations
cryptographic_key_compromise_incidents
cryptographic_key_ceremonies

cryptographic_trust_registries
cryptographic_trust_registry_versions
cryptographic_trust_decisions

organisation_key_custody_policies
organisation_recovery_key_assignments
organisation_key_recovery_requests


For immutable evidence, key references must continue pointing to the exact historical key identity and epoch.

A database migration must never silently replace an old envelope's key reference with a new one.
20. Audit events
I'd add:
cryptographic.key.registered
cryptographic.key.activated
cryptographic.key.rotation_requested
cryptographic.key.rotated
cryptographic.key.retired
cryptographic.key.revoked
cryptographic.key.compromise_reported
cryptographic.key.destroyed

cryptographic.recipient_key.enrolled
cryptographic.recipient_key.revoked
cryptographic.envelope.rewrapped
cryptographic.content.reencrypted

cryptographic.recovery.requested
cryptographic.recovery.approved
cryptographic.recovery.completed
cryptographic.recovery.denied

cryptographic.trust_registry.updated
cryptographic.trust_decision.changed
cryptographic.key_ceremony.completed


Each event should record the key identity, epoch, purpose, custody provider, actor, authorisation reference, policy fingerprint and outcome.

Audit records should not contain private keys, plaintext DEKs or decrypted submission content.
21. Implementation priorities
I'd deliver v4.4 in this order:
1. Unified key identity and lifecycle metadata.
2. Separate signing and E2EE key-governance interfaces.
3. Local protected signing-key provider.
4. Versioned public-key trust registries.
5. Receipt/evidence/audit signing-key rotation.
6. Recipient-key enrolment and proof of possession.
7. E2EE key epochs, revocation and historical access assessment.
8. Recovery custody policies and approval workflows.
9. Optional KMS/HSM signing providers.
10. Compromise handling, ceremonies, dashboard and audit.
The governing principle is that Secure Intake controls which keys are trusted and which operations are authorised, while private-key custody remains with the appropriate deployment operator, reviewer or organisation-controlled cryptographic service.

22. Next decision: organisation onboarding and provisioning
The cryptographic governance model is now defined. The next architectural decision is how new organisations are provisioned with tenant storage, identity, initial roles, security policies and key requirements.

Operator-provisioned organisations

Every organisation is created and activated by a platform operator, including tenant resources and initial administration.


Configurable manual, API and self-service provisioning

Deployment operators choose which onboarding methods are enabled. All methods use one provisioning pipeline with approval, validation and tenant isolation.


Automated self-service provisioning

Eligible organisations register and are provisioned automatically under predefined deployment policies, with manual review for exceptions.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, organisation onboarding should use configurable manual, API and self-service provisioning through a unified controlled pipeline. Continue planning organisation creation, tenant resource assignment, provisioning states, initial administrators, identity setup, key enrolment, security-policy inheritance, activation, suspension, deletion and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v4.5
Organisation provisioning · Manual, API and self-service onboarding · Tenant isolation · Identity · Key enrolment · Security policies · Lifecycle · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will support manual, API-driven and self-service organisation onboarding through one unified provisioning pipeline.

Deployment operators decide which methods are enabled. All methods must enforce the same tenant isolation, security policies, resource validation and activation requirements.

Creating an organisation is not the same as activating it. An organisation must pass provisioning and security checks before it can accept submissions.
1. Provisioning architecture
Admin UI


API / CLI


Self-service




Unified provisioning service

Eligibility · Validation · Idempotency · Approval · Workflow state


Tenant database routing


Storage profile


Identity and initial roles


Effective security policy


Key requirements


Audit and retention




Organisation activated

Tenant isolation verified · Administrators established · Security controls satisfied


No onboarding entry point may bypass the provisioning service by inserting directly into organisation tables.
2. Provisioning methods
I'd define a deployment-wide policy:
{
  "organisationProvisioning": {
    "manualEnabled": true,
    "apiEnabled": true,
    "selfServiceEnabled": false,
    "selfServiceRequiresApproval": true,
    "defaultTenantDatabaseProfile": "shared-postgres",
    "defaultStorageProfile": "primary-s3",
    "requireInitialAdministratorMfa": true
  }
}


The values above are illustrative.
Method-specific rules
Method	Initiator	Approval behaviour
Manual	Platform administrator	Policy-controlled
API	Authorised service account	Policy-controlled
Self-service	Eligible external applicant	Operator-configurable
CLI	Authorised platform operator	Same as manual/API

An API token with organisation-creation permission must not automatically gain access to the organisation it creates.

Self-service registration must not automatically grant platform-level privileges.
3. Organisation lifecycle
I'd use a durable lifecycle state machine.
1

Requested

Application received; identity and eligibility checked



2

Pending approval

Waiting for required operator or security approval



3

Provisioning

Resources, identity and policy assignments created



4

Pending activation

Provisioning complete; final security validation outstanding



5

Active

Organisation may operate under its assigned policies



Additional lifecycle states:
State	Meaning
rejected	Onboarding request declined
provisioning_failed	Provisioning stopped with recoverable findings
suspended	Existing organisation temporarily restricted
deletion_pending	Approved deletion/retention workflow in progress
deleted	Logical deletion completed, subject to retained evidence and backups

The state machine must distinguish provisioning failure from rejection.

A failed storage check should not automatically mean the applicant was rejected.
4. Organisation creation request
I'd use a common request model regardless of onboarding method.
public sealed record CreateOrganisationRequest(
    string DisplayName,
    string Slug,
    OrganisationProvisioningMethod Method,
    Guid? RequestedByPrincipalId,
    string? InitialAdministratorEmail,
    string? DatabaseProfileId,
    string? StorageProfileId,
    string? IdempotencyKey);

public enum OrganisationProvisioningMethod
{
    Manual,
    Api,
    SelfService
}


The client may request profiles only from an operator-approved catalogue.

Self-service applicants should not be able to supply arbitrary database connection strings, storage credentials or internal infrastructure identifiers.
Organisation identifiers
I'd use immutable internal IDs.
org_01J...


The display name and slug can change under policy.

The immutable ID is used for tenancy, audit, encryption context and resource mapping.

A renamed organisation must not cause existing cryptographic evidence to become invalid.
5. Tenant resource assignment
The provisioning service should resolve two independent infrastructure assignments:
1. Tenant database profile.
2. Tenant storage profile.
These assignments may be shared or dedicated.
Supported combinations
Database	Storage	Supported
Shared PostgreSQL	Shared S3/R2 bucket	Yes
Shared PostgreSQL	Dedicated bucket	Yes
Dedicated PostgreSQL	Shared bucket	Yes
Dedicated PostgreSQL	Dedicated bucket	Yes
Standalone SQLite	Local filesystem	Yes
Standalone SQLite	S3-compatible storage	Yes

The standalone SQLite deployment remains single-node.

A dedicated tenant database does not automatically require a dedicated storage bucket.
Resource assignment record
public sealed record OrganisationResourceAssignment(
    Guid OrganisationId,
    string DatabaseProfileId,
    string StorageProfileId,
    long AssignmentVersion,
    DateTimeOffset AssignedAt);


Once files exist, resource changes must use an explicit migration workflow rather than editing a profile reference in place.

Existing files remain pinned to their storage profile and object identity.
6. Provisioning transaction boundaries
Provisioning can involve multiple databases, object stores and identity providers.

A single ACID transaction cannot cover all of them.

I'd use a durable provisioning workflow with idempotent steps and compensating actions.
Create organisation record
        ↓
Reserve slug and identity
        ↓
Assign database profile
        ↓
Initialise tenant schema
        ↓
Assign storage profile
        ↓
Validate storage access
        ↓
Create identity/membership records
        ↓
Apply security-policy baseline
        ↓
Evaluate key requirements
        ↓
Run activation checks


Each step records its status and retry information.

If a later step fails, the organisation remains inactive.
Compensation
Compensation must be conservative.

For example, an unused provisional storage prefix may be cleaned up after verification.

A dedicated database containing retained evidence must not be deleted automatically because a provisioning workflow is retried.
7. Tenant isolation validation
Before activation, I'd require checks appropriate to the assigned database and storage architecture.
Shared PostgreSQL
- Organisation ID correctly assigned.
- Row-level security policies present.
- FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY applied where required.
- Runtime database roles cannot bypass RLS.
- Tenant-scoped queries execute under the correct context.
- Cross-tenant access tests pass.
Dedicated PostgreSQL
- Correct database endpoint.
- Expected deployment identity.
- Correct schema version.
- Tenant ownership metadata matches.
- Credentials restricted to the assigned database.
Object storage
- Storage profile resolves successfully.
- Required read/write/delete capabilities are verified safely.
- Object namespace is isolated.
- Presigned URL policy matches the configured host and expiry requirements.
- Storage credentials cannot access unauthorised resources beyond their intended scope where provider capabilities permit.
A generic connectivity check is not sufficient to prove tenant isolation.
8. Initial administrator
I'd require at least one initial organisation administrator before activation.

However, merely supplying an email address must not create an authenticated administrator.
Bootstrap sequence
Organisation created
        ↓
Initial administrator nominated
        ↓
Invitation issued
        ↓
Identity verified
        ↓
MFA enrolled
        ↓
Membership accepted
        ↓
Administrator activated


The nominated person must authenticate and accept the organisation membership.

Email verification alone does not establish legal identity.
Initial privileges
I'd grant the initial administrator only the standard organisation Owner role and associated base permissions.

JIT elevation and independent approvals still apply.

The initial administrator does not receive automatic access to E2EE private keys.
9. Identity setup
I'd support the existing authentication options:
- Built-in identity.
- Deployment-wide OIDC.
- Organisation-specific OIDC where enabled.
Organisation OIDC configuration should be established through an authorised setup workflow.
OIDC identity mapping
Use the issuer and subject pair:
issuer + subject


Do not use email address alone as a permanent identity key.

An organisation must not be able to register an arbitrary OIDC issuer that impersonates another organisation's trusted identity domain.

Issuer configuration requires validation, trusted metadata retrieval, redirect URI restrictions and administrative approval where policy requires it.
10. Key enrolment requirements
An organisation may need recipient keys before it can accept encrypted submissions.

I'd distinguish:

Organisation activation: Administrative and infrastructure setup is complete.

Dropbox publication: Required recipient-key coverage exists for that dropbox.

This avoids requiring every organisation to configure all possible encryption models before it can use the administration interface.
Example
An organisation using individual reviewer encryption may activate after its administrator is established.

Its first dropbox cannot be published until at least one eligible recipient key exists and the recipient policy is valid.

An organisation whose policy mandates a recovery key must satisfy that requirement before publishing a dropbox that accepts submissions.
Key onboarding checklist
- Approved recipient-key policy.
- Required reviewer/team/organisation public keys.
- Proof of possession or custody verification.
- Key trust validation.
- Recovery policy satisfied.
- Encryption suite supported by public uploader clients.
11. Security-policy inheritance
Every new organisation inherits the current deployment baseline from v4.3.

I'd create an initial organisation policy version containing only explicit strengthening overrides.

If there are no overrides, the organisation simply inherits the baseline.
Effective policy
Deployment baseline version 12
        +
Organisation override version 1
        =
Effective organisation policy


The effective policy fingerprint should be recorded at activation.

Future deployment baseline changes automatically affect the organisation according to the established policy rollout rules.

A new organisation must not be able to weaken the baseline through onboarding parameters.
12. Activation gates
I'd make activation a formal operation.
Gate	Requirement
Organisation identity	Valid immutable ID and unique slug
Approval	Required onboarding approvals satisfied
Database	Assigned and validated
Storage	Assigned and validated
Administrator	Verified, accepted membership, required MFA
Authentication	Trusted provider configuration valid
Security policy	Effective policy valid
Audit	Organisation audit context initialised
Retention	Required baseline policies assigned
Key governance	Organisation-level mandatory controls satisfied
Provisioning	No unresolved critical findings

Activation should produce an immutable activation report.
Activation API
POST /api/platform/organisations/{id}/activation-check
POST /api/platform/organisations/{id}/activate


The activation command should recheck all critical gates at execution time.

A previously successful preflight report is not sufficient if infrastructure or policy changed afterwards.
13. Self-service onboarding
Self-service should be optional and disabled by default for private deployments.

I'd support two modes.
Request-only
An applicant submits an organisation request.

A platform operator reviews and approves it before provisioning begins.
Policy-controlled automatic
Eligible applicants are provisioned automatically using an operator-approved resource profile.

This should require explicit deployment configuration and abuse controls.
Self-service restrictions
- No arbitrary infrastructure selection.
- No platform role assignment.
- No custom secret-provider references.
- No unrestricted organisation creation.
- Rate limits and anti-abuse controls.
- Verified contact method.
- Clear pending/approved/rejected status.
- No exposure of internal provisioning details.
Self-service registration should not imply that an organisation is commercially verified or legally incorporated.
14. API-driven provisioning
For automation, I'd support scoped service accounts.
POST /api/platform/organisations
GET  /api/platform/organisations/{id}
GET  /api/platform/organisations/{id}/provisioning


API creation should be idempotent.

Example:
POST /api/platform/organisations
Idempotency-Key: create-customer-123
Content-Type: application/json


{
  "displayName": "Example Organisation",
  "slug": "example-organisation",
  "databaseProfileId": "shared-postgres",
  "storageProfileId": "primary-s3",
  "initialAdministratorEmail": "admin@example.org"
}


A repeated request with the same idempotency key and equivalent payload should return the original provisioning operation.

The same key with conflicting parameters should be rejected.
15. Provisioning CLI
I'd provide:
# Create a new organisation.
secureintake organisations create \
  --name "Example Organisation" \
  --slug example-organisation \
  --database-profile shared-postgres \
  --storage-profile primary-s3

# Inspect provisioning state.
secureintake organisations provisioning status \
  --organisation <organisation-id>

# Validate activation requirements.
secureintake organisations activation-check \
  --organisation <organisation-id>

# Activate after approval.
secureintake organisations activate \
  --organisation <organisation-id>


The CLI must use the same authorisation and provisioning services as the API.

It must not bypass approval or tenant-isolation checks.
16. Suspension
Suspension should be reversible and distinct from deletion.

I'd support configurable suspension modes.
Mode	Effect
Administrative	Blocks organisation administration except authorised recovery actions
Intake	Blocks new uploads and finalisation
Full operational	Blocks new intake and ordinary reviewer operations
Security lockdown	Restricts activity to explicitly authorised security/recovery operations

Suspension must not automatically delete ciphertext or revoke cryptographic keys.

Already-issued presigned upload URLs may remain usable until expiry. The finalisation service must recheck organisation state and reject unauthorised completion.
Suspension record
public sealed record OrganisationSuspension(
    Guid OrganisationId,
    string Mode,
    string ReasonCode,
    DateTimeOffset EffectiveAt,
    Guid AuthorisedBy,
    Guid? ApprovedOperationId);


Suspension and reactivation should be privileged operations under v4.2.
17. Deletion and offboarding
I'd retain a staged deletion process.
Active / Suspended
        ↓
Deletion requested
        ↓
Retention and legal-hold assessment
        ↓
Independent approval
        ↓
Intake and access disabled
        ↓
Eligible data deletion
        ↓
Storage reconciliation
        ↓
Logical deletion completed


Deletion constraints
An organisation cannot be physically erased while required evidence or legal holds remain.

Deletion should account for:
- Submission ciphertext.
- Encrypted form responses.
- Server-readable metadata.
- Receipt and evidence-package records.
- Audit history.
- Organisation configuration.
- Storage objects and orphaned uploads.
- External webhook and notification records.
- Backup retention limitations.
I'd use a tombstone record to preserve the minimum identifiers necessary for audit, duplicate prevention and legal obligations.

Private E2EE keys remain under their custodians' control. The platform cannot guarantee destruction of externally held copies.
18. Organisation administration dashboard
I'd provide a desktop-first provisioning interface.
Organisation Provisioning
Pending activation

Example Organisation

org_01JEXAMPLE · Manual provisioning


Organisation identity
Complete

Database assignment
Verified

Storage assignment
Verified

Initial administrator
Awaiting MFA

Security policy
Valid

Audit context
Initialised


Activation is blocked until the initial administrator completes the required MFA enrolment.

Illustrative interface. No live organisation is being provisioned.
The dashboard should show actionable provisioning failures, not generic status messages.

For example, “Storage validation failed: write permission denied” is more useful than “Provisioning error”.
19. Provisioning database model
I'd introduce:
organisation_provisioning_requests
organisation_provisioning_operations
organisation_provisioning_steps
organisation_provisioning_findings

organisation_resource_assignments
organisation_initial_administrator_invitations
organisation_activation_reports

organisation_suspensions
organisation_reactivation_operations
organisation_deletion_requests
organisation_deletion_operations
organisation_tombstones


The existing organisations table remains the authoritative lifecycle record.

Provisioning steps should have stable IDs and idempotency keys so retries cannot accidentally create duplicate resources.
20. Audit events
I'd add:
organisation.provisioning.requested
organisation.provisioning.approved
organisation.provisioning.rejected
organisation.provisioning.started
organisation.provisioning.step_completed
organisation.provisioning.failed

organisation.resource_assigned
organisation.initial_admin_invited
organisation.initial_admin_activated

organisation.activation_checked
organisation.activated

organisation.suspended
organisation.reactivated

organisation.deletion_requested
organisation.deletion_approved
organisation.deletion_started
organisation.deletion_completed


Audit records should include the initiating method, actor/service identity, organisation ID, provisioning operation ID, policy fingerprint, approval references and outcome.

They should not contain initial administrator invitation tokens, infrastructure credentials or other secrets.
21. Implementation priorities
I'd implement v4.5 in this order:
1. Unified organisation provisioning service and state machine.
2. Durable idempotent provisioning steps.
3. Database/storage profile assignment and validation.
4. Initial administrator invitation and MFA bootstrap.
5. Security-policy inheritance and activation gates.
6. Manual administration and CLI provisioning.
7. Scoped API-driven provisioning.
8. Configurable self-service onboarding.
9. Suspension and reactivation.
10. Deletion/offboarding workflows and audit.
The governing principle is that all onboarding methods create the same type of organisation, pass through the same security boundaries and produce the same auditable activation state.

22. Next decision: tenant resource migration
With organisations provisioned into shared or dedicated database and storage profiles, the next architectural area is moving an existing organisation between those profiles without losing ciphertext, receipts, audit history or tenant isolation.

Operator-managed migration only

Secure Intake supplies export, validation and reconciliation tools. Operators coordinate database and storage migration externally.


Controlled in-platform migration workflow

Secure Intake orchestrates tenant-scoped migrations between preconfigured resource profiles, with maintenance fencing, copy verification, approval and rollback safeguards.


Online migration with minimal interruption

Adds change capture, dual-read or replication strategies and controlled cutover for high-availability tenant migrations.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, tenant resource migration should use operator-managed migration with validation and reconciliation tooling. Continue planning tenant database migration, ciphertext storage migration, maintenance fencing, object verification, cutover, rollback, approvals, audit and operational recovery.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v4.6
Operator-managed tenant migration · Database and ciphertext relocation · Maintenance fencing · Verification · Cutover · Rollback · Recovery · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will not automatically migrate organisations between databases or storage providers.

Operators will perform the physical data transfer using their own infrastructure tooling. Secure Intake will provide migration planning, maintenance fencing, integrity manifests, validation, reconciliation, cutover controls and audit.

This is consistent with the existing operator-managed backup and deployment architecture.

The key distinction is:

Secure Intake controls whether a tenant can safely move and when its new resource assignments become authoritative. The operator controls how the data is physically copied.
1. Migration architecture
Existing organisation

Authoritative database · Storage profile · Audit state


Secure Intake migration plan

Target validation · Approval · Resource inventory · Immutable migration ID


Maintenance fence and consistent checkpoint

Stop writes · Drain jobs · Expire upload capabilities · Record audit position


Database copy

Operator tooling


Ciphertext copy

Operator tooling




Validation and reconciliation

Tenant records · Object digests · Receipts · Audit · Keys · Jobs


Approved authoritative cutover

New assignment generation · Old source fenced · Verification report


No tenant data is deleted from the source as part of the cutover transaction.

Source cleanup is a separate, approved operation after the rollback window and retention checks.
2. Supported migration types
I'd support the following scenarios.
Migration	Example	Complexity
Database only	Shared PostgreSQL to dedicated PostgreSQL	Medium
Storage only	Shared S3 bucket to dedicated R2 bucket	Medium
Database and storage	Shared infrastructure to dedicated infrastructure	High
Dedicated to shared	Dedicated PostgreSQL to shared PostgreSQL	High
Local to distributed	SQLite/local filesystem to PostgreSQL/S3	High
Provider relocation	S3-compatible provider A to provider B	Medium

These are migration classes, not promises of universal automated compatibility.

SQLite-to-PostgreSQL migration needs an explicit logical data transformation or supported migration utility. A SQLite database file cannot simply be restored into PostgreSQL.
Migration scope
I'd restrict a migration to one organisation at a time.

Batch migrations can be supported by operator scripts that invoke the tenant migration tooling sequentially, but the platform should not silently coordinate hundreds of simultaneous tenant moves.
3. Migration state machine
I'd define:
Draft
  ↓
Validated
  ↓
Approved
  ↓
Fencing
  ↓
Fenced
  ↓
Checkpointed
  ↓
External Copy
  ↓
Reconciling
  ↓
Ready for Cutover
  ↓
Cutover
  ↓
Post-Cutover Validation
  ↓
Completed


Additional terminal or exceptional states:
- validation_failed
- copy_failed
- reconciliation_failed
- cutover_failed
- rollback_required
- rolled_back
- abandoned
- source_cleanup_pending
The platform should not infer that external copying succeeded merely because an operator marks a step complete.

The destination must pass independent validation.
4. Migration plan
Each migration should begin with an immutable plan.

Example:
{
  "migrationId": "mig_01J...",
  "organisationId": "org_123",
  "source": {
    "databaseProfile": "shared-postgres",
    "storageProfile": "primary-s3",
    "assignmentGeneration": 7
  },
  "destination": {
    "databaseProfile": "dedicated-postgres-01",
    "storageProfile": "dedicated-r2-01"
  },
  "scope": "database_and_storage",
  "mode": "offline_fenced",
  "requiresApproval": true
}


The plan should include the expected schema versions, source resource identities, destination resource identities, storage namespace mapping and retention constraints.

Any material change creates a new plan version and invalidates approvals bound to the previous plan.
5. Maintenance fencing
Maintenance fencing is the most important operational control.

Simply disabling an organisation in the UI is insufficient.

The system must prevent new authoritative writes across all service components.
Fence requirements
When a tenant enters migration maintenance:
1. Reject new upload sessions.
2. Reject submission finalisation.
3. Stop accepting revisions.
4. Stop workflow transitions.
5. Stop new evidence export/import operations.
6. Drain or suspend tenant-scoped background jobs.
7. Prevent new tenant configuration writes.
8. Block new privileged operations that modify tenant state.
9. Account for outstanding presigned upload capabilities.
10. Establish a consistent source checkpoint.
Read-only access may remain available if the migration policy permits it and the source is stable.
Presigned URL limitation
Previously issued storage URLs may remain usable until expiry.

The migration fence must therefore include a capability-drain period or equivalent storage-side revocation strategy.

The system must not assume that an application-level fence instantly invalidates an already-issued S3/R2 presigned URL.

Temporary ciphertext uploaded after the fence but before capability expiry must be identified and reconciled.
6. Fencing token
I'd introduce a monotonic tenant resource generation.
public sealed record TenantResourceFence(
    Guid OrganisationId,
    long Generation,
    Guid MigrationId,
    DateTimeOffset ActivatedAt,
    bool WritesBlocked);


Every authoritative tenant write should validate the current generation.

A worker holding an old generation must not commit a write after the fence becomes active.

For shared PostgreSQL, use a transactionally enforced fence check in the authoritative database. Merely checking a cached generation before starting work is not sufficient.

For dedicated databases, the control-plane assignment and tenant-side fence must be coordinated using a durable protocol.

If the control plane is unavailable, protected writes should fail closed rather than assume the old assignment remains valid indefinitely.
7. Database migration
The operator performs the physical database copy.

Secure Intake provides:
- Source inventory.
- Tenant-scoped logical export validation.
- Schema compatibility checks.
- Destination identity verification.
- Record-count and digest reconciliation.
- Tenant isolation validation.
- Cutover readiness assessment.
Shared PostgreSQL to dedicated PostgreSQL
The operator must copy only the organisation's permitted tenant data.

The migration tooling should identify all tenant-owned tables and relationships.

It must also include relevant organisation configuration, workflow versions, submission manifests, encryption envelopes, retention records and job state.
Control-plane records
Some records belong to the deployment control plane rather than the tenant database.

These should not be blindly duplicated.

The migration plan must define which control-plane references are updated and which tenant-owned records are copied.
Referential integrity
I'd validate:
- Submission-to-dropbox relationships.
- Form and workflow version references.
- File and ciphertext object references.
- Recipient envelope references.
- Receipt and evidence manifest references.
- Retention and legal-hold references.
- Durable job and outbox references.
No cross-database foreign keys should be assumed.
8. Logical migration manifest
I'd generate a signed or otherwise authenticated migration checkpoint manifest.
{
  "migrationId": "mig_01J...",
  "organisationId": "org_123",
  "sourceGeneration": 7,
  "checkpointId": "chk_456",
  "schemaVersion": 31,
  "recordInventories": {
    "submissions": {
      "count": 12500,
      "digest": "sha256:..."
    },
    "files": {
      "count": 38600,
      "digest": "sha256:..."
    }
  },
  "objectInventoryDigest": "sha256:...",
  "auditCheckpointId": "audit_789"
}


Counts alone are not sufficient.

For record reconciliation, I'd use canonical, deterministic representations with stable ordering and explicit exclusions for legitimate destination-specific fields.

The manifest must not expose server-readable personal information unnecessarily.
9. Ciphertext storage migration
Storage migration must preserve ciphertext integrity.

I'd support two approaches.
Identity-preserving copy
Copy ciphertext bytes without modifying encryption envelopes or file manifests.

This is the preferred approach.
Object-reference remapping
When destination object keys differ, create an explicit mapping between source and destination storage identities.

The ciphertext digest must remain unchanged.

The original receipt evidence must not be rewritten merely because the physical storage location changed.
Object mapping
public sealed record MigratedObjectMapping(
    Guid FileId,
    string SourceStorageProfile,
    string SourceObjectKey,
    string DestinationStorageProfile,
    string DestinationObjectKey,
    string ExpectedSha256,
    long ExpectedLength);


Storage object keys are operational identifiers, not cryptographic proof of content identity.
10. Object verification
I'd require verification at the destination.

For each referenced ciphertext object:
- Object exists.
- Expected byte length matches.
- Cryptographic digest matches.
- Object version or immutable identity is recorded where available.
- Required metadata is present.
- Destination access policy is correct.
- No unexpected public access is introduced.
Do not use S3 ETags as universal SHA-256 integrity proofs.

For large datasets, the verification CLI should stream object contents in bounded memory.
Verification levels
Level	Behaviour	Cutover suitability
Metadata	Existence, length, provider metadata	Insufficient alone
Sampled	Digest verification on a sample	Diagnostic only
Full	Digest verification of all referenced objects	Required by default
Full + policy	Digests plus access, retention and namespace checks	Recommended

For high-assurance migrations, full object verification should be mandatory.
11. Storage consistency
The destination must contain every object referenced by the migrated database.

Extra objects may exist temporarily, but they must be classified.

I'd distinguish:
- Referenced and verified.
- Referenced but missing.
- Referenced but digest-mismatched.
- Unreferenced temporary upload.
- Unreferenced orphan.
- Retained by legal hold.
- Unknown object requiring investigation.
A missing or mismatched referenced ciphertext object blocks cutover.

Orphaned objects may be reconciled after migration, but should not be deleted automatically during the cutover process.
12. Receipt and evidence preservation
The original signed receipts and evidence manifests must remain unchanged.

Moving ciphertext between storage providers does not change the underlying accepted evidence.

I'd verify:
- Receipt signatures.
- Receipt issuer trust.
- Evidence manifest digests.
- Ciphertext digests.
- Recipient envelope snapshots.
- Original acceptance timestamps.
- Evidence package references.
A migration may produce a new signed migration attestation, but that attestation must not replace the original receipt.
Migration attestation
I'd issue a destination migration attestation containing:
migration_id
organisation_id
source_assignment
destination_assignment
source_checkpoint_digest
destination_reconciliation_digest
cutover_time
authorising_approvals
signing_key_id


This proves the destination's signed claim about the migration, subject to the signing authority's trust.

It does not independently prove that the operator copied every byte unless the reconciliation evidence supports that claim.
13. Audit continuity
The organisation audit chain must survive migration.

I'd preserve:
- Original audit event sequence.
- Hash-chain links.
- Signed checkpoints.
- Signing-key trust history.
- External checkpoint references.
If audit records move to a different database, their canonical contents and ordering must not be rewritten.

I'd add a migration boundary event linking the old and new resource assignments.
Audit boundary
Source audit checkpoint
        ↓
Migration fence event
        ↓
Migration attestation
        ↓
Destination activation event
        ↓
Continued audit chain


Where the existing chain format cannot continue seamlessly across the storage boundary, use an explicitly linked new audit epoch rather than pretending continuity.
14. Cutover procedure
I'd make cutover a short, controlled operation after full reconciliation.
Cutover prerequisites
- Source tenant fenced.
- No active authoritative writers.
- Presigned capability drain complete or safely accounted for.
- Destination database validated.
- Destination ciphertext fully verified.
- Receipt and audit verification passed.
- Required keys and trust registries available.
- Durable jobs and outbox state reconciled.
- Independent approvals valid.
- Rollback plan documented.
Atomic assignment change
The control plane should update the authoritative tenant resource assignment using compare-and-swap semantics.

Example:
UPDATE organisation_resource_assignments
SET
    database_profile_id = @destination_database,
    storage_profile_id = @destination_storage,
    assignment_generation = assignment_generation + 1
WHERE organisation_id = @organisation_id
  AND assignment_generation = @expected_generation;


The actual operation must run in a transaction with the relevant migration and fence state updates.

If zero rows are updated, the cutover fails due to a concurrent state change.
After cutover
Keep the tenant in restricted maintenance mode until destination validation completes.

Only then should normal intake and reviewer operations resume.
15. Rollback
I'd distinguish pre-activation and post-activation rollback.
Before destination writes resume
Rollback is comparatively straightforward:
- Keep destination inactive.
- Restore source assignment.
- Verify source fence and state.
- Resume source operations.
- Record rollback.
After destination writes resume
Rollback is much more difficult.

New submissions, receipts, audit events and workflow changes may exist only at the destination.

Simply switching back to the source would lose authoritative changes.

I'd therefore define a rollback boundary.

Once destination writes are accepted, returning to the source requires a new controlled migration or coordinated recovery procedure.

The UI and CLI must make this distinction explicit.
16. Rollback policy
Stage	Permitted recovery
Before fencing	Cancel migration
Fenced, before copy	Release fence
Copying	Abandon destination copy and release fence after validation
Reconciled, before cutover	Cancel cutover and resume source
Cutover, before destination writes	Controlled assignment rollback
After destination writes	Forward recovery or new migration

The source should remain protected for an operator-defined rollback window.

It must not be automatically deleted when the migration reports success.
17. Migration CLI
I'd provide a dedicated command group.
# Create a migration plan.
secureintake tenant-migration plan \
  --organisation <organisation-id> \
  --database-profile dedicated-postgres-01 \
  --storage-profile dedicated-r2-01

# Validate source and destination.
secureintake tenant-migration preflight \
  --migration <migration-id>

# Enter tenant maintenance.
secureintake tenant-migration fence \
  --migration <migration-id>

# Create the consistency checkpoint.
secureintake tenant-migration checkpoint \
  --migration <migration-id>

# Operator performs physical database and object copying externally.

# Verify destination.
secureintake tenant-migration verify \
  --migration <migration-id> \
  --full-objects

# Review cutover readiness.
secureintake tenant-migration cutover-check \
  --migration <migration-id>

# Execute approved assignment change.
secureintake tenant-migration cutover \
  --migration <migration-id>

# Verify post-cutover state.
secureintake tenant-migration postflight \
  --migration <migration-id>


The CLI must use the same v4.2 JIT and approval controls as the administrative API.

No CLI flag should bypass missing ciphertext, invalid receipts or tenant-isolation failures.
18. Operational recovery
I'd define recovery procedures for common migration failures.
Failure	Response
Source fence cannot complete	Abort before checkpoint; investigate active writers
Database copy incomplete	Keep source fenced or safely resume source
Missing destination objects	Recopy missing objects; rerun verification
Ciphertext digest mismatch	Quarantine affected destination object; block cutover
Receipt verification failure	Investigate trust/evidence; block cutover
Destination credentials fail	Repair resource profile; rerun preflight
Cutover transaction fails	Keep fence; inspect authoritative assignment
Destination fails before writes	Controlled rollback to source
Destination fails after writes	Forward recovery or new migration

Recovery actions must be idempotent where possible.

The platform should never infer that the source is safe to reactivate without checking the authoritative assignment generation and writer fences.
19. Administrative UI
I'd provide a desktop-only tenant migration page.
Tenant Migration
Awaiting verification

Example Organisation

Migration mig_01JEXAMPLE

Source

Shared PostgreSQL

Primary S3


Destination

Dedicated PostgreSQL

Dedicated R2




Tenant maintenance fence
Complete

Database copy
Operator confirmed

Object verification
In progress

Receipt verification
Pending

Audit continuity
Pending

Cutover approval
Pending

Cutover blocked

Destination object verification and audit reconciliation must complete before the assignment can change.

Illustrative migration state. No live resources are being modified.
The interface should show the exact failed checks and operator actions required.

It should not include a generic “force migration” button.
20. Database additions
I'd introduce:
tenant_migration_operations
tenant_migration_plan_versions
tenant_migration_approvals

tenant_migration_fences
tenant_migration_checkpoints

tenant_migration_record_inventories
tenant_migration_object_mappings
tenant_migration_verification_runs
tenant_migration_verification_findings

tenant_migration_cutover_records
tenant_migration_attestations
tenant_migration_rollback_records
tenant_migration_source_cleanup_records


The authoritative organisation resource assignment remains in the control plane.

Migration records must not become an alternative routing source.
21. Audit events
I'd add:
tenant_migration.planned
tenant_migration.approved
tenant_migration.fence_started
tenant_migration.fence_completed

tenant_migration.checkpoint_created
tenant_migration.external_copy_declared

tenant_migration.verification_started
tenant_migration.verification_completed
tenant_migration.verification_failed

tenant_migration.cutover_requested
tenant_migration.cutover_completed
tenant_migration.cutover_failed

tenant_migration.rollback_requested
tenant_migration.rollback_completed

tenant_migration.source_cleanup_requested
tenant_migration.source_cleanup_completed


Audit events should include the migration ID, organisation ID, resource assignment generations, manifest digests, verification reports, approvals and outcome.

No storage credentials, private keys or decrypted submission data should appear in migration audit records.
22. Implementation priorities
I'd implement v4.6 in this order:
1. Migration plan and resource compatibility validation.
2. Tenant maintenance fencing with monotonic generations.
3. Consistent checkpoint and record inventory.
4. Destination database reconciliation.
5. Full ciphertext object verification.
6. Receipt, evidence and audit continuity validation.
7. Approval-controlled authoritative cutover.
8. Post-cutover validation and controlled rollback.
9. Source cleanup governance.
10. CLI, desktop administration and audit.
The governing principle is that physical migration remains operator-managed, while Secure Intake provides the controls necessary to establish a consistent source, verify the destination and safely change the authoritative tenant routing assignment.

23. Next decision: tenant quotas and resource governance
With tenant provisioning and migration established, the next architectural area is controlling storage consumption, upload concurrency, submission limits and operational resource usage across shared and dedicated deployments.

Simple per-organisation quotas

Storage limits, maximum file size, submission limits and basic rate limiting.


Hierarchical resource governance

Deployment, organisation and dropbox quotas with atomic reservations, concurrency controls, fair scheduling, usage reporting and administrative overrides.


Adaptive resource governance

Adds dynamic throttling, workload prioritisation, automated capacity policies and adaptive scheduling based on operational metrics.


Continue architecture 

















For the secure file-intake platform, tenant quotas and resource governance should use hierarchical deployment, organisation and dropbox quotas with reservations and fair scheduling. Continue planning storage quotas, upload limits, concurrency, reservations, worker fairness, rate limits, usage reporting, overrides, enforcement, reconciliation and audit.













Secure File Intake — Architecture v4.7
Hierarchical quotas · Storage accounting · Upload reservations · Concurrency · Fair scheduling · Rate limits · Usage reporting · Overrides · Reconciliation · Audit

Decision confirmed: Secure Intake will enforce hierarchical resource governance at deployment, organisation and dropbox levels, with durable reservations and fair background-job scheduling.

The design remains self-hostable, works with SQLite or PostgreSQL, and requires no Redis, message broker or external rate-limiting service.

The main rule is:

Every resource-consuming operation must be admitted against the applicable limits before it consumes capacity. Reserved capacity must be accounted for atomically, and one organisation must not be able to monopolise shared infrastructure.
1. Resource governance architecture
Deployment limits

Total storage budget · Global concurrency · Worker capacity · Request limits


Organisation limits

Storage · Submissions · Upload sessions · Background-job share


Dropbox limits

File size · Submission size · File count · Active uploads · Rate limits


Admission and reservation service

Atomic checks · Durable reservations · Expiry · Reconciliation


Uploads


Storage


Workers




The deployment limit is an aggregate capacity constraint. Organisation and dropbox limits are additional constraints, not allocations that must sum to the deployment total.

For example, 100 organisations may each have a 100 GB maximum even when the deployment's overall storage budget is 2 TB. Admission checks enforce the actual aggregate capacity.
2. Quota categories
I'd define six categories initially.
Category	Controls
Storage	Committed ciphertext, reserved bytes, temporary uploads
Submission	Maximum files, ciphertext bytes, submission count
Upload	Concurrent sessions, chunk size, upload duration
API	Request rate, burst capacity, expensive operations
Workers	Concurrent jobs, weighted scheduling, queue backlog
Evidence	Concurrent exports, export size, temporary package storage

Retention and legal holds are separate policies.

A quota must never authorise deletion of legally retained evidence.
Default enforcement behaviour
Limit	Behaviour
Maximum file size	Reject oversized file
Maximum submission size	Reject submission exceeding permitted total
Storage quota	Reject new reservation
Concurrent upload limit	Return retryable admission failure
API rate limit	Return HTTP 429
Worker concurrency	Queue eligible job
Evidence export concurrency	Queue or reject according to operation policy

An organisation already over quota must still be able to perform authorised administration, evidence review, retention checks and appropriate deletion operations.
3. Quota hierarchy
I'd implement typed quota resolution rather than arbitrary JSON merging.
Example configuration
{
  "resourceGovernance": {
    "deployment": {
      "storageBudgetBytes": 2199023255552,
      "maxConcurrentUploads": 500,
      "maxConcurrentWorkers": 32
    },
    "organisationDefaults": {
      "storageQuotaBytes": 107374182400,
      "maxConcurrentUploads": 20,
      "maxFileBytes": 1073741824
    },
    "dropboxDefaults": {
      "maxConcurrentUploads": 5,
      "maxFilesPerSubmission": 25,
      "maxSubmissionBytes": 5368709120
    }
  }
}


These are illustrative values, not recommended universal production limits.
Resolution
Effective admission =
    Deployment capacity available
    AND Organisation allowance available
    AND Dropbox allowance available
    AND Operation-specific constraints satisfied


Unlike v4.3 security policies, quota overrides are not necessarily strengthening-only.

An authorised operator may increase an organisation's quota within the deployment's permitted resource policy.

An organisation administrator may lower dropbox limits, but must not exceed the organisation's delegated ceilings.
4. Storage accounting model
I'd distinguish four storage quantities.
Quantity	Meaning
Committed	Authoritative retained ciphertext bytes
Reserved	Capacity promised to in-progress operations
Temporary	Uploaded ciphertext not yet finalised
Reclaimable	Eligible for cleanup, but not yet physically removed

Reclaimable bytes must still count against physical capacity until deletion is confirmed.

I'd also distinguish logical quota accounting from actual provider billing.

For example, object versioning, replication and multipart upload remnants may consume provider storage that is not represented by the logical ciphertext total.

The platform can report known logical usage and observed physical usage, but should not claim exact provider billing totals without authoritative provider data.
5. Reservation lifecycle
Every upload session should obtain a durable reservation before receiving upload capabilities.
Requested
    ↓
Reserved
    ↓
Uploading
    ↓
Finalising
    ↓
Committed


Exceptional states:
Expired
Cancelled
Released
Reconciliation required


A reservation must not be released merely because the browser disconnected.

The system must account for already-issued presigned URLs, outstanding multipart uploads and potentially uploaded ciphertext.
Reservation record
public sealed record StorageReservation(
    Guid ReservationId,
    Guid OrganisationId,
    Guid DropboxId,
    Guid UploadSessionId,
    long ReservedBytes,
    DateTimeOffset ExpiresAt,
    string State,
    long ResourceGeneration);


The reservation should be bound to the current tenant resource assignment generation from v4.6.

A stale reservation must not authorise writes to a migrated or suspended tenant.
6. Atomic reservation enforcement
